## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/operating-systems-en

Most operating-systems textbooks start with definitions. This one starts by breaking things. Every chapter opens with a concrete scenario of what fails if the OS did not provide a particular abstraction — a program that holds the whole machine hostage, two threads whose increments silently disappear, a file whose contents turn to stale garbage after a crash — and then introduces the mechanism that repairs the breakage. By the end you do not just know what a page table is; you know why paging has to exist.

Three storylines run the length of the book's 26 chapters. One follows a single running process as competitors appear one at a time, from Chapter 2's solitary owner of the machine to Chapter 26's containers and clusters. Another traces a single memory reference — int *x = malloc(...); *x = 42; — through every hardware and kernel layer that stands between the store instruction and the byte landing in RAM. A third takes a broken concurrent counter and, over six chapters, evolves it into a correct program, introducing a new synchronization primitive at each step and showing exactly one thing the previous primitive could not cleanly express.

The book is uncommonly honest about modern hardware. Chapter 7 gives the actual sched_prio_to_weight[] values Linux uses today, not a pedagogical approximation. Chapter 22 distinguishes SATA from NVMe latencies and decomposes SSD write amplification into its three real sources. Chapter 25 enumerates all eight outcomes of a crash during a three-write file-system operation and maps each to its journaling recovery behavior. Chapter 26 walks a kubectl apply from cluster scheduler down through clone(2) , cgroups, and overlayfs, pointing at the exact earlier chapter each step reuses — so the cloud ends the book not as a new topic but as a recomposition of what came before.

For students who want to open a Linux kernel commit or a man page and understand why a mechanism is designed the way it is — not just what it does — this is the book to read.

**Target audience**：CS undergraduates (juniors/seniors) taking a first operating systems course; software engineers who want to understand what happens "below" their code; early-stage systems programmers working on drivers, kernels, or performance-critical infrastructure.

**Prerequisites**：Basic programming (preferably C/C++); data structures and algorithms at the level of a standard undergraduate course; minimal computer architecture background (what a register and an instruction are) — additional hardware ideas are introduced as needed.

**Study hours**：120–150 hours of active study, corresponding to a one-semester undergraduate course plus exercise work.

## Introduction

This is a textbook about operating systems written from a particular stance: that an operating system is best understood not as a collection of algorithms and data structures, but as a set of _abstractions_ the OS designer invents to paper over the gap between what the hardware offers and what programs need. Every chapter in this book is, at bottom, an answer to one recurring question: _Without this mechanism, what breaks?_ Each chapter begins by showing you the broken world and then introduces the abstraction as the patch.

This approach is not original. Silberschatz's _Operating System Concepts_ , Tanenbaum's _Modern Operating Systems_ , and most centrally Remzi and Andrea Arpaci-Dusseau's _Operating Systems: Three Easy Pieces_ all shape the field's teaching tradition, and this book owes a deep debt to each — most especially to _Three Easy Pieces_ , whose organization around the three core virtualizations (CPU, memory, persistence) is the framing we adopt and extend. What we try to add is a stricter commitment to _the example-then-pattern-then-definition_ order: no definition appears until the reader has already seen the thing being defined in action. When a concept has a counterintuitive consequence, we surface it as a "Common Misconception" box before you have a chance to form the misconception on your own.

The book is designed for a single semester — roughly twelve to fourteen weeks — for computer science undergraduates who have completed a systems programming course and are comfortable with C. We assume familiarity with pointers, arrays, and basic data structures; we assume no prior OS exposure. We do not assume familiarity with assembly language, but we do show small assembly fragments where hardware mechanics must be inspected directly.

#### How the book is organized

The twenty-six chapters are grouped into four parts, corresponding to the four "virtualization stories" an OS tells.

**Part 1 (Chapters 1–8): The process abstraction and CPU virtualization.** What is a process? How does the OS convince hundreds of programs that each owns the CPU? What are the hardware mechanisms — interrupts, mode bits, context switches — that make this possible? Why do these mechanisms need to interact with scheduling, and what scheduling policies are worth knowing? Chapter 8 closes the arc by showing how the kernel itself handles interrupts and exceptions in a way that preserves these illusions.

**Part 2 (Chapters 9–15): The address space and memory virtualization.** How does the OS give each process a flat, private, effectively-infinite address space on top of a small, shared, physical memory? The journey moves from base-and-bounds through segmentation, paging, TLBs, multi-level page tables, and culminates in swapping and page-replacement policies. This part ends in Chapter 15, where the reader can trace a single memory access from an application's `*ptr` all the way through the MMU, TLB, page table walk, page fault handler, disk I/O, and back.

**Part 3 (Chapters 16–21): Concurrency.** Once a single process can have multiple threads and processes can share state via the file system and shared memory, a new class of problems opens up. Threads race; locks are needed; locks have cost and have bugs. This part builds from the broken counter in Chapter 16 through hardware atomic instructions, locked data structures, condition variables, semaphores, and ends with a survey of concurrency bugs and deadlock. The single concrete example — a mutex evolving from a spinlock to a fair, performant blocking lock — threads through all six chapters as a worked example.

**Part 4 (Chapters 22–26): Persistence and beyond.** How do files end up on disks? What prevents the file system from melting when the power fails? How do modern file systems balance performance and durability? And how do the OS concepts of the first twenty-five chapters extend to virtual machines, containers, and distributed systems?

#### Three threading plans

A concrete worry about OS textbooks is that the subject naturally fragments into loosely-connected topics: scheduling does not need to talk about paging, paging does not need to talk about file systems, and file systems do not seem to need concurrency. The book threads three running stories through its chapters to prevent fragmentation.

**Plan A — Process multiplication.** A single example (initially a word processor) is "forked" in Chapter 2, and we watch over several chapters as that single process becomes a tree of processes with shared file descriptors, pipe endpoints, and cooperating coroutines. When Chapter 23 returns to file descriptors and fork, the reader has already met `fork`'s consequences in the CPU-virtualization arc. The file system arc then extends those consequences.

**Plan B — The page fault story.** Chapter 9 introduces lazy allocation by asking "what happens when `malloc` returns a pointer to memory we have not yet touched?" The same example is reused, sharpened, and extended across paging, TLBs, multi-level page tables, and faults, until Chapter 14 traces the complete 11-step journey of a single write through the MMU on the first touch. Chapter 15 then examines what page-replacement policy decides _which_ page should be evicted to make room for this new one.

**Plan C — The mutex evolution.** Chapter 16 shows a broken counter under concurrent increments. Chapter 17 fixes it with a spinlock built on test-and-set. Chapter 18 uses locks inside data structures. Chapter 19 introduces condition variables by observing that busy-waiting locks are wasteful when the waiter must block on a condition. Chapter 20 generalizes with semaphores. Chapter 21 returns to the mutex and catalogs the ways it — and any lock — can go wrong. One example, six chapters, one growing story.

A reader who wants to see any of these threads in isolation can follow its chapters in order; a reader taking the course in sequence will find them mutually reinforcing.

#### Exercises

Each chapter ends with 8–15 exercises in three tiers.

_Concept Check_ (≈4 per chapter). Short questions that test whether the reader followed the chapter's main claims. Each has a definite answer that a careful reader can produce without re-reading.

_Application Problems_ (≈4 per chapter). Problems that ask the reader to apply the chapter's mechanisms to concrete scenarios — designing a scheduler, sizing a page table, computing the cost of an fsync, writing pseudocode for a container launcher. These require synthesis, and many have multiple reasonable answers.

_Think Deeper_ (2–3 per chapter). Open-ended questions that connect the chapter's material to larger design trade-offs, to historical debates in the field, or to the reader's own experience. These have no single correct answer and are designed for written response or class discussion.

At least two exercises per chapter make the reader recall material from an earlier chapter, reinforcing the threading plans above.

#### On the book's limitations

An undergraduate OS book cannot be comprehensive. We chose depth over breadth. Topics we give short shrift to include: security (sandboxing, capabilities, modern exploit mitigation), real-time scheduling, formal verification of kernels, kernel-bypass I/O (io_uring, RDMA, DPDK), and heterogeneous compute (GPUs, TPUs, FPGAs as scheduled resources). Chapter 26 surveys these briefly; a reader hungry for more should turn to research papers, the Linux kernel source, or follow-on texts listed at the end.

#### A personal note from the Socratopia team

We built this textbook for our own product: Socratopia, a learning app in which an AI companion teaches students through Socratic dialogue using a carefully-curated library of texts. The textbook had to be unusually careful about structural markers — every definition, every misconception, every exercise label — because an AI reading the book must be able to locate these structures automatically. We hope the structural discipline also helps human readers: you should always know, from the surrounding markers, whether a paragraph is introducing an example, stating a pattern, defining a concept, flagging a misconception, or posing a question. If you ever feel lost, look for the nearest **Definition:** or **Pattern.** sentence; that is the invariant you can hold on to.

Happy reading. Operating systems are one of the most conceptually dense topics in all of computer science. By the end of Chapter 26, every layer between your keyboard and the electrons moving through your CPU will be less mysterious — and what remains mysterious will be mysterious in a more productive way.

— SocraLab

Cambridge / San Francisco / 2026

## Chapter 1: Why the Operating System Exists

Imagine we delete the operating system from the computer in front of you. Not the kernel, not the drivers, not the libraries — everything. The laptop boots and hands the CPU to the first program in memory. That program has complete, unmediated, exclusive use of the machine.

What is the first thing you notice?

You cannot run a second program. There is no one to load it, no one to move the first program out of the way, no one to decide whose turn it is to run. If your code has a bug and writes past the end of an array, it happily overwrites whatever it hits — maybe part of itself, maybe part of the firmware, maybe the device registers that control the disk. If you press the power button, nothing catches the signal: the CPU is running your loop. If you wanted to save a document, you would have to hand-write commands that move disk heads to specific cylinders and issue raw SATA packets to the disk controller.

This is the world without an operating system. It is a world where a single program has direct, unmediated access to unreliable, exclusive, hard-to-program hardware. Every problem in this book — every mechanism, every abstraction, every data structure we will study — exists because this world is intolerable.

The question we will ask twenty-six times in this book is simple: **without this mechanism, what breaks?** We will first show a concrete failure. Then we will introduce the mechanism that repairs it. By the end, you will be able to read a Linux `man` page or a kernel commit message and understand, at three layers, _why_ the designers did what they did.

### 1.1 The Three Problems

Strip away the marketing, and the operating system is the answer to three questions.

**Virtualization.** The hardware gives us a small number of physical resources — one CPU (or a few), one pool of DRAM, one disk. Thousands of programs want to run as if each had the whole machine to itself. The OS takes a physical resource and presents it as many virtual copies, each isolated and each giving its user the illusion of private ownership.

**Concurrency.** Those virtual copies must all make progress in parallel on the same physical hardware. The OS must coordinate the sharing so that the guarantees of virtualization are preserved even under simultaneous access. Inside the OS itself, and inside modern applications, multiple threads race against each other. Protecting shared state is the hardest technical problem in this book; six chapters are devoted to it.

**Persistence.** The CPU and memory forget everything when the power goes out. Disks and SSDs do not. The OS bridges these two worlds: it provides files that survive crashes and reboots while giving programs a simple interface (`open`, `read`, `write`) rather than raw block-level device commands. The persistence story ends with crash consistency — guaranteeing that a file system is well-formed even if the power fails mid-update.

Every chapter you are about to read sits under one of these three headings. The book's structure is not arbitrary; it retraces, problem by problem, the architecture of any real operating system.

#### Definition: Operating System

An **operating system** is the layer of software that:

  1. Virtualizes each physical resource of the computer (CPU, memory, devices) into private-feeling abstractions for each running program, and
  2. Coordinates sharing of those resources among all running programs, and
  3. Provides persistent storage that survives across crashes and reboots.

In plain language, this means: the OS is the thing that makes your laptop feel like it has infinite CPUs and infinite memory and a reliable filesystem, when in fact it has one CPU, finite memory, and a disk that might lose power mid-write at any moment. The OS is the illusion-maker.

That definition sounds soft, but every abstraction the OS provides is backed by concrete hardware and concrete software mechanisms. Let us see why the mechanisms are required at all.

### 1.2 Without Virtualization, What Breaks?

Consider a program that allocates a 1 MB array and writes the value 7 into element zero. The assembly is roughly:
    
    
      mov  rax, [heap_pointer]       ; rax := address of our array
      mov  qword [rax], 7            ; write 7 at that address
    

On bare hardware without virtualization, `heap_pointer` is just a number — a byte offset into the single shared DRAM. Suppose we set it to `0x1000`. The `mov` writes the literal byte 7 at physical address `0x1000`.

Now suppose a second program wants to do the same thing. It loads _its_ heap pointer, which also holds `0x1000`, and writes the value 42. Both programs "allocated memory." Neither knew the other existed. One of them just stomped on the other's data.

The bug is worse than data corruption. On a real machine, address `0x1000` might not be free memory at all — it might be the firmware's interrupt vector table, or the beginning of the disk controller's memory-mapped registers. "Writing the value 7 to memory" can, without protection, turn off the disk, reboot the machine, or silently corrupt the next instruction the CPU is about to execute.

The OS fixes this by **giving each program its own virtual address space**. In program 1, `heap_pointer = 0x1000` means _program 1's_ address `0x1000`. In program 2, `heap_pointer = 0x1000` means _program 2's_ address `0x1000`. The OS, with hardware help, translates each of those addresses into a different physical address before any actual memory cell is touched. Neither program can name, reach, or damage the other. Chapters 9–15 build this mechanism piece by piece. Right now, the point is: **without virtualization, a typo in any program can crash any other.**

#### Common Misconception

> _The OS just keeps programs out of each other's memory by being careful with pointers._

No. The OS does not inspect each load and store — that would be impossibly slow. The OS configures _hardware_ (specifically, the memory management unit, the MMU) to perform the translation and protection. Every load and store goes through hardware translation automatically. The OS only gets involved on setup and on exceptions. If you confuse "OS protection" with "software checks," you will keep asking how the OS can afford to check every pointer. The answer is: it does not. Hardware checks every pointer on the OS's behalf. We will build up to this in Chapter 10 and Chapter 11.

### 1.3 Without Concurrency Control, What Breaks?

Turn off the CPU's timer interrupt and hand one program the CPU. That program runs until it voluntarily yields or terminates. This is called _cooperative multitasking_. The entire Mac operating system worked this way until 2001.

The failure mode is obvious after one minute of use. A program with a bug enters an infinite loop; no other program runs again until you power-cycle the machine. A program opens a modal dialog; the animation on another screen freezes. A poorly written application hangs; your music player can no longer even refill its audio buffer, and the speakers click into silence.

The hardware fix is the **timer interrupt** : once every few milliseconds, the hardware raises a signal that steals the CPU from whatever program is running and transfers control to the kernel. The kernel then decides which program to run next. This is _preemptive_ multitasking, and it is what lets a single runaway process fail to hang your laptop. Chapter 4 walks through the exact mechanism; Chapters 5–8 decide who the CPU should be given to.

So far, concurrency looks like a scheduling problem. But concurrency also appears _inside_ a single program. Suppose two threads each run
    
    
      mov  rax, [counter]
      add  rax, 1
      mov  [counter], rax
    

If both threads execute that sequence at the same time, an interleaving exists where both read the same value (say, `41`), both add 1 (`42`), both write back (`42`). Two increments happened but the counter only moved once. The second thread's work vanished. No compiler warned anyone. No machine check fired. The bug is silent, rare, and catastrophically hard to reproduce.

This is the **race condition** , and it is where most production OS bugs live. Chapters 16–21 study it. We will see the hardware instructions that make atomic updates possible (`test-and-set`, `compare-and-swap`), the locks that use those instructions, and — most importantly — the reasoning style (invariant-based, not trace-based) you need to argue that concurrent code is correct.

### 1.4 Without Persistence, What Breaks?

Push the power button. All of DRAM is gone. The CPU registers are gone. The program you were running is gone.

You need a place to store state that survives the power cycle. That place is the disk (or SSD, or flash chip — the details differ, but the logical role is the same). Hardware gives us block-level access: "read this 4 KB sector," "write this 4 KB sector."

Programs should not think in sectors. If every program had to manage block allocation, track free space, and remember which sector held which byte, each program would reinvent a file system, and none would agree with any other. Data would be unreadable across programs.

The OS provides files — named sequences of bytes — and directories — named hierarchical maps from names to files. A program says `open("/home/alice/note.txt")` and does not know, does not need to know, whether that name is on a spinning disk at sector 10,384,192, on an SSD whose flash translation layer remaps logical sectors to physical cells, or on a server in another building. Chapters 23–26 show how that interface is built on top of unreliable sectors that can lose power mid-write.

And power _will_ fail mid-write. The last chapter of the persistence arc (Chapter 25) is entirely about guaranteeing file-system consistency across crashes — a problem whose naive solution ("just write carefully") turns out to be insufficient in subtle and expensive ways.

### 1.5 Kernel Mode and User Mode

Now a structural question. The mechanisms we have sketched — virtualizing memory, preempting the CPU, mediating access to disk — all require a piece of software that _cannot be bypassed_. If ordinary programs could turn off the timer, edit the page table, or write directly to the disk controller's registers, every protection we set up would collapse the moment a malicious or buggy program tried.

So hardware provides two modes of execution.

**User mode** is the restricted mode. In user mode, the CPU refuses to execute certain privileged instructions. It will not let you `hlt` the processor, modify the page table base register, or change the interrupt mask. If you try, the CPU raises an exception.

**Kernel mode** is unrestricted. In kernel mode, every instruction is available, every memory region is accessible (subject to the page tables the kernel itself installed), every device can be touched.

The mode is stored in a single bit in a CPU register. Who controls that bit? The hardware, together with a carefully designed mechanism: user code cannot flip the bit to kernel mode directly. The only way from user mode to kernel mode is via a **trap** : the user program executes a special instruction (`syscall` on x86-64), the hardware atomically saves the current state, flips the mode bit to kernel, and jumps to a fixed kernel entry point. The kernel code at that entry point services the request, then executes `sysret` (or similar), which flips the mode bit back to user and resumes user code. Chapter 4 walks through every step of this dance.

#### Definition: Privileged Instruction

A **privileged instruction** is a CPU instruction that the hardware refuses to execute while the CPU is in user mode, raising an exception instead.

In plain language, this means: some instructions are so dangerous (they can disable protections, halt the CPU, or access devices) that the hardware guarantees only the kernel can run them. User programs may _request_ equivalent effects by making a system call — the kernel considers the request, decides whether to honor it, and, if so, executes the privileged instruction on the program's behalf.

The user-mode / kernel-mode split is the single most important structural fact about the operating system. Almost every mechanism we will study presupposes it. When you read "the OS" going forward, you should mentally translate: "the software that runs in kernel mode."

### 1.6 A Very Brief History

The problems in this book were not identified all at once. They appeared roughly in the order below as hardware and usage patterns changed.

  1. **Batch systems (1950s).** A computer ran one program at a time, read from punched cards. The "operating system" was a small piece of code that loaded the next job when the previous one finished. There was no interactivity.
  2. **Multiprogramming (early 1960s).** I/O is very slow; a batch system burned 90% of CPU cycles waiting for the tape drive. Multiprogramming put a second program in memory, ready to run while the first blocked on I/O. This required memory protection — the first instance of virtualization.
  3. **Time-sharing (late 1960s, early 1970s).** Users wanted to type at a terminal and get a response in seconds, not overnight. The CPU was divided among many interactive users. This required preemption and scheduling — the first instance of concurrency control above the hardware layer.
  4. **Personal computers (late 1970s–1990s).** One person, one machine, many programs. Cooperative multitasking dominated early Mac OS and Windows; it took until the 1990s and 2000s for preemptive multitasking and protected memory to reach the desktop. Every consumer-scale collapse of an 80s desktop was an instance of a missing OS mechanism this book will describe.
  5. **Mobile (2000s).** Battery life mattered. Scheduling acquired an energy dimension. Memory became precious again, since phones had less of it than desktops.
  6. **Cloud and virtualization (2000s–).** Data centers wanted to consolidate many low-utilization servers onto one physical machine. Virtual machines — operating systems running inside operating systems — arose. Hardware added VT-x and AMD-V. Chapter 26 closes the book here.
  7. **Containers (2010s–).** VMs are heavyweight. The Linux kernel added namespaces and cgroups, letting the OS virtualize itself from the inside rather than running a separate guest OS. Every modern deployment story — Docker, Kubernetes — is about these features.

At each step, new hardware or new usage revealed a gap, and the OS grew a new mechanism to fill it. No major category of problem has gone away: today's containers solve the same sharing and protection problems that time-sharing solved in 1970, but with finer granularity and at a different scale.

### 1.7 How to Read This Book

A warning before we begin.

You will constantly be tempted to ask "what is this mechanism?" when the more productive question is "which problem does this mechanism solve?" Every mechanism is the answer to a design pressure. If you learn the mechanism without the pressure, you will be able to recite definitions and fail to generalize. If you learn the pressure, you will reinvent the mechanism — possibly in a better form than the one the industry standardized on.

A second warning. You will frequently encounter a layered system where the same word ("read") means something different at every layer. `read()` at the user level returns bytes from a file descriptor. The file system layer translates that into "fetch blocks from an inode." The block layer translates that into "issue a SATA command to sector N." The device driver translates that into "write this byte to memory-mapped address X, wait for an interrupt." The hardware translates that into "spin up the platter, seek the head, read the magnetic flux at the requested track." Same word, six meanings. You must always know which layer you are thinking in. Every chapter will anchor the mechanism at three layers — hardware, kernel, user-space — explicitly. If you lose track of the layer, backtrack; do not try to keep going.

A third warning about concurrency. Concurrent code is not debuggable the way sequential code is. A trace you see once does not repeat. You will have to reason about invariants — things that must always be true — rather than execute traces in your head. This style of reasoning is hard for everyone when they first meet it. Chapter 16 will warn you again, and the chapters that follow will teach the style.

Finally: do the exercises. Each chapter contains 8–15. They are not optional. Many will reference material from earlier chapters — that is the whole point. The exercises are where the course becomes yours.

### 1.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Name the three problems an operating system solves. For each, give one concrete failure you would observe on a machine that solved it poorly.

Hint

Re-read Section 1.1. The three problems are virtualization, concurrency, and persistence.

**Answer:**

* **Virtualization.** Without it, a program's stray pointer can overwrite another program's data (or, worse, firmware), crashing the whole system on any bug.
* **Concurrency.** Without coordination, two threads incrementing a shared counter can lose updates silently; at the system level, one runaway program can hang the whole machine because the OS cannot preempt it.
* **Persistence.** Without it, every power cycle erases all data, and programs must re-issue raw device commands directly, duplicating disk-management code.

A credible answer identifies the _symptom a user sees_. The problem categories themselves are not enough — the point of this chapter is that abstractions are answers to user-visible failures.

**Q2.** Explain the difference between user mode and kernel mode. Why does the hardware, not the software, enforce the boundary?

**Answer:** User mode is a restricted execution mode in which certain instructions (those that could disable protections, halt the CPU, or directly access devices) cause an exception rather than executing. Kernel mode has no such restriction. The hardware enforces the boundary because if the boundary were enforced by software, a user program could simply patch that software to bypass the check. The hardware check is mandatory, cannot be patched from user mode, and is fast (it amounts to checking a single bit on each instruction decode). Switching modes is only possible via a controlled trap path that the hardware arranges to land at a fixed kernel entry point, so the user program cannot jump into arbitrary kernel code.

**Q3.** What does "preemptive multitasking" mean, and what hardware feature makes it possible?

**Answer:** Preemptive multitasking is a scheduling discipline in which the OS can forcibly take the CPU away from a running program — without the program's cooperation — and give it to another program. The hardware feature that makes it possible is the timer interrupt: the hardware, on a schedule independent of any running program, raises a signal that diverts the CPU into the kernel, where the scheduler decides whether to switch. Without a timer interrupt, a program in an infinite loop would hold the CPU forever; this was exactly the failure mode of cooperative multitasking in classic Mac OS.

**Q4.** State the book's recurring question. Why is it useful?

**Answer:** The recurring question is: _without this mechanism, what breaks?_ It is useful because it forces you to learn _why_ each mechanism exists, not just what it is. If you know what breaks, you can generalize: if you meet a new system, you can predict which mechanisms it must have and what they should look like. If you only know definitions, you will recognize familiar mechanisms by name but fail to reason about unfamiliar ones.

**Q5.** Give a concrete example of a privileged instruction and describe what would happen if user code tried to execute it directly.

**Answer:** Any instruction that modifies the CPU's privilege level, halts the CPU, or writes the page-table base register is privileged. A concrete x86 example is `hlt` (halt the CPU). If user code executes `hlt`, the CPU raises a general-protection fault. The fault diverts control to a kernel handler that will typically terminate the offending process (delivering `SIGSEGV` on Linux, for instance). The user instruction never takes effect.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** You are asked to design a "single-tasking" operating system for an embedded device that runs exactly one program. The designer argues that since only one program runs, virtualization and concurrency mechanisms are unnecessary. Is she right? Give two arguments for or against.

Hint

Think about (a) bugs in the program itself, and (b) the boundary between the program and the hardware devices. Even with one program, does _every_ piece of code run at the same privilege?

**Answer:** The designer is partly right and partly wrong.

_For her view:_ If there is truly only one program and it is trusted, a protected address space adds no value. The cost of trap-based system calls is wasted overhead; eliminating the user/kernel split makes the system faster and simpler. This is exactly the design used in many microcontroller firmwares (TI-RTOS, older Contiki configurations).

_Against her view:_ Even a "single program" is rarely atomic. The program's drivers, interrupt handlers, and application logic are often written by different people and have different reliability assumptions. If an interrupt handler corrupts memory the application code holds live, the whole system misbehaves. Some embedded OSes (e.g., certain safety-critical configurations) therefore still run the application in a limited mode, even on single-application devices, so that a buggy interrupt service routine cannot corrupt the application's state. The tradeoff is performance vs robustness, and it depends on the safety requirements of the device.

**Q2.** Two programs each perform the following sequence on a machine without virtual memory:
    
    
      load r0 ← 0x1000        ; read 8 bytes at physical 0x1000
      add  r0 ← r0 + 1
      store 0x1000 ← r0
    

Both programs are started and run concurrently under a simple round-robin scheduler. Describe one interleaving that causes observable misbehavior, and state exactly what goes wrong.

Hint

Address `0x1000` is physical — there is no translation. What does "the same address in two programs" mean on such a machine?

**Answer:** Without virtualization, `0x1000` refers to a single physical memory cell shared by both programs. Consider the interleaving:

  1. Program A loads `0x1000` into its register. Value read: 5.
  2. Scheduler preempts; program B runs.
  3. Program B loads `0x1000` into its register. Value read: 5.
  4. Program B stores 6.
  5. Scheduler switches back to program A.
  6. Program A stores 6.

Two increments were attempted; only one took effect. Moreover, if `0x1000` happened to be a device register — say, a disk-controller status byte — the stored value is now garbage, and the disk controller may misbehave (issue spurious reads, fail to notify the CPU of completions). This is simultaneously the race-condition problem (Chapter 16) and the address-space-protection problem (Chapter 9). Virtualization separates the two cells; concurrency control prevents the interleaving for the one cell the programs _do_ share. This chapter's point is that _both_ are needed, and they are needed for different reasons.

**Q3.** A student claims: "The OS checks each memory access to ensure the program is not touching another program's memory." Explain why this claim, taken literally, is wrong. What is actually true, and what hardware feature enables the real story?

**Answer:** The claim is wrong because a modern CPU executes billions of loads and stores per second. If the _OS_ (a piece of software) had to intervene on every one, the machine would slow by five or six orders of magnitude — no such computer would be usable. What is actually true is that the CPU's memory management unit (MMU), a hardware component, translates every virtual address to a physical address on the fly and checks protection bits in the page table. The OS sets up the page tables but does not participate in any particular translation. The MMU raises an exception only when something is wrong (e.g., the access targets an invalid or protected page); only then does control trap into the OS. So the OS is involved only on setup and on exceptions, not on each memory reference. Chapters 10–13 construct this story in full.

**Q4.** Re-read Section 1.6. For each of the seven historical steps (batch, multiprogramming, time-sharing, PC, mobile, cloud/VM, containers), name the OS mechanism in this book whose arrival roughly coincides with that step.

**Answer:**

* Batch: loader + minimal job scheduler (no preemption).
* Multiprogramming: memory protection (base-and-bounds — Chapter 10); cooperative context switching (Chapter 4, in an early form).
* Time-sharing: preemption via timer interrupt (Chapter 4); scheduling policy (Chapters 5–7); full virtual memory with paging (Chapter 11) in later time-sharing systems.
* PC: protected address spaces become universal (Chapters 9–14); modern file systems with crash consistency (Chapters 23–25) become expected.
* Mobile: energy-aware scheduling refinements (generalizations of Chapters 6–7); tight memory policies and OOM killing (variants of the thrashing discussion in Chapter 15).
* Cloud/VM: hypervisors (Chapter 26), which reuse the trap/privilege machinery of Chapter 4 but one level deeper.
* Containers: namespaces and cgroups (Chapter 26), which reuse the process-abstraction machinery of Chapter 2 at a finer granularity.

This exercise maps the historical narrative onto the structural narrative of the book, reinforcing that the book's order is not arbitrary — it recapitulates the logical order in which the industry actually discovered these problems.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** The chapter argues that the OS is an "illusion-maker." Some researchers have argued that operating systems should instead expose as much of the raw hardware as possible, pushing abstraction into libraries (the "exokernel" idea). Sketch one advantage of each position. Which argument do you find more persuasive, and why?

**Discussion:** The illusion-making position's strongest argument is uniformity: applications written to an abstract interface port across wildly different hardware, and the kernel's shared code benefits from careful optimization that no individual application developer would have time to do. The exokernel position's strongest argument is flexibility: a database system may want to control its own buffer cache because it knows access patterns the kernel cannot guess; an abstraction that hides disk details from such a system costs performance. The historical answer is: real systems compromise. Mainstream OSes expose "hint" APIs (`madvise`, `O_DIRECT`) to let sophisticated applications bypass abstractions when they want. The exokernel argument is strongest for specialized workloads (databases, high-frequency-trading engines, kernel-bypass networking stacks like DPDK) and weakest for general-purpose applications where nobody wants to reinvent a buffer cache.

A good answer names at least one concrete system in each camp and identifies _why_ one picks each. It is not a question with a single right answer.

**Q2.** Consider a hypothetical CPU that had no mode bit — every program ran at the same privilege level. What would have to change about the design of an operating system on such a machine? Could it still provide any of the three guarantees (virtualization, concurrency, persistence)?

**Discussion:** Without a hardware privilege level, the kernel cannot rely on the hardware to prevent user code from doing dangerous things. Any protection must be software-enforced. Historically, this has been attempted via language-based security — write all user code in a memory-safe, verified language, and refuse to load any code that does not pass verification. Examples include the original Singularity research OS (Microsoft) and, in a different form, unikernels written in statically typed languages.

Such a design can, in principle, provide virtualization and concurrency guarantees — the language compiler and runtime enforce the isolation. Persistence is unchanged, since it depends on disks, not modes. The catch is that every program must be in the safe language (or must pass verification before running), and the verifier becomes part of the trusted computing base. One compiler bug replaces the hardware's mode bit as the single point of failure. Mainstream systems reject this tradeoff and rely on hardware privilege instead; research systems occasionally revisit the idea when the safety requirement is extreme (e.g., flight-control software).

A thoughtful answer names the trust shift (from hardware to compiler/verifier) and identifies at least one historical system that attempted this design.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter introduced the operating system as the software that solves three problems: virtualization, concurrency, and persistence. We motivated each by asking _what breaks without it_ — a stray pointer corrupts shared state; a runaway process hangs the machine; a power failure erases everything. The hardware mechanism underlying every OS-level enforcement is the **user/kernel mode bit** , which restricts user programs from executing privileged instructions; the only path from user to kernel is the **trap**. The book will revisit this trap mechanism in detail in Chapter 4.

The three problems organize the rest of the book. Chapters 2–8 build CPU virtualization and scheduling, paying off the "many processes, one CPU" question. Chapters 9–15 build memory virtualization, telling the full story of a single memory reference from a `malloc` call to a page fault served from disk — Threading Plan B begins in Chapter 9. Chapters 16–21 address concurrency, following a broken counter from one line of racy assembly (Chapter 16) through six successively more powerful synchronization tools (Threading Plan C). Chapters 22–25 address persistence, ending with journaling and log-structured file systems. Chapter 26 closes the loop one level up: the OS itself can be virtualized, and the same ideas recur.

When you open Chapter 2, we will zoom in on one program and ask: what does it mean for a program to "run"? That is the process abstraction — and the moment the book's first mechanism takes shape.

---

## Chapter 2: Processes — The Abstraction of a Running Program

In the last chapter we argued that without an operating system, one program has total and unmediated control of the machine. Now let us look inside that single program and ask: what exactly _is_ a running program?

You might think the answer is obvious. "It is the code I wrote, being executed." That is true in the same way that "weather is just air" is true. Once you try to write down what a running program consists of — everything the OS would need to remember if it wanted to pause that program, let another one run for a while, and then resume the first one exactly where it left off — the answer stops being obvious.

Start with a thought experiment. You are running a program that is halfway through a sort. You press a key on the keyboard to bring up a menu. The machine somehow switches from your sort program to the menu handler, runs the menu handler, then comes back to the sort. When the sort resumes, it picks up exactly where it left off: the partially-sorted array is still partially sorted, the loop counter still has its value, the comparison the program was about to finish still finishes correctly. No calculation is redone; no element is lost.

What did the OS have to preserve to make that work?

The answer is the **process** : the operating system's abstraction for a running program. It is the unit of work the scheduler operates on, the unit of isolation the memory system protects, and the unit of scope for nearly every guarantee the rest of this book will discuss. Getting the process abstraction right is the first mechanism the OS provides — without it, none of the later mechanisms have anything to protect or to schedule.

### 2.1 From Program to Process

A **program** is a static thing: a file on disk, a blob of bytes, the compiled output of your source code. Open it with a hex editor and you will see machine instructions, strings, constants, metadata — all sitting there inertly.

A **process** is what exists when you run that program. It is the set of everything the CPU needs to continue executing that program from wherever it currently is.

What is in that set?

**Memory.** The process has a region of memory containing (at minimum) its machine code (the _text segment_), its global variables (the _data segment_), its dynamically allocated objects (the _heap_), and its function-call state (the _stack_). Take away any of these and you cannot continue execution. If you lose the stack, you cannot return from the current function. If you lose the heap, every `malloc`-ed object vanishes.

**Registers.** The CPU has perhaps a few dozen general-purpose registers and a handful of special ones. At any instant, the process's logical state includes the exact contents of every one of them. The **program counter** (PC, sometimes called the _instruction pointer_ , IP) says which instruction comes next. The **stack pointer** (SP) says where the current stack top is. The general-purpose registers hold whatever local values the program is currently computing. Lose a single register's value and the program's arithmetic can go wrong.

**Open files.** A process usually has a notion of files it has opened — a position in each open file, the file itself, and permissions describing what it is allowed to do. A `read()` call needs to know _which file_ and _where in the file_. If the OS loses that record, the next read reads the wrong bytes.

**Some other small pieces.** Signal mask, current working directory, process ID, parent process ID, and a few more. These are all secondary compared to memory, registers, and files — but the OS keeps them all, because the running program has _made observable claims about them_ and expects them to persist.

#### Definition: Process

A **process** is the operating system's abstraction of a running program, consisting of everything the OS needs to track in order to continue the program's execution: its memory, its CPU-register state (including the program counter), its open files, and a handful of other per-program resources.

In plain language, this means: a process is the thing that would happen if you froze the running machine at an instant, carefully wrote down every bit of state that has to do with _this particular running program_ , and put that description in a box labeled "process N." To resume the program, you restore the contents of that box.

The word "process" is sometimes used loosely — "this Linux process is using too much memory" — but its strict definition is exactly the state list above. When you hear "process" in this book, picture that box.

#### Common Misconception

> _A process is just a running program; one program always corresponds to one process._

Wrong on two counts.

First, the same program can be running as many different processes at once. Open ten terminal windows, each running `bash`. One program (`/bin/bash`), ten processes. Each has its own memory, its own open files, its own register state. What they share is code text; what they do not share is anything else.

Second, a process can outlive the "running" state in the intuitive sense. A process that is _blocked_ — waiting for input, sleeping, waiting for a file read — is not currently running on any CPU, yet it is clearly still a process. The OS still tracks all of its state and will resume it when the wait ends. Chapters 17–21 make this blocked state central to everything we learn.

### 2.2 Why the Process Exists: The One-Process World

To see why the process is a useful abstraction, consider a machine that is running exactly one program right now. Nothing else. That program:

* Owns the entire CPU. Every cycle goes to its instructions. There is nothing else to run.
* Owns all of physical memory. Its text, data, heap, and stack can live wherever convenient. No other program is competing for memory cells.
* Owns every file descriptor. The keyboard, the screen, the disk — every I/O device — talks only to this program.

In this world, the "process abstraction" sounds unnecessary. Why would the OS bother packaging up everything about this one program? Nothing else is running.

The answer is that even in a one-process world, the _computer itself_ occasionally interrupts the program. A timer tick fires. A keystroke arrives. The disk completes a sector read and raises an interrupt. In each case, the CPU branches away from the program's code to run a handler. When the handler finishes, the program must resume — and to resume correctly, every register and every memory location the program was using must be exactly as it left them.

The process abstraction exists to make that resumption possible. Even when there is only one program, the OS needs to describe "everything this program needs" so that a hardware interrupt can borrow the CPU for 50 microseconds and give it back as if nothing had happened.

This is the first thread of the book, **Threading Plan A**. We start with one process and ask: what goes wrong when we add a second? Then a tenth? Then threads inside the same process? Each new piece of competition forces the OS to virtualize one more resource. By Chapter 16 the number of things competing inside a single address space will force an entirely new chapter of the story — concurrency. But the journey begins here, with a single program that owns everything.

### 2.3 Machine State: What Must Be Preserved

Let us sharpen the list. The machine state of a process has three big pieces.

#### 2.3.1 Memory

Every running program has an **address space** : a range of memory addresses it can read and write. On a 64-bit machine, the address space is conceptually huge — up to 2642^{64}264 bytes, which is 16 exabytes, far more than any real machine has. The operating system arranges that only _some_ of those addresses are actually backed by physical memory, and only _some_ regions within the backed range are meaningful to the program. We will learn how this arrangement works in Chapters 9–13.

For now, picture the address space as a linear range from address 0 to some maximum. Somewhere near the bottom is the **text** region (the machine instructions). Above it is the **data** region (globals initialized at program start). Above that is the **heap** , growing upward as `malloc` hands out memory. Way at the top is the **stack** , growing downward as functions call functions and push frames.

A typical layout on 64-bit Linux looks roughly like this (addresses in hex, low-to-high):
    
    
    0x0000000000400000   text segment (program code)
    0x0000000000600000   data segment (globals)
    0x0000000000601000   heap (grows up)   ↑ malloc extends this
            ...
             (large gap)
            ...
    0x00007fffffffe000   stack (grows down) ↓ function calls extend this
    0x00007ffffffff000   kernel boundary
    

When we talk about a process's memory, we mean _everything in that layout_. If the OS wants to pause this process and later resume it, the OS must preserve every byte of that layout — at least, every byte that matters to the program.

#### 2.3.2 Registers

The CPU has a fixed set of registers. On x86-64, the general-purpose set is 16 registers (`%rax`, `%rbx`, `%rcx`, `%rdx`, `%rsi`, `%rdi`, `%rbp`, `%rsp`, and `%r8` through `%r15`). There are also special registers:

* **Program counter** (`%rip` on x86-64). The address of the next instruction to execute. _The single most important register_ ; lose it and the program cannot continue.
* **Stack pointer** (`%rsp`). The address of the top of the stack. Functions push their return addresses and locals below it.
* **Frame pointer** (`%rbp`), used by some calling conventions.
* **Flags register** (`%rflags`), holding the last arithmetic result's sign, zero, overflow, etc. If a program just compared `a < b` and is about to branch on that result, the flags register encodes the comparison.

And a few others specific to floating-point and SIMD units (`xmm0`–`xmm15`, etc.), which hold non-trivial in-flight state for numeric code.

All of these registers together constitute the CPU's "current snapshot" of the program. To pause a process, the OS must save every register value somewhere. To resume it, the OS must restore every register value. Chapter 4 traces this save-and-restore in detail.

#### 2.3.3 Open Files and Other Resources

A process is connected to the outside world through **file descriptors**. On Unix-like systems, a file descriptor is a small integer — 0 for standard input, 1 for standard output, 2 for standard error, and others returned by `open()`. Behind each descriptor the OS tracks:

* Which file (or pipe, or socket, or device) the descriptor refers to.
* The current position in that file (a byte offset).
* The mode the file was opened with (read-only, read-write, append, and so on).

When a process calls `read(fd, buf, 4096)`, the OS looks up `fd` in that process's file descriptor table, finds the underlying file, reads from the current position, advances the position, and returns. The state of the file descriptor must persist across every pause: if a process reads 4096 bytes, gets preempted, and resumes, the _next_ read must continue from byte 4097, not start over.

Other per-process state rounds out the picture: the current working directory, the process's user ID, the signal mask (which signals are currently blocked), and so on. We will meet each of these as it becomes relevant.

### 2.4 The Process State Machine

A process is not always running. In fact, at any moment on a machine with many processes, only a few are running (one per CPU core); the rest are in various kinds of _not running_. The OS tracks which state each process is in.

Here is the minimal state machine every OS textbook draws, in plain prose:

* **Running.** The process is currently executing on a CPU core. There is at most one process in this state per core.
* **Ready.** The process could run — it has everything it needs — but no CPU is available for it. It is waiting for the scheduler to pick it.
* **Blocked.** The process cannot make progress until something external happens: a disk I/O completes, a network packet arrives, a timer expires, another process sends it data. While blocked, it is not eligible to run.

Transitions (the arrows between states) are triggered by specific events:

* **Ready → Running:** The scheduler picks this process to run on some CPU core. This happens when the previous process on that core blocks, terminates, or exhausts its time slice.
* **Running → Ready:** The process is preempted. Either its time slice ended (the timer interrupt fired) or a higher-priority process became ready.
* **Running → Blocked:** The process issued a system call that must wait. It called `read()` on a network socket with no data yet; it called `sleep(10)`; it tried to acquire a lock held by another thread. The kernel puts it in the blocked state and picks another process to run.
* **Blocked → Ready:** The event the process was waiting for happened. The disk returned data; the timer fired; the network packet arrived. The OS moves the process back to ready, where it waits its turn at the CPU.

Later chapters will add two more states. A **newly created** process exists after `fork()` but before it has been fully initialized; a **zombie** process has terminated but has not yet been reaped by its parent. Chapter 3 will treat both. For this chapter, the three states above are enough.

#### Common Misconception

> _A blocked process is "off" — it costs the system nothing._

Blocked processes cost memory. Their address space, their kernel stack, their PCB, their open-file table — all of it is kept alive. A process that has been blocked for six hours is consuming essentially the same resources as a process that blocks for six milliseconds. What blocked processes do not consume is CPU: the scheduler skips them. But nothing else about them is free. A system with 50,000 blocked connections still uses the memory for all 50,000 processes' PCBs. This is why high-performance servers often avoid one-process-per-connection designs in favor of lightweight threads or event loops.

### 2.5 The Process Control Block

To track all of this, the OS stores a data structure per process called the **Process Control Block (PCB)**. Different OSes call it different things — Linux calls it `struct task_struct` and it is enormous, several kilobytes — but every OS has one, and every OS's PCB contains (at least) the following categories of information.

**Identification.**

* Process ID (PID): a small integer uniquely identifying this process.
* Parent process ID (PPID): the PID of the process that created this one.
* User ID and group ID: for permission checks.

**CPU state (when not currently running).**

* Saved registers: enough space to hold every register value. When the process is preempted, the OS writes the CPU's register values here; when resumed, the OS copies these values back into CPU registers.
* Saved program counter: where to resume execution.

**Memory state.**

* A pointer (or set of pointers) to the process's page tables, describing its address space. Chapters 9–13 will explain these in detail.
* Often, a summary of the address space layout: where the text, data, heap, and stack live.

**Scheduling information.**

* Current state (running / ready / blocked).
* Priority, accumulated CPU time, last-run timestamp — data the scheduler needs to pick the next process. Chapters 5–8 will use these.

**Open files and I/O.**

* Pointer to the process's file descriptor table.
* Current working directory.

**Other per-process data.**

* Signal handlers and signal mask.
* Exit status (set when the process exits, read by the parent via `wait()`).

Here is a conceptual sketch of a Linux `task_struct`, drastically simplified:
    
    
    task_struct {
      pid_t           pid;               // process ID
      pid_t           ppid;              // parent PID
      int             state;             // running / ready / blocked / zombie
      struct regs     saved_regs;        // saved CPU state
      struct mm       *mm;               // memory layout and page tables
      struct files    *files;            // open file descriptors
      struct signal   *signal;           // signal handlers, signal mask
      long            priority;          // scheduling priority
      u64             utime, stime;      // CPU time consumed in user / kernel mode
      struct task_struct *parent;        // pointer to parent
      struct list_head children;         // list of child processes
      // ... dozens of other fields
    }
    

The actual Linux struct is over a hundred fields; we can ignore most for now. The structural point is: **the PCB is the operating system's definition of a process**. Whatever fields are in the PCB is what "a process is" in that OS. The state machine above is a field (`state`). The address space is reached via a pointer (`mm`). The process abstraction is nothing more than "whatever the OS tracks in its per-process data structure," and the PCB _is_ that data structure.

### 2.6 Creating, Running, and Terminating a Process

With the PCB in mind, a process's life cycle becomes concrete.

**Creation.** When the OS creates a process, it:

  1. Allocates a new PCB.
  2. Assigns it a fresh PID.
  3. Sets up the process's initial memory — typically by loading the program from disk into the address space, or by copying the parent's address space in the case of `fork()` (Chapter 3).
  4. Sets up the initial CPU state: PC pointing at the program's entry point, stack pointer pointing at a freshly allocated stack, other registers initialized.
  5. Sets up the file descriptor table, starting with the standard 0/1/2 pointing at the terminal (or whatever the parent was using).
  6. Marks the process as _ready_ and hands it to the scheduler.

**Running.** When the scheduler picks this process, it:

  1. Restores the PCB's saved registers into the CPU.
  2. Sets the program counter to the saved PC.
  3. Returns from the kernel into user mode, at which point the CPU starts executing the user program's instructions.

**Termination.** When a process calls `exit()` (or is killed):

  1. The kernel closes all its open file descriptors.
  2. Releases its memory.
  3. Moves the PCB into the _zombie_ state, keeping only the exit status.
  4. Notifies the parent process (usually via the `SIGCHLD` signal).
  5. When the parent collects the exit status via `wait()`, the kernel finally frees the PCB.

The zombie state exists because the exit status — a small integer the parent wants to see — has to be preserved between the moment a process dies and the moment its parent asks for it. Chapter 3 will walk through `wait()` in detail.

### 2.7 Why Put the Program Counter in a Register?

A brief aside that many students find clarifying.

Why does the CPU have a dedicated register to hold the address of the next instruction? Why is there no equivalent dedicated register for "address of the next instruction after the one after that"?

The answer lies in how instructions flow. The CPU fetches an instruction, decodes it, executes it, and advances to the next. The "address of the next instruction" is needed _every single cycle_ ; it is the most-read piece of state in the machine. A general-purpose register would not do — we need a specific hardware unit that reads the PC, uses it to fetch, and increments it (or alters it, on a branch). Making it a first-class register lets us reason about "where the process is in its execution" as a single number.

When a process is paused, "saving the program counter" means copying this register's value into the PCB. When the process resumes, "restoring the program counter" means the opposite. Pausing without saving the PC is fatal: on resume, the CPU would start executing at the wrong instruction, and the behavior would be complete garbage.

Now extrapolate. Every register we listed in Section 2.3.2 plays a similar role for some part of the program's state. The flags register holds the last comparison's outcome. The stack pointer holds the current top of the stack. The integer registers hold whatever arithmetic is in flight. Every one of them is, implicitly, a place where "some aspect of the running program" lives at this exact moment. And every one of them must be saved and restored when the OS pauses and resumes the process. This is why context switches — which we will examine in Chapter 4 — copy tens of registers on the way in and out.

### 2.8 A First Look at the "One Process Owns Everything" World

Threading Plan A — one of the three storylines running through the book — begins with a machine executing exactly one process. Let us pause and take it seriously.

**The CPU.** The single process has every cycle. There is no scheduler to run. There is no context switch. The PC advances instruction by instruction, interrupted only by hardware interrupts (which we will discuss in Chapter 4).

**The memory.** The single process owns all of physical memory. No translation is needed because no other process is competing for the same physical cells. An `int *p = malloc(...)` gives the process a real, physical address; a store through `p` goes straight to that physical cell. There is no page table, no TLB miss, no swap.

**The files.** The single process has every file descriptor. The disk belongs to it. The screen belongs to it. The keyboard belongs to it. There is no mediation because nobody else is asking for any of them.

In this world, the operating system is nearly trivial. It is a loader: it puts the program in memory, sets PC to the entry point, and gets out of the way. This is essentially what early batch systems (1950s) did. The OS existed, but only barely.

The moment we add a second process, every one of the three statements above breaks.

* **The CPU** must be shared. Some mechanism must interleave two programs' instructions. Chapter 4 introduces context switching; Chapters 5–8 decide whose turn it is.
* **The memory** must be shared. If both processes naïvely use physical address 0x1000, they corrupt each other. Chapters 9–13 introduce virtual memory, translation, and protection.
* **The files** must be shared. Two processes may open the same file; the OS must keep their positions independent. Chapter 23 introduces the open file table.

This book's main job is to walk you through every one of those "must be shared" solutions in precise detail. But it is easier to appreciate the solutions if you remember the baseline — the world where one process has everything — because every mechanism we add is answering some failure of that baseline.

As Plan A unfolds, each new chapter adds competitors. Chapter 4 adds a second process. Chapter 5 adds ten. Chapter 9 generalizes to "many processes each wanting address 0." Chapter 16 adds threads inside a single process — bringing all the sharing problems _inside_ the abstraction that was supposed to solve them. Chapter 23 adds concurrent access to shared files. Chapter 26, the final chapter, adds whole _machines_ running concurrently.

Every step is preceded by a question of the form: "What breaks when we add N competitors?" Every step is answered by a mechanism. The mechanisms are the content of the book. The process abstraction, introduced in this chapter, is the container they all live inside.

### 2.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** List the four most important categories of state the OS must track for each process.

Hint

Section 2.3. Four big buckets.

**Answer:** (1) **Memory** (the process's address space: text, data, heap, stack). (2) **CPU register state** (general-purpose registers, program counter, stack pointer, flags, and any special-purpose registers — everything that must be identical on resume for the program to continue correctly). (3) **Open files and I/O state** (the file descriptor table, with per-descriptor information like current offset and mode). (4) **Scheduling and identification metadata** (PID, state, priority, signal mask, parent PID, working directory, etc.). All four categories together form the content of the PCB.

**Q2.** Define "process" in a single sentence. Contrast it with "program."

**Answer:** A _process_ is the OS's abstraction for a running program — the complete set of state the OS tracks so that the program's execution can be paused and resumed correctly. A _program_ is a static artifact on disk (a compiled executable file); it has no state, no memory, no register values. Running a program creates a process; running it twice creates two independent processes sharing at most the program's code text.

**Q3.** Name the three main states of a process and give the event that causes each transition between them.

**Answer:**

* **Running → Ready** triggered by _preemption_ (time slice expires or higher-priority process becomes ready).
* **Running → Blocked** triggered by _waiting for a resource_ (I/O syscall, waiting for a lock, sleep).
* **Blocked → Ready** triggered by _the awaited event occurring_ (I/O completes, lock released, sleep expires).
* **Ready → Running** triggered by the _scheduler picking this process_ for a free CPU core.

**Q4.** What is a Process Control Block, and what would happen if the OS accidentally zeroed out one process's PCB while that process was ready to run?

**Answer:** The PCB is the per-process data structure where the OS stores all state that belongs to that process — PID, saved registers, memory layout pointer, open-file table, scheduling state, and so on. If it were zeroed out while the process was ready, the OS would lose the process's saved register values (the program counter included), the pointer to its address space, and the file descriptor table. When the scheduler next tried to run the process, it would restore zeros into the CPU registers and jump to address 0, which would almost certainly be invalid; the process would die with a fault. Its memory would remain allocated but unreachable (leaking). The OS itself might crash or deadlock depending on how badly the PCB was relied upon elsewhere.

**Q5.** Why is the program counter considered the most important register? What would go wrong if it were saved but all other registers were not?

**Answer:** The program counter tells the CPU which instruction to execute next; without it, the CPU cannot continue at the correct location after a context switch, and resumption is impossible. But saving only the PC is not enough. Suppose a process was halfway through `rax = rbx + rcx; rdx = rax * 2;` when it was preempted, and the OS saved only the PC (pointing at the multiply). On resume, `rax` contains a value from some _other_ process (the one that ran in between), and the multiply produces garbage. Every register participates in the program's logical state; the PC is the single most important, but all must be saved, or the program's computation is corrupted.

**Q6.** In Threading Plan A, the book starts with one process that owns everything. Name three resources that must be virtualized the moment a second process is added, and state what breaks if they are not.

**Answer:**

* **CPU time.** Without sharing, the first process runs forever; the second never starts. The OS must preempt and context-switch (Chapter 4) and choose whose turn is next (Chapter 5).
* **Memory.** Without virtualization, both processes reference the same physical addresses and corrupt each other. The OS must give each process a private address space (Chapters 9–13).
* **Open files.** Without separation, the two processes share file positions and descriptors; one's `read` advances the other's position. The OS must give each process a private file descriptor table (Chapter 23, with per-process and shared components covered there).

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** A program reads a file of integers, sorts them in memory, and writes them back out. Midway through the sort, a hardware interrupt fires — say, a network packet arrives. List, in order, every piece of state the OS must save before running the interrupt handler if it wants the sort to continue correctly afterward.

Hint

Imagine you are the OS and you are about to run entirely unrelated code on the same CPU. What does the sort program have in flight _right now_?

**Answer:** At minimum, the OS (or, more precisely, the hardware together with the kernel's interrupt handler) must save:

* **All general-purpose registers** — the sort is probably using some of them to hold loop indices, partial comparisons, and pointers into the array.
* **The program counter** , so that execution resumes at the correct instruction.
* **The stack pointer** , since the sort's function call chain lives on the stack.
* **The flags register** , because the sort might have just executed a comparison and be about to branch on the result — that branch depends on the flag bits.
* **The floating-point / SIMD register state** if the sort uses them.

What the OS does _not_ need to save is the contents of memory. Memory is not overwritten by running another piece of code on the same CPU _unless that code explicitly writes to memory that the sort uses_ , which, if the OS has its memory isolation story working (Chapters 9–13), it will not. Memory persists because it is not a per-CPU resource like the registers are.

This exercise previews **Chapter 4** (context switching), where we will trace this exact save-and-restore in full mechanical detail.

**Q2.** A process opens `/tmp/data.txt`, reads 4 KB, and gets preempted. Describe what is in the process's file descriptor state that the OS must preserve. On resume, the process reads another 4 KB; what should it see?

**Answer:** The process's file descriptor table contains an entry for `/tmp/data.txt`, pointing to an open-file structure that tracks: (a) the underlying file (or, more precisely, the inode identifying the file), (b) the current offset — at this moment, 4096 — and (c) the open mode (read, write, etc.). The OS preserves this structure across preemption; when the process resumes, its next `read(fd, buf, 4096)` reads bytes at offset 4096 through 8191 and advances the offset to 8192.

The subtlety (which Chapter 23 elaborates) is that the "offset" is _per open-file instance_ , not per-file descriptor and not per-file. Two processes that each `open()` the same file have _different_ offsets. A process that `dup()`s a descriptor shares the offset with its copy. This sharing pattern is exactly what the shell exploits to redirect `stdout` to a file across a pipeline — we will see it in Chapter 3.

**Q3.** Design a minimal PCB for a toy OS that supports ten processes, each running a simple user program that can call one of three system calls: `putchar`, `getchar`, and `exit`. What fields are absolutely necessary? What can be omitted that a real OS has?

Hint

Think about what information each of the three system calls needs to track. Do you need a file descriptor table? A working directory?

**Answer:** A minimal PCB for this toy OS needs:

* **PID** (small integer to distinguish the ten processes).
* **State** (running / ready / blocked).
* **Saved register values** , including PC and stack pointer. Without these, the toy OS cannot preempt.
* **Pointer to the process's memory** (where its code and stack live). Since this toy OS probably does not implement virtual memory, this could be just "the base address of this process's memory region."
* **A pointer to the current system-call argument** , if the process is blocked on a system call (e.g., `getchar` waiting for a keystroke). Otherwise unused.

What can be omitted:

* **File descriptor table** — the three system calls read and write a single terminal, not named files. No file descriptors are needed; `putchar` and `getchar` implicitly use the terminal.
* **Working directory** — there is no file system.
* **Parent/child relationships** — nothing here spawns children.
* **Signal mask** — no signals in this toy.
* **Complex scheduling metadata** — a simple round-robin only needs "is this process ready?" plus a queue.

A real Linux PCB has many more fields because it supports many more features (the file system, signals, capabilities, namespaces, cgroup membership, audit, ptrace, perf counters, etc.). This exercise drives home that **the PCB's content is exactly the OS's feature set** : every feature the OS offers shows up as a field in the PCB (or a pointer from it). To design an OS is to decide which fields belong there.

**Q4.** On a 1 GHz CPU with a 10 ms timer interrupt, approximately how many instructions does a process get to execute between two preemptions, in the simplest model (ignoring other interrupts)? Why does this back-of-the-envelope number matter for scheduler design?

**Answer:** A 1 GHz CPU executes approximately one instruction per nanosecond, so one clock cycle per nanosecond, so about 10910^9109 cycles per second. In 10 ms (10 ms = 10710^7107 ns), the CPU executes roughly 10710^7107 instructions — _ten million_ — before the next timer interrupt. Even accounting for cache misses and branch stalls that slow real throughput (say, to one instruction per three cycles on average), that still leaves a few million instructions per time slice.

This matters for scheduler design in two ways. First, the _overhead_ of a context switch (thousands of cycles at most, in the ballpark of microseconds) is small compared to a time slice, so preemption is cheap relative to the work done between preemptions. Chapter 4 will quantify context-switch cost. Second, the _time slice length_ is a tunable knob: a shorter slice makes the system feel more responsive (interactive tasks preempt each other sooner) but spends proportionally more time context-switching. Chapters 5–8 use this tradeoff repeatedly.

**Q5.** A process has been blocked for an hour, waiting on a network socket that has received no data. Which PCB fields are still being used, and which are effectively idle? What does this tell you about memory consumption in a server with 100,000 blocked processes?

**Answer:** The entire PCB remains allocated. Its PID, state field (set to "blocked"), pointer to the process's address space, open file descriptor table, signal mask, scheduling priority — all of it stays in memory. The only piece that is "idle" is the CPU-register save area (the process is not running, so its saved registers sit there unused, but they must still be retained).

For 100,000 blocked processes on a server, even if each PCB is a modest 4 KB, that is 400 MB of kernel memory consumed by PCBs alone — and the larger memory cost is usually elsewhere: each process has its own address space, kernel stack (typically 16 KB), open file table, etc. Realistically, a process consumes tens to hundreds of KB of kernel memory, plus whatever user memory it has allocated. 100,000 processes is gigabytes.

This is why high-connection-count servers (proxies, load balancers, message brokers) typically use **threads** (which share an address space) or, better, **asynchronous event loops** (which multiplex many logical connections onto a single OS thread). One OS process with 100,000 event-loop-managed connections costs one PCB, not 100,000. Chapters 16–21 will introduce threads; but the cost argument for avoiding one-PCB-per-connection is best seen in this chapter, where you can _point at the PCB_ and say "this structure, multiplied by the connection count."

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** The process abstraction packages _everything_ about a running program. Some modern research operating systems try to split this abstraction: they separate "an address space" from "a thread of execution" from "a set of open files," letting each be independently allocated and recombined. Sketch one advantage of such a split. What is one serious difficulty?

**Discussion:** The advantage is flexibility. A classic Unix `fork()` bundles "new address space" with "new thread of control" — but sometimes you want only one of them. If you want a new thread of control in the same address space (so it can share data), you want a _thread_ , not a process. If you want a new address space but you want to share file descriptors with the original process (a sandboxed helper that can still write to the same log file), you want that sharing decoupled from address-space cloning. Systems like L4 and the Hydra research OS from the 1970s explored exactly this decomposition.

The difficulty is semantic complexity. Unix's process is a single unit whose lifecycle is well-understood: a process is created, runs, and exits. Splitting it into three orthogonal concepts (address space, control flow, resource handles) means each concept has its own lifecycle and its own ways of sharing and destruction. Every combination of "which of these three does this operation affect?" must be specified. The resulting API is strictly more expressive but also strictly harder to reason about. In practice, Linux has retained the process as a unified concept and added threads (`clone()` with selective sharing flags) as a lower-level escape hatch — a pragmatic compromise.

A thoughtful answer names the tradeoff explicitly and identifies at least one concrete system that has tried the split.

**Q2.** The chapter argues that "a process is the OS's abstraction of a running program." But not every running program has a process in the classical sense — firmware, device drivers that run in kernel context, and interrupt handlers all execute CPU instructions without being packaged as processes. Are they "running programs"? What does the absence of a PCB mean for them?

**Discussion:** They are running programs in the narrow sense that the CPU is executing instructions they contain, but they are not _user-level_ running programs; they are parts of the kernel or the hardware firmware. The absence of a PCB means:
