* They do not appear in the scheduler's list of schedulable entities.
* They do not have an independent address space (they share the kernel's address space, or they run on bare hardware below all address-space management).
* They cannot be preempted in the same way user processes can (a driver running in an interrupt handler will finish before any user process resumes).

This puts them outside the process abstraction entirely. They are "kernel code" or "firmware," not processes. Modern research OSes (microkernels like seL4) push even device drivers out into user-level processes, gaining isolation but paying overhead for the cross-boundary calls. Mainstream OSes keep drivers in-kernel for performance, at the cost of reliability (a buggy driver can crash the whole system, as every Windows and Linux user has occasionally discovered).

Recall from Chapter 1 that the OS is "software running in kernel mode." Drivers and interrupt handlers running in kernel mode are, by that definition, _part of_ the operating system — not programs running _on top of_ it. That is why they do not need PCBs: they are on the other side of the boundary the PCB describes. A good answer distinguishes "things the OS schedules" from "things that execute on the CPU," and notes that the process abstraction applies only to the former.

### Chapter Summary

We introduced the **process** as the operating system's abstraction for a running program — the container that holds every piece of state the OS must track in order to pause and resume execution correctly. A process consists of its memory (address space), its CPU register state (including the program counter), its open files, and a fistful of scheduling and identification metadata. The OS stores this state in a per-process **Process Control Block (PCB)** ; a process's content in the OS's ontology is exactly the PCB's fields.

A process is not always running. It passes among the states **running** , **ready** , and **blocked** as the scheduler moves it on and off the CPU and as it waits for events. Transitions are triggered by specific hardware and software events — the timer interrupt, an I/O completion, a system call that blocks. Chapter 4 will show the exact mechanism by which these transitions happen.

We started **Threading Plan A** : one process that owns everything. Adding a second process will break CPU sharing (fixed in Chapter 4), memory sharing (fixed in Chapters 9–13), and file sharing (fixed in Chapter 23). Each of the book's subsequent chapters is the answer to some failure of this baseline; the next chapter, Chapter 3, is the user-space API (`fork`, `exec`, `wait`) that actually creates and coordinates the second, tenth, and thousandth processes. From there, Chapter 4 will expose the kernel mechanism that makes preemptive multitasking work — and we will finally be able to explain how two processes share one CPU at the instruction level.

---

## Chapter 3: The Process API — fork, exec, and wait

At the end of Chapter 2 we had a precise definition of what a process is, but we did not yet have a way to _make one_. In a living operating system, processes are created all the time: every time you open a terminal, every time that terminal runs a command, every time a web browser opens a helper for a sandboxed PDF, every time `make` compiles a source file, a new process comes into existence. How? Who asks the kernel? What does the API look like?

There are only two interesting traditions. Windows offers a single function, `CreateProcess`, which takes about a dozen parameters and does everything — allocate a PCB, load a program, set up handles, launch. Unix offers two very small functions that together do the same work: `fork()`, which duplicates the current process, and `exec()`, which replaces the current process's program with a different one. `fork()` by itself does not run a new program; `exec()` by itself does not make a new process. You need both.

At first the Unix design looks bizarre. Why create an identical copy of the current process only to immediately throw away its program text and replace it with something else? The answer is a beautiful bit of engineering: the gap between `fork()` and `exec()` is the only place where the shell can reach in and configure the child process before the new program starts running. That configuration is what makes pipes, redirection, and every piece of environmental fine-tuning in Unix possible. Keep reading — by the end of this chapter, you will agree that "fork then exec" is the better design.

### 3.1 The Problem Before the Solution

Imagine we had no process API. A terminal wants to run `ls /home`. What must it do?

It must somehow ask the kernel: _please create a new process, load`/bin/ls` into it, set its first argument to `/home`, set its standard output to my pipe's write end, set its working directory to the same as mine, inherit all of my environment variables except `PATH`, and begin execution_.

A single kernel call to do all of that would need every parameter at once: program path, argument vector, environment, open files to inherit, ignored signals, starting working directory, user and group IDs, ulimits, cgroup, namespaces, and more. `CreateProcess` on Windows has indeed grown to roughly this shape — about a dozen parameters, and even those are insufficient, so there are about five different `CreateProcess` variants (`CreateProcessAsUser`, `CreateProcessWithLogonW`, `CreateProcessWithTokenW`, ...). Every time someone wants to add a new piece of per-process state, the kernel API must grow.

Unix answered the same problem very differently. It split the creation of a process from the loading of a program. `fork()` duplicates the current process — no new state to specify, because the child inherits exactly the parent's state. Then the child, running in user space, configures whatever it wants before finally calling `exec()` to load the new program. Every piece of process configuration happens in _user code_ between `fork` and `exec`, not through a growing parameter list to the kernel.

We will see over this chapter why that split is so good. First, the primitives.

### 3.2 fork()
    
    
    #include <unistd.h>
    pid_t fork(void);
    

`fork()` creates a new process that is a copy of the calling process. Both processes — the **parent** (which called `fork`) and the **child** (the newly created one) — continue running after the call. Both see the call return. The parent sees a positive integer, the PID of the new child. The child sees `0`. On error, only the parent sees `-1` and no child was created.

Consider:
    
    
    #include <stdio.h>
    #include <unistd.h>
    
    int main(void) {
        printf("Hello\n");
        pid_t rc = fork();
        if (rc < 0) {
            fprintf(stderr, "fork failed\n");
            return 1;
        } else if (rc == 0) {
            printf("child: my PID is %d\n", (int)getpid());
        } else {
            printf("parent: I created child with PID %d\n", (int)rc);
        }
        return 0;
    }
    

What happens when you run this? First the parent prints `Hello`. Then `fork()` returns, and suddenly there are _two_ processes continuing from that line. One process sees `rc = 0` (it is the child); the other sees `rc = some positive integer` (it is the parent). Each takes a different branch of the `if`. The order in which the two prints appear is _not defined_. Both outcomes are correct:
    
    
    Hello
    parent: I created child with PID 42901
    child: my PID is 42901
    

or
    
    
    Hello
    child: my PID is 42901
    parent: I created child with PID 42901
    

The scheduler (Chapter 5) decides which process runs first; the writer of the program must not assume either order.

#### Why does the child see the same code, the same variables, the same open files?

Because `fork()` duplicates everything. The child gets:

* A copy of the parent's address space — same text, same data, same heap contents, same stack contents. In Chapter 14 we will learn this is done lazily via **copy-on-write** to avoid the expense of literally copying every byte, but conceptually the child has its own independent memory.
* A copy of the parent's register state, _including_ the program counter that points at the instruction _after_ the `fork()` call. That is why both processes continue executing at the same place.
* A copy of the parent's file descriptor table. If the parent had `fd 3` pointing to `/tmp/foo.txt` at offset 1024, the child also has `fd 3` pointing to the same file. Critically — for reasons we will exploit in Section 3.5 — these two descriptors **share the underlying open-file structure** (on Linux, `struct file`), including the file position. If the child reads 512 bytes, the position advances to 1536 _in both parent and child_.
* A copy of the parent's environment variables, working directory, signal handlers, and other per-process state.

The only thing the parent and child differ on is the PID (they have distinct ones) and the return value of `fork()`. Every other bit of their state is, at the moment of fork, identical.

#### Common Misconception

> _After`fork()`, the parent and child share their memory; a write by one is seen by the other._

No. They share their _initial contents_ — the child starts with the same values — but the memory is _private_ from that point on. If the parent modifies a variable, the child does not see the change, and vice versa. Internally, the kernel uses copy-on-write tricks to delay the actual copy until a write occurs (Chapter 14), but from the user program's perspective, the two processes have independent memory.

The one exception is file descriptors. Those genuinely are shared: if parent and child both have the same fd and both read from it, they consume the same bytes from the same position. This matters. Section 3.5 will walk through the consequences.

### 3.3 exec()
    
    
    #include <unistd.h>
    int execv(const char *path, char *const argv[]);
    int execvp(const char *file, char *const argv[]);
    int execve(const char *path, char *const argv[], char *const envp[]);
    // ... and four more variants ...
    

`exec()` does not create a new process. It _replaces_ the program text in the current process with a new program loaded from disk. The memory layout is thrown away (text, data, heap, stack — all reset). A fresh stack is set up, arguments and environment are placed on it, and execution begins at the new program's entry point.

Here is the key sentence: **after a successful`exec()`, nothing of the old program remains.** The PID is the same. The PCB is the same. The file descriptor table (mostly — see Section 3.5) is the same. But the code that is running is different, and the memory the code operates on is different. It is as if the process went to sleep, had its entire body replaced with a new program, and woke up.

A successful `exec()` does not return; execution has moved to the new program. If `exec()` returns, the exec failed (bad path, no permission, out of memory, etc.) and the old program continues.
    
    
    char *args[] = { "/bin/ls", "-l", "/home", NULL };
    execv(args[0], args);
    // We get here ONLY if exec failed.
    fprintf(stderr, "exec failed: %s\n", strerror(errno));
    exit(1);
    

There are seven variants of `exec` in the standard C library — `execv`, `execvp`, `execl`, `execlp`, `execvpe`, `execle`, `execve` — differing in how they take arguments (list vs array, with or without environment, with or without PATH search). They all call the same underlying system call (`execve` on Linux). The variety is pure convenience; pick the one that matches how you want to pass arguments.

### 3.4 wait()
    
    
    #include <sys/wait.h>
    pid_t wait(int *status);
    pid_t waitpid(pid_t pid, int *status, int options);
    

A process that has forked a child usually wants to know when the child finishes. `wait()` blocks the caller until any child terminates, and returns that child's PID. The exit status — what the child passed to `exit()` — is written into the pointer argument. `waitpid()` is a more specific version that waits for a particular child and allows options (e.g., "don't block if no child is ready").

Why must the parent call `wait()`? Recall the _zombie_ state from Chapter 2. When a child terminates, its PCB is not freed immediately — the exit status is kept alive until the parent collects it. `wait()` is the collection. A parent that never calls `wait()` leaves zombie PCBs accumulating, slowly eating kernel memory until the system runs out of PIDs.

The shell is the canonical user of `wait()`. When you type `ls`, the shell forks, the child execs `ls`, and the shell calls `wait()` — blocking until `ls` finishes. The shell then prints the next prompt. Without the `wait()`, the shell would print the prompt immediately, and the output from `ls` would scroll past mixed with your next command.

### 3.5 Why fork-then-exec Is a Brilliant Design

Now we can answer the opening question. Why doesn't Unix just have `fork_and_exec(program, args, ...)`? Why split them?

Because the gap between `fork()` and `exec()` is the perfect place to configure the child. In that gap, the child is still running its own user code (copied from the parent), but it has not yet become the new program. Anything it does in that window — changing its working directory, redirecting its file descriptors, masking signals, setting its environment, dropping privileges, joining a cgroup — will be inherited by the new program the moment `exec()` succeeds.

Let us make this concrete with the single most important example: **output redirection**.

#### Redirecting stdout to a file

Suppose you type `ls > output.txt` at a shell. The shell wants to run `ls`, but with `ls`'s standard output (file descriptor 1) pointing to `output.txt` instead of the terminal. How?

With `fork_and_exec`, you would need a parameter: "please set file descriptor 1 in the new process to open(output.txt)." This is one of many possible configurations. Every possible piece of per-process state would become a parameter.

With fork-then-exec, the shell does this:
    
    
    pid_t rc = fork();
    if (rc == 0) {
        // Child. Reconfigure stdout before exec'ing.
        close(STDOUT_FILENO);              // close fd 1
        open("output.txt", O_CREAT | O_WRONLY | O_TRUNC, 0644);
        // open() returns the lowest unused fd — which is 1, since we just closed it.
        // Now fd 1 points to output.txt.
    
        char *args[] = { "/bin/ls", NULL };
        execv(args[0], args);
        exit(1);                           // only reached on exec failure
    } else {
        // Parent. Wait for child.
        wait(NULL);
    }
    

The child, still running shell code, closes its standard output and then opens `output.txt`. Because `open()` returns the lowest available descriptor, and 1 was just freed, the newly opened file takes fd 1. Then the child `exec`s `ls`. The new program `ls` inherits a file descriptor table where fd 1 is `output.txt`. When `ls` writes to stdout, it writes to the file. `ls` has no idea this is happening — it thinks stdout is just stdout.

Now the elegance. _Any_ configuration can be done this way. Want to redirect stderr too? Close fd 2, open another file. Want to pipe to another program? Create a pipe, dup its read end onto fd 0 in one child, dup its write end onto fd 1 in another, fork and exec each child. Want to drop privileges before running a user command? `setuid()` before `exec()`. Want to run the child in a different directory? `chdir()` before `exec()`. Every one of these is done by user-level code in the child between `fork()` and `exec()`. The kernel does not need to know about any of them. The `fork()` and `exec()` kernel calls are each tiny — `fork()` duplicates state, `exec()` loads a program — and all the complexity is composed by the user.

This is the design's triumph: a near-infinite space of "how I want the new process to start" configurations is expressible in ordinary user code between two small kernel calls. Windows, by comparison, keeps adding `CreateProcess*` variants and keeps adding fields to them, because user code cannot configure a process that does not yet exist. The Unix design makes the process exist first (as a copy of the shell), then lets user code configure it, then replaces its program.

#### Pipes

A pipe (`|` in the shell) is the most-cited example. `cmd1 | cmd2` wants `cmd1`'s stdout to be `cmd2`'s stdin. Here is the shell's logic, in sketch form:

  1. Create a pipe: `int fds[2]; pipe(fds);`. Now `fds[0]` is the read end and `fds[1]` is the write end.
  2. Fork a child for `cmd1`. In that child: close `fds[0]` (we do not read from this end), dup2 `fds[1]` onto stdout (fd 1), close the original `fds[1]`, and exec `cmd1`. `cmd1` now writes its output into the pipe.
  3. Fork another child for `cmd2`. In that child: close `fds[1]` (we do not write to this end), dup2 `fds[0]` onto stdin (fd 0), close the original `fds[0]`, and exec `cmd2`. `cmd2` now reads its input from the pipe.
  4. In the parent shell: close both `fds[0]` and `fds[1]` (the shell does not use either end itself). Call `wait()` twice to reap both children.

At no point did the kernel know what "a pipeline" is. The pipe is a kernel object; the fork/exec/close dance is user code that arranges for two programs to share it. Composing more pipes — `cmd1 | cmd2 | cmd3` — just repeats the pattern. This is how `|` has worked in every Unix shell since 1972.

### 3.6 A Shell Is Just a fork/exec/wait Loop

Now we can sketch a minimal Unix shell:
    
    
    while (1) {
        printf("$ ");
        char *line = read_a_line();
        if (line == NULL) break;                // EOF
        char **argv = parse_arguments(line);
    
        pid_t rc = fork();
        if (rc < 0) { perror("fork"); continue; }
        if (rc == 0) {
            // Child: apply any redirections parsed from the line, then exec.
            apply_redirections(argv);
            execvp(argv[0], argv);
            perror("execvp");
            exit(1);
        } else {
            // Parent: wait for child to finish.
            int status;
            waitpid(rc, &status, 0);
        }
    }
    

That's a shell. Complete, functional. All of `bash`, `zsh`, `fish`, `sh` is this loop, wrapped in syntactic niceties (globbing, history, tab completion, variables, control flow) and richer redirection logic. The core is fork, exec, wait. Every time you type a command, this loop runs.

There is something almost poetic about the fact that the two simplest primitives compose into every program-launching mechanism in the operating system.

### 3.7 Zombies and Orphans

A child process always ends in one of two ways: it either exits with a status, or it is terminated by a signal. In either case, its PCB hangs around in the **zombie** state until the parent calls `wait()` (or its more specific cousin `waitpid()`). The parent reads the exit status, the kernel frees the PCB. End of child.

What if the parent dies before the child does? The child is now an **orphan** — it still has a PID, still runs, but has no one to report its exit status to when it eventually dies. Unix handles this by reassigning orphaned children to `init` (PID 1, or on modern Linux systems, a subreaper process). `init` calls `wait()` in a loop, perpetually. Any orphaned child is eventually reaped by `init`, so orphans do not become permanent zombies.

What if the parent stays alive but never calls `wait()`? The child becomes a zombie and stays one. The PCB is not freed. The process table slowly fills with zombie entries. Eventually the system runs out of PIDs (there is a maximum — on Linux, tunable, commonly 32,768 or higher). A long-running server that forks children without waiting is a classic bug.

The two signal-handling idioms for avoiding zombies are:

* Install a SIGCHLD handler that calls `waitpid(-1, ..., WNOHANG)` in a loop until it returns `0`. The kernel delivers SIGCHLD every time a child terminates; the handler reaps all available zombies.
* Set SIGCHLD to SIG_IGN (with the Linux-specific `SA_NOCLDWAIT` flag). This tells the kernel: do not turn exiting children into zombies; just let them go.

Either works. The first is more portable and more explicit. The second is a nice shortcut on Linux.

### 3.8 The System Call Boundary

Stepping back: `fork`, `exec`, and `wait` are **system calls**. They are not ordinary function calls. When user code invokes them, a specific instruction (`syscall` on x86-64; `int 0x80` on older x86; `svc` on ARM) traps into the kernel. The kernel examines the call, does its privileged work, and returns.

We will not yet trace the mechanical details of the trap — Chapter 4 is devoted to it. For this chapter, the picture to carry is: `fork()` is a small user-library wrapper around a syscall instruction that signals the kernel, asks it to create a new process, and returns with either `0` (in the child) or the child's PID (in the parent). Everything else (argument parsing, error handling, manipulating file descriptors) is done in kernel mode where the required operations are privileged (allocating a PCB, duplicating page tables, touching the scheduler queue).

The syscall is also where the user/kernel mode switch from Chapter 1 happens. Before the syscall: user mode, restricted. During the syscall: kernel mode, unrestricted. After: user mode, restricted again. The mode flip is _the_ reason system calls are expensive (hundreds to thousands of cycles, because the CPU must save and restore a lot of state and also flush security predictors). That cost is why, in high-performance code, we avoid making unnecessary syscalls, and why `fork()` in particular is one of the more expensive syscalls (it has to duplicate everything).

### 3.9 Common Pitfalls

A few mistakes are so common they deserve a catalog.

**Race between child exit and parent wait.** If the parent does some work before `wait()`, the child may exit in the meantime and sit as a zombie. That is fine — `wait()` will reap it regardless of whether the child has already exited. But a parent that exits without waiting leaks a zombie.

**Assuming a fork order.** After `fork()`, either the parent or the child may run first. Code that depends on one or the other running first is broken; the scheduler's choice is not your choice.

**Forgetting that fds are shared.** If parent forks a child and both read from the same file descriptor, they advance the same position. Some bytes go to the parent, some to the child, and neither gets the whole file. This is usually a bug, but in producer-consumer patterns (parent writes to a pipe, child reads from it), it is the whole point.

**Forgetting to close pipe ends.** In the pipeline example, the shell must close both ends of the pipe _in itself_ after forking, because a pipe's reader gets EOF only when every write end is closed. If the shell keeps the write end open, the reader child blocks forever waiting for EOF that never comes.

**exec failure not handled.** If you write `execv(path, args);` without an `exit()` after it, and the exec fails, the child returns from `main()` back into whatever the parent would have done — which is to say, into confused, duplicated behavior. Always follow an `exec` call with something that handles exec failure (usually `perror` then `exit(1)`).

### 3.10 Windows: CreateProcess for Contrast

Before leaving the chapter, a brief look at the alternative.
    
    
    BOOL CreateProcessA(
        LPCSTR                lpApplicationName,
        LPSTR                 lpCommandLine,
        LPSECURITY_ATTRIBUTES lpProcessAttributes,
        LPSECURITY_ATTRIBUTES lpThreadAttributes,
        BOOL                  bInheritHandles,
        DWORD                 dwCreationFlags,
        LPVOID                lpEnvironment,
        LPCSTR                lpCurrentDirectory,
        LPSTARTUPINFOA        lpStartupInfo,
        LPPROCESS_INFORMATION lpProcessInformation
    );
    

Ten parameters. `STARTUPINFO` is itself a struct of 15 or so fields. And this does not cover: running the process as a different user (`CreateProcessAsUser`), running with an impersonation token (`CreateProcessWithTokenW`), running with a logon session (`CreateProcessWithLogonW`). Each added parameter is a possible per-process configuration that must be specified before the child exists, because user code cannot run in a not-yet-existing child.

The Unix design replaces _all of this_ with two syscalls, each with two or three parameters, plus the user's ability to run arbitrary code in the child between them. It is strictly more expressive (any Windows option can be expressed via fork-then-exec; fork-then-exec has options — like arbitrary pre-exec custom code — that Windows cannot express without wrappers) at lower API complexity.

That said, `fork()` has its own problems. Duplicating an address space is expensive even with copy-on-write; forking a 4 GB process and immediately exec-ing a 10 MB binary is wasteful. On big servers with thousands of processes, the fork cost is measurable. Linux has added `posix_spawn()` and `vfork()` as specialized alternatives for the common fork-then-exec pattern. But the _model_ of "create by copying" remains intellectually central, and the overwhelming majority of process creation in Unix still uses it.

### 3.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What does `fork()` return in the parent? In the child? What does it return on error?

Hint

Section 3.2. Three distinct return values, all from one function call.

**Answer:** In the parent (the process that called `fork`), `fork()` returns the **PID of the newly created child** — a positive integer. In the child, `fork()` returns **0**. On error (e.g., system out of PIDs, out of memory), only the original caller sees the return, and it is **-1** , with `errno` set to describe the error. No child was created in that case.

**Q2.** After a successful `exec()` call, what happens to the calling process's memory? Its PID? Its open file descriptors?

**Answer:** The **memory** (text, data, heap, stack) is completely replaced by the new program's initial memory layout. **The PID stays the same** — the PCB is reused, only its contents change. Most **open file descriptors stay open** (so that the new program inherits stdin, stdout, stderr and any redirections the pre-exec code set up), except for any that were marked with the close-on-exec flag (`FD_CLOEXEC`), which are closed automatically.

**Q3.** What is a zombie process? What event removes a zombie from the system?

**Answer:** A **zombie** is a process that has exited (its code has finished running and it has called `exit` or been killed) but whose PCB is still present in the kernel because its parent has not yet collected its exit status. The zombie's memory and file descriptors have been released, but its PCB, its PID, and its exit status linger. The event that removes the zombie is the parent calling `wait()` (or `waitpid()`) and collecting the exit status, or the parent itself dying (in which case the zombie is reparented to `init`, which reaps it).

**Q4.** Explain, in one or two sentences, why Unix split process creation into `fork` and `exec` instead of providing a single `spawn` call.

**Answer:** Splitting creation from program loading gives the child a window — after `fork` but before `exec` — in which ordinary user code can configure the not-yet-started program: redirecting file descriptors, changing directory, dropping privileges, joining a cgroup, setting signals, and so on. With a single `spawn` call, every one of these configurations would have to be a parameter to the kernel API, making it complex and inflexible. The split lets the kernel stay simple while letting user programs express arbitrary pre-program configuration.

**Q5.** In the parent-child pipe example in Section 3.5, why must the shell close _both_ ends of the pipe after forking the two children?

**Answer:** The shell does not use the pipe itself — the children do. If the shell keeps the write end of the pipe open, then even when the writer child exits, the pipe is still considered to have an open writer (the shell), and the reader child will block forever on `read()` waiting for EOF, which will never come. If the shell keeps the read end open, the kernel's EOF condition (which fires only when all read ends are closed — for SIGPIPE on the writer's side) cannot behave correctly. The shell must close both so that the pipe lifecycle depends only on the children's open ends.

**Q6.** The chapter claims `fork()` gives the child a copy of the file descriptor table but that parent and child **share** the underlying open-file structure. Why is this subtle distinction important?

**Answer:** Because the shared open-file structure holds the **file offset** (the current read/write position). A file descriptor in each process's table points to the same open-file structure. If the parent reads 512 bytes, the offset advances to 512 — and the child also sees the offset as 512, because both fds point to the same structure. This is what lets a shell redirect `ls` into a file via fork-then-exec: the shell opens the file (offset 0), forks, and the child's inherited fd keeps writing where the shell left off. If each fork duplicated the _open-file structure_ and not just the _fd table entry_ , the parent and child would have independent offsets, and shell redirection as we know it would not work. The design is deliberate. Chapter 23 returns to this.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Trace the following program and give all possible outputs:
    
    
    #include <stdio.h>
    #include <unistd.h>
    int main(void) {
        printf("A");
        pid_t rc = fork();
        if (rc == 0) printf("B");
        else         printf("C");
        return 0;
    }
    

Assume stdout is line-buffered when connected to a terminal and fully buffered when connected to a file. How does the output differ in those two cases?

Hint

The buffering rule interacts with fork in a counter-intuitive way. Whatever was in the stdout buffer at the moment of fork is duplicated along with the rest of memory.

**Answer:** Consider what is in stdout's in-process buffer at the moment of `fork()`.

_Terminal (line-buffered)._ The "A" is only flushed when a newline appears. There is no newline, so "A" sits in the in-process buffer when `fork()` is called. The buffer is duplicated: both parent and child have "A" buffered. Each then appends its own letter ("B" or "C"). On `return 0`, each process flushes its buffer. The terminal ends up with **AB** and **AC** in some order — so either "ABAC", "ACAB", possibly interleaved. Typical observed outputs: `ABAC`, `ACAB`, or scrambled variants.

Wait — re-examine. "Terminal is line-buffered" actually means `stdout` is flushed _on newline_. Since there is no newline, nothing gets flushed until exit. Actually, many modern stdlibs flush line-buffered stdout on program exit too. So on terminal, "A" is flushed eventually, but _after_ `fork()` duplicated the buffer. Each process's buffer still contains "A"; both append their own letter and flush. Output: "AB" and "AC" in some interleaving. Four total characters.

_File (fully buffered)._ No flushes happen at all until exit. Same reasoning: both parent and child have "A" buffered at fork; each appends its own letter; each flushes on exit. Same output: **AB** and **AC** across two processes, totaling four characters in the file.

So in both cases the output is four characters — the "A" appears **twice**. The lesson is that buffered I/O and fork interact surprisingly: any unflushed data is duplicated. Before forking a process that has written to stdout, it is wise to call `fflush(stdout)` to avoid duplicated output.

(If the program used `write(1, "A", 1)` instead of `printf`, the "A" would go straight to the kernel, and it would appear only once. `write` is unbuffered.)

**Q2.** Implement output redirection for a simple shell command. Write the child-side code, in C pseudocode, that, before calling `exec("/bin/ls", ...)`, redirects stdout to the file `"out.txt"`. Annotate each call with _why_ it is needed.

Hint

`close`, `open`, and the fact that `open` returns the lowest free descriptor.

**Answer:**
    
    
    // Running in the child, after fork, before exec.
    
    // 1. Close file descriptor 1 (stdout). Why: fd 1 currently points to the
    //    shell's terminal; we want it to point to our file instead.
    close(STDOUT_FILENO);
    
    // 2. Open the target file. Why: open() returns the lowest available fd,
    //    and since we just closed fd 1, the returned fd will be 1. Flags:
    //    O_CREAT (create if missing), O_WRONLY (we only write), O_TRUNC
    //    (start fresh — this is > redirection; for >> it would be O_APPEND).
    int fd = open("out.txt", O_CREAT | O_WRONLY | O_TRUNC, 0644);
    if (fd < 0) { perror("open"); exit(1); }
    // Invariant: fd == 1 here. If not, something else was wrong.
    
    // 3. Exec the target program. Why: now fd 1 points to "out.txt", so
    //    when /bin/ls writes to stdout (fd 1), it writes to the file.
    char *args[] = { "/bin/ls", NULL };
    execv(args[0], args);
    perror("execv"); exit(1);
    

Alternative: use `dup2(fd, 1)` after opening into some arbitrary fd. `dup2` atomically reassigns fd 1 to point at the open file, even if fd 1 was already open to something else. This is slightly more robust and is what real shells use.

**Q3.** A student claims: "`fork()` is slow because it copies every byte of the parent's memory. For a 1 GB process this takes hundreds of milliseconds, so fork-based shells are inefficient." Critique this claim.

**Answer:** The claim is wrong in practice for two reasons.

First, modern Unix kernels implement **copy-on-write** (COW) for fork. After `fork()`, the child's page tables reference the _same_ physical frames as the parent's, marked read-only. When either writes to a page, a page fault triggers, and the kernel copies that single page to a new frame at that moment. So only pages that are actually modified after fork are copied — typically a small fraction of the parent's memory. The time complexity of fork is proportional to the number of page-table entries, not to the total memory. Chapter 14 will describe COW in detail.

Second, shells fork small programs, not giant ones. The shell itself is a few hundred KB; its fork takes microseconds. The expensive cases are applications like Chromium or the JVM that fork 4 GB processes just to exec a small helper — and precisely _these_ applications often use `posix_spawn()` or manually-coded `vfork()` (which does not copy the address space at all) to avoid the wasted page-table work.

So the student's claim conflates two things: the naive model of fork (linear in memory size) and the optimized real implementation (linear in page-table size). In practice, fork is cheap for small processes and has efficient alternatives for the pathological cases.

**Q4.** Design an experiment you could run on your laptop to confirm that `fork()` uses copy-on-write. You may write pseudocode. What would you observe that distinguishes COW from a naive full copy?

**Answer:**
    
    
    // Pseudocode.
    char *big = malloc(1 GiB);
    memset(big, 42, 1 GiB);       // touch it all, so it is actually allocated
    
    struct timeval t1, t2;
    gettimeofday(&t1, NULL);
    pid_t rc = fork();
    gettimeofday(&t2, NULL);
    
    if (rc == 0) exit(0);         // child: just exit
    wait(NULL);
    // Report t2 - t1.
    

Measure the parent's observed time from "just before fork" to "just after fork." With a naive full copy, fork would take proportional to 1 GB of memory movement — roughly hundreds of milliseconds on current hardware (DRAM bandwidth around 20 GB/s; 1 GB / 20 GB/s = 50 ms, roughly). With copy-on-write, fork takes time proportional only to the page-table size — on x86-64 with 4 KB pages, 1 GB is 256K page-table entries across several levels, requiring the kernel to create copies of the last-level entries (the other levels are shared and marked read-only). This completes in well under 10 milliseconds on modern hardware, often closer to 1 ms.

Observing sub-10 ms fork times for a 1 GB process confirms that the kernel is not literally copying all of memory; COW is in action. To further confirm, the experiment could also measure what happens when the child actually _writes_ to the big buffer: the first writes will be much slower than subsequent writes on the same page, because each first write triggers a page fault and a copy.

**Q5.** A shell wants to run `A | B | C` (three-stage pipeline). Extend the two-stage pipeline idea from Section 3.5: how many pipes are needed, and which file descriptors get dup2'ed in each of the three children?

**Answer:** Two pipes are needed — pipe1 between A and B, pipe2 between B and C.
    
    
    pipe(pipe1);  // fds [0]=read, [1]=write
    pipe(pipe2);
    
    fork A:
      child: dup2(pipe1[1], 1)    // A's stdout -> pipe1 write end
             close all pipe fds (the originals)
             exec A
    
    fork B:
      child: dup2(pipe1[0], 0)    // B's stdin  <- pipe1 read end
             dup2(pipe2[1], 1)    // B's stdout -> pipe2 write end
             close all pipe fds (the originals)
             exec B
    
    fork C:
      child: dup2(pipe2[0], 0)    // C's stdin  <- pipe2 read end
             close all pipe fds (the originals)
             exec C
    
    parent: close pipe1[0], pipe1[1], pipe2[0], pipe2[1]
            wait for A, B, C
    

Important: each child must close _both_ ends of both pipes _after_ dup2'ing, so that (i) EOF propagates correctly — pipe1 is considered "writer-closed" only when A exits (and the shell's write-end is closed), and (ii) every child has only its intended fd 0/1/2 pointing where it should, with no stray descriptors keeping pipe ends alive.

The general rule: for an N-stage pipeline, you need N-1 pipes. The first stage has only stdout-to-pipe1; the last stage has only pipe_{N-1}-to-stdin; middle stages have both. This generalizes cleanly, which is how real shells implement arbitrary-length pipelines with a loop.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Posix introduced `posix_spawn()`, a single function that combines fork and exec with a list of per-child "actions" (open, close, dup2, setenv, ...). Its argument list is structurally similar to Windows' `CreateProcess` but encoded as a vector of operations. Is this a retreat from the fork/exec philosophy, a clever compromise, or something else?

**Discussion:** `posix_spawn` is primarily a _performance_ compromise. The fork/exec model is expressive but expensive for large processes, because fork must duplicate the parent's page tables (even with copy-on-write). For a 64 GB web-server process that wants to launch a 10 MB `ls`, this is wasted work — `exec` will immediately throw the page tables away. `posix_spawn` skips the duplication by going directly from "set up a new process" to "apply these actions" to "load this program." It is useful in systems programming where fork costs matter.

Philosophically, it is a mild retreat: by serializing a fixed set of actions instead of letting user code run arbitrarily between fork and exec, it loses some flexibility. You cannot, for example, run a custom computation that decides _based on the current state of the world_ which file to redirect into. In practice, the supported action types (open, dup2, close, chdir, setuid, etc.) cover the vast majority of uses, and where they don't, fork/exec is still available.

Most of the Unix world still uses fork/exec. `posix_spawn` is a specialized tool, not a replacement — a honest acknowledgment that fork's cost is sometimes too high, paired with a carefully-designed alternative for the common cases.

**Q2.** The chapter argues the Unix design is elegant because the kernel stays simple while user code composes configurations. What principle of system design is this an example of, and where else in the book might you expect to see the same principle?

**Discussion:** The principle is often called **mechanism and policy separation** : the kernel provides minimal, general mechanisms (create-by-copy, replace-program), and user code composes them into policies (redirect, pipe, privilege drop). The kernel does not encode particular policies; it provides the primitives from which any policy can be built.

You will see this repeatedly:

* **Scheduling** (Chapters 5–7): the kernel provides context switching (mechanism); the scheduler chooses whom to run next (policy). Different kernels can plug in different scheduler policies without changing the switching mechanism.
* **Paging** (Chapters 11–15): the kernel provides the hardware-assisted translation mechanism; the page replacement algorithm is a separable policy layered on top.
* **File system** (Chapters 22–25): the kernel provides block I/O mechanisms; different file systems (ext4, XFS, btrfs, ZFS) implement different allocation policies on top.

The pattern recurs because it lets OS implementations evolve. If the policy is separable from the mechanism, a new policy can be introduced without a new mechanism, and vice versa. fork/exec is perhaps the most visible _user-facing_ instance of this design principle, but the book is full of other instances. Keep an eye out.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter introduced the Unix **process API** — three system calls that, together, express every way a program can be started in the system. `fork()` duplicates the calling process, giving both parent and child a chance to run. `exec()` replaces the calling process's program with a new one loaded from disk. `wait()` collects a child's exit status and lets the kernel reclaim its PCB.

The power of the design comes from the gap between `fork` and `exec`: user code running in the child, after it is created but before its program is replaced, can perform arbitrary configuration that will be inherited by the new program. This is how shells implement redirection, pipes, privilege drops, cgroup placement, and every other "start program X in configuration Y" pattern — all without the kernel needing to know about any of them. The shell itself is a fork-exec-wait loop; every command you run passes through it.

We began **Threading Plan A** in Chapter 2 with one process; this chapter gave us the API to create more. But we have not yet seen how the kernel shares a CPU among these newly forked children — when one process calls `fork`, both processes exist, but only one CPU runs both of them. That is the subject of Chapter 4: **limited direct execution** and **context switching** — the mechanisms by which the kernel keeps control of a CPU it has briefly lent to user code. Chapter 4 will also finally explain, at the instruction level, what a system call like `fork` actually _does_ when it enters the kernel, closing the abstraction gap we left open here.

---

## Chapter 4: Limited Direct Execution and Context Switching

At the end of Chapter 3 we had two processes, both created by `fork`, both wanting to run. We left open the mechanical question: _how does one CPU run two processes?_

The naïve answer — "just let one run and then let the other run" — hides two hard subproblems.

First, the user program runs at full CPU speed in its own address space. How does the kernel ever _regain control_ once the user program has the CPU? If the user program decides not to yield, there is no software mechanism for the kernel to interrupt it — the kernel is not running.

Second, even supposing the kernel does regain control, how does it _switch_ from running user program A's code and register state to running user program B's code and register state, and back again, without either program observing the switch? The kernel must save every single register value of A, restore every single register value of B, change which memory is visible, change which code is executing — all atomically, and correctly.

This chapter solves both subproblems. The CPU's hardware helps with the first through **traps** and **interrupts** ; the kernel's design solves the second through the **context switch** sequence. Together these mechanisms make up what is called **limited direct execution** : user code runs directly on the hardware (at full speed, no interpreter), but under controlled limits that the kernel imposes and the hardware enforces.

### 4.1 Direct Execution, Unlimited

Imagine the simplest possible strategy for running a user program: the kernel loads the program into memory, jumps to its entry point, and lets the CPU execute.

This is fast — the user program runs at bare-metal speed — but it has two fatal problems.

**Problem 1: The user program can do anything.** If the program decides to write to the disk controller's memory-mapped registers, it writes. If it decides to modify the page tables and remap another process's memory into its own address space, it does. If it decides to turn off the timer interrupt so no one can preempt it, it does. Every protection we want to provide collapses if user code has unrestricted privileges.

**Problem 2: The kernel never runs again.** Once the CPU is executing the user program, _nothing_ brings it back. The kernel's code is not running. No one is checking that the user program has had its time slice. No one is dispatching the next process. The user program holds the CPU forever (or until it exits, if it ever does).

Chapter 1's user/kernel mode bit solves Problem 1. Executing in user mode, the CPU refuses to run privileged instructions. The user program is limited: it can do arithmetic, it can load and store from its own memory, it can branch. It cannot write to page tables, cannot touch devices, cannot modify protection state.

Problem 2 is the subject of the rest of this chapter. We need a mechanism that transfers control back to the kernel at two kinds of moments:

* **Synchronous moments** : when the user program wants kernel services — a system call, like `fork()` or `read()`. The user program initiates the transfer.
* **Asynchronous moments** : when the kernel needs to wake up whether the user program wants it or not — the timer expired, an I/O device completed, a hardware error fired. The hardware initiates the transfer.

The unified mechanism is the **trap**.

### 4.2 Traps and Trap Tables

A **trap** is a hardware-supported jump from user mode into kernel mode. The hardware, when triggered, performs atomically:

  1. Saves the current user-mode PC and some flags.
  2. Switches the mode bit to kernel.
  3. Switches the stack pointer to a kernel stack.
  4. Jumps to a fixed address in the kernel — specifically, the address stored in the **trap table** for this kind of trap.

The trap table is a data structure the kernel sets up at boot time. It contains, for each possible trap cause (system call, timer interrupt, page fault, divide-by-zero, etc.), the address of the kernel handler that should run. On x86, the trap table is called the Interrupt Descriptor Table (IDT) and has 256 entries; on ARM, it is called the Exception Vector Table.

When the kernel boots, it fills in the IDT with pointers to its handlers and then tells the CPU, via a privileged instruction (`lidt` on x86), where the IDT lives in memory. From that point on, any trap automatically directs the CPU into the correct handler at the correct entry point — the user program has no say in where it lands.

#### Why this is secure

The user program cannot arbitrarily jump into kernel code. If it tries to branch to the address of the kernel's handler for `read()`, the CPU refuses — that address is in memory marked "kernel only," and user-mode code cannot execute it. The only path into kernel code is through a trap, and every trap lands the CPU at the address the IDT specifies. The kernel, not the user, controls the IDT.

This is how the privilege boundary is enforced at the instruction level. The mode bit prevents the user program from doing anything privileged; the IDT funnels every mode-changing event into code the kernel chose.

### 4.3 A System Call, Step by Step

Let us trace what happens when a user program calls `read(fd, buf, n)`.

  1. **User program prepares arguments.** The C library wraps `read()` in a small assembly stub. It places the system call number (a small integer identifying `read`; on Linux x86-64, 0) in a designated register (`%rax`) and the arguments in others (`%rdi`, `%rsi`, `%rdx`).

  2. **User program executes the`syscall` instruction.** This is a special instruction that initiates a trap. The hardware:

     * Saves the user PC (the instruction after `syscall`) somewhere the kernel can find it — on x86-64, in `%rcx`.
     * Saves the user flags register — on x86-64, in `%r11`.
     * Loads the kernel-mode PC from a specific model-specific register (`IA32_LSTAR`), jumping there.
     * Switches to a kernel stack — the per-process kernel stack, set up by the OS when the process was created.
     * Sets the mode bit to kernel.

None of this involves any user-level code. The CPU does it all atomically, in hardware.

  3. **Kernel entry stub runs.** The first code executed is a kernel assembly stub, common to all syscalls. It saves every general-purpose register onto the kernel stack (so they can be restored on return), possibly switches the mode stack, and calls the C-level syscall dispatcher.

  4. **Syscall dispatcher.** A C function in the kernel — on Linux, roughly `do_syscall_64()` — looks at the syscall number in `%rax`, checks it is in range, and indexes into the syscall table to find the corresponding handler. For `read`, it calls `sys_read(fd, buf, n)`.

  5. **Handler runs.** `sys_read()` looks up the file descriptor in the current process's file descriptor table, validates `buf` is a legal user address (to avoid the kernel writing data into kernel memory by mistake), and calls the appropriate file system or device handler to fetch bytes. This can be fast (data already cached) or slow (must issue disk I/O, in which case the kernel marks the process blocked and calls the scheduler — we will see this later).

  6. **Return to entry stub.** When the handler finishes, it returns its result (number of bytes read, or `-1` with `errno`) as a return value. The entry stub restores every saved register, then executes `sysret`, the x86-64 instruction that atomically:

     * Restores the user PC from `%rcx`.
     * Restores flags from `%r11`.
     * Switches back to the user stack.
     * Sets the mode bit back to user.
  7. **User program resumes** at the instruction after `syscall`, with the result of the read in `%rax`. The C library returns that to the caller.

The entire sequence, for a cached `read()` that does not block, takes a few hundred nanoseconds. Each cycle spent — saving registers, switching stacks, looking up the handler, returning — is the overhead of crossing the user/kernel boundary. Modern CPUs have added hardware features to make this fast (`syscall`/`sysret` replaced the slower `int 0x80` path specifically to reduce this overhead). Kernel bypass techniques in high-performance networking (DPDK, io_uring) exist precisely to avoid syscalls when the bytes flow fast enough that even 200 ns per call is unacceptable.

#### The kernel stack

Why does the CPU switch to a different stack when entering the kernel? Two reasons.

First, the user stack might be corrupted or inaccessible. If the user program misused its stack pointer, the CPU cannot rely on the user stack being usable. The kernel needs a known-good stack to push its return addresses and local variables onto.

Second, security. If the kernel pushed sensitive data (return addresses, saved registers from other processes) onto the user stack, a malicious user program could inspect those values by reading its own stack. Using a kernel-only stack prevents this leak.

Each process has its own kernel stack — not the user stack. When the CPU traps, the hardware knows which kernel stack to use (from a register the OS set up when scheduling this process). When the syscall returns, the kernel stack is left empty for next time.

### 4.4 Cooperative vs Preemptive

So far we have only discussed synchronous traps: traps that the user program itself initiated. The kernel gets control only if the user program cooperates — by making a syscall, or by running code that accidentally faults (divide by zero, bad memory access). This is **cooperative multitasking**.

Cooperative multitasking was the dominant scheme in classic Mac OS (until 2001) and Windows 3.x. Its failure mode is obvious: a user program in an infinite loop makes no syscalls, never faults, and runs forever. No other program gets the CPU. Anyone who used a pre-X Mac remembers the experience: a single misbehaving application required a power cycle.

The fix is the **timer interrupt**. Hardware — specifically, a programmable timer chip (the APIC on x86, a system timer on ARM) — is configured at boot to raise a signal every few milliseconds. The signal is an _interrupt_ , a kind of trap. The CPU, regardless of what user program is running, handles the interrupt by consulting the IDT and jumping to the kernel's timer handler.

The timer handler runs in kernel mode. It does a few bookkeeping tasks (updates the current process's accumulated CPU time, maybe updates the clock) and then decides: has this process used enough CPU? If yes, it calls into the scheduler, which picks a different process to run and performs a context switch.

**Preemptive multitasking** is multitasking where the kernel forcibly preempts the running process via the timer, not by waiting for it to cooperate. Every serious OS since the 1970s has been preemptive. The hardware cost — a few cycles per timer interrupt — is trivial compared to the guarantee it provides: no user program can hold the CPU forever.

#### Common Misconception

> _The timer interrupt happens so often it is a major cost._

On a 1 GHz CPU, a typical timer tick rate is 100 to 1000 Hz. At 1000 Hz, the timer fires once every millisecond — every 10610^6106 cycles. The interrupt handler itself takes maybe 1000 cycles (pessimistically). So the interrupt costs 1000/106=0.1%1000 / 10^6 = 0.1\%1000/106=0.1% of CPU. Doubling or halving the tick rate has barely any effect. The time slices between interrupts, though, are what determine scheduling latency and responsiveness — we will tune these in Chapter 5.

Modern systems have further reduced the cost via **tickless kernels** : if no process needs scheduling attention soon, the kernel tells the timer not to fire for a longer interval, saving power on idle machines. But the fundamental mechanism is unchanged: the timer can fire, and when it does, the kernel is back in control.

### 4.5 The Context Switch

Now the second hard subproblem. The kernel has control. It wants to run a _different_ process. What does that require?

The running process is A; the process we are switching to is B. The kernel's job is:

  1. Save every register of A into A's PCB.
  2. Restore every register of B from B's PCB.
  3. Switch the address space from A's to B's (Chapter 10 will explain how this happens via the page table).
  4. Resume execution.

Conceptually, simple. Mechanically, subtle — because at step 2, the very code running _is_ kernel code that was invoked from A's context, and at step 4, the code running is kernel code that will return to _B's_ context. The switch happens in the middle of a function call that started in one process and ends in another.

Here is the skeleton, in pseudocode:
    
    
    void schedule() {
        struct task *prev = current_process;
        struct task *next = pick_next();
        if (prev == next) return;
        current_process = next;
        switch_to(prev, next);    // magic happens here
    }
    

And `switch_to` — the magic — is implemented in assembly:
    
    
    switch_to(prev, next):
        // Save callee-saved registers onto the current stack
        push %rbp
        push %rbx
        push %r12; push %r13; push %r14; push %r15
    
        // Save the current stack pointer into prev's PCB
        mov %rsp, [prev.kernel_sp]
    
        // Load next's stack pointer from its PCB
        mov [next.kernel_sp], %rsp
    
        // Now we are running on next's kernel stack.
        // Pop its saved callee-saved registers.
        pop %r15; pop %r14; pop %r13; pop %r12
        pop %rbx
        pop %rbp
    
        // Return. The return address on this stack is wherever next
        // called switch_to from last time it was switched out.
        ret
    

Examine the middle step carefully. Before `mov %rsp, [prev.kernel_sp]`, we are running on A's kernel stack. After `mov [next.kernel_sp], %rsp`, we are running on B's kernel stack — with the same function still conceptually executing. The `ret` at the end pops a return address _off B's stack_ , returning into B's kernel code at whatever point B was last suspended at.

The subtlety is that `switch_to` is called from A, and when it returns, it returns into B. This is like a function that crosses between two universes — the call side and the return side are in different processes. It works because the kernel stack of each process _is_ that process's half of the function's state. When the function saves to one stack and loads from another, it has effectively swapped which process is in mid-execution.

Why only callee-saved registers? The C calling convention says that caller-saved registers (`%rax`, `%rdi`, `%rsi`, etc.) are already saved by the caller of `switch_to` (because they are scratch). Only callee-saved registers need explicit saving here. But on the return to user mode — which will happen after we return from the syscall handler that called `schedule()` — every register of the new process will be restored from its saved syscall entry state. Nothing is forgotten.

#### A concrete timeline

Let us trace a full context switch driven by a timer interrupt. A is running in user mode.

  1. **Timer fires** , hardware traps into the kernel. The hardware saves the user PC and flags; the kernel entry stub saves all other registers onto A's kernel stack.
  2. **Interrupt handler runs.** Finds the timer cause, calls `schedule()`.
  3. **Schedule picks next.** The scheduler (Chapter 5) says: run B next. It calls `switch_to(A, B)`.
  4. **Switch_to does the magic.** A's stack pointer is saved in A's PCB; B's stack pointer is loaded from B's PCB. We are now on B's kernel stack, still inside `switch_to`.
  5. **Return to interrupt handler, but in B.** The return address popped off B's stack is wherever B left off — maybe a different instance of `schedule()` from when B was last preempted.
  6. **Interrupt handler returns.** The kernel entry stub pops B's saved registers from B's kernel stack. Finally, `iret` (or `sysret`) — the hardware instruction to leave kernel mode — loads B's user PC and flags, switches back to B's user stack, and flips the mode bit.
  7. **B resumes** in user mode, at exactly the instruction it was about to execute when _it_ was last preempted.

Every process, after its first context switch, always wakes up inside the kernel — in the middle of some interrupt handler or some syscall — because that is where it fell asleep. New processes, the first time they are scheduled, use a special entry path that sets up the initial user-mode state.

### 4.6 Context Switch Cost

How expensive is a context switch?

The direct cost is the number of instructions executed: save ~15 registers, load ~15 registers, change the address space pointer, maybe a few hundred instructions of scheduler logic. On modern hardware, a few hundred cycles. At 1 GHz, this is a fraction of a microsecond.

The indirect cost is larger. When the CPU switches to B:

* **L1 and L2 caches** were warm with A's data. B has different data. Cache misses on B's first accesses are 100+ cycles each. Over the next several microseconds, B slowly refills the cache with its own data. Measurable cost: tens of microseconds of reduced throughput.
* **TLB** (translation lookaside buffer — the cache for page table lookups, Chapter 12) was full of A's translations. If the TLB does not tag entries by address space ID, the whole thing is flushed on context switch, and B faces hundreds of nanoseconds of TLB-miss cost. Modern TLBs support ASIDs (Address Space IDs), reducing this cost, but not eliminating it.
* **Branch predictors** learned A's branches. B has different branches. Mispredictions cost for a while.

The rule of thumb on modern x86 servers: **a context switch costs about 1–10 microseconds, depending on workload.** If a process runs for only 10 microseconds before being switched out, the overhead is 10–50% — crippling. If a process runs for 10 milliseconds, the overhead is 0.01–0.1% — negligible. This is why scheduling decides _how long_ to let each process run (Chapters 5–7); a well-chosen time slice amortizes context-switch cost over enough useful work to make it invisible.

A single machine doing 10,000 context switches per second (a typical value for a loaded desktop) spends 10–100 milliseconds per second on context-switch overhead — 1–10% of CPU. Not terrible; not free. High-performance servers sometimes _pin_ threads to CPUs and use lock-free data structures to reduce context switches — because even at the low percentage, 1% on a server matters.

### 4.7 Bringing It All Together

Let us look at the big picture of Plan A. Chapter 2 gave us the process abstraction. Chapter 3 gave us the API to create more than one. This chapter gave us:

* The **user/kernel mode bit** , enforced by hardware, which keeps user programs from doing privileged things.
* The **trap** , the only path from user mode to kernel mode, directed through the OS-controlled IDT.
* The **syscall** mechanism, by which user programs request kernel services.
* The **timer interrupt** , by which the kernel regains control even without cooperation.
* The **context switch** , by which the kernel moves the CPU from one process's state to another's.

These mechanisms together are _limited direct execution_. "Direct" because user code runs at full CPU speed, on the real hardware, with no virtual machine or interpreter in between. "Limited" because at every moment, the kernel is either in control or can take control, and no user program can permanently hijack the machine.

From here, we move to the policy question: given that the kernel _can_ preempt any process and switch to another, _which_ process should run next? Chapter 5 begins the scheduling story.

### 4.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Why is "direct execution without limits" fatal as a scheme for running user programs?

Hint

Section 4.1 lists two distinct problems.

**Answer:** Two problems. (1) **Security/isolation:** without limits, user programs can execute any instruction and touch any memory or device, so a single buggy or malicious program can destroy the system. (2) **No return to kernel:** once user code starts, nothing brings the kernel back; a program that never voluntarily yields holds the CPU forever. The mode bit + traps solve problem 1; the timer interrupt solves problem 2.

**Q2.** What is a trap table? What stops user programs from installing their own?

**Answer:** The trap table maps each trap cause (syscall, timer interrupt, page fault, etc.) to the address of its kernel handler. User programs cannot install their own because the CPU instruction to install the trap table's base address (`lidt` on x86, for example) is privileged — it traps and fails if executed in user mode. Only kernel code, running in kernel mode, can set up the table. This ensures every trap reaches kernel code the kernel chose.

**Q3.** During a system call, when does the mode bit switch from user to kernel, and when does it switch back? Why does the user program have no ability to alter the mode bit directly?

**Answer:** It switches from user to kernel **automatically, as part of the hardware action of the`syscall` (or trap) instruction**. It switches back as part of `sysret` (or `iret`). Both transitions are done by hardware, not software, so user code cannot influence the timing or target. There is no instruction exposed to user mode that sets the mode bit — it is implicitly set by traps (and only traps route through addresses the kernel controls), so user programs cannot acquire kernel privileges without going through the kernel's chosen entry points.

**Q4.** Preemptive multitasking depends on a specific hardware feature. Name it and explain its role.

**Answer:** The **timer interrupt**. A programmable timer chip, configured by the kernel at boot, raises an interrupt signal at a chosen frequency (typically 100–1000 Hz). Each interrupt pulls the CPU out of whatever user program it was running and transfers control to the kernel's timer handler. Without the timer interrupt, the kernel can only regain control when the user program voluntarily traps (via syscall or fault), so a user program in an infinite loop with no syscalls holds the CPU forever — the cooperative-multitasking failure mode.

**Q5.** Describe the two kernel stacks (or the one kernel stack) involved in a context switch, and why separate kernel stacks matter.

**Answer:** Each process has its own kernel stack. When process A is running in kernel mode (e.g., inside a syscall), it uses A's kernel stack. When the kernel context-switches to process B, `switch_to` saves the current stack pointer into A's PCB and loads B's stack pointer from B's PCB — so the kernel now runs on B's kernel stack. Separate kernel stacks matter because (a) each process can be suspended in kernel code (blocked on a lock, for example), and its kernel-side state must be preserved independently; (b) multiple processes can be in the kernel concurrently on multiprocessors, and each must have its own stack to avoid clobbering another's state.

**Q6.** Why are both direct costs (register saves, cache misses) and indirect costs (TLB flushes, branch predictor state) relevant when measuring context-switch overhead?

**Answer:** Direct costs are the fixed cycles spent saving and restoring register state; they are predictable and small (a few hundred cycles on x86). Indirect costs come from the incoming process finding the caches and prediction structures full of the _outgoing_ process's data and therefore taking extra cycles on its first accesses. These are variable, workload-dependent, and typically dominate total switch overhead (microseconds rather than hundreds of nanoseconds). Ignoring the indirect cost leads to overly-optimistic estimates that do not match observed performance.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** A 2 GHz CPU is configured to fire a timer interrupt at 1000 Hz. Each interrupt handler runs for 2000 cycles on average. What percentage of CPU time is consumed by the interrupt mechanism itself, independent of scheduling decisions?

Hint

Cycles per second of interrupt handling, divided by cycles per second of the CPU.

**Answer:** The CPU runs 2×1092 \times 10^92×109 cycles per second. The timer fires 1000 times per second, and each handler consumes 2000 cycles. Total interrupt cycles per second: 1000×2000=2×1061000 \times 2000 = 2 \times 10^61000×2000=2×106. Fraction of CPU: 2×1062×109=10−3=0.1%\frac{2 \times 10^6}{2 \times 10^9} = 10^{-3} = 0.1\%2×1092×106​=10−3=0.1%. One-tenth of one percent — effectively free.

This is why timer-driven preemption is so widely adopted: it imposes trivial overhead while providing complete immunity from runaway processes. The scheduler's decisions are what determine overall efficiency, not the timer mechanism.

**Q2.** The context-switch routine given in Section 4.5 only saves callee-saved registers. Why is this safe? What _else_ is saved, and by whom?

**Answer:** The context-switch routine runs inside the kernel, called from the scheduler, which itself was called from (say) the timer interrupt handler. When the timer interrupt first trapped, the kernel's interrupt entry stub saved _all_ general-purpose registers of the user process onto the process's kernel stack. So user-visible register state is fully preserved before `switch_to` is called.

`switch_to` then only needs to save _itself_ — its own callee-saved registers, so that when it eventually returns, the rest of the kernel function that called it can continue correctly. Caller-saved registers are not saved here because the C caller of `switch_to` has already saved any it needed.

When the outgoing process later returns to user mode, its saved user registers come off its kernel stack (where they were placed at the original trap), not off `switch_to`'s state. So the layered save/restore is: hardware + entry stub save user registers; `switch_to` saves kernel callee-saved registers; return path reverses both.

**Q3.** A server process spends 50% of its time in user code and 50% in kernel code (handling syscalls). The average context switch costs 5 microseconds. The server is context-switched 20,000 times per second. How much CPU is consumed by context switching alone? How much useful time does that leave?

**Answer:** 20,000 context switches × 5 μs = 100,000 μs = 100 ms of context-switching per second of wall clock. This is 10% of CPU. The server has 900 ms of CPU per second left for its own work (user + kernel). This is high but not catastrophic. If the switch rate doubles (e.g., due to more active clients), so does the overhead — at 40,000 switches per second, 20% of CPU is gone to context switching.

This exercise motivates scheduler design choices: longer time slices reduce switch count but hurt responsiveness; shorter slices improve responsiveness but raise overhead. Chapter 5 will tune this tradeoff.

**Q4.** The syscall path in Section 4.3 described six or seven steps. On modern x86-64 Linux, a `getpid()` call — which returns a small cached integer — takes about 70 nanoseconds. On a 3 GHz CPU, that is about 210 cycles. Roughly how does this cost compare to an ordinary function call? What does that imply about when syscalls are worth optimizing away?

**Answer:** An ordinary function call takes about 1–3 cycles (if everything is in cache). A syscall is thus **70–200× more expensive** than a function call. If an application makes syscalls in a hot loop — for example, a network server calling `recvmsg` once per packet — the syscall overhead becomes a significant fraction of its CPU cost. At 10 Gbps with small packets (say, 64 bytes each), the server handles ~15 million packets per second; 70 ns per syscall means 1.05 seconds of syscall time per second of wall clock — the CPU is saturated with syscalls alone, before any useful work.

This is precisely why high-performance network software (DPDK, XDP, io_uring) uses kernel-bypass or batching: to reduce syscalls per packet from O(1) to O(1/batch). In contrast, for a workload that makes 100 syscalls per second, the overhead is 7 μs per second — entirely invisible. The optimization target scales with syscall rate, not syscall existence.

**Q5.** Trace the concrete register flow for `syscall` on x86-64, listing what the hardware saves into which registers and where kernel code picks up. You do not need to memorize the register names — just track which logical piece of state ends up where.

**Answer:**

* **Before`syscall`:** user process has set `%rax` = syscall number, `%rdi`/`%rsi`/`%rdx`/etc. = arguments.
* **Hardware on`syscall`:**
    * Saves user **PC** (the instruction after syscall) into `%rcx`.
    * Saves user **RFLAGS** into `%r11`.
    * Loads new PC from **IA32_LSTAR** (the model-specific register the kernel wrote at boot).
    * Sets mode bit to kernel.
    * Does _not_ switch stacks automatically; the kernel entry stub does that using the `swapgs` instruction to find the kernel stack pointer.
* **Kernel entry stub:**
    * Uses `swapgs` to bring in kernel GS segment.
    * Switches to the kernel stack.
    * Pushes every general-purpose register the handler might clobber (including `%rcx` and `%r11`, so they can be restored on return).
    * Calls the C-level dispatcher with the syscall number in `%rax`.
* **Dispatcher:** indexes syscall table, calls handler, returns result in `%rax`.
* **Kernel exit stub:** pops all saved registers, uses `swapgs` to restore user GS, executes `sysret`.
* **Hardware on`sysret`:**
    * Loads user PC from `%rcx`.
    * Loads user RFLAGS from `%r11`.
    * Sets mode bit to user.
* **After`sysret`:** user program resumes at the instruction after `syscall`, with `%rax` containing the syscall's return value.

The key insight is that the hardware and software split responsibilities: hardware handles the mode/PC switch atomically (so the boundary cannot be breached mid-transition), and software handles the register save/restore (so the rest of the kernel can use ordinary C calling conventions).

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** Some systems (notably the Linux `sysenter`/`sysexit` path and the older `int 0x80` path) have _not_ matched the modern `syscall`/`sysret` path in performance. What design principle explains why adding a hardware-specialized syscall instruction sped things up so much, and what does it tell us about the general cost of abstraction boundaries?

**Discussion:** The old `int 0x80` mechanism treated a syscall like any other interrupt — going through the full IDT lookup, pushing more state than strictly necessary, doing extra checks. `syscall` is a _narrower_ instruction specialized for the common case: it assumes a single known target address (from a dedicated MSR), saves only the two registers the kernel actually needs to preserve, and does not go through the IDT at all. The cost savings come from the CPU being told up front what case it is handling, letting it use dedicated hardware paths instead of generic ones.

The general lesson: abstraction boundaries are expensive partly because the hardware cannot specialize across them. When the hardware knows "this is always a syscall, to a known address, with a fixed register save pattern," it can drop a lot of generality. The same principle appears elsewhere — custom DMA paths for networking, specialized cache hierarchies for matrix-heavy workloads, vector instructions instead of generic ALU operations. Every time an architectural primitive becomes common enough, making it faster is a form of reducing abstraction cost.

The counter-argument is that specialization locks the architecture into today's usage patterns. If tomorrow's OS wants syscalls to carry more context, or have two kernel entry points, the specialized instruction resists change. Part of the tension in CPU design is deciding which abstractions to accelerate (and thereby freeze) and which to leave general.

**Q2.** On machines with many CPU cores (say, 64), context switches are no longer the main concern — each core can run its own process. But context switches _between different types of work_ on the same core still matter. Give one example of a workload where reducing context switches between user code and kernel code, even on a many-core machine, would be worth substantial engineering effort. Why?

**Discussion:** High-performance networking at line rate. A 100 Gbps NIC, with 64-byte packets, delivers 200 million packets per second. If each packet requires a syscall (recv, send), and a syscall costs 100 ns, the CPU spends 20 seconds of syscall time per second of wall clock — impossible. Even with perfect parallelism across 64 cores, each core would spend 300 ms per second on syscalls for 1/64 of the traffic — still about 30% of CPU per core, and only for entering the kernel.

Solutions include io_uring (shared-memory rings between user and kernel, so a batch of I/Os uses one or zero syscalls), DPDK (move the entire network stack into user space, bypassing the kernel entirely), and XDP (run packet-filter programs in the kernel eBPF VM to handle most packets without ever entering general kernel code). All three address the same concern: the user/kernel boundary crossing is too expensive to pay per packet at modern line rates.

More cores does not help here because the bottleneck is the boundary-crossing rate per packet, not the total compute. Add cores and you just pay the boundary-crossing tax in parallel. Reducing crossings per packet is the only fix.

### Chapter Summary

This chapter completed the Plan A arc from "one process" (Chapter 2) through "an API to create more" (Chapter 3) to "a mechanism that shares one CPU among them." The ingredients are **limited direct execution** : user code runs at full speed on the real CPU (direct), but the kernel retains control through the user/kernel mode bit, traps, and the timer interrupt (limited).

Traps are the universal entry into the kernel. Synchronous traps (system calls, faults) are initiated by the user program. Asynchronous traps (interrupts, most importantly the timer) are initiated by hardware. Both land the CPU at an address the kernel chose when it filled in the interrupt descriptor table. The mode bit cannot be set by user code; the IDT cannot be rewritten by user code. These are the two hardware-enforced guarantees that make everything else in this book possible.

The **context switch** is the kernel's routine for moving the CPU from one process to another — save every register of the outgoing process, restore every register of the incoming process, change the address space. Most of the cost is indirect: cache misses, TLB flushes, branch predictor state. Typical cost on modern hardware: 1–10 microseconds.

From this chapter's foundation, Chapter 5 begins the **scheduling** story: _which_ process should the kernel pick, and for _how long_ should it run? The mechanism (context switch) is now in place; the policy question is open, and it will occupy Chapters 5–8.

---

## Chapter 5: Scheduling I — FIFO, SJF, and Round Robin

At the end of Chapter 4, the kernel has everything it needs to _run one process, then another_. The context switch is in place. The timer interrupt gives the kernel the power to preempt whenever it likes. What the kernel has _not yet answered_ is the question every scheduler must answer: out of the many ready processes, **which one runs next, and for how long?**

This is a policy question, not a mechanism question. The mechanism is settled. But the policy has deep consequences: it determines whether interactive programs feel snappy, whether batch workloads finish quickly, whether the machine spends its time doing real work or just bouncing between processes that never get enough CPU to accomplish anything.

To think about scheduling policy, we need a vocabulary for judging candidate policies. This chapter introduces the vocabulary, then three foundational policies — FIFO, SJF/SRTF, and Round Robin — each illustrating a fundamental tradeoff. Chapter 6 refines with MLFQ; Chapter 7 with CFS. But the baselines come first.

Threading Plan A expands here. Chapter 2 had one process that owned everything; Chapter 4 had two; now, imagine **ten**. Ten processes are in the ready queue. The scheduler must pick. How it picks determines the user experience.

### 5.1 Metrics: Turnaround Time and Response Time

Before evaluating a scheduler, we have to agree on what "good" means. Two metrics matter.

**Turnaround time** is the time from when a job arrives in the system to when it completes. Tturnaround=Tcompletion−TarrivalT_{\text{turnaround}} = T_{\text{completion}} - T_{\text{arrival}}Tturnaround​=Tcompletion​−Tarrival​. For a one-hour batch job that starts immediately when it arrives, the turnaround is one hour. If that same job has to wait two hours in the queue before running, its turnaround is three hours.
