We usually care about **average** turnaround time across many jobs: the mean turnaround is a single number summarizing how fast a scheduler completes its workload.

**Response time** is the time from when a job arrives until it first runs. Tresponse=Tfirst-run−TarrivalT_{\text{response}} = T_{\text{first-run}} - T_{\text{arrival}}Tresponse​=Tfirst-run​−Tarrival​. A batch job does not care about response time. An interactive job — a text editor responding to your keystroke, a browser responding to your click — cares a lot. If response time is 200 milliseconds, the user notices lag. If it is 50 ms, the interaction feels instantaneous.

These two metrics are in tension. Optimizing one often hurts the other. A scheduler that minimizes turnaround by running jobs to completion one at a time gives terrible response time. A scheduler that switches often to keep response time low pays context-switch overhead that raises turnaround. The art of scheduling is navigating this tension.

#### Common Misconception

> _A single metric — say, "throughput" — captures scheduler quality._

No. A scheduler optimized for throughput (maximizing completed work per second) might starve small interactive jobs so that the big batch job runs without preemption. The user with the editor sees lag. A scheduler optimized for response time might switch so often that throughput collapses. Good schedulers balance multiple metrics, and which balance is "correct" depends on the workload. Interactive desktops value response time; batch clusters value turnaround; mixed workloads want both. Keep multiple metrics in mind throughout this chapter and the next.

### 5.2 Workload Assumptions

To analyze scheduling algorithms, we will imagine the simplest possible workload, then loosen assumptions one by one. Our initial model:

* Each job runs for the same amount of time: **run length = T**.
* All jobs arrive at the same time (time 0).
* Once started, each job runs to completion without I/O (no blocking).
* The run length of each job is **known in advance**.
* There is one CPU.

With these assumptions, scheduling looks trivial. We will see it is not. Then we will remove the assumptions, starting with run-length equality, and each relaxation will force a scheduler revision.

### 5.3 FIFO: First-In, First-Out

The simplest conceivable policy: run jobs in the order they arrived. Also called First-Come, First-Served (FCFS).

**Example.** Three jobs, A, B, C, all arrive at time 0, each takes 10 seconds. FIFO runs A first, then B, then C.

Job | Start | Finish | Turnaround  
---|---|---|---  
A | 0 | 10 | 10  
B | 10 | 20 | 20  
C | 20 | 30 | 30  
  
Average turnaround: (10+20+30)/3=20(10 + 20 + 30) / 3 = 20(10+20+30)/3=20 seconds.

With equal-length jobs, FIFO is fine. Now loosen: suppose A takes 100 seconds while B and C still take 10.

Job | Start | Finish | Turnaround  
---|---|---|---  
A | 0 | 100 | 100  
B | 100 | 110 | 110  
C | 110 | 120 | 120  
  
Average turnaround: (100+110+120)/3=110(100 + 110 + 120) / 3 = 110(100+110+120)/3=110 seconds.

This is terrible. A short job (B) arrived at the same time as everyone else but has to wait 100 seconds behind the long one. This is the **convoy effect** : short jobs stuck behind long jobs, their turnaround dominated by the long job's runtime.

The convoy effect is FIFO's fatal flaw. In any realistic workload with mixed job lengths, FIFO produces huge average turnaround, because short jobs (which should finish quickly) are held up by long jobs.

### 5.4 SJF: Shortest Job First

If we know each job's run length in advance, we can fix the convoy effect: run the shortest job first.

**Same example:** A (100 sec), B (10 sec), C (10 sec), all at time 0.

SJF order: B, C, A.

Job | Start | Finish | Turnaround  
---|---|---|---  
B | 0 | 10 | 10  
C | 10 | 20 | 20  
A | 20 | 120 | 120  
  
Average turnaround: (10+20+120)/3=50(10 + 20 + 120) / 3 = 50(10+20+120)/3=50 seconds.

Much better — less than half of FIFO. SJF is in fact **optimal** for average turnaround when all jobs arrive at the same time and their run lengths are known. (A quick argument: any reordering that swaps a shorter job with a later longer one makes the sum of completion times bigger.)

But SJF has two serious practical problems.

**Problem 1: What if jobs arrive at different times?** Suppose A (100 sec) arrives at time 0, then B and C (10 sec each) arrive at time 10. By that time, A has been running for 10 seconds; SJF as stated would wait for A to finish (at time 100) before running B. But once B and C arrive and are shorter than the remaining work on A (90 sec remaining), it would be better to preempt A and run them. That leads to **SRTF**.

**Problem 2: How do we know run lengths in advance?** In general, we do not. The scheduler can _estimate_ based on past behavior (a shell that ran for 100 ms last time probably runs for 100 ms this time), but it cannot know exactly. Real schedulers use SJF-like behavior where it can be estimated; we will see MLFQ (Chapter 6) do exactly this.

### 5.5 SRTF: Shortest Remaining Time First

Preemptive version of SJF. Whenever a new job arrives, the scheduler checks: does the new job's total run length beat the remaining time of the currently running job? If so, preempt and switch.

**Example.** A (100 sec) arrives at 0. B (10 sec) arrives at 10. C (10 sec) arrives at 10.

At time 0, A starts. At time 10, A has 90 sec remaining. B and C have 10 sec each. SRTF preempts A, runs B. At time 20, B finishes. SRTF runs C. At time 30, C finishes. SRTF resumes A. At time 120, A finishes.

Job | Arrival | Start (first) | Finish | Turnaround  
---|---|---|---|---  
A | 0 | 0 (preempted at 10, resumed at 30) | 120 | 120  
B | 10 | 10 | 20 | 10  
C | 10 | 20 | 30 | 20  
  
Average turnaround: (120+10+20)/3=50(120 + 10 + 20) / 3 = 50(120+10+20)/3=50 seconds.

SRTF gives short-arriving jobs priority and is optimal for average turnaround under run-length knowledge, at the cost of needing preemption support (which, from Chapter 4, we have) and run-length foreknowledge (which, in general, we do not).

**Response time for SRTF?** B arrived at 10 and first ran at 10 — response time 0. C arrived at 10 but first ran at 20 — response time 10. A arrived at 0 and first ran at 0 — response time 0. Not bad _for these jobs_ , but consider what happens in a workload of many interactive jobs, each of unknown length. SRTF can't recognize which is interactive; any long-running job gets delayed if shorter jobs arrive. Sometimes this is what you want; sometimes it is starvation.

### 5.6 Round Robin

The response-time problem with FIFO and SJF/SRTF is clear: when a job arrives, it may have to wait for a long job to complete (or at least, for a long job's large remaining time to be surpassed by something shorter). For interactive workloads, what we want is for _every_ job to run a little, often.

**Round Robin** (RR) takes this literally. Define a **time slice** (also called a quantum), say 10 milliseconds. Every time a process has run for a time slice, the scheduler preempts it and moves it to the back of the ready queue. The scheduler then picks the next process from the front of the queue. Every process gets its turn every N × slice, where N is the number of ready processes.

**Example.** Three jobs, A, B, C, all CPU-bound, all 1 second long. Time slice 100 ms.

At time 0, A runs for 100 ms. At time 100 ms, A is preempted; B runs for 100 ms. At time 200 ms, B is preempted; C runs for 100 ms. At time 300 ms, C is preempted; A resumes. ... and so on, interleaving in chunks of 100 ms each.

Let us trace this explicitly. Each round consists of three 100 ms slices (A, B, C). After each full round, every job has accumulated one more 100 ms slice. Every job needs 1000 ms total, so every job needs ten slices, and the scheduler runs ten full rounds.

Round | A runs | B runs | C runs | A remaining (after) | B remaining | C remaining  
---|---|---|---|---|---|---  
1 | 0–100 | 100–200 | 200–300 | 900 | 900 | 900  
2 | 300–400 | 400–500 | 500–600 | 800 | 800 | 800  
... | ... | ... | ... | ... | ... | ...  
10 | 2700–2800 | 2800–2900 | 2900–3000 | 0 | 0 | 0  
  
So A finishes at 2800, B at 2900, C at 3000.

Turnarounds: A = 2800, B = 2900, C = 3000. Average: (2800+2900+3000)/3=2900(2800 + 2900 + 3000)/3 = 2900(2800+2900+3000)/3=2900 ms.

Compare FIFO on the same workload: A runs 0–1000, B runs 1000–2000, C runs 2000–3000. Turnarounds 1000, 2000, 3000, average 2000 ms. Round Robin is _worse_ for average turnaround than FIFO on equal-length jobs.

Why? Round Robin does a bad job at turnaround because it does not let jobs finish quickly — it stretches every job out by running the others in between. But it does a great job at **response time** : every job's first slice runs within one round (300 ms of arrival), regardless of how many other jobs are ready. Compare FIFO, where the third job waits 2000 ms before running at all.

#### Tradeoff: slice length

Short slice → low response time (every job runs sooner) but high overhead (more context switches per second). Long slice → low overhead but high response time.

Quick math. Context switch cost (from Chapter 4): ~5 μs. If slice = 1 ms, there are 1000 switches per second, costing 5 ms of overhead per second — 0.5% overhead. If slice = 10 ms, 100 switches per second, 0.5 ms overhead per second — 0.05% overhead. Both are fine. But if slice = 100 μs, 10,000 switches per second, 50 ms overhead per second — 5% overhead, noticeable.

Desktop systems typically use slices in the 1–20 ms range. Server systems doing mostly throughput work can use longer slices (50–100 ms) with no user impact. Real-time systems sometimes use much shorter slices to guarantee bounded latency. The choice is tuned to the workload.

### 5.7 Incorporating I/O

So far we assumed jobs run to completion without blocking. Real jobs do I/O: they issue a disk read and wait, they send a network packet and wait, they read from the terminal and wait. What should the scheduler do?

Simple answer: when a job blocks on I/O, remove it from the ready queue. When the I/O completes, put it back on the ready queue. In between, the scheduler sees only the ready jobs.

This has a profound consequence: a CPU-bound job that never blocks gets full slices; an I/O-bound job that blocks after every 10 ms of CPU gets only 10 ms slices (less than the full time slice). If both are in the system, the I/O-bound job effectively yields some of its fair share of the CPU — because it is not using it. The CPU-bound job does _not_ get a bigger slice; it just runs while the I/O-bound job is blocked.

This is the scheduler-level story behind why interactive programs feel responsive on a loaded system. Interactive programs block often (waiting for keystrokes, network responses), so even when many CPU-bound jobs compete, the interactive jobs are usually at the front of the ready queue when they become ready. The scheduler does not even need to know they are "interactive" — the CPU usage pattern does the sorting.

Chapter 6's MLFQ will exploit this pattern deliberately, promoting I/O-bound processes to higher priority because they behave like jobs the scheduler would want to prioritize.

### 5.8 A Policy-Level Summary

We have four schedulers, each a position on the response-time vs turnaround-time axis.

Scheduler | Idea | Turnaround | Response | Needs knowledge?  
---|---|---|---|---  
FIFO | Run in arrival order | Bad (convoy) | Terrible (long jobs block short) | No  
SJF | Run shortest first | Optimal (no arrivals) | Good | Yes  
SRTF | Preemptive SJF | Optimal | Good | Yes  
RR | Every job gets a small slice | Bad (longer than FIFO) | Excellent | No  
  
The ideal scheduler would know job lengths, so it could prioritize short jobs, _and_ would preempt to give interactive jobs good response, _and_ would avoid the context-switch overhead of tiny slices. This ideal is approximated by MLFQ in Chapter 6 — using observed behavior as a proxy for prior knowledge — and by CFS in Chapter 7 using a different approach entirely (proportional share).

The other lesson, subtler, is that **there is no perfect scheduler**. The workload decides what "perfect" means. Batch workloads want turnaround; interactive workloads want response; mixed workloads want balance. The history of scheduling is the history of policies that balance better or worse on specific workload distributions.

### 5.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Define turnaround time and response time. Give one example workload where each is the more important metric.

Hint

Interactive vs batch.

**Answer:** **Turnaround time** is the time from a job's arrival to its completion. **Response time** is the time from arrival until it first runs. Turnaround matters for batch jobs (compile farms, nightly report generation) where users care only about completion. Response matters for interactive jobs (text editors, GUIs) where users perceive any delay before _some_ activity as lag. A compile cluster optimizing for average turnaround is doing its job; a desktop optimizing for average turnaround without regard to response would make every user's editor feel sluggish.

**Q2.** What is the convoy effect? Which scheduling policies exhibit it, and which policies avoid it?

**Answer:** The **convoy effect** happens when short jobs arrive behind long jobs and must wait for the long job to complete before they get to run. **FIFO** suffers from it; any scheduling order that uses strict arrival order does. **SJF** avoids it by running short jobs first; **SRTF** avoids it by preempting in favor of shorter jobs; **Round Robin** avoids it by rotating every job forward on a fixed cadence, so no job waits longer than `(N-1) × slice` for its next turn.

**Q3.** Why is SJF impractical without oracle knowledge of job run lengths? What do real schedulers substitute for oracle knowledge?

**Answer:** SJF as stated assumes the scheduler knows each job's total CPU time in advance. In general, the scheduler does not — the job is a running program whose behavior depends on inputs, branches, allocations. Real schedulers substitute _past behavior_ for prior knowledge: they estimate a job's likely future based on how it has behaved so far. MLFQ (Chapter 6) does this by watching whether a job uses its whole time slice (suggesting it is CPU-bound and should be deprioritized) or blocks quickly (suggesting it is interactive and should stay at high priority). This is not perfect but is a good approximation.

**Q4.** In a Round Robin scheduler with time slice 10 ms and 100 ready processes, what is the _worst-case_ response time for a newly arriving interactive process?

**Answer:** In the worst case, the new process arrives just after a full round has begun, meaning all 100 existing processes must run their 10 ms slice before the new one gets its turn. Worst-case response time is 100×10100 \times 10100×10 ms = **1000 ms = 1 second**. This is a response time disaster for interactive workloads. In practice, interactive processes are at the front of the ready queue more often (because they block on I/O frequently), and real schedulers (MLFQ, CFS) boost their priority. But the pure RR worst case is tied to the product of slice length and ready-set size.

**Q5.** With 1 GHz CPU and 5 μs context-switch cost, compute the overhead percentage for Round Robin with slice lengths of 100 μs, 1 ms, 10 ms, and 100 ms.

**Answer:** Overhead per switch = 5 μs. Switches per second = 106 μs/sslice\frac{10^6 \text{ μs/s}}{\text{slice}}slice106 μs/s​.

* **100 μs slice:** 10,000 switches/s → 50 ms overhead/s → **5%**.
* **1 ms slice:** 1000 switches/s → 5 ms overhead/s → **0.5%**.
* **10 ms slice:** 100 switches/s → 0.5 ms overhead/s → **0.05%**.
* **100 ms slice:** 10 switches/s → 0.05 ms overhead/s → **0.005%**.

So slices much shorter than context-switch cost are wasteful; slices much longer dilute response time without benefiting overhead. The "sweet spot" of 1–20 ms balances both in practice, which is what real schedulers use.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Four jobs arrive at time 0: A (8 sec), B (4 sec), C (2 sec), D (6 sec). Compute average turnaround time for FIFO (in arrival order A, B, C, D), SJF, and Round Robin with a 1-second slice. Which performs best, and by how much?

Hint

Make a table for each scheduler showing each job's completion time, then average.

**Answer:**

_FIFO (A, B, C, D):_

* A: 0-8, turnaround 8
* B: 8-12, turnaround 12
* C: 12-14, turnaround 14
* D: 14-20, turnaround 20
* Average: (8+12+14+20)/4=54/4=13.5(8 + 12 + 14 + 20)/4 = 54/4 = 13.5(8+12+14+20)/4=54/4=13.5 sec.

_SJF (C, B, D, A sorted by length):_

* C: 0-2, turnaround 2
* B: 2-6, turnaround 6
* D: 6-12, turnaround 12
* A: 12-20, turnaround 20
* Average: (2+6+12+20)/4=40/4=10(2 + 6 + 12 + 20)/4 = 40/4 = 10(2+6+12+20)/4=40/4=10 sec.

_Round Robin, 1-sec slice:_ Jobs arrive in order A, B, C, D at time 0 and enter the ready queue in that order. With a 1-second slice, the scheduler runs the head of the queue for 1 sec and then moves it to the tail (unless it has finished). Total work across all jobs is 8 + 4 + 2 + 6 = 20 slices.

Time slots (each 1 sec):

* t=0: A runs. A has 7 remaining. B, C, D queued.
* t=1: B runs. B has 3 remaining. C, D, A queued.
* t=2: C runs. C has 1 remaining. D, A, B queued.
* t=3: D runs. D has 5 remaining. A, B, C queued.
* t=4: A runs. A has 6 remaining. B, C, D queued.
* t=5: B runs. B has 2 remaining. C, D, A queued.
* t=6: C runs. C has 0 remaining — C done at time 7! Turnaround 7.
* t=7: D runs. D has 4 remaining. A, B queued.
* t=8: A runs. A has 5. B, D queued.
* t=9: B runs. B has 1. D, A queued.
* t=10: D runs. D has 3. A, B queued.
* t=11: A runs. A has 4. B, D queued.
* t=12: B runs. B has 0 — B done at time 13! Turnaround 13.
* t=13: D runs. D has 2. A queued.
* t=14: A runs. A has 3. D queued.
* t=15: D runs. D has 1. A queued.
* t=16: A runs. A has 2. D queued.
* t=17: D runs. D has 0 — D done at time 18! Turnaround 18.
* t=18: A runs. A has 1.
* t=19: A runs. A has 0 — A done at time 20! Turnaround 20.

RR turnarounds: A=20, B=13, C=7, D=18. Average: (20+13+7+18)/4=58/4=14.5(20 + 13 + 7 + 18)/4 = 58/4 = 14.5(20+13+7+18)/4=58/4=14.5 sec.

_Summary:_ SJF wins at **10 sec** average, FIFO is **13.5** , RR is **14.5**. SJF is best for turnaround; RR is worst. But RR has the best response time — every job starts by time 3.

**Q2.** Redo the RR calculation in Q1 with a 2-second slice. Is turnaround better or worse? Why?

**Answer:** With a 2-second slice:

* t=0-2: A runs, A has 6 left.
* t=2-4: B runs, B has 2 left.
* t=4-6: C runs, C has 0 left. C done at 6. Turnaround 6.
* t=6-8: D runs, D has 4 left.
* t=8-10: A runs, A has 4 left.
* t=10-12: B runs, B has 0 left. B done at 12. Turnaround 12.
* t=12-14: D runs, D has 2 left.
* t=14-16: A runs, A has 2 left.
* t=16-18: D runs, D has 0 left. D done at 18. Turnaround 18.
* t=18-20: A runs, A done at 20. Turnaround 20.

Average: (20+12+6+18)/4=56/4=14(20 + 12 + 6 + 18)/4 = 56/4 = 14(20+12+6+18)/4=56/4=14 sec.

Slightly better than 1-sec slice (14.5) because larger slices let short jobs finish earlier before context-switching to others. Not as good as SJF (10) because RR does not preferentially schedule short jobs — it just rotates. Response time suffers with longer slices: worst-case response moves from 3 to 6.

**Q3.** A system runs two types of jobs: (i) CPU-bound jobs that each want 100 ms of pure CPU; (ii) interactive jobs that want 2 ms of CPU, then block for 50 ms on I/O, then want another 2 ms of CPU, and so on. With FIFO (non-preemptive), describe the experience of an interactive user when ten CPU-bound jobs and one interactive job are submitted simultaneously.

**Answer:** Under non-preemptive FIFO, the ten CPU-bound jobs run in arrival order, each for 100 ms. If the interactive job arrives behind them, it waits 1000 ms (= 10 × 100 ms) before first running. Its response time is a full second. Every time it blocks for I/O (50 ms), the scheduler runs the next CPU-bound job to completion — another 100 ms — during which the interactive job's I/O finishes but it still cannot run. The interactive job's effective responsiveness is measured in seconds rather than tens of milliseconds. The user experience is unusable.

Under Round Robin (10 ms slice), the interactive job gets a slice within 110 ms of arrival, and every time it unblocks, it is scheduled within roughly 110 ms. Not great, but much better. Under a smart scheduler that recognizes I/O-bound jobs as interactive and boosts their priority (MLFQ, Chapter 6), the interactive job gets its slice within a few milliseconds of unblocking — the user experience is close to dedicated machine. This exercise motivates every scheduler discussion in Chapters 6–7.

**Q4.** A cluster runs SRTF on a workload where jobs arrive with a heavy-tail distribution: 99% of jobs complete in under 1 second; 1% take more than 1 hour. Under SRTF, what happens to the 1% (the long jobs)?

**Answer:** They suffer **starvation**. Every time a long job is mid-execution, short jobs keep arriving. Since short jobs have shorter remaining time than the long job, SRTF preempts the long job to run each short arrival. As long as short jobs keep arriving — which, in a heavy-tail workload, they continually do — the long job never gets to run.

Formally, the long job's turnaround time is unbounded if the arrival rate of short jobs is high enough. This is why pure SRTF is rarely used in production. Real schedulers add **anti-starvation** provisions: MLFQ periodically boosts long jobs' priority (Chapter 6); CFS uses vruntime so long-running jobs still get proportional share (Chapter 7). Any scheduler that gives absolute preference to a class of jobs eventually needs a mechanism to prevent the other class from starving.

**Q5.** A Round Robin scheduler with time slice 10 ms serves 10 interactive processes. Each process wants 5 ms of CPU, then blocks for 100 ms on I/O, then wants 5 ms, and so on. What fraction of the CPU does the scheduler keep busy? What happens if 5 CPU-bound processes (each wanting full slices, never blocking) are added?

**Answer:** With only the 10 interactive processes: each uses 5 ms out of every 105 ms cycle. Aggregate CPU demand is 10×(5/105)=50/105≈47.6%10 \times (5 / 105) = 50/105 \approx 47.6\%10×(5/105)=50/105≈47.6%. The scheduler is idle (no ready process, all waiting on I/O) about 52.4% of the time. Throughput is entirely demand-limited; any scheduler that is not pathological performs well.

Add 5 CPU-bound processes. They are always ready. Whenever an interactive process is blocked, the scheduler runs a CPU-bound one. CPU utilization jumps to 100%. In a Round Robin scheduler with 10 ms slices:

* Interactive processes still run for 5 ms each when ready (they block voluntarily before the slice expires).
* CPU-bound processes get full 10 ms slices.
* When an interactive process unblocks, it joins the back of the ready queue. It may wait behind one or more CPU-bound slices before running — so response time degrades to about 50 ms (5 CPU-bound × 10 ms) in the worst case.

Chapter 6 will show how MLFQ _preserves_ good response time for the interactive jobs even when CPU-bound jobs compete, by priority-boosting processes that block voluntarily.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** SJF is optimal for average turnaround under the assumption that all jobs arrive at time 0 and run lengths are known. Argue informally why no scheduler can do better.

**Discussion:** Consider any schedule of the jobs, laid out on a timeline. Each job's turnaround time is its completion time (since arrivals are all at time 0). The sum of turnaround times equals the sum of completion times. To minimize this sum, we want jobs to complete as early as possible.

The earliest any single job can complete is at time equal to its length. The second job completes at time = first job's length + second job's length, etc. So if jobs are ordered with lengths L1,L2,...,LnL_1, L_2, ..., L_nL1​,L2​,...,Ln​, total sum of completion times is ∑k=1n(n−k+1)Lk\sum_{k=1}^n (n - k + 1) L_k∑k=1n​(n−k+1)Lk​. Each LkL_kLk​ is multiplied by the number of jobs that _wait_ behind it (including itself). To minimize the sum, put the shortest job first (so it gets the biggest multiplier times the smallest LLL). By a classic exchange argument: swapping a longer job ahead of a shorter one increases the sum.

This is the scheduling version of a more general principle: when summing multiples ∑aiwi\sum a_i w_i∑ai​wi​ where you can choose the order of aia_iai​, smaller aia_iai​ should get larger weights. SJF pairs the smallest LLL with the largest multiplier and so minimizes the sum. Mean turnaround is this sum divided by nnn, so SJF minimizes mean turnaround.

**Q2.** Real-world schedulers must balance multiple metrics that are all in tension: turnaround, response, fairness, priority. Which other approach could one take, besides adjusting the weighting of these metrics?

**Discussion:** One approach is **multiple classes of scheduling**. Rather than one scheduler optimizing a blend of metrics, the OS maintains multiple scheduling classes, each with its own policy. Real-time processes go into a real-time class with priority-based, no-preemption-within-class semantics. Interactive processes go into a CFS-like class. Batch processes go into a low-priority class. The kernel picks a class to service based on priorities, and within each class, that class's policy applies.

Linux does exactly this: `SCHED_FIFO` and `SCHED_RR` for hard real-time, `SCHED_DEADLINE` for deadline-driven real-time, `SCHED_NORMAL` (CFS) for general workloads, `SCHED_IDLE` for lowest-priority background work. The tension is not between metrics but between _classes of work_ , with different policies for different classes.

Another approach is **user-visible knobs** : let users (or admins) express preferences via priorities (Unix nice values), cgroup weights, or explicit deadlines. The scheduler does not guess what matters; it is told.

Both approaches acknowledge the chapter's main lesson: no single metric captures scheduling quality, and the optimal policy depends on what the workload is. Classes and knobs turn "scheduling policy" from a fixed algorithm into a configurable surface.

### Chapter Summary

Scheduling is a policy decision on top of the mechanism (context switching) we built in Chapter 4. We introduced two core metrics — turnaround time and response time — and three baseline policies: FIFO (simple but suffers the convoy effect), SJF and SRTF (optimal for turnaround but need run-length knowledge and can starve long jobs), and Round Robin (excellent for response time but mediocre for turnaround). No single policy dominates; each occupies a point on the response-time-vs-turnaround tradeoff.

We also introduced the reality of I/O: when processes block, they leave the ready queue and do not compete for CPU. This implicitly rewards I/O-bound processes with small effective slice sizes, which aligns with wanting interactive programs to feel snappy. But a naïve scheduler does not exploit this pattern deliberately.

Chapter 6 will show how the **Multi-Level Feedback Queue (MLFQ)** combines the best of the schedulers seen here: SRTF-like behavior for unknown job lengths by observing behavior, Round-Robin-like responsiveness via small slices, and anti-starvation via periodic priority boosting. It is the scheduler behind every classic Unix (Solaris, FreeBSD, older Linux), and understanding it is the bridge between this chapter's idealized policies and the pragmatic designs in production.

---

## Chapter 6: The Multi-Level Feedback Queue

The scheduling policies of Chapter 5 split into two camps. On one side, SJF and SRTF optimize turnaround time but require foreknowledge of job lengths. On the other, FIFO and Round Robin work with no foreknowledge but produce bad turnaround (FIFO), bad response time (FIFO again), or mediocre turnaround (RR).

What we really want is an algorithm that _learns_ how long each job is going to be and treats short and long jobs appropriately — without ever being told in advance. In particular, we want:

* Interactive programs (those that block on I/O frequently, running only briefly between blocks) to feel responsive: their infrequent CPU bursts should run promptly.
* Batch programs (those that compute for long stretches without blocking) to make steady progress without hogging the CPU at the expense of interactive programs.
* No process to starve indefinitely, even if higher-priority work keeps arriving.

The **Multi-Level Feedback Queue** (MLFQ) is the answer. It was first introduced in the Corbató compatible time-sharing system (CTSS) in 1962 and has appeared, in various forms, in the schedulers of Solaris, FreeBSD, Windows NT through 10, and Linux from the late 1990s through 2007. It learns job behavior by observation and uses that behavior to approximate SJF — without ever being told a job's length.

This chapter builds MLFQ one rule at a time, starting from a naïve base and adding each rule to fix a specific failure mode. The final scheduler has five rules that together produce surprisingly good behavior on real workloads.

### 6.1 The Core Idea: Priorities That Learn

MLFQ maintains a set of **priority queues** — say, 8, from highest to lowest priority. Each queue holds processes at that priority level. When the scheduler needs to pick a process, it picks from the highest-priority non-empty queue.

The central novelty is that _a process's priority is not fixed_. MLFQ watches the process's behavior and adjusts its priority based on what it observes. Specifically:

* A process that runs briefly before blocking on I/O is assumed to be interactive and is kept at high priority.
* A process that runs for a full time slice without blocking is assumed to be CPU-bound and is demoted to a lower priority.

The word "feedback" in the name refers to this continuous recalibration. The scheduler does not need to know what a process is; it watches how the process behaves and treats it accordingly.

#### The first two rules

**Rule 1.** If Priority(A) > Priority(B), A runs. B does not.

**Rule 2.** If Priority(A) == Priority(B), A and B run in Round Robin.

These alone are not MLFQ yet — they just describe how a static priority scheme would work. The remaining rules define how priorities change.

### 6.2 How to Change Priority

A reasonable first attempt:

**Rule 3a (first attempt).** When a process enters the system, it starts at the highest priority.

**Rule 4a (first attempt).** If a process uses up its entire time slice at its current priority, its priority is reduced (it moves one level down).

**Rule 4b (first attempt).** If a process gives up its CPU before the time slice expires (blocks on I/O), its priority stays the same.

These rules capture the basic intuition.

* A new job starts at the top. If it is interactive (which MLFQ hopes), it will keep its high priority by blocking before each slice expires.
* A CPU-bound job, running through its full slice, drops a level each time. After a few slices, it settles at a low priority where it runs round-robin with other CPU-bound jobs but does not interfere with anything more interactive.
* An interactive job stays at top priority because it never consumes a full slice before blocking.

Let us trace an example. A long CPU-bound process A arrives at time 0. A short interactive process B arrives at time 50 ms. Time slice = 20 ms at every priority. Assume 3 priorities (Q2 highest, Q0 lowest).

* t=0: A arrives at Q2. A runs at Q2.
* t=20 ms: A used a full slice at Q2 → demoted to Q1. A now runs at Q1.
* t=40 ms: A used a full slice at Q1 → demoted to Q0.
* t=50 ms: B arrives at Q2. B preempts A because Q2 > Q0.
* t=55 ms (say B runs for 5 ms then blocks for I/O): B blocks. Priority stays Q2. A resumes at Q0.
* t=75 ms: A used a full slice at Q0 (it was already there, stays).
* ... B unblocks eventually, preempts A again, runs 5 ms, blocks. Repeat.

The pattern is healthy: B gets the CPU promptly every time it needs it (because it is at Q2 and A has sunk to Q0), while A gets the CPU whenever B is blocked. The long job does not starve, and the interactive job has essentially dedicated-machine latency.

### 6.3 Problems with the First Attempt

The rules are good but not good enough. Three problems appear.

#### Problem 1: Starvation

If there are enough high-priority processes to keep the Q2 queue always busy, processes at Q0 never run. In our example, if many interactive jobs keep arriving, A at Q0 could wait forever.

**Fix:** Every so often (say, every 1 second), boost all processes to the top priority. This guarantees no process is stuck at the bottom indefinitely; the boost periodically gives every job a chance at the top. We will make this Rule 5.

#### Problem 2: Gaming the Scheduler

Suppose a malicious process calls `yield()` or a cheap I/O operation (like `write` of 1 byte to `/dev/null`) just before its time slice expires. The process blocks or yields at 19 ms of its 20 ms slice. By Rule 4b (as stated), its priority does not drop — it voluntarily gave up the CPU.

Now the gamer runs effectively 19/20 = 95% of the time at high priority, using the voluntary-yield trick to stay at Q2 forever. It defeats the purpose of priority demotion.

**Fix:** Do not check whether the process voluntarily yielded. Check whether it _has used up its total allotted time at this priority_. Track per-process allotment: if the process has run for a total of 20 ms at Q2 across all its slices (whether interrupted by I/O or not), it drops a level.

With this fix, a gaming process that yields at 19 ms still accumulates 19 ms toward its 20 ms quota. Its second run accumulates 1 more ms, reaching 20 ms, and it gets demoted. The gamer cannot defeat the accounting.

#### Problem 3: Parameters

MLFQ has parameters: the number of queues, the time slice at each queue, the priority-boost interval. The right values depend on the workload. Too-short slices cause excessive context switching; too-long slices hurt response time. Too-frequent boosts erase the scheduler's learning; too-rare boosts risk starvation.

Real systems work around this with either (a) hand-tuned defaults chosen by testing, (b) adaptive mechanisms that adjust parameters based on observed behavior, or (c) a hybrid like Solaris's `dispadmin`, which exposes slice lengths per priority as a tunable table.

There is no getting around parameter choice — this is the "voodoo constants" problem. Modern CFS (Chapter 7) avoids some of it by using a different abstraction (virtual runtime) that does not require per-priority slice tables. But CFS has its own parameters (sched_min_granularity, sched_latency). Some tuning is irreducible.

### 6.4 The Refined MLFQ Rules

Putting it all together:

**Rule 1.** If Priority(A) > Priority(B), A runs. B does not.

**Rule 2.** If Priority(A) == Priority(B), A and B run in Round Robin with the time slice of that queue.

**Rule 3.** When a process enters the system, it starts at the highest priority.

**Rule 4.** Once a process has used its total time allotment at a given priority (regardless of how many times it gave up the CPU), it is demoted to the next lower priority.

**Rule 5.** Every S time units (where S is the priority-boost interval), every process is moved back to the highest priority.

These five rules produce what most practitioners call "MLFQ." Real implementations add nuance — Solaris uses different time slices at different priorities (slow at high priority, fast at low), Windows uses priority boosts for I/O completions — but the essential algorithm is captured by these five rules.

### 6.5 Why MLFQ Approximates SJF

Here is the key insight. Suppose a short, CPU-bound job arrives. It runs at Q2 for a full slice (20 ms). Demoted to Q1. Runs for a full slice at Q1 (40 ms total, say — longer slice). Demoted to Q0. If the job completes in under 50 ms total, it never spent long at low priorities; its turnaround is close to its run time. Short jobs finish in roughly their CPU cost plus some overhead.

A long CPU-bound job, by contrast, sinks to Q0 and stays there (until the next boost). Its slices at Q0 are interleaved with other Q0 jobs in Round Robin — so its progress is slow but fair. Its turnaround is longer, roughly proportional to (total CPU demand from all Q0 jobs) × (its share).

In effect: short jobs finish fast because they never sink low; long jobs finish slowly because they do sink. This is SJF-like behavior, emerging from rules that never mention job length. MLFQ observes how long each job has been running and uses that as a proxy for how much longer it will run.

The proxy is imperfect. A job that had been running for 500 ms without blocking might be about to terminate in the next millisecond; MLFQ treats it as CPU-bound. But in realistic workloads, the correlation between past and future run length is high enough that the proxy works well.

#### Common Misconception

> _MLFQ prioritizes I/O-bound processes because they do I/O._

MLFQ does not examine whether a process does I/O. It examines whether the process _consumes_ its time slice. An I/O-bound process tends to block before its slice expires, which happens to keep it at high priority (Rules 3 and 4 together). But the scheduler's decision is based on CPU usage accounting, not on detecting I/O syscalls. The effect — I/O-bound processes getting high priority — is a _consequence_ of how they consume CPU, not a targeted recognition.

This matters because it means MLFQ handles new, unfamiliar patterns correctly. A process doing heavy computation with periodic tiny yields (say, a compute-bound job that occasionally sends status updates) is treated as CPU-bound, because despite the yields, it keeps consuming full allotments. The scheduler does not need special cases.

### 6.6 Tuning the Parameters

**Number of queues.** Too few and the scheduler cannot distinguish priorities finely; too many and most queues are empty most of the time. Common choices: 3 to 8 priority levels for user-facing schedulers; the same systems often offer additional real-time priority levels outside MLFQ.

**Time slices.** Many MLFQ implementations use longer time slices at lower priorities. The intuition: high priority is for interactive jobs that block fast anyway — give them small slices so they get scheduled promptly. Low priority is for CPU-bound jobs — give them large slices so each run is productive and overhead is low. Solaris, for example, uses 20 ms at high priority growing to 200 ms at low priority.

**Priority boost interval.** Short (say, 100 ms) — processes reset to top often; prevents starvation; erases learning. Long (say, 10 seconds) — learning persists; rare starvation. A canonical choice is 1 second, common across Solaris and early Linux.

**Allotment per priority.** How much total CPU at a priority before demotion? Same intuition as time slice: more at lower priorities (since CPU-bound jobs living there should be treated uniformly).

The parameters together define the scheduler's character. Changing them changes behavior in subtle ways that only show up in realistic workloads. Real kernels have careful tuning and sometimes dynamic adjustment; embedded systems pick once and move on.

### 6.7 A Full Trace

To make MLFQ concrete, a 5-second trace.

Setup: 3 queues, Q2 (slice 10 ms, allotment 20 ms), Q1 (slice 20 ms, allotment 40 ms), Q0 (slice 50 ms, allotment unbounded). Priority boost every 1 s.

Arrivals: process A (CPU-bound, needs 300 ms) at t=0. Process B (interactive, wants 2 ms CPU, then 30 ms I/O, cyclically) at t=30 ms.

The table below tracks every scheduling event. Each row is "what just ran"; the rightmost columns show each process's priority queue and allotment used at that priority **after** the event.

Time (ms) | Runs | Event | A: queue / Q-allotment | B: queue / Q-allotment | Notes  
---|---|---|---|---|---  
0–10 | A | Q2 slice (10 ms) | Q2 / 10 | — | A at limit-10 of Q2's 20  
10–20 | A | Q2 slice (10 ms) → allotment hit | Q1 / 0 | — | A demoted to Q1  
20–30 | A | Q1 slice (first 10 of 20 ms) | Q1 / 10 | — | A has used 10 of Q1's 40 allotment  
30 | — | B arrives at Q2; preempts A | Q1 / 10 | Q2 / 0 | Higher-priority arrival  
30–32 | B | B runs 2 ms, blocks for I/O | Q1 / 10 | Q2 / 2 | B blocked until t=62  
32–62 | A | A resumes Q1, runs 30 ms | Q0 / 0 | Q2 / 2 (blocked) | A hits Q1 allotment (10+30=40); demoted to Q0  
62 | — | B unblocks, preempts A | Q0 / 0 | Q2 / 2 | Q2 > Q0  
62–64 | B | B runs 2 ms, blocks for I/O | Q0 / 0 | Q2 / 4 | B blocked until t=94  
64–114 | A | A runs Q0 slice (50 ms) | Q0 / 50 | Q2 / 4 (blocked) | Q0 has unbounded allotment  
  
The pattern repeats: B oscillates between a short burst at Q2 and being blocked for I/O; A accumulates CPU at Q0 with occasional preemptions each time B unblocks.

At t=1000 the periodic priority boost resets every process to Q2. A runs 20 ms at Q2 (allotment exhausted, demoted to Q1), then 40 ms at Q1 (demoted to Q0), then settles back into Q0 — so the boost costs B about 60 ms of responsiveness every second, a small price for starvation freedom.

B never consumes a full allotment at any priority because it blocks after 2 ms every cycle; its priority stays at Q2, and it gets the CPU promptly every time it unblocks. A accumulates a lot of time at Q0 with occasional Q1/Q2 interludes after boosts.

B's response time (from unblock to run): typically under 10 ms, because B is at Q2 and A is at Q0. Excellent for interactive workload.

A's throughput: steady progress, about 90% of the CPU (B consumes about 6% — 2 ms/32 ms cycle). Most of A's time is at Q0 where it runs in long chunks.

This is the scheduler's magic: both workloads are treated well, simultaneously, without the scheduler being told anything about either one.

### 6.8 Relationship to Other Schedulers

MLFQ is a family. Different members vary the queues, the slices, the allotments, the demotion and boost policies. Solaris's scheduler is a tuned MLFQ with many priority levels. Windows NT's is an MLFQ with priority boosts for specific events (foreground window, I/O completion). Pre-2007 Linux used an O(1) scheduler that was MLFQ-like.

The CFS (Completely Fair Scheduler, Chapter 7) replaced Linux's MLFQ-like O(1) scheduler in 2007 with a fundamentally different approach — proportional share based on virtual runtime. The reasons: MLFQ's "voodoo constants" (parameters with no clean semantics) made it hard to reason about, and CFS's vruntime gave a cleaner, more compositional way to express "fairness." But MLFQ remains an important scheduler in textbooks and in many OSes where it has not been replaced, and understanding it is a prerequisite for understanding why CFS is a different approach, not a strictly better version.

### 6.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** State the five rules of the refined MLFQ. For each, say what problem the rule solves.

Hint

Section 6.4.

**Answer:**

* **Rule 1** (higher priority runs): establishes that priorities actually determine scheduling.
* **Rule 2** (same priority → Round Robin): avoids starvation within a priority level.
* **Rule 3** (new process starts at top): gives every new job the benefit of the doubt — treats it as interactive until proven otherwise.
* **Rule 4** (demote after full allotment): recognizes CPU-bound behavior and demotes accordingly, driving the approximation of SJF.
* **Rule 5** (periodic boost to top): prevents starvation of demoted processes and gives changed behavior a chance to be re-measured.

**Q2.** Explain how MLFQ approximates SJF without knowing job lengths.

**Answer:** A short CPU-bound job runs briefly at high priority, demotes, runs briefly at medium priority, demotes, completes. It never spends much time at low priority, so its turnaround is close to its CPU cost. A long CPU-bound job sinks to the lowest queue quickly and accumulates most of its CPU time there, sharing the CPU with other long jobs via Round Robin. Short jobs finish faster than long jobs — the behavioral pattern of SJF — without any length foreknowledge. The scheduler measures past behavior (CPU consumed at each priority) as a proxy for future length.

**Q3.** Why is the per-priority allotment (Rule 4) necessary instead of "demote on each full time slice"?

**Answer:** Without the allotment, a process could game the scheduler by voluntarily yielding (e.g., calling `yield()` or doing a tiny I/O) at 99% of its time slice. Under a "demote on full slice" rule, the yielder never gets demoted because it never uses an entire slice. With an allotment (e.g., 20 ms at Q2), each partial slice still counts against the quota, and after using 20 ms total at Q2 (across any number of slices), the process is demoted. The allotment prevents gaming.

**Q4.** Why do many MLFQ implementations use longer time slices at lower priorities?

**Answer:** Low-priority queues typically hold CPU-bound processes that run their full slice. Giving them longer slices reduces context-switch overhead (fewer switches per unit CPU work) and lets each run be productive before the next switch. High-priority queues typically hold interactive processes that block quickly anyway; giving them shorter slices lets the scheduler check in more often without much waste (since the process will likely block before the short slice ends). The design aligns slice length with expected run length.

**Q5.** What is the "priority boost," and what happens if the boost interval is set to one hour?

**Answer:** The **priority boost** (Rule 5) periodically resets all processes to the top priority queue. It prevents starvation of demoted processes and lets processes whose behavior changed be re-measured. A one-hour interval would be far too long: a demoted CPU-bound job with many interactive jobs arriving would sit at Q0 for an hour before getting any chance to run, even if the interactive jobs left after a few seconds. Also, a formerly CPU-bound job that became interactive would not be recognized as such for up to an hour. Typical intervals are 100 ms to 10 seconds; 1 second is a common default.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Simulate MLFQ with 3 priorities, time slice 10 ms per priority, allotment 10 ms per priority (so each time slice fully uses the allotment at every level), no priority boost, for this arrival schedule:

* t=0: A arrives (CPU-bound, needs 50 ms).
* t=15 ms: B arrives (needs 15 ms CPU, will complete in one run).

Report the order in which each 10-ms slice is served, which process runs, and the final turnaround of each process.

Hint

Track each process's priority and remaining CPU demand. Check priorities whenever a process arrives or a slice ends.

**Answer:**

Priorities: Q2 (top), Q1, Q0 (bottom). Slice 10 ms; allotment 10 ms (so each slice = full allotment → demote).

* t=0-10: A runs at Q2 (Q2 full-allotment → demote to Q1). A has 40 ms left.
* t=10-15: A runs at Q1. A has accumulated 5 ms of 10 at Q1. At t=15, B arrives at Q2, preempting A.
* t=15-25: B runs at Q2 (Q2 full-allotment → demote to Q1). B has 5 ms left.
* t=25-?: A at Q1 has 5 ms remaining in its Q1 allotment. Both A and B are now at Q1. Round robin: the one that was just at Q1 most recently is at the back of the queue. B was just demoted from Q2; let's say it goes to the back. A's turn first. 
    * t=25-30: A runs at Q1 for 5 ms (completing its Q1 allotment → demote to Q0). A has 30 ms left.
    * t=30-35: B runs at Q1 for 5 ms (B's only remaining work — B completes at t=30+5=35!). B turnaround: 35-15 = 20 ms.
* t=35-45: A runs at Q0 (no further demotion). A has 20 ms left.
* t=45-55: A runs at Q0. A has 10 ms left.
* t=55-65: A runs at Q0. A has 0 ms — A completes at t=65. A turnaround: 65-0 = 65 ms.

Slice order: A@Q2, A@Q1(partial), B@Q2, A@Q1(rest), B@Q1(rest), A@Q0, A@Q0, A@Q0.

Turnaround: A = 65 ms, B = 20 ms. Average = 42.5 ms. Under pure FIFO, A would run 0-50, B would run 50-65, giving turnaround A=50, B=50, average 50 — so MLFQ has lower average turnaround here because B is treated as short and prioritized appropriately.

**Q2.** With MLFQ as in Q1 but now with a 100 ms priority boost, and the arrival schedule:

* t=0: A arrives (CPU-bound, wants 200 ms).
* t=50: B arrives (interactive: 2 ms of CPU, then blocks for 30 ms, then 2 ms, etc.).

Describe qualitatively what happens to each process's priority and response time.

**Answer:** A starts at Q2, quickly demotes through Q1 to Q0 (by t=30 ms roughly). B arrives at t=50 at Q2 and starts running immediately (preempting A at Q0). B's 2 ms burst does not exhaust its Q2 allotment, so B stays at Q2. B blocks at t=52, unblocks at t=82. During B's blocked interval, A runs at Q0 and accumulates CPU.

At t=82, B preempts A again — B is at Q2, A at Q0. B runs 2 ms, blocks. Pattern repeats.

At t=100, priority boost: all processes go to Q2. A is now at Q2. A runs its allotment at Q2 (10 ms total including any previous accumulation — wait, boost resets allotment too, typically), then Q1 allotment, then back to Q0. During Q2 run, A does not interfere with B (B is blocked most of the time; when B unblocks, both are at Q2, so RR between them, each gets 10 ms slice — but B blocks after 2 ms, so effectively B still responds quickly).

B's response time: consistently within a few milliseconds of unblocking throughout the trace. A's progress: slow but steady; the boost temporarily lifts A above B for brief moments but the RR scheduling within Q2 still lets B run within one slice.

Qualitatively: B has excellent response (~2 ms typical); A progresses at roughly 80%-90% of the CPU (since B uses 2 ms / 32 ms = 6% of cycles when ready, plus a bit during boosts). The boost prevents A from being permanently stuck at Q0 during busy periods but does not disrupt B's responsiveness.

**Q3.** A gaming process deliberately calls a cheap system call (like `getpid()`) every 9 ms to keep its slice just under the 10 ms threshold. Under the "demote on full slice" version of MLFQ (no allotment), how much CPU does it consume at top priority over 1 second? Under the refined MLFQ (with 10 ms allotment), how much?

**Answer:**

_No allotment:_ Every 9 ms, the process yields via the syscall, so the scheduler does not detect a full slice. The process never demotes — it stays at Q2. Over 1 second, it runs ~90% of CPU at top priority (9 ms out of every 10 ms is run time; the 1 ms of syscall is kernel overhead). The gamer defeats the scheduler.

_With 10 ms allotment:_ The syscall does not reset the per-priority accounting. After the first 10 ms of run time (accumulated across slices or partial slices), the process is demoted to Q1. After another 10 ms at Q1, demoted to Q0. At Q0, it runs interleaved with any other Q0 jobs in Round Robin. The gamer still runs, but at low priority, where it competes with other low-priority jobs. It cannot monopolize high priority by gaming, because the accounting is based on accumulated CPU, not on slice completion.

This exercise is exactly why the per-priority allotment was added in the refined rules.

**Q4.** You observe that in your system, interactive processes have worse response time on Friday afternoons than on Monday mornings, and you attribute this to heavier CPU-bound loads on Friday. Given the MLFQ mechanism, explain whether this is a failure of MLFQ or an expected consequence of the workload.

**Answer:** This is a mixed answer.

MLFQ's design claim is that interactive processes stay at top priority and have good response time _regardless of the CPU-bound load at lower priorities_. If the boost interval is 1 s, the worst-case response for an interactive process is bounded by the time since the last boost plus one slice at the current priority — around 1 s + 10 ms. So moderately higher CPU-bound load should not harm interactive processes much.

But several failure modes can creep in:

  1. **Too many interactive processes at Q2.** If the high-priority queue is itself busy, response time goes up due to RR sharing at Q2. More interactive processes = more competition at Q2 = worse per-process response. This is a workload consequence, not a scheduler failure.
  2. **Parameter choice.** If the boost interval is too long, CPU-bound processes miss boosts, and when an interactive process enters after being idle, it finds that demoted CPU-bound processes have accumulated at Q0 without reset. The scheduler's tuning, not its design, is at fault.
  3. **Cache and memory pressure.** More processes mean more cache pollution and more pagefaults. Response time rises for reasons unrelated to the scheduler.

A careful analysis should separate scheduler vs non-scheduler causes. If response degradation scales with total CPU demand but not with scheduler fairness, it is likely cache/memory, not MLFQ. If the scheduler's boost parameter is off, retuning fixes it.

**Q5.** In MLFQ, a process that has been CPU-bound for hours gets boosted to Q2 and runs for its full allotment there. Does this hurt the response time of interactive processes currently at Q2? How much?

**Answer:** Yes — during the CPU-bound process's Q2 allotment, it shares Q2 round-robin with interactive processes. Response time for any interactive process is roughly one slice × number of processes at Q2. With a 10 ms slice, 5 interactive processes, and 1 newly-boosted CPU-bound process (6 total), worst-case response rises from 50 ms (without the extra) to 60 ms. If the CPU-bound process runs its full 10 ms slice, that slice is a 10 ms delay for any interactive process queued behind it.

The fix is baked into the design: the CPU-bound process demotes as soon as it uses its Q2 allotment (10 ms), returning Q2 to purely interactive processes after one slice. The disruption is a single slice per boost. With a 1-second boost interval, the disruption is 10 ms every 1000 ms — about 1% of CPU time, spread thin enough to be imperceptible.

This is the priority-boost tradeoff: it prevents starvation at a small, predictable cost to interactive response immediately after each boost. Most workloads find this an acceptable exchange.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** MLFQ's approximation of SJF uses "past behavior predicts future behavior." In what kinds of workloads is this approximation terrible, and what does the scheduler do wrong in those cases?

**Discussion:** The approximation fails when a process's behavior _changes_ dramatically. Two patterns to consider:

_A CPU-bound process that becomes interactive._ A scientific simulation runs for an hour of pure computation, then loads a GUI for exploring results, becoming interactive. MLFQ has demoted it to Q0. When the user first interacts with the GUI, response is slow — the process has to wait for Q0's turn. The priority boost eventually (within 1 s) moves the process back to Q2; response improves. But the first second after the transition feels sluggish. This is a real effect visible in long-running-then-interactive applications.

_An interactive process that becomes CPU-bound._ A web browser that has been idle (interactive) suddenly loads a CPU-heavy web app (becomes CPU-bound). MLFQ keeps it at Q2 until it exhausts its Q2 allotment, then Q1, then Q0. During that transition, it briefly interferes with other interactive processes at Q2. The effect is usually small and short-lived.

The lesson: MLFQ's past-behavior heuristic works well for processes whose behavior is stable over seconds-to-minutes scales. It performs worst on processes whose behavior changes on that same scale. CFS (Chapter 7) sidesteps this problem differently — by giving every process proportional share based on vruntime, without treating "interactive" and "CPU-bound" as distinct categories at all.

**Q2.** Some schedulers assign different time slices to different priority levels but keep the allotment equal. Others keep time slices equal and vary allotments. Argue for one design over the other.

**Discussion:** _Varying slice, fixed allotment._ Higher priority gets shorter slices; lower priority gets longer slices. Consequence: at high priority, quick preemption and fine-grained RR among interactive jobs; at low priority, larger chunks reduce context-switch overhead for CPU-bound jobs. Demotion happens after the same amount of CPU at each level, so all processes experience similar "testing" for priority.

_Fixed slice, varying allotment._ Every priority has the same slice (say 10 ms), but higher priorities demote faster (small allotment — 10 ms) and lower priorities stay longer (larger allotments — 100 ms). Consequence: interactive jobs quickly demote if they misbehave; CPU-bound jobs at Q0 run longer without interruption before being reconsidered.

The first design (Solaris-style) is more common in practice, probably because slice length is the more immediate knob — slice length directly controls context-switch overhead and response time. Allotment is a secondary, subtler knob. By varying slice, you get the direct knob; by varying allotment only, you get a subtler effect that is harder to reason about and tune.

Either works; neither is wrong. The choice reflects what the designer finds more natural to think about. The deeper point, as always with MLFQ, is the "voodoo constants" problem: any set of parameters produces some workload where behavior is surprising. A scheduler designer has to decide which parameters are _control surfaces for admins_ vs _internal details_ and document accordingly.

### Chapter Summary

The Multi-Level Feedback Queue learns job behavior over time and adjusts process priorities to approximate SJF without requiring foreknowledge of run lengths. Five rules — priority-based scheduling within queues, RR within a priority, top-priority start, demotion after full allotment, periodic priority boost — together deliver good response time for interactive workloads and fair progress for CPU-bound workloads. The per-priority allotment prevents gaming via voluntary yields; the periodic boost prevents starvation of demoted processes.

MLFQ's weaknesses are inherent to its learning approach: parameters must be tuned, and processes whose behavior changes abruptly may be misclassified for a brief interval. Its strength is that, across a wide variety of realistic workloads, it produces reasonable behavior without detailed per-workload configuration.

Chapter 7 introduces the Linux **Completely Fair Scheduler** (CFS), which takes a fundamentally different approach: rather than learning "interactive vs CPU-bound," it gives each process a precisely-proportional share of CPU time using a single metric, virtual runtime, implemented with a red-black tree. CFS's cleaner formulation is why Linux replaced its MLFQ-descendant scheduler with it in 2007. Understanding MLFQ first — its successes and its tuning headaches — is the best way to appreciate why CFS took a different path.

---

## Chapter 7: Proportional Share and the Linux CFS

MLFQ (Chapter 6) optimizes for turnaround-like behavior by approximating SJF, treating "short jobs" and "interactive jobs" as aligned. A different school of thought dispenses with the classification entirely and asks: what if we just give each process a fair, proportional share of the CPU?

**Proportional-share scheduling** is a family of algorithms where the scheduler's goal is to guarantee that over a time window, each process receives a share of CPU proportional to its assigned weight. A process with weight 2 gets twice the CPU of a process with weight 1, in the same wall-clock interval.

This chapter introduces the idea with two simple proportional-share schemes — **lottery scheduling** and **stride scheduling** — then builds up to the Linux **Completely Fair Scheduler (CFS)** , which has been Linux's default scheduler since 2007 and runs on a majority of the world's servers today. CFS's central idea is the **virtual runtime (vruntime)** : a per-process counter that accumulates CPU usage, weighted by process priority, and is used to pick the next process to run. The mechanism is simple; the semantics are clean; the implementation is a red-black tree.

### 7.1 Why Proportional Share?

MLFQ's categorical thinking — interactive vs CPU-bound — is a heuristic. It works for most workloads, but it has quirks:

* Parameters (queue count, slice lengths, allotments, boost interval) are not motivated by a single principle. They are tuned.
* A process that changes behavior is misclassified for a while after the transition.
* "Priority" in MLFQ is binary-like: you are either at Q2 or not, and priority nudges (nice values in Unix terms) influence the scheduler in ad-hoc ways.

Proportional-share thinking starts from a different premise. Say "process X should receive 30% of the CPU" and mean it literally: over a 100 ms window, X should run 30 ms. No classifications. No feedback queues. The scheduler's job is to deliver the promised share, period.

If every process has equal weight, every process gets equal share. If you tell the scheduler "this process is more important" (via a priority / nice value / cgroup weight), that process gets proportionally more. The scheduling rule is one line: _pick the process whose measured usage is furthest behind its target_.

This single principle elegantly handles every case MLFQ handles with rules:

* Interactive processes that block often consume less than their target share while blocked, so when they unblock they are "behind" and run immediately (good response time).
* CPU-bound processes that run continuously use their share steadily and are never dramatically behind — they do not preempt other processes except at scheduling boundaries.
* Priority is naturally expressed as weight: weight 2 = twice the share = twice the CPU.

The question is how to make proportional share efficient. Naïve implementations can be slow or unfair. Three increasingly sophisticated algorithms follow.

### 7.2 Lottery Scheduling

The simplest proportional-share scheme. Each process holds some number of **tickets** corresponding to its weight. A process with 100 tickets has more claim to the CPU than one with 50.

At each scheduling decision, the scheduler picks a random ticket. The process holding that ticket runs. Expected share: proportional to tickets held.

**Example.** Process A has 75 tickets, B has 25. Total 100 tickets. In expectation, A runs 75% of scheduling slots, B runs 25%. Over many slots, the proportions converge to these values.

Lottery scheduling's virtues:

* **Simple** : the entire scheduling decision is one random number and one comparison (plus a ticket-search structure).
* **Compositional** : adding processes or changing weights does not require reorganizing queues.
* **Probabilistically fair** : over large enough time windows, shares match weights.

Its vices:

* **Bursty fairness** : in any given 10-slot window, the random choice could favor A (say 9 of 10 slots) or B; fairness is only good in the long run.
* **No good story for a process that just arrived** : random choice does not care about whether a process has been idle or active.

Stride scheduling addresses these.

### 7.3 Stride Scheduling

Deterministic variant of lottery scheduling. Each process has a **stride** inversely proportional to its weight: a heavy process has a short stride, a light process has a long stride. A process has a **pass** value, initially 0. The scheduler picks the process with the minimum pass value, runs it, and then increments its pass by its stride. The process with the next-lowest pass runs next, and so on.

**Example.** A has weight 100 (stride = 1000/100 = 10, say), B has weight 50 (stride = 20). Both start with pass = 0.

* Pick lowest pass: tie. Run A (arbitrary tie-break). A's pass becomes 10.
* Pick lowest: B (pass 0). Run B. B's pass becomes 20.
* Pick lowest: A (pass 10). Run A. A's pass becomes 20.
* Pick lowest: tie. Run one (say A). A's pass becomes 30.
* Pick lowest: B (pass 20). Run B. B's pass becomes 40.
* Pick lowest: A (pass 30). Run A. A's pass becomes 40.
* Pick lowest: tie. Run A. A's pass becomes 50.
* ...

The pattern: over any longer interval, A runs twice as often as B, matching the weight ratio. Deterministic, precise, and always within a bounded margin of the exact ratio.

Stride scheduling's niceness is deterministic fairness. Its weakness is that an arriving process with pass = 0 can dominate the next several scheduling slots simply because it is furthest behind. To handle arrivals, new processes are initialized to the minimum current pass (so they are "just caught up"). This small adjustment is one of several details that CFS handles more cleanly.

### 7.4 The Completely Fair Scheduler (CFS)

Linux's CFS, introduced in 2.6.23 (2007) by Ingo Molnár, took the proportional-share idea and made it the default Linux scheduler. It replaced the O(1) scheduler (an MLFQ descendant) for three reasons:

  1. **Cleaner semantics.** Proportional share is one rule, not five. No voodoo constants for queue counts and boost intervals.
  2. **Better fairness.** Under the O(1) scheduler, certain workload mixes could produce unfair results; CFS gives every process its proportional share by construction.
  3. **Competitive performance.** Despite its simplicity, CFS benchmarks competitively with the O(1) scheduler on most workloads.

#### The vruntime

CFS gives each process a **virtual runtime (vruntime)** : a monotonically increasing counter tracking CPU time used, weighted by process priority.

vruntime increase=actual runtime×default weightprocess weight\text{vruntime increase} = \text{actual runtime} \times \frac{\text{default weight}}{\text{process weight}}vruntime increase=actual runtime×process weightdefault weight​

Units: both `actual runtime` and the `vruntime increase` are measured in the same time unit (nanoseconds in the Linux implementation); the weight ratio is dimensionless. So vruntime itself is a nanosecond counter, scaled by priority.

For a process with default weight, vruntime increases at the same rate as real time (one ns of vruntime per ns of CPU used). For a higher-weight process, vruntime increases more slowly per real-time unit. For a lower-weight process, vruntime increases faster.

The scheduling rule: **pick the process with the smallest vruntime**.

Why does this work? Imagine three equal-weight processes. They all accumulate vruntime at the same rate when running. At any instant, the one with the smallest vruntime is the one that has run _least_ — so giving it the CPU next is exactly the fairness rule. Over time, all three accumulate vruntime at the same long-run rate, and each gets 1/3 of the CPU.

With unequal weights: a process with twice the weight accumulates vruntime at half the rate. When it shares the CPU with a single-weight process, it is picked (has lower vruntime) more often. Specifically, twice as often, delivering twice the share.

#### The red-black tree

CFS keeps all ready processes in a self-balancing binary tree (red-black tree) ordered by vruntime. The process with the smallest vruntime is always the leftmost node. `pick_next_task()` is O(log n) for selection (finding the leftmost) and O(log n) for reinsertion (after the process runs and its vruntime changes).

This is excellent scaling: even with 1000 ready processes, selection is about 10 tree traversals. CFS handles loads of tens of thousands of ready threads without degrading.

#### Time slicing in CFS

CFS does not have a fixed time slice per priority. Instead, it has a **scheduling latency** target — say, 48 ms — within which every ready process should get a turn. The per-process slice is:

slice=sched_latency×process weighttotal weight of runqueue\text{slice} = \text{sched\\_latency} \times \frac{\text{process weight}}{\text{total weight of runqueue}}slice=sched_latency×total weight of runqueueprocess weight​

With 6 equal-weight processes and sched_latency of 48 ms: each gets 8 ms. With 24 equal-weight processes: each would get 2 ms — approaching the context-switch overhead.

To avoid this, CFS has a minimum granularity: `sched_min_granularity` (6 ms typically). No process runs for less than this, even if that means the total window expands beyond sched_latency. With 24 equal-weight processes and 6 ms minimum, each slot is 6 ms and total round is 144 ms (longer than the nominal latency, but each preemption gains enough work to amortize).

Between these two knobs (sched_latency and sched_min_granularity), CFS adapts to load: lightly loaded, each process runs a relatively long slice; heavily loaded, slice length floors at min_granularity and the total round expands. Modern Linux tunes these parameters based on CPU count and other factors.

#### Priorities via nice values

Unix priorities are expressed via **nice values** : integers from -20 (highest priority) to +19 (lowest), with 0 being default. Positive values mean "be nice to others" (lower priority); negative means "be important" (higher).

CFS maps nice values to weights via a fixed 40-entry lookup table (`sched_prio_to_weight[]` in the kernel), not a live calculation. The table is calibrated so that each step in nice corresponds to roughly a 1.25× multiplicative change in weight — the actual entries are hand-tuned integers close to (but not exactly equal to) 1024×1.25−nice1024 \times 1.25^{-\text{nice}}1024×1.25−nice. Representative values:

nice | weight  
---|---  
−20 | 88761  
−10 | 9548  
−5 | 3121  
0 | 1024  
+5 | 335  
+10 | 110  
+19 | 15  
  
The factor (1.25 per step) is chosen so a 10-step difference in nice yields roughly 1.2510≈9.3×1.25^{10} \approx 9.3\times1.2510≈9.3× the CPU share — a useful granularity for operators who want noticeable, not tiny, priority differences. Because the table is discrete, the "1.25×" multiplier is always an approximation; the authoritative source is `sched_prio_to_weight[]`.

The weight enters vruntime calculation directly: a process with higher weight accumulates vruntime slower, so it stays at the front of the red-black tree longer, so it runs more.

#### Fair handling of sleep

When a process blocks (on I/O, a lock, sleep) and later unblocks, naïvely it would return with the vruntime it had when blocking. If it blocked for 10 seconds while other processes ran, it has very low vruntime — lower than everyone else's. Giving it the CPU for as long as it takes to "catch up" would let the just-woken process dominate the CPU, bad for fairness (it already enjoyed its time by not running) and terrible for other processes.

CFS handles this by **capping the vruntime of a newly-wakened process to the current minimum vruntime in the runqueue**. The just-woken process is treated as "just caught up" — it is placed at the front (same vruntime as the current minimum), so it runs next but doesn't dominate. This is equivalent to the stride-scheduling trick of initializing new arrivals to the current minimum pass.

#### Common Misconception

> _CFS runs the process that has run the least, so interactive processes block for a long time and accumulate unfairness in their favor._

False, because of the sleep-handling adjustment above. A process that has been sleeping does not accumulate a "credit" of missed CPU time. When it wakes up, its vruntime is snapped forward to the runqueue's current minimum. It gets to run next (small positive gap from its sleep), but it does not receive any bonus for the time it slept. CFS's fairness is about **share of CPU when ready** , not about share of total time. A process that sleeps is making no claim during that interval.

### 7.5 CFS in Practice

How does CFS compare to MLFQ in the two scenarios Chapter 6 highlighted?

**Scenario: CPU-bound job + interactive job.** CPU-bound process A and interactive process B (blocks often). Under CFS, A and B have equal weight (assume). B is runnable only during its 2 ms bursts. When B becomes ready, its vruntime has been snapped to the minimum (A's vruntime so far). B is picked (tied but preferred by convention). B runs 2 ms, blocks. A resumes. Each time B becomes ready, it preempts A almost immediately. Response time: a few microseconds (one context switch).

Progress: A runs whenever B is blocked (the vast majority of the time). B uses whatever tiny share it wants. Both workloads treated well.

No priority queues, no boost interval, no demotion, no allotments. The single rule — "pick the process with the smallest vruntime" — yields the same behavior MLFQ achieves via five rules.

**Scenario: priority.** User wants process X to run with higher priority. Assign X nice -10 (or a high cgroup weight). X's vruntime accumulates slower per unit CPU. X is picked more often than processes with default priority. The share ratio is approximately 1.2510≈9.31.25^{10} \approx 9.31.2510≈9.3 — X gets ~9× the CPU of a default process. Precise and controllable.

Compare MLFQ: nice -10 would bump X into a higher priority queue, where it gets scheduling priority over lower queues. The effect is similar but harder to quantify — what fraction of CPU does a Q3 process get versus a Q2 process? Depends on the arrival pattern.

### 7.6 Edge Cases and Refinements

CFS has real-world complications that the core algorithm glosses over.

**Group scheduling.** A system with 100 processes from one user and 1 process from another is unfair if treated flatly — the lone process gets 1/101 of CPU, the 100 share 100/101. Fairness might demand 50/50 between users. Group scheduling nests runqueues: users (or cgroups) have weighted shares, and processes within a group compete for the group's share. CFS's implementation, `sched_fair.c`, handles multi-level group nesting.

**Load balancing across CPUs.** On multi-core machines, each core has its own runqueue. CFS periodically rebalances: if one core's runqueue is heavier (more weighted work) than another's, processes migrate. We will discuss this in Chapter 8.

**Real-time classes.** Hard real-time workloads need guarantees CFS cannot provide (because CFS always gives _some_ share to every runnable process, which can delay a real-time process). Linux's scheduler framework has separate classes (`SCHED_FIFO`, `SCHED_RR`, `SCHED_DEADLINE`) for these; CFS is used only for `SCHED_NORMAL`.

**Latency tuning.** Laptop and desktop users want low latency — interactive programs should respond fast. CFS exposes `sched_latency` as a knob; many distributions tune it by CPU count. There is no one-size-fits-all.

### 7.7 Summary: Why CFS Is the Default

The progression from Chapter 5 to Chapter 7 is about increasing sophistication in scheduling.

* FIFO/SJF/RR (Chapter 5): single-rule baselines that each optimize for one metric.
* MLFQ (Chapter 6): five rules that learn job behavior to approximate SJF while maintaining response time.
* CFS (Chapter 7): one rule (pick the process with the smallest vruntime) that delivers strict proportional share, with weight acting as priority.

Each step replaces ad-hoc rules with cleaner abstractions. CFS's vruntime is the cleanest — it is a single per-process scalar with well-defined semantics, implemented via a data structure (red-black tree) that scales to many processes. For multi-user, multi-process workloads on general-purpose servers and desktops, CFS is the natural choice.

CFS is not strictly better than MLFQ in every way — MLFQ's targeting of interactive processes can deliver slightly better interactive experience on a pure mixed workload, and MLFQ implementations are simpler to understand. But CFS's fairness guarantees and tuning simplicity have won in practice.

Chapter 8 closes the scheduling arc by looking at **multiprocessor** scheduling: when the machine has many CPU cores, how does CFS (or any scheduler) keep them all busy without dropping cache locality or spending too much time on cross-core synchronization?

### 7.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Define vruntime. How does it differ from actual CPU runtime, and why is the difference important?

Hint

Section 7.4.

**Answer:** **Vruntime** is a per-process counter that increases as the process runs, scaled by the inverse of the process's weight: Δvruntime=Δruntime×wdefaultwprocess\Delta \text{vruntime} = \Delta \text{runtime} \times \frac{w_\text{default}}{w_\text{process}}Δvruntime=Δruntime×wprocess​wdefault​​. For default-weight processes, vruntime increases at the same rate as runtime. For higher-weight processes, vruntime increases slower per second of runtime. For lower-weight processes, faster. The difference matters because CFS schedules the process with the smallest vruntime — a higher-weight process accumulates vruntime slowly, so it "stays at the front" longer and is picked more often. The weighting is how proportional share emerges from a single rule.

**Q2.** Lottery scheduling gives each process tickets proportional to its share. Why might this be problematic for bursty workloads?

**Answer:** Lottery scheduling's fairness is probabilistic — short-term outcomes can deviate significantly from the expected share. In a 10-slot window, a 50/50 ticket split between A and B might produce 8 slots for A and 2 for B (a plausible random outcome). For a batch workload over minutes, the averaging washes this out. For a bursty workload where a user is running a short interactive program, the same randomness can show up as noticeable lag: the interactive process might not be picked for several scheduling cycles just due to bad luck. Stride scheduling and CFS are deterministic and give tighter fairness on short time scales.

**Q3.** Why does CFS use a red-black tree rather than a simple sorted list or a heap?

**Answer:** A sorted list supports O(1) pop-min but O(n) insertion — every time a process's vruntime updates, it must be re-inserted at the correct position. A binary heap supports O(log n) pop-min and O(log n) insertion but does not support _removing_ a specific (non-min) process efficiently, which CFS needs when a process blocks or has its priority changed. A red-black tree supports O(log n) for all three operations — insert, remove, find-min — because (a) it is balanced, (b) it is sorted (find-min is the leftmost node), and (c) it supports efficient lookup of any node. CFS requires all three operations at scale, making the red-black tree the appropriate choice.

**Q4.** A process sleeps for 10 seconds. When it wakes up, what happens to its vruntime under CFS, and why?

**Answer:** Its vruntime is **snapped forward to the current minimum vruntime in the runqueue** (sometimes with a small catch-up buffer subtracted for responsiveness). This prevents the just-woken process from having a vruntime far below everyone else's, which would otherwise entitle it to dominate the CPU for a long time "catching up." Fairness in CFS is about share while ready, not about total wall time — a sleeping process is not making claims on CPU during its sleep, so no credit is accumulated. On wakeup, the process is treated as having been "fresh" for purposes of scheduling.

**Q5.** Explain in one sentence how nice values map to CPU share in CFS.

**Answer:** Each step of nice value corresponds to a ~1.25× multiplicative change in weight — so a process at nice -10 has weight roughly 1.2510≈9.31.25^{10} \approx 9.31.2510≈9.3× that of a default-nice process, and gets ~9× the CPU share when they share a runqueue.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** Two processes, A and B, have weights 1024 and 512, respectively. Using CFS's vruntime accounting rule, what is the ratio of CPU time each receives over a 60-second interval in which both are continuously ready? Verify your answer matches the weight ratio.

Hint

Remember that vruntime accumulates at rate wdefaultwprocess\frac{w_\text{default}}{w_\text{process}}wprocess​wdefault​​ per second of runtime.

**Answer:** Take weight 1024 as the default. A's vruntime rate = 1024/1024 = 1 per second of runtime. B's vruntime rate = 1024/512 = 2 per second of runtime.
