Since CFS always picks the smaller vruntime, A and B's vruntimes stay close; in equilibrium, each second of A's runtime corresponds to 0.5 seconds of B's runtime (because B's vruntime catches up twice as fast).

Over 60 seconds of wall clock: let A run for x seconds, B for 60-x. At equilibrium, A's vruntime (= x × 1) ≈ B's vruntime (= (60-x) × 2). Solving x = 120 - 2x → 3x = 120 → x = 40. So A runs 40 s, B runs 20 s. Ratio 2:1, matching weight ratio 1024:512 = 2:1. ✓

**Q2.** A system has four equal-weight processes. `sched_latency` = 40 ms, `sched_min_granularity` = 5 ms. What is the per-process time slice? Now increase the number of processes to 20 — what is the per-process slice, and what is the total round length?

**Answer:**

_4 processes:_ Per-process slice = sched_latency / n = 40 / 4 = 10 ms. Round length = 40 ms (equals sched_latency). Each process is scheduled once every 40 ms.

_20 processes:_ Naïve slice would be 40 / 20 = 2 ms, below min_granularity. CFS enforces min_granularity: slice = 5 ms. Round length = 20 × 5 = 100 ms.

The round is longer than the latency target because CFS prioritizes keeping slices meaningful over strict latency adherence. With 20 processes, 5 ms slices are the floor — shorter would spend too much on context switches.

**Q3.** In stride scheduling, processes A (weight 100) and B (weight 50) start with pass = 0. Stride = 1000/weight. Trace the first 10 scheduling decisions. How close does the schedule come to the weight-proportional ratio?

**Answer:** A stride = 1000/100 = 10. B stride = 1000/50 = 20.

Step | A pass | B pass | Pick | After  
---|---|---|---|---  
1 | 0 | 0 | A | A=10, B=0  
2 | 10 | 0 | B | A=10, B=20  
3 | 10 | 20 | A | A=20, B=20  
4 | 20 | 20 | A | A=30, B=20  
5 | 30 | 20 | B | A=30, B=40  
6 | 30 | 40 | A | A=40, B=40  
7 | 40 | 40 | A | A=50, B=40  
8 | 50 | 40 | B | A=50, B=60  
9 | 50 | 60 | A | A=60, B=60  
10 | 60 | 60 | A | A=70, B=60  
  
A ran 7 times, B ran 3. Ratio 7:3 ≈ 2.33:1. Target ratio 100:50 = 2:1. Close but not exact in 10 steps; over longer runs it converges. The tiebreak rule (A vs B on ties) biases slightly toward A here.

**Q4.** CFS promises that a newly-wakened process does not receive a "credit" for its sleep time. Why is this design correct? What would go wrong if the scheduler _did_ give sleep-time credits?

**Answer:** The design is correct because fairness in CFS is about share-when-ready, not total wall time. A sleeping process is not competing for the CPU; giving it a credit would mean that when it wakes up, it dominates the CPU at the expense of processes that have been diligently ready. Concrete failure if sleep credits were given: a process that sleeps for a week and wakes up would preempt everyone else for a week straight, starving them completely. Fairness would collapse on any workload that includes sleeping processes.

The implementation (snapping vruntime to the minimum on wakeup) is equivalent to saying "you are as deserving as the least-deserving ready process," which is fair in the shared sense. A small discount (recent Linux uses `sched_wakeup_granularity` to give the wakened process a slight push to preempt faster) makes response time better without opening the starvation door.

**Q5.** Argue whether a CPU-bound process running alongside a bursty interactive process is treated better under CFS or under MLFQ.

**Answer:** Both schedulers handle this workload well, but via different mechanisms.

_MLFQ:_ The CPU-bound process demotes to Q0 within a few slices. The interactive process stays at Q2 because it blocks before exhausting its allotment. Interactive response is prompt (preempts Q0); CPU-bound progresses whenever interactive is blocked.

_CFS:_ Both have equal weight. The interactive process accumulates vruntime slowly (because it runs little). When ready, its vruntime is at the minimum (after sleep-snap), so it preempts the CPU-bound process. Interactive response: one context switch. CPU-bound progresses whenever interactive is blocked.

The outcomes are similar. Subjective differences: MLFQ's boost can briefly elevate the CPU-bound process to Q2, causing a 10-ms disruption in interactive response once per second. CFS has no analogous disruption — but under very heavy loads with many processes, CFS's proportional share gives the interactive process exactly its fair slice (maybe 2% of CPU), which could be worse than MLFQ's unconditional priority at Q2 (close to 100% of CPU when ready).

Verdict: CFS is more fair; MLFQ is more aggressive for interactive. For most desktop workloads, CFS is indistinguishable or better. For workloads with a known interactive vs batch split, a tuned MLFQ can marginally win. Real systems often offer both — CFS as default, real-time classes for known-interactive or known-batch workloads.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** CFS treats every process uniformly: no distinct category for "interactive." Nevertheless, interactive processes get good response time. What does this tell us about how much intelligence a scheduler needs?

**Discussion:** A surprising amount of scheduler "intelligence" is not about classification but about accounting. CFS does not _know_ any process is interactive. It tracks CPU usage and gives each process its proportional share. Interactive processes appear responsive as an emergent property: they use little CPU, so their vruntime stays low, so they are picked quickly when ready. No categorization was needed.

The lesson generalizes: a good primitive (proportional share + accurate accounting) often replaces complex heuristic logic. MLFQ's five rules were necessary in part because MLFQ's abstraction (priority queues with rules-based promotion/demotion) was too coarse to express what it wanted. Vruntime is finer; one rule captures what five rules approximated.

A related observation: schedulers in modern research (e.g., Linux's BPF-based scheduler framework, Google's Ghost) push more scheduling logic into user space, with the kernel providing primitives. If the primitives are right, user-space policy can be simple. CFS is one instantiation of this principle; other schedulers for specialized workloads can layer on top.

The counter-point: some workloads need explicit category knowledge. Real-time deadlines, for example, cannot be inferred from CPU-use patterns — they have to be declared. That's why Linux has a separate `SCHED_DEADLINE` class outside CFS. Categorization remains useful when the category expresses a constraint the scheduler could not learn on its own.

**Q2.** Suppose you are designing a scheduler for a high-performance web server that handles 100,000 connections per second. Would CFS be your first choice? What aspects would you keep, what would you change?

**Discussion:** CFS would be a reasonable _starting point_ but probably not final. Keep the proportional-share idea and the red-black tree — these scale well and are well-understood. Change or bypass:

* **Thread creation cost.** For 100,000 connections per second as 100,000 separate threads, CFS's per-process tracking becomes expensive (millions of processes live). Event-loop designs (epoll + coroutines or async runtimes) multiplex many connections onto one OS thread, drastically reducing the per-thread count.
* **CPU affinity and NUMA awareness.** High-performance servers pin threads to specific cores and memory nodes. CFS has load balancing, but it can make decisions that hurt cache locality. Explicit pinning (set_affinity) or tools like `isolcpus` take threads out of CFS's domain entirely for critical work.
* **Kernel bypass.** The scheduler is only part of the story. For 100 Gbps networks, kernel-space scheduling at all becomes overhead. DPDK, io_uring, and kernel-bypass stacks move packet processing into user space with dedicated polling threads — no scheduling at all.

So the answer is: CFS is fine for the machine's general workload, but the server's hot path probably runs outside CFS's scheduling, using pinning and polling. A thoughtful designer knows which threads are "CFS threads" (best-effort, fair) and which are "specialized threads" (pinned, polling, never preempted).

### Chapter Summary

Proportional-share scheduling is a different philosophy from MLFQ's categorical learning. Instead of classifying processes and applying priority-queue rules, it gives each process a share of CPU proportional to its weight. Lottery scheduling uses randomness to achieve proportional share; stride scheduling uses determinism; CFS uses vruntime — a single per-process counter that captures both CPU consumption and weight in one number.

CFS's rule is simple: pick the process with the smallest vruntime. The red-black tree makes the selection O(log n), scaling to large runqueues. Nice values map to weights multiplicatively. Sleeping processes are snapped to the current minimum on wakeup to prevent sleep credits. The whole scheduler fits in a few hundred lines of well-structured kernel code, and it has been Linux's default since 2007.

CFS's cleaner semantics and fairness guarantees have won in the desktop/server space. MLFQ remains intellectually important (Chapter 6) and is still used in Solaris, FreeBSD, and Windows NT derivatives. The deeper lesson is that a good primitive with accurate accounting can replace complex heuristic logic — a theme that recurs throughout this book whenever we meet new layers of system design.

Chapter 8 closes the scheduling arc by addressing multi-core machines: many CPUs, many runqueues, and the new problems that arise when work must balance across them.

---

## Chapter 8: Multiprocessor Scheduling

Every scheduler we have seen — FIFO, SJF, Round Robin, MLFQ, CFS — has been described as though the machine had a single CPU. That has not been true of production hardware for two decades. Modern servers have 16, 32, 64, even 128 cores. Laptops have 8 or more. Even phones have 8. Each core is a separate execution unit that can run a process independently. Scheduling a single-CPU machine is about _when_ to run each process; scheduling a multiprocessor is about _when_ **and** _where_.

This chapter addresses the new problems that arise when many cores must cooperate. A naïve solution — one giant shared runqueue, with every CPU taking the next process from the top — runs headfirst into contention: when every core needs the runqueue lock to schedule, the lock becomes a bottleneck and adding more cores stops helping. Real schedulers use **per-CPU runqueues** and carefully balance work across them, trading some theoretical fairness for dramatic gains in scalability.

Along the way, we introduce a concept we will meet repeatedly in later chapters: **cache affinity**. A process that has recently run on CPU 3 has its working set warm in CPU 3's caches. Migrating it to CPU 7 throws away that warmth; CPU 7 starts with cold caches and pays for every memory access until caches refill. Good multiprocessor schedulers respect affinity even when it slightly hurts instantaneous fairness — because the cache cost of ignoring affinity is often larger than the unfairness saved.

This is the last chapter of Threading Plan A's CPU-sharing arc. Chapter 9 begins the memory arc.

### 8.1 Cache Affinity: The Hidden Cost of Migration

Before we discuss scheduler design, consider what happens to a process's performance when it is moved from one CPU to another.

Every modern CPU core has private L1 and L2 caches (tens of KB to a few MB), sized to hold the working set of a running process. As the process runs, its code, stack, and hot data fill the cache. On a typical 2020s-era server CPU (Intel Ice Lake, AMD Zen 3), L1 hits take ~4 cycles, L2 hits ~12 cycles, L3 hits ~40–80 cycles, and main memory ~200–400 cycles. The difference between a process hitting its L1 and missing all the way to DRAM is roughly 100× in per-access latency. (Specific numbers depend on microarchitecture; ARM server parts and older Intel parts are in the same order of magnitude.)

A process running on CPU 3 for the last millisecond has, by now, a significant fraction of its working set resident in CPU 3's caches. Reschedule that process to CPU 7. CPU 7's caches contain a _different_ process's data. Every memory access the migrated process does — code fetch, stack access, data lookup — misses. Over the next few milliseconds, the process's misses refill CPU 7's caches (at hundreds of cycles per miss, trending toward 200–400 for DRAM), slowly returning to normal performance.

The total cost of migration can be millions of cycles, or tens of milliseconds of reduced throughput. This cost is invisible to any scheduler that thinks only about CPU time shares. It manifests as real application slowdown.

#### Definition: Cache Affinity

**Cache affinity** is the principle that a process performs best on the CPU where its working set is already cached. Moving a process to a different CPU incurs a performance penalty proportional to how much of its data was warm on the old CPU.

In plain language, this means: CPUs have memory of their own (caches), and processes benefit from returning to the CPU they ran on last. A scheduler that migrates processes freely, ignoring affinity, will have apparently-fair CPU accounting but measurably worse throughput.

Because of affinity, good multiprocessor schedulers have a rule of thumb: _prefer to leave a process on its last CPU_. Migrate only when the imbalance between CPUs is large enough to outweigh affinity cost. How to quantify that threshold is part of the scheduler's design.

### 8.2 Single-Queue Multiprocessor Scheduling

The simplest design extends single-CPU scheduling: one shared runqueue, every CPU consumes from it. Call this **single-queue multiprocessor scheduling (SQMS)**.

When a CPU becomes idle (its current process blocks or time-slice-expires), it acquires the runqueue lock, picks the next process, updates the queue, releases the lock, and runs the process. Every CPU sees the exact same runqueue ordering; scheduling decisions are globally consistent.

Two problems make this design impractical at scale.

#### Problem 1: Lock contention

Every scheduling decision requires the runqueue lock. With 2 CPUs, the lock is held briefly, released quickly. With 64 CPUs all scheduling frequently (timer interrupts every 1 ms, plus additional events), the lock is contended: CPUs spin waiting for it, wasting cycles. The fraction of time CPUs spend waiting on the runqueue lock grows with the CPU count, and eventually scheduling overhead dominates.

This is a classic scalability wall: doubling the CPU count does less than double the throughput because a shared resource (the runqueue lock) has serialized access. The wall is not far off — on busy 32-core machines, SQMS lock contention can consume 5–15% of CPU in spin time alone. At 64 cores, it gets worse.

#### Problem 2: No natural affinity

Under SQMS, a process's "home CPU" is effectively random: whichever CPU happened to be idle when the process was scheduled. The next time the process is chosen, a different CPU might pick it. Over even a few scheduling rounds, a process bounces among many CPUs, and its working set is cold on every one of them.

Some SQMS implementations add affinity hints: associate each process with a "preferred CPU," and only let that CPU pick it (unless that CPU is fully idle for too long). This imposes more complex bookkeeping and blurs the simplicity of the single-queue design.

### 8.3 Multi-Queue Multiprocessor Scheduling

The modern answer: **multi-queue multiprocessor scheduling (MQMS)**. Each CPU has its own runqueue. Scheduling decisions on each CPU happen independently; no inter-CPU locks are taken for the common case.

A new process, when created, is placed on one specific CPU's queue. That CPU schedules it. When it blocks, it remains associated with that CPU; when it unblocks, it is placed back on the same CPU's queue (assuming it is still a reasonable choice — more on this below).

Advantages:

* **No global lock.** Each CPU's runqueue lock is held briefly and only by that CPU (plus occasional cross-CPU operations we will discuss). Lock contention per CPU is independent of the CPU count.
* **Natural affinity.** A process stays on its home CPU by default. Its caches stay warm.
* **Scalable.** Doubling the CPU count doubles the runqueue count; scheduling overhead stays roughly constant per CPU.

Disadvantages:

* **Load imbalance.** If CPU 0's queue has 10 processes and CPU 7's queue has 1, CPU 7 is underused while CPU 0's processes wait. Something must balance.
* **Unfair proportional share.** If the scheduler is CFS-like (Chapter 7), each CPU gives its processes proportional share _locally_. A process alone on CPU 7 gets 100% of CPU 7; a process on CPU 0 sharing with 9 others gets 10% of CPU 0. The weights say they should be equal, but the per-CPU accounting does not know that.

Both disadvantages are addressed by **load balancing**.

### 8.4 Load Balancing

Every so often, the scheduler examines all runqueues and decides whether to migrate processes from heavier queues to lighter ones. The policy is:

  1. Identify the most-loaded and least-loaded CPUs.
  2. If the difference exceeds a threshold (say, 25% of load), migrate one or more processes from busy to idle.
  3. Otherwise, do nothing.

The "load" measure can be count of ready processes, total weight, or expected CPU demand. Linux's CFS uses total weight (sum of per-process weights), because that reflects proportional-share fairness: a CPU with a high-weight process is "heavy" even if it has only one process.

Migration is expensive because of affinity. Load balancing tries to minimize migration: don't move a process if the imbalance is minor; pick the process with the smallest cache footprint if possible.

#### Work stealing

An alternative, popular in user-level task schedulers (Cilk, Go's runtime, Rust's Tokio), is **work stealing** : when a CPU becomes idle, it "steals" work from a busier CPU's queue. The idle CPU initiates the migration, pulling work rather than the source CPU pushing.

Advantages: lazy — migration happens only when needed (when a CPU would otherwise idle). No threshold heuristics; the idle CPU just grabs work.

Kernel schedulers use a hybrid: periodic passive balancing (every few hundred ms) plus active balancing when a CPU is about to idle with work available elsewhere.

### 8.5 Dealing with Unequal Queues

What if load balancing is not enough? Consider 4 processes on a 3-CPU machine:

* CPU 0: A, D
* CPU 1: B
* CPU 2: C

CPU 0 has 2 processes; CPUs 1 and 2 have 1 each. The imbalance is 1 process. Load balancing might or might not act, depending on the threshold.

If CFS-like scheduling is used locally, A and D each get 50% of CPU 0 (total CPU: 0.5 + 0.5 = 1.0 for CPU 0). B gets 100% of CPU 1 (total: 1.0). C gets 100% of CPU 2 (total: 1.0). Global share: A=0.5, B=1.0, C=1.0, D=0.5. Not equal, despite all having equal weight.

Linux's load balancer periodically migrates A (or D) from CPU 0 to, say, CPU 2, so that over time each process gets roughly equal CPU. With 4 processes and 3 CPUs, the target share per process is 4/3 = ~1.33 "CPU seconds per second" — 75% each, averaged.

Achieving this requires migration: at some times A runs on CPU 0 alone, other times A runs on CPU 2, sharing with C. The migration penalties accumulate, but by keeping migrations infrequent, the overhead is small compared to the gained fairness.

### 8.6 Gang Scheduling and Specialized Workloads

A final category of multiprocessor scheduling: workloads where processes need to run _together_ on multiple CPUs for coordinated work.

**Gang scheduling** schedules a group of related processes (a "gang") simultaneously. If they cannot all be scheduled, none is. This is critical for parallel scientific codes that use barriers: if one process is late to the barrier, every other process is stuck waiting, wasting CPU.

Gang scheduling is outside CFS's default behavior (which schedules each process independently). It is used in HPC batch systems (Slurm, Moab, old IBM LoadLeveler) and some VM schedulers (VMware ESXi for SMP VMs). The tradeoff is fragmentation: a gang of 32 processes can only be scheduled when 32 CPUs are available — if some are busy, the gang waits, even if nonmembers are ready to run.

For typical server and desktop workloads, gang scheduling is overkill. Most processes do not need synchronized scheduling. Linux does not gang-schedule by default; admins who need it configure it explicitly.

### 8.7 NUMA: When Memory Is Not Uniform

Modern multi-socket servers have a wrinkle: memory is not uniform. A 2-socket server has two CPU sockets, each with its own memory. A process on socket 0 accessing memory physically attached to socket 0 is fast (~80 ns). Accessing memory on socket 1 is slower (~150 ns) because the access traverses the inter-socket interconnect.

This is **Non-Uniform Memory Access (NUMA)**. It is not a problem on single-socket machines; it emerges in 2-socket, 4-socket, 8-socket servers.

NUMA affects scheduling because a process's memory is usually allocated on the NUMA node where it first ran. Migrating the process to a different NUMA node means its memory is now "remote" — every access is slower. This is worse than L1/L2 cache cost because memory is larger and the penalty is a fixed 2× or so on every access, not just the first.

NUMA-aware schedulers add a rule to the load balancer: _prefer to migrate within a NUMA node; migrate across nodes only when imbalance is severe_. Linux's CFS became NUMA-aware in 2.6.30+ and has been refined repeatedly.

Additionally, the OS may migrate a process's _memory_ when migrating the process — copying physical pages to the new NUMA node so subsequent accesses are local again. This is a significant operation (copying megabytes of memory) and is done only for processes expected to stay on the new node for a long time.

The rule of thumb: for multi-socket NUMA machines, keep processes on their original socket whenever possible. Modern Linux does this; older versions did not, with measurable performance cost.

### 8.8 Tying Back to the Single-CPU Story

How does everything we learned in Chapters 5–7 apply to multi-CPU?

**Scheduling policy** (FIFO, SJF, RR, MLFQ, CFS) applies _within_ each CPU's runqueue. Linux uses CFS locally on each CPU, with its own red-black tree per CPU. Fairness is a two-level story: locally within a CPU, globally across CPUs via load balancing.

**Context switches** (Chapter 4) are per-CPU events. The mechanism is unchanged.

**Timer interrupts** fire on every CPU independently, triggering local scheduling decisions. Inter-CPU interrupts (IPIs) are used for cross-CPU coordination (e.g., triggering load balancing on an idle CPU).

**Priority** is expressed the same way (nice values, cgroup weights) and propagates naturally: a high-weight process is more attractive to keep on its local CPU, and the load balancer takes weight into account.

So multi-CPU scheduling adds a _new_ layer (per-CPU queues, balancing, affinity) without replacing anything. The scheduler designers and kernel implementers carry the cost of that layer; the user-visible model ("processes get fair, proportional CPU shares") is preserved.

### 8.9 Wrapping Up Threading Plan A

This chapter closes the CPU-sharing arc. Over seven chapters we traced:

* **Chapter 2** : one process owns the whole CPU.
* **Chapter 3** : the `fork` API creates more processes. Now we have many; the CPU must be shared.
* **Chapter 4** : the kernel can preempt and context-switch — the mechanism of sharing.
* **Chapters 5–7** : the policy of sharing — which process runs, for how long, at what priority.
* **Chapter 8** : the reality of modern hardware — many CPUs, cache affinity, NUMA, load balancing.

A process, from its perspective, has a virtual CPU: the OS gives it CPU cycles in some pattern, and it runs as if it owns the machine (except for being paused occasionally). The virtual CPU's capacity is the process's share of real CPU time. Across all the processes on a system, the real hardware's CPU seconds are divided among them according to scheduling policy.

Now we turn to **memory**. The next seven chapters build the second virtual resource: the address space. Every process will believe it owns a large, private memory — starting at address 0, extending to the end of the address space — while actually sharing the finite physical DRAM with every other process. The mechanisms for achieving this — base-and-bounds, segmentation, paging, TLBs, multi-level page tables, swapping — are the subject of Chapters 9–15. Along the way, Threading Plan B ("the page fault — a complete story of one memory reference") will unfold: by the end of Chapter 14 you will be able to trace every nanosecond of `int *x = malloc(1024); *x = 42;`.

### 8.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Define cache affinity. Why does it matter for scheduler design?

Hint

Section 8.1.

**Answer:** **Cache affinity** is the principle that a process runs best on the CPU where its working set is already cached. Moving it to a different CPU incurs many cache misses as the new CPU refills caches with the process's data. It matters for scheduler design because a scheduler that migrates processes freely (for fairness) loses the affinity benefit — migration has a performance penalty on the order of microseconds per migration. Good schedulers prefer leaving processes on their last CPU unless the imbalance is severe enough to justify the affinity cost.

**Q2.** Explain the lock-contention problem of single-queue multiprocessor scheduling. Why does it scale badly with CPU count?

**Answer:** In SQMS, every scheduling decision on any CPU requires the runqueue lock. With a few CPUs, the lock is briefly held and released; contention is minor. As CPU count grows, more CPUs want the lock at the same time. The hardware serializes access (one CPU at a time can hold the lock); the rest spin waiting. The fraction of CPU time spent spinning grows with CPU count. At high core counts (32+), this can easily consume 5–15% of CPU in spin time alone, and the system's scheduling throughput approaches a ceiling set by the lock's bandwidth, not by the work being done. This is a classic scalability wall.

**Q3.** What is the main advantage of multi-queue multiprocessor scheduling? What problem does it introduce?

**Answer:** The main advantage is scalability: each CPU has its own runqueue and its own lock, so scheduling decisions happen in parallel across CPUs without cross-CPU contention. Additionally, processes stay on their home CPU by default, preserving cache affinity. The problem introduced is **load imbalance** : if processes are not evenly distributed across CPUs, some are busy while others are idle, and proportional-share fairness across CPUs is not guaranteed locally. Periodic load balancing addresses this but adds complexity and costs migrations.

**Q4.** What is NUMA, and why does it complicate multiprocessor scheduling?

**Answer:** **NUMA (Non-Uniform Memory Access)** describes systems where different CPUs (or sockets) have different access speeds to different regions of physical memory. A CPU accessing memory on its local NUMA node is fast; accessing memory on a remote node is slower by 2× or more. This complicates scheduling because a process's memory is typically allocated on the node where it first ran. Migrating the process to another node makes all its subsequent memory accesses remote, potentially halving its effective speed. NUMA-aware schedulers prefer intra-node migrations and avoid cross-node migrations unless imbalance is severe. In some cases, the OS migrates the memory too, copying physical pages to follow the process.

**Q5.** What is gang scheduling, and when is it useful?

**Answer:** **Gang scheduling** schedules a group of related processes simultaneously — if the whole gang cannot run, none does. It is useful for parallel workloads that synchronize at barriers: if some gang members are delayed, others waste CPU at the barrier waiting. By ensuring all members run together, gang scheduling maximizes progress. It is common in HPC batch systems (Slurm, older LoadLeveler) and in SMP VM scheduling (VMware ESXi). It is not the default in general-purpose OSes because (a) most workloads do not need it; (b) it can fragment CPU availability, blocking the gang until enough CPUs are free simultaneously. Linux does not gang-schedule by default; it is an explicit configuration for specialized workloads.

#### Application Problems

**Q1.** A 16-CPU server runs 80 equal-weight processes under CFS. Load is evenly distributed: 5 processes per CPU. Each process receives 20% of CPU time (sched_latency / 5). Now one CPU crashes (15 CPUs left). What happens if the scheduler does not rebalance? What happens if it does?

Hint

Track where the 5 processes from the crashed CPU go, and what share each process ends up with.

**Answer:**

_No rebalance:_ The 5 processes on the crashed CPU are moved to some other CPU (OS must recover them). If they are all dumped on CPU 0, CPU 0 now has 10 processes, CPUs 1–14 still have 5 each. Processes on CPU 0 get 10% each of CPU 0 (half their old share); processes on other CPUs still get 20% each. Unfair: 5 processes get 10%, 75 processes get 20%.

_With rebalance:_ 5 processes must be redistributed. If they are spread one each to 5 other CPUs, those CPUs now have 6 processes instead of 5. Each process on those CPUs gets ~16.7% (100/6). Processes on the other 9 CPUs still get 20%. Less uneven but still some disparity. A better rebalance moves processes to equalize: total processes is 80, total CPUs is 15, so ~5.33 per CPU. 5 CPUs get 6 processes, 10 CPUs get 5 processes. Minimum per-process share: 16.7% (on busier CPUs), 20% (on others). Fairness is approximately preserved, trading some migrations for smoothed shares.

This exercise shows the tradeoff: perfect fairness requires continuous rebalancing (and many migrations); coarse rebalancing accepts some unfairness for affinity.

**Q2.** A process has been running on CPU 3 for 10 ms, during which it has brought 200 KB of working set into CPU 3's L1 and L2 caches. Its next 10 ms of execution, running on CPU 3, executes 10 million instructions. If migrated to CPU 7 (cold cache), estimate the overhead cost: what fraction of those 10 million instructions are delayed by cache misses?

**Answer:** Assume 200 KB of working set maps to ~3000 cache lines (64 bytes each). On cold CPU 7, each line must be fetched — say, 300 cycles per fetch (mix of L3 and DRAM). Total miss cost: 3000 × 300 = 900,000 cycles.

Assume CPU 7 runs at 3 GHz. Without misses, 10 ms of execution corresponds to 30 million cycles. With 900 K cycles of miss overhead, execution takes 30.9 M cycles, or about 3% longer. Only the first ~3 ms are slow (while the cache refills); later milliseconds run at normal speed.

Across 10 million instructions, about 3% are delayed — 300,000 instructions are effectively redone (on cold L1) compared to the uninterrupted case. In throughput terms, migration cost this process ~3% of its next 10 ms of work. On a loaded system, where migrations might happen every few ms, this adds up.

A scheduler that migrates every process every 1 ms would sustain 10% overhead or worse. Real schedulers migrate much more rarely — perhaps once per 100 ms at the fastest — keeping overhead under 0.1–1%.

**Q3.** Under Linux CFS with 4 CPUs, 16 equal-weight processes run. Processes P1–P4 are on CPU 0; P5–P8 on CPU 1; P9–P12 on CPU 2; P13–P16 on CPU 3. A new process P17 is created and placed on CPU 0 (making it busier). What does the load balancer do, and how soon?

**Answer:** The new process on CPU 0 makes CPU 0 have 5 processes while others have 4. Imbalance is 1 process. Linux CFS's load balancer runs periodically (default every 100–1000 ms on idle CPUs, more often for busy systems) and on demand when a CPU runs out of work.

With 25% imbalance threshold (one common default), 1/4 = 25% imbalance exactly matches the threshold. The load balancer migrates one process from CPU 0 to one of CPUs 1–3 — in fact, often to CPU 3 if that CPU recently went idle, or spreads to any CPU that looks less loaded. Specifically, it picks a migration candidate from CPU 0 — typically the least-recently-run process (which has lost more cache affinity) to minimize migration cost.

Timing: within a scheduling-tick (on the order of 1–10 ms) to within a load-balance interval (100 ms to 1 s). Typical: within 100 ms of P17's creation, one of {P1..P5} is migrated off CPU 0, restoring balance.

If imbalance is transient (P17 exits immediately), the balancer may not act. Linux tuning avoids excessive migration for short-lived imbalances.

**Q4.** A 2-socket NUMA server has 16 cores per socket (32 total). A database process was running on socket 0 with 8 GB of memory allocated locally. The load balancer decides to migrate it to socket 1. Describe the performance impact immediately after migration and over time.

**Answer:**

_Immediately after:_ Every memory access the process makes goes across the inter-socket interconnect, costing 150 ns instead of 80 ns per access — ~2× slower. For a memory-bound database, this is a major slowdown: roughly 50% less effective throughput.

_Over time:_ The OS may detect the migration and decide to _migrate the memory_ — copy the 8 GB of physical pages to socket 1's memory. This takes time: 8 GB at, say, 10 GB/s memory bandwidth → 800 ms of copying. During that copy, the process can run but any access to still-remote pages is slow.

After memory migration completes: all accesses are local again, full performance restored.

_What the scheduler should have done:_ Prefer to migrate within the same socket. If it had migrated from core 3 to core 5 (both on socket 0), no memory migration would be needed. Only cross-socket migrations trigger the 2× slowdown.

This is why Linux's NUMA-aware scheduler has a strong preference for intra-socket migrations and avoids cross-socket migrations unless imbalance between sockets is severe. Without NUMA awareness, such cross-socket migrations are common in standard load balancers and result in mysterious database slowdowns — one of the more famous "why is my server suddenly slow?" debugging stories of the 2000s.

**Q5.** On a 32-core machine, what is more scalable: SQMS with a per-CPU affinity cache (so processes are soft-assigned a home CPU, and the single runqueue is consulted only when no affine CPU is idle), or pure MQMS with periodic load balancing? Argue your answer.

**Answer:** Pure MQMS is more scalable.

The affinity-cache SQMS reduces but does not eliminate contention on the shared runqueue. Every time a CPU is idle and its affine process is already running elsewhere (common during load imbalance or for new processes), the CPU consults the shared queue, contending for the global lock. At 32 cores with frequent context switches (say, every few ms), this contention rises with core count.

MQMS has no shared lock on the common path. Each CPU's runqueue lock is local and contended only briefly. Load balancing, which does require cross-CPU coordination, happens rarely (every 100 ms or more) and touches only a couple of CPUs at a time. Overhead is amortized.

For small core counts (say 2–4), the difference is minor. For 32+ cores, MQMS scales essentially linearly while SQMS (even with affinity cache) plateaus. Linux's scheduler moved fully to MQMS in the O(1) scheduler (2001) and has kept that structure in CFS.

#### Think Deeper

**Q1.** CFS's red-black tree per CPU gives O(log n) scheduling on each CPU. Load balancing, done every 100 ms or so, is O(n) across all CPUs. For a 1000-core machine, is there a scalability concern with this model? What might a research scheduler do differently?

**Discussion:** At 1000 cores, even O(log n) per-CPU operations are cheap (log 100 < 7). The concern is load balancing: an O(n) scan across 1000 CPUs every 100 ms means potentially evaluating millions of processes, visiting 1000 runqueues, taking and releasing 1000 locks. This has been measured as a real overhead on large servers.

Research approaches include:

* **Hierarchical load balancing.** Group CPUs into clusters (e.g., 16 per socket, 4 sockets per node). Balance within a cluster frequently, across clusters rarely. The total work is proportional to log n or smaller.
* **Lazy balancing.** Do not scan; act only when a CPU would otherwise idle. A work-stealing approach fits here.
* **Affinity-based partitioning.** Explicitly pin classes of work to subsets of CPUs (cgroups, containers with CPU masks). The scheduler never considers migrating out of the mask. At 1000 cores, this is a normal administrative practice.

Linux's CFS has evolved to include all three ideas in various forms. Pure "scan all CPUs periodically" does not scale; production schedulers combine many heuristics.

**Q2.** Gang scheduling is criticized for fragmentation: if a gang of 64 processes cannot all be scheduled together because 62 CPUs are free, none runs. Is there a way to get gang scheduling's benefit (no wasted barriers) without its cost (fragmentation)?

**Discussion:** Several partial answers:

* **Relax the gang requirement.** Instead of requiring simultaneous scheduling, schedule gang members "close together in time" — e.g., all 64 within a 1 ms window. This avoids the worst of the barrier problem (barrier waits would be at most 1 ms) while allowing the scheduler to pack smaller gangs in gaps. This is sometimes called "co-scheduling."
* **Spin-then-block barriers.** Change the workload: instead of blocking on a barrier (which wastes CPU when gang members are not scheduled), spin briefly and then block. If other gang members arrive within spin time, no blocking. If not, the blocking releases CPU.
* **Invest in scheduler-visible barriers.** If the scheduler knows a process is waiting at a barrier for other processes, it can preemptively schedule those processes to let the barrier clear. MCS-style "waited-for" hints.

The tension is: gang scheduling pushes complexity into the scheduler; alternatives push it into the workload (modified barriers, cooperative blocking). Modern HPC uses both — Slurm (gang-scheduling batch manager) plus MPI barrier libraries that spin briefly.

### Chapter Summary

Multiprocessor scheduling extends single-CPU scheduling with new challenges: cache affinity (a process's working set lives in a specific CPU's caches and migrating damages performance), lock scalability (a shared runqueue does not scale past a handful of cores), and NUMA (cross-socket access is meaningfully slower than local). Modern schedulers like Linux's CFS handle this with per-CPU runqueues, periodic load balancing that respects affinity, and NUMA-aware placement. Gang scheduling addresses tightly-coupled parallel workloads but is not the default.

Threading Plan A, which began in Chapter 2 with one process owning everything, concludes here. Each chapter added competitors and mechanisms: fork (Ch. 3), context switch (Ch. 4), scheduling policy (Ch. 5–7), and multi-core realities (Ch. 8). A process now sees a virtual CPU that shares real CPU resources fairly and efficiently with many others.

From here, Chapter 9 opens the memory arc and begins Threading Plan B. The fundamental question: when many processes each want to use memory, how does the OS give each one a private, protected address space? The answer is virtual memory — the most important abstraction after the process itself. We begin by defining the address space and asking _what a memory reference actually does._

---

## Chapter 9. The Address Space Abstraction

### 9.1 A Pointer That Doesn't Point Where You Think

Run this small C program on a modern Linux machine:
    
    
    #include <stdio.h>
    #include <stdlib.h>
    
    int main(void) {
        int *x = malloc(sizeof(int));
        printf("address of x: %p\n", (void *)x);
        *x = 42;
        printf("value at x:   %d\n", *x);
        return 0;
    }
    

A plausible output is:
    
    
    address of x: 0x55f3a1b4a2a0
    value at x:   42
    

Now run it again in another terminal, at the same time:
    
    
    address of x: 0x55f3a1b4a2a0
    value at x:   42
    

Two separate processes. Two copies of the program running simultaneously. Both report the _same address_ `0x55f3a1b4a2a0`. Yet they do not interfere: each writes `42` into "its" address, and neither overwrites the other.

If addresses are just numbers naming cells of DRAM, this is impossible. Two processes cannot both own cell `0x55f3a1b4a2a0` if cell `0x55f3a1b4a2a0` is a physical location on a physical chip.

The resolution: the number the program sees is not a physical location. It is a **virtual address** , and it has meaning only inside the process that used it. Each process has its own **address space** — a private map from virtual addresses to physical storage — and the OS plus the hardware cooperate to maintain the illusion that each program has the entire machine's memory to itself.

This chapter opens the memory arc. Chapters 2-8 answered _how does the CPU get shared?_ The next six chapters answer _how does memory get shared?_ We will follow a single memory reference — the write `*x = 42` in the program above — through every layer of the stack, from the moment `malloc` returns a number to the moment a value lands in DRAM. That thread is **Plan B: The Page Fault.** It begins now.

### 9.2 What the Address Space Is

#### Example: A process's view of memory

Imagine a process that has just started. It declares a global array, calls `malloc`, and has a call stack a few frames deep. From inside the process, memory appears to be a single contiguous range of bytes from address `0` to some maximum. The process sees roughly this layout:
    
    
     high addresses
    ┌──────────────────────┐
    │ stack                │   grows down as functions call
    │   ↓                  │
    │                      │
    │      (unused)        │
    │                      │
    │   ↑                  │
    │ heap                 │   grows up as malloc is called
    ├──────────────────────┤
    │ data / bss           │   global and static variables
    ├──────────────────────┤
    │ text (code)          │   program instructions
    └──────────────────────┘
     low addresses
    

Four regions, each with a purpose:

* **Text.** The compiled machine code of the program. Read-only and shared across processes running the same binary.
* **Data (and BSS).** Initialized and uninitialized global/static variables. Fixed size, known at load time.
* **Heap.** Dynamically allocated memory. Grows on `malloc`, shrinks on `free`.
* **Stack.** Function call frames. Grows on call, shrinks on return.

#### Pattern

This layout is not a physical arrangement. No two of these regions have to be adjacent in DRAM. The layout is a _contract_ between the OS and the process: "Your view of memory will look like this — a flat space from low to high addresses, with these regions in these positions."

The process need not know, and cannot observe, where these regions actually live in physical memory. It uses virtual addresses; the hardware translates them to physical addresses on every access.

#### Definition: Address space

**Definition:** A process's _address space_ is the set of all memory addresses the process is allowed to use, together with a mapping that tells the hardware where to find the data at each valid address. Every process has its own address space. Two processes may use the same virtual address and have it refer to entirely different physical locations — or to no physical location at all.

The address space is the abstraction. **Virtual memory** is the mechanism the OS and hardware use to implement it.

### 9.3 Three Goals of Memory Virtualization

Any solution to "many processes share one DRAM" must deliver three properties. If any of them fails, something important breaks.

#### Goal 1: Transparency

The process should see a private, contiguous memory. It should not know, need to know, or be able to tell that its memory is shared with others, relocated by the OS, or backed by mechanisms more exotic than "bytes in DRAM."

_Without this mechanism, what breaks?_ Every program would need to be rewritten for every machine and every set of co-resident processes. A program that said `load from address 0x1000` would have to be relinked depending on whether another program happened to be using physical address `0x1000` at the same time. Programming becomes impossible.

#### Goal 2: Efficiency

Address translation happens on every memory reference — billions of times per second. If translation is slow, every program is slow. The system must translate addresses with _hardware-level_ speed: nanoseconds, not microseconds. This means specialized hardware (we will meet the TLB in Chapter 12) and careful data structures (we will meet multi-level page tables in Chapter 13).

_Without this mechanism, what breaks?_ The abstraction exists but is unusable. Imagine if every memory read took 100 extra nanoseconds. A tight loop that touches memory a billion times would lose 100 seconds to translation overhead alone. Programs would run at a fraction of hardware speed.

#### Goal 3: Protection

Process A must not be able to read or write Process B's memory — and neither may read or write the kernel's memory. If a buggy or malicious program reads an address, it must touch only its own data, and only the regions of its own data it is allowed to touch (the stack should not be able to overwrite the code).

_Without this mechanism, what breaks?_ Any program can corrupt any other program. A single buggy process can crash the entire system. A single malicious process can steal any other process's secrets. Modern computing — multi-user machines, web browsers running untrusted JavaScript, cloud VMs — depends on this.

#### Common Misconception

_"Virtual memory is about swapping memory to disk."_

Swapping is one feature that virtual memory enables, and we will study it in Chapter 14. But the _core_ purpose of virtual memory is not disk-backed memory — it is the three goals above. Even on a system with more DRAM than any process could use, virtual memory is essential because it provides transparency, speed, and protection. Swapping is a bonus.

### 9.4 What `malloc` Actually Returns

Let us return to the opening example. The program calls `malloc(sizeof(int))`, receives `0x55f3a1b4a2a0`, and writes `42`. What happens under the hood, and — crucially — what does _not_ happen?

#### Example: The life of an allocation

Step by step:

  1. **`malloc` runs entirely in user space.** It consults the process's heap data structures (the C library's allocator maintains a free list). If there is space on the heap, `malloc` picks a chunk and returns a virtual address pointing into it.
  2. **The virtual address is handed to the program.** The program stores the address in the variable `x`. So far, nothing has touched physical memory except the allocator's own bookkeeping.
  3. **The program executes`*x = 42`.** This is a store instruction that the CPU issues to virtual address `0x55f3a1b4a2a0`.
  4. **The memory hardware translates the virtual address to a physical address.** This is the step we will spend five chapters understanding.
  5. **The value`42` lands in a physical DRAM cell** whose physical address is the translated one.

Notice what happened in step 2: `malloc` _promised_ that `0x55f3a1b4a2a0` was usable, but it did not _bind_ that address to any physical location. The binding is lazy. It might not be resolved until step 3, when the hardware asks, "where does `0x55f3a1b4a2a0` live?"

#### Pattern: The promise

This pattern — allocate now, bind later — is a recurring theme in OS design. The OS makes promises (an address is valid; a file exists; a connection is open) but delays the physical work until the promise is cashed in. This is how the OS supports programs that _ask_ for far more resources than the machine physically has: most of the asked-for resources are never used, and those that are used get bound to physical resources on demand.

#### Definition: Lazy allocation

**Definition:** _Lazy allocation_ (also called _demand paging_ in the memory context) is the technique of creating a virtual resource without immediately binding it to a physical resource. The physical resource is assigned only when the program first uses the virtual one. If it never uses it, no physical resource is consumed.

We will see concrete mechanisms for lazy allocation in Chapter 14 (page faults).

### 9.5 A World Without Address Spaces

#### Example: Early batch systems

In the 1950s, a computer typically ran one program at a time. The program sat at low addresses, the OS sat at some fixed high address, and there was no translation — a virtual address was a physical address. When the program finished, the operator loaded the next one.

This is trivial to understand but gives us nothing. There is no multiprogramming, no protection (a buggy program can overwrite the OS), no sharing, no swapping. If a program needs more memory than the machine has, it cannot run. If a program crashes mid-execution, the machine needs to be rebooted.

#### Example: Static relocation

In the 1960s, OSes started running multiple programs. At load time, the OS rewrote every address in the program to place it at some available physical range. Program A is loaded at `0x10000`, Program B at `0x50000`. Every `jump` instruction in Program A is patched so its target is in the `0x10000` range.

This works — sort of. But it fails all three goals:

* **Transparency:** The program must be relinked for every layout; it cannot simply be copied and run.
* **Efficiency:** Rewriting every address in the binary at load time is slow.
* **Protection:** Nothing stops Program A from computing `0x50010` and writing there. Program B gets corrupted.

Modern systems use _dynamic_ relocation — translation on every memory access, done by hardware — because the static version provides neither protection nor flexibility.

#### Pattern

Each mechanism the OS introduces (static relocation, then base-and-bounds, then segmentation, then paging) is a partial answer. Each fixes one problem and leaves another. By Chapter 13, we will have a full solution: translation via per-process page tables. But first we walk the path historically, because each step illuminates why the final design looks the way it does.

### 9.6 The Cost of the Abstraction

The three goals pull against each other. Transparency and protection are satisfied by adding translation; translation is an extra step on every memory access, which hurts efficiency. Efficiency is satisfied by caching translations; caching opens the question of consistency when the mapping changes. Protection is satisfied by trapping illegal accesses; trapping opens the question of how to handle the trap without crashing the program unnecessarily.

Every design decision from here through Chapter 15 is about balancing these three goals.

#### Common Misconception

_"Virtual memory slows programs down."_

In rare microbenchmarks that stress the memory system, yes — translation has a cost. But in practice, virtual memory makes programs _faster_ overall, because it enables features (sharing, copy-on-write, demand paging) that would otherwise require expensive workarounds. The translation machinery (TLBs, prefetchers) is so well optimized that the measurable slowdown is usually under 1%.

The alternative — static relocation or no translation at all — is not faster. It is untenable.

### 9.7 Chapter Summary

* A _virtual address_ is a number the program uses to refer to memory. It is not a physical DRAM location; the hardware translates it on every access.
* An _address space_ is a process's private view of memory, consisting of text, data, heap, and stack regions, organized as a flat range from low to high addresses.
* _Virtual memory_ is the mechanism the OS and hardware use to implement address spaces. Its three goals are **transparency, efficiency, and protection.** Every mechanism in the next six chapters serves one or more of these goals.
* Memory is allocated lazily: `malloc` returns a virtual address before any physical memory is bound to it. The binding happens on first use.
* Without address space abstraction, multiprogramming, protection, and flexible memory management are impossible. Static relocation is not a substitute.

In Chapter 10, we begin adding mechanism. The simplest form of address translation — base-and-bounds — relocates every memory access by a per-process offset. It gives us protection and transparency, but wastes enormous amounts of memory. Segmentation is the next refinement. The page fault story continues.

### 9.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A process reads from virtual address `0x4000`. Is there data at physical address `0x4000`? Explain.

**Answer:** Not necessarily, and there usually is not. Virtual address `0x4000` maps to whatever physical address the OS and hardware have assigned for that region of this process's address space. It might be `0x800000` in DRAM. It might not yet be backed by any physical memory (the mapping is lazy). The whole point of virtualization is that the program's addresses are decoupled from physical locations.

**Q2.** Name the three goals of memory virtualization and state, in one sentence each, what breaks without them.

**Answer:**

* _Transparency:_ Without it, programs cannot be written portably; every program must be aware of all other programs' memory layouts.
* _Efficiency:_ Without it, translation overhead would dominate execution time and programs would run at a fraction of hardware speed.
* _Protection:_ Without it, any bug or malicious action in one process can corrupt or spy on any other process, including the OS.

**Q3.** Why is `malloc`'s return value described as a "promise" rather than a "binding"?

**Answer:** `malloc` returns a virtual address without allocating physical memory for it. The address is guaranteed (by the OS and the allocator) to be valid — but no physical DRAM cell has yet been reserved. The physical binding occurs later, typically when the program first writes to the address and the hardware signals a page fault, which the OS handles by binding a physical page on demand.

**Q4.** In static relocation, the OS patches all addresses at load time. Name two properties of memory virtualization that static relocation fails to deliver.

**Answer:** Static relocation fails at _protection_ (nothing prevents a process from computing an address outside its region and writing there) and at _efficiency in dynamic scenarios_ (the binary cannot be moved once loaded, so the OS cannot compact memory or swap to disk without redoing the whole patch). It does provide a form of transparency at load time, but that transparency is one-shot and brittle.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Two instances of the same program print the same value for `&main`. What does this tell you about virtual addresses? If the two programs share a physical page for their code segment, does that contradict your answer?

**Answer:** The identical printed value shows that `&main` is a virtual address — each process has its own address space, and both processes' copies of the text segment are mapped at the same _virtual_ address. It does not contradict physical sharing: at the hardware level, both virtual addresses can translate to the _same_ physical page (the OS shares read-only code pages across processes running the same binary). Virtual identity and physical identity are independent.

**Q6.** A malicious program wants to read another process's private data. Explain, using the three goals, which goal must hold and how.

**Answer:** The _protection_ goal must hold. Each process's address space maps only its own regions to physical pages that hold its data. When the malicious program issues a load from a virtual address, the hardware consults _the malicious program's own mappings_ , not the victim's. Any address the attacker can generate either maps to the attacker's own memory or is invalid (causing a trap). It cannot reach the victim's physical pages because the attacker's address space provides no mapping that names them.

**Q7.** A program calls `malloc(1 << 30)` (one gigabyte). It then writes only to the first byte. Roughly how much physical memory is consumed? Why?

**Answer:** Roughly one page's worth (4 KB on typical systems), not one gigabyte. `malloc` returns a virtual address pointing to a region of one billion bytes that the OS _promises_ is valid, but does not bind to physical memory. When the program writes the first byte, the hardware faults, and the OS binds a single physical page to the touched virtual page. The remaining gigabyte-minus-one-page is never touched and never backed by DRAM. This is lazy allocation in action.

**Q8.** Describe one practical situation in which the "promise" semantics of memory allocation is exploited deliberately, not merely tolerated.

**Answer:** Sparse data structures. A hash table might reserve 10 GB of virtual address space for its backing array, knowing that typical use will fill only 100 MB. Because of lazy allocation, only the pages actually touched consume physical memory. The program enjoys O(1) lookups without paying for unused buckets. Virtual memory size and physical footprint are decoupled.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** Chapter 4 introduced limited direct execution: the OS lets the program run on the CPU directly, then reclaims control via traps and timer interrupts. Compare this to memory virtualization: the OS lets the program use memory directly, but with hardware translation and protection on every access. What is the common pattern, and why does the OS rely on it?

**Answer:** Both are examples of **hardware-assisted mediation.** The OS cannot afford to sit between the program and the resource on every access — the performance cost would be intolerable, because every memory reference or every CPU cycle would require a kernel entry. Instead, the OS configures the hardware (the interrupt vector, the page table base register) once, then steps aside. The hardware enforces the OS's policies at full speed on every access. The OS regains control only on exceptional events: a trap, a timer, an illegal memory access. This pattern is the defining technique of modern OS design: _set up, get out of the way, reassert when required._ Without it, efficiency (goal 2) is impossible.

**Q10.** The chapter claims that "virtual memory is not primarily about swapping to disk." But the term "virtual memory" is often used loosely to mean "memory backed by disk." Propose a better name for the abstraction and argue why precise terminology matters here.

**Answer:** A precise name would be _address space virtualization_ or _memory translation._ The historical term "virtual memory" arose when the first implementations were motivated by extending RAM with disk, so the name fixated on that feature. But the abstraction — a private, translated address space per process — is what matters; disk backing is one optional mechanism.

Precise terminology matters because students and engineers often infer that "virtual memory is slow because of disk" or "if I have enough RAM I don't need virtual memory." Both are wrong. A machine with abundant DRAM still uses translation on every access to provide transparency and protection. Renaming the abstraction would make this clearer and prevent the conflation of the _abstraction_ (address space) with one of its _optional features_ (swapping). In this book we use _address space_ and _virtual memory_ interchangeably but always with the address space as the primary concept.

---

## Chapter 10. Base-and-Bounds and Segmentation

### 10.1 The Simplest Possible Translation

We ended Chapter 9 with a promise and an unanswered question. The promise: `malloc` returned a virtual address `0x55f3a1b4a2a0`. The question: how does the hardware turn that virtual number into a physical DRAM cell, on every memory access, with no perceptible slowdown?

Before we reach the full answer (pages and page tables, Chapters 11-13), we take a simpler path — one that real hardware actually used from the late 1960s through the 1980s. This chapter covers two steps along the road:

  1. **Base-and-bounds** (also called _dynamic relocation_): every virtual address is added to a per-process offset, and any address past the process's size triggers a trap.
  2. **Segmentation** : three or four independent base-and-bounds pairs, one for each region of the address space (code, heap, stack).

Both mechanisms implement the three goals of Chapter 9 — transparency, efficiency, protection — but at different levels of ambition. Seeing what they achieve and where they fail motivates paging, which is how real systems do it today.

Plan B, the page-fault story, continues: we are still following `*x = 42` from the previous chapter. For now, imagine the hardware translates the virtual address through a base-and-bounds register pair. We will pull that assumption apart piece by piece.

### 10.2 Base-and-Bounds

#### Example: One offset register

Consider a machine with 16 KB of DRAM. The OS loads a 4 KB process and places it at physical addresses `[32 KB, 36 KB)`. From the program's point of view, its own address space runs from virtual address `0` to `4 KB - 1`. From the hardware's point of view, the program actually sits at `32 KB` in physical DRAM.

The hardware has two special registers in the CPU, called the **memory management unit (MMU)** :

* **Base register:** holds `32 KB` (the start of this process's physical region).
* **Bounds register:** holds `4 KB` (the size of this process's address space).

On every memory access, the CPU performs:
    
    
    if virtual_address >= bounds:
        raise protection fault (trap to OS)
    physical_address = virtual_address + base
    

So when the program issues `load from 0x100`, the hardware checks `0x100 < 0x1000` (4 KB), adds `0x8000` (32 KB), and fetches from physical `0x8100`.

#### Pattern

Two register reads, one comparison, one addition — all in parallel inside the MMU, all on the CPU's critical path for memory access. The translation is fast enough that it does not show up as an extra cycle in the pipeline. This satisfies goal 2 (efficiency).

When a context switch happens, the OS reloads the base and bounds registers for the incoming process. That is all the per-process memory state the hardware needs. Two registers per process — a tiny overhead.

#### Definition: Base-and-bounds translation

**Definition:** _Base-and-bounds_ is a hardware translation mechanism in which each process is associated with two registers — a base and a bounds. Every virtual address is bounds-checked, then added to the base to produce the physical address. A process's address space is therefore a contiguous range `[base, base + bounds)` in physical memory.

#### What goal 3 (protection) now looks like

Process A has base = `32 KB`, bounds = `4 KB`. Process B has base = `64 KB`, bounds = `8 KB`. When A is running, its MMU uses A's base and bounds. If A computes virtual address `0x5000` — which is past A's 4 KB bound — the hardware traps, and the OS terminates A with a segmentation fault. A cannot reach B's physical memory, because A cannot even produce a virtual address that would translate into B's region.

_Without base-and-bounds, what breaks?_ A program that computes a wild pointer (from a buffer overflow, say) can write directly to another program's physical memory. Programs that are supposed to be isolated are not. The machine is insecure and unstable. Base-and-bounds is the first real answer to goal 3.

#### Common Misconception

_"If every process has its own base and bounds, surely every process can expand at will — just increase the bounds register."_

No. The bounds register limits the process to `[base, base + bounds)` in _physical_ memory. To grow the process's address space, the OS must either grow the bound (which requires that the following physical addresses be unused) or _move_ the entire process to a new physical range and update the base register. Both are expensive. This limitation is one reason base-and-bounds is insufficient for modern systems — we will see why in 10.4.

### 10.3 Relocation in the OS

Base-and-bounds pushes most of the work to hardware, but the OS still has responsibilities:

  1. **Allocating memory to a new process.** When `fork` creates a child, the OS must find a free physical range large enough to hold the child's address space. It maintains a free list of physical memory regions — a job that gets thorny when processes come and go at different sizes, creating _external fragmentation_.
  2. **Reclaiming memory.** When a process exits, the OS returns its physical range to the free list.
  3. **Switching base and bounds on context switch.** On every context switch, the OS saves the old process's base/bounds (usually they live in the PCB, Chapter 2) and loads the new process's values into the MMU.
  4. **Handling traps.** When a process generates an out-of-bounds address, the CPU traps to the OS. The OS typically kills the offending process (SIGSEGV).

#### Example: The fragmentation trap

Three processes run, each 4 KB, on a 16 KB machine. The OS places them at physical `[0, 4K)`, `[4K, 8K)`, `[8K, 12K)`. Free space: `[12K, 16K)`.

Process B exits, freeing `[4K, 8K)`. Now the free list has two holes: `[4K, 8K)` and `[12K, 16K)` — 8 KB free in total. A new 8 KB process arrives. It cannot fit, even though 8 KB is free, because no _contiguous_ 8 KB range exists.

This is **external fragmentation** : memory broken into many small pieces, none individually large enough for a new arrival.

The OS can fix this by _compaction_ — temporarily stopping all processes, moving them to eliminate the holes, and resuming — but compaction is expensive. Every process must pause; every byte must be physically copied; every base register must be updated. A machine serving real workloads cannot afford compaction often enough to keep fragmentation under control.

#### Pattern

This is the first serious limitation of base-and-bounds. The mechanism is simple and fast, but the _allocation problem_ it creates (find a contiguous physical range) is hard. Every real system that uses base-and-bounds ends up paying the fragmentation cost one way or another — either by wasting memory in holes, or by spending CPU time on compaction.

### 10.4 What Goes Wrong

Base-and-bounds satisfies the three goals of Chapter 9, but only barely. Three problems emerge in practice:

**Problem 1: Internal fragmentation.** The process's address space includes a potentially huge gap between the top of the heap and the bottom of the stack (recall the diagram in 9.2). The heap grows up, the stack grows down, and the unused middle region is wasted _physical_ memory — because base-and-bounds gives the process a contiguous physical range equal to the full bounds, including the gap.

A program that declares a 4 KB stack and a 4 KB heap but anticipates an 8 KB growth window in the middle must reserve 16 KB of physical memory. 8 KB of that 16 KB is empty space — but it is "in use" from the system's point of view because it sits inside the process's bounds.

**Problem 2: External fragmentation.** We saw this in 10.3.

**Problem 3: Inflexibility.** A process cannot _share_ code with another process (even identical programs) because sharing would require overlapping physical ranges, which base-and-bounds forbids. A process cannot easily _grow_ its heap past the initial bounds, because the physical range beyond it may be occupied.

All three problems have one root cause: **the address space is a single contiguous physical range.** Relaxing that assumption — letting different regions of the address space live in _different_ physical ranges — is the idea behind segmentation.

### 10.5 Segmentation: Several Bases and Bounds

#### Example: Three segments

Give each process not one base-and-bounds pair but three: one for code, one for heap, one for stack.
    
    
    Segment 0: code   base = 32K, bounds = 2K
    Segment 1: heap   base = 48K, bounds = 2K  (grows up)
    Segment 2: stack  base = 66K, bounds = 2K  (grows down)
    

When the program issues a virtual address, the hardware uses the top bits of the address to pick a segment, then applies that segment's base-and-bounds.

Virtual addresses are split:
    
    
    ┌──────────┬────────────────────────────┐
    │ seg bits │ offset within segment      │
    └──────────┴────────────────────────────┘
    

Say two bits for the segment number, the rest for offset. Virtual `0x0100` is segment 0 (code), offset `0x100`. Virtual `0x4080` is segment 1 (heap), offset `0x80`.

Translation per segment:
    
    
    seg = top bits of virtual_address
    offset = low bits of virtual_address
    if offset >= bounds[seg]:
        trap
    physical_address = base[seg] + offset
    

#### Pattern

Segmentation is base-and-bounds _applied independently_ to each region of the address space. The address space is no longer a single contiguous physical range — it is several, one per segment. Each region can be:

* placed anywhere in physical memory, independently of the others
* grown without moving the others
* shared with other processes (two processes can have the same base for their code segment)
* marked read-only (code) or read-write (heap, stack)

#### Definition: Segmentation

**Definition:** _Segmentation_ is a memory translation scheme that divides a process's address space into several variable-sized regions called _segments_. Each segment has its own base and bounds register, and translation selects the appropriate segment using bits from the virtual address. Segments can be relocated, grown, shared, and protected independently.

#### The gap problem, solved

In the three-segment design, the huge gap between heap and stack is _not_ backed by any physical memory. The heap segment occupies only as much physical memory as the heap needs; the stack segment only as much as the stack needs. The gap costs nothing. Internal fragmentation vanishes.

#### The sharing problem, solved

If two processes run the same program, both can have their code segment base point to the _same_ physical range (marked read-only in hardware). One copy of the code in physical memory, used by many processes. Segmentation makes this natural.

#### Common Misconception

_"Segmentation eliminates fragmentation entirely."_

Internal fragmentation, yes. External fragmentation, no — in fact, segmentation makes external fragmentation _worse_. Instead of one allocation per process, the OS now makes several variable-sized allocations (one per segment), and each one must fit in a contiguous physical region. The OS spends more time managing a more fragmented free list. Compaction is still the only clean solution, and compaction is still expensive. Segmentation improves _internal_ fragmentation at the cost of _external_ fragmentation complexity.

### 10.6 Segment Fault Handling

When a program accesses an invalid address, the hardware traps. Historically, this trap's name was _segmentation fault_ — a name that survives to the present on Unix systems even though modern Unix uses paging, not segmentation.

#### Example: A stack overflow

A deep recursion drives the stack pointer below the stack segment's lower bound (recall the stack grows down). The CPU issues a load from the offending address. The MMU checks: segment 2 (stack), offset is negative relative to the segment start. Out of bounds. Trap.

The OS receives the trap. It could do several things:

* kill the process (the default for SIGSEGV)
* if the access was _almost_ in bounds, try to grow the stack segment and retry
* if the process is allowed to grow, update the bounds register and return

Handling stack growth is a classic case: most of the time, the stack has room, but occasionally a program's recursion depth exceeds the initial bound. A polite OS grows the segment rather than killing the process.

#### Pattern

The OS responds to protection faults by deciding whether to kill, to repair, or to grow. The distinction between "bug" and "growth" cannot be made by hardware alone; the OS has policy, the hardware has mechanism.

### 10.7 Why Segmentation Is Not Enough

Segmentation solved the gap problem but inherited external fragmentation. It also introduced a new difficulty:

**Variable-sized allocations.** Each segment has a different size. The OS free list must support arbitrary-sized requests and arbitrary-sized frees. Finding a best-fit or first-fit segment is a nontrivial allocation problem. Fragmentation accumulates over time — the free list splinters into many small, unusable holes.

**Shared libraries.** Two processes can share one code segment if both use identical code. But modern programs use many shared libraries, each a distinct region of memory. Segmenting a process into 50 different library segments is unwieldy; segment registers (historically) were a scarce hardware resource.

**Large sparse address spaces.** 64-bit processes routinely have 256 TB of virtual address space, most of it unused. Segmentation requires each allocated region to be contiguous in _physical_ memory. That model does not fit modern usage.

Pages — the mechanism we introduce in Chapter 11 — solve these problems by using _fixed-size_ allocations. Fixed-size means no external fragmentation of physical memory (only internal fragmentation, bounded at half a page per segment). Fixed-size means fast allocation (a bit-map of free pages, O(1) allocation). Fixed-size means simple sharing (map two virtual pages to one physical page). Paging is not obviously simpler at first glance — we pay with a table of mappings per process, which can be large — but it wins overwhelmingly in practice.

### 10.8 Chapter Summary

* _Base-and-bounds_ translates virtual addresses to physical by adding a per-process offset and checking a per-process size. Two registers suffice. It gives protection and transparency, but imposes a contiguous physical layout per process.
* _Internal fragmentation_ is the unused space inside an allocation (e.g., the gap between heap and stack). _External fragmentation_ is the unused space outside all allocations (the holes between them).
* _Segmentation_ gives each region of the address space (code, heap, stack) its own base and bounds. It eliminates the internal gap, supports sharing, and allows region-specific protection.
* Segmentation worsens external fragmentation, because the OS must allocate variable-sized pieces of physical memory.
* Both mechanisms translate fast (goal 2) and protect (goal 3). Both struggle to provide flexibility — which is why real systems move to paging.

The page fault story continues in Chapter 11. The key idea: break physical memory into fixed-size slots (_page frames_) and the virtual address space into fixed-size _pages_. Map any virtual page to any physical frame, and keep the mapping in a per-process _page table_. This is the foundation of modern virtual memory.

### 10.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Base-and-bounds uses two registers per process. Name them and what each represents.

**Answer:** The _base register_ holds the physical address at which the process's address space begins. The _bounds register_ holds the size of the process's address space (or, equivalently, the largest valid virtual address + 1). The hardware computes `physical = virtual + base` and checks `virtual < bounds`.

**Q2.** Define internal fragmentation and external fragmentation, and give one example of each.

**Answer:**

* _Internal fragmentation:_ unused space inside an allocation. Example: a process with a 4 KB heap and 4 KB stack is given a 16 KB bounds, with 8 KB of the middle gap sitting in the process's physical range but unused.
* _External fragmentation:_ unused space outside all allocations, split into holes too small to satisfy new requests. Example: after three 4 KB processes come and go in a 16 KB machine, two separate 4 KB holes remain, but a new 8 KB process cannot fit.

**Q3.** In segmentation, what information in the virtual address selects which segment is being accessed?

**Answer:** The top bits of the virtual address encode the segment number. The remaining bits encode the offset within the selected segment. The hardware uses the segment bits to pick the corresponding (base, bounds) pair and applies them to the offset.

**Q4.** Why does segmentation support code sharing while base-and-bounds does not?

**Answer:** Base-and-bounds requires each process's _entire_ address space to occupy a single contiguous physical range, so two processes cannot share one region without sharing _everything_. Segmentation gives each region its own base register, so two processes can independently point their code-segment base to the same physical range (marked read-only) while their heap and stack segments remain private.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** A machine uses base-and-bounds with base = `0x10000` and bounds = `0x4000`. A process issues loads at virtual addresses `0x0100`, `0x3FFF`, and `0x5000`. For each, give the physical address or describe the trap.

**Answer:**

* `0x0100`: within bounds (`< 0x4000`). Physical = `0x0100 + 0x10000 = 0x10100`.
* `0x3FFF`: within bounds (`0x3FFF < 0x4000`). Physical = `0x3FFF + 0x10000 = 0x13FFF`.
* `0x5000`: out of bounds (`0x5000 >= 0x4000`). Hardware traps; OS handles (typically SIGSEGV).

**Q6.** A segmented machine uses 14-bit virtual addresses with the top 2 bits selecting among four segments. Segment 1 has base = `0x40000` and bounds = `0x800`. A process accesses virtual address `0x4040`. Give the segment number, offset, and physical address.

**Answer:** `0x4040` in 14 bits is `01 00 0000 0100 0000`. Top 2 bits `01` select segment 1. Offset is `0x040` (the bottom 12 bits). Since `0x040 < 0x800`, the access is in bounds. Physical address = `0x40000 + 0x040 = 0x40040`.

**Q7.** Recall `fork` from Chapter 3. Under base-and-bounds, what must the OS do to create a child process's memory? Under segmentation? Under what circumstances could the OS avoid copying entirely?

**Answer:**

* _Base-and-bounds:_ allocate a new contiguous physical range equal to the parent's bounds, copy the parent's entire address space byte-by-byte into it, and set the child's base/bounds to the new range.
* _Segmentation:_ allocate physical ranges for each segment (code, heap, stack) that the child needs private. The code segment, being read-only, can be _shared_ — the child's code-segment base points to the same physical range as the parent's.
* _Copy-avoidance:_ read-only segments can always be shared. For writable segments, OSes use _copy-on-write_ : both processes share physical pages until one writes, at which point the OS traps, copies the page, and updates the writer's mapping. Copy-on-write requires fine-grained protection, which base-and-bounds and segmentation handle only coarsely (per segment). Paging handles copy-on-write naturally (per page) — another reason modern systems use paging.

**Q8.** Consider a system running 20 processes, each with a code segment of 1 MB, a heap of varying size, and a stack of 64 KB. Assume 30% of processes run the same program. Compare the total physical memory required under (a) base-and-bounds with no sharing, (b) segmentation with code sharing. Use a heap size of 2 MB per process for estimation.

**Answer:**

* _(a) Base-and-bounds._ Each process occupies a contiguous physical range large enough for code + heap + stack = 1 MB + 2 MB + 64 KB ≈ 3.07 MB. No sharing possible. Total = 20 × 3.07 MB ≈ 61.4 MB.
