* _(b) Segmentation with code sharing._ 30% × 20 = 6 processes share one 1 MB code segment (one copy). The other 14 run distinct programs, one 1 MB code segment each = 14 MB. All 20 processes have private 2 MB heap + 64 KB stack = 20 × 2.06 MB ≈ 41.2 MB. Total = 1 + 14 + 41.2 ≈ 56.2 MB.

Segmentation saves roughly 5 MB in this example, and the relative savings grow as more processes share code (e.g., web browsers, shells, standard libraries).

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** External fragmentation is sometimes resolved by compaction: the OS pauses all processes, moves them to eliminate holes, and resumes. Under base-and-bounds, compaction requires only updating each process's base register. Why, under segmentation, is compaction still only register-updates (not a rewrite of pointers), even though a process now has several bases? And why, despite this, is compaction nonetheless harder under segmentation than under base-and-bounds?

**Answer:** _Why it is still register-updates:_ Every pointer in the program is a _virtual_ address, not a physical one. Translation happens in hardware on every access. If the OS moves a segment from physical range X to physical range Y, it need only update that segment's base register — all the pointers in the program still name the same virtual addresses and will now translate to the new physical location. The program is unaware of the move.

_Why it is still harder:_ Under base-and-bounds, each process has one contiguous range to move. The free-list model is "N processes, N ranges." Under segmentation, each process has K segments, each a separately-movable range. The free-list model becomes "N × K ranges." The allocator must juggle many more pieces, and the choices interact: moving process A's heap might free a hole that process B's stack could use, but moving process A's heap requires updating only A's heap-segment base while all other pointers into A's heap (stack locals, global pointers) go through that register and work unchanged. The _mechanics_ of compaction remain simple; the _scheduling_ of what to move where becomes combinatorially harder.

**Q10.** The chapter asserts that fixed-size allocations (pages, coming in Chapter 11) solve external fragmentation but cause internal fragmentation. Reason from first principles: why is fixed-size internal fragmentation _bounded_ while variable-size external fragmentation is _unbounded_? How does this trade-off shape modern memory design?

**Answer:** _Fixed-size internal fragmentation is bounded._ If pages are 4 KB, each process wastes at most 4 KB - 1 bytes per allocated region (the unused tail of the last page). If a process has R regions, waste is at most `R × (4 KB - 1)`. This is a constant factor of the number of regions, not the memory footprint. On a large machine with gigabytes of memory, the waste is rounding error.

_Variable-size external fragmentation is unbounded._ A classic result: under worst-case allocation patterns, external fragmentation can approach 50% of memory — half the machine's RAM lies in unusable holes. Even under reasonable workloads, fragmentation grows over time and can only be reclaimed by compaction. There is no a priori bound as a function of the number of processes or their sizes.

_How this shapes design:_ Modern systems universally use fixed-size pages (typically 4 KB, with 2 MB and 1 GB "huge pages" for special cases). The internal fragmentation is accepted as a small, predictable cost. The payoffs — constant-time allocation, no compaction, simple sharing, simple swapping — are decisive. Even specialized systems like real-time OSes, which care about worst-case latency more than average performance, overwhelmingly choose paging, because _any_ allocation mechanism that can fail due to fragmentation is incompatible with worst-case guarantees. The page-fault story continues with this insight in hand.

---

## Chapter 11. Introduction to Paging

### 11.1 From Variable Sizes to Fixed Sizes

At the end of Chapter 10, we were stuck. Base-and-bounds gave protection but wasted huge amounts of physical memory to the gap between heap and stack. Segmentation plugged the gap but made external fragmentation worse, because the OS now allocated several variable-sized chunks per process and the free list splintered over time.

What if we stopped using variable-sized chunks altogether?

Break the virtual address space into fixed-size pieces — **pages**. Break physical memory into same-size pieces — **page frames**. Map any virtual page to any physical frame, independently. The mapping lives in a per-process table called a **page table**.

That is the idea. It sounds almost naive. It is — and yet it solves every problem base-and-bounds and segmentation couldn't. External fragmentation of physical memory vanishes, because every allocation is the same size. Internal fragmentation is bounded by at most one page per region. Sharing, copy-on-write, swapping, and protection all fall out cleanly.

The cost is real: the page table itself takes memory, and address translation now requires an extra lookup on every memory reference. Chapter 12 will show how TLBs make translation fast. Chapter 13 will show how multi-level tables keep the table itself small. For now, we focus on the mechanism.

Plan B continues: our virtual address `0x55f3a1b4a2a0` from `*x = 42` is now a page-structured address. Let us see what that means.

### 11.2 Pages and Frames

#### Example: A tiny machine

Consider a machine with:

* 64 bytes of virtual address space per process (6-bit virtual addresses)
* 128 bytes of physical memory (7-bit physical addresses)
* 16-byte pages

The virtual address space breaks into 64 / 16 = **4 virtual pages** : VPN 0, 1, 2, 3. Physical memory breaks into 128 / 16 = **8 physical frames** : PFN 0, 1, 2, ..., 7.

Suppose the OS maps:

* VPN 0 → PFN 3
* VPN 1 → PFN 7
* VPN 2 → PFN 5
* VPN 3 → PFN 2

The process's view is a flat 64-byte address space. Under the hood, its memory is scattered across four separated 16-byte frames in physical memory.

#### Pattern

Each virtual address decomposes into two parts:
    
    
     6-bit virtual address
    ┌──┬──┬────────────┐
    │VPN │   offset    │
    └────┴─────────────┘
      2 bits  4 bits
    

* The top bits select the **virtual page number** (VPN).
* The low bits are the **offset within the page**.

Translation replaces the VPN with the corresponding PFN, leaving the offset unchanged. Physical address = (PFN × page size) + offset.

#### Definition: Paging

**Definition:** _Paging_ is a memory translation scheme that divides the virtual address space into fixed-size _pages_ and physical memory into same-size _page frames_. Each process has a _page table_ — a mapping from virtual page number to physical frame number. Translation replaces the VPN with the PFN and passes the offset through unchanged.

#### Example: A translation

Our process issues a load from virtual address `0x15` (binary `010101`). Splitting: VPN = `01` (decimal 1), offset = `0101` (decimal 5).

Look up VPN 1 in the page table: PFN 7. Physical address = 7 × 16 + 5 = `117` (binary `1110101`). The hardware fetches from physical byte 117.

The program said `0x15`. The DRAM chip got `0x75`. The translation is invisible.

### 11.3 The Page Table

Each process has its own page table. Logically it is an array indexed by VPN, returning the PFN and a handful of flags.

#### Example: A page table entry (PTE)

A typical 32-bit PTE might contain:
    
    
    ┌──────────────────────────┬──┬──┬──┬──┬──┬──┬──┬──┐
    │      PFN (20 bits)       │P │W │U │A │D │PS│...│V │
    └──────────────────────────┴──┴──┴──┴──┴──┴──┴──┴──┘
    

Some typical flags:

* **V (valid):** is this mapping usable? If V = 0, any access to this VPN traps.
* **W (write):** is write allowed? If W = 0, any write traps.
* **U (user):** is user-mode access allowed? If U = 0, the kernel can access but user code cannot.
* **A (accessed):** set by the hardware whenever the page is read; used by replacement policies.
* **D (dirty):** set by the hardware whenever the page is written; tells the OS whether the page must be written back to disk before eviction.
* **P (present):** is the page resident in physical memory or swapped out? (We will meet this flag again in Chapter 14.)

The flags turn the page table into a protection mechanism as well as a translation mechanism. Goal 3 of Chapter 9 — protection — is enforced at the _page_ granularity, which is much finer than the segment granularity of Chapter 10.

#### Pattern

Every memory access is gated by a PTE lookup that produces _both_ a physical address and a permission check. If the check fails, the hardware traps to the OS. The OS decides whether the trap is a real error (kill the process) or an opportunity (allocate a physical frame, set V = 1, return) — as we will see in Chapter 14.

#### Common Misconception

_"The page table is stored in the MMU."_

No. On a 32-bit system with 4 KB pages, a linear page table has `2^20 = 1,048,576` entries. At 4 bytes each, that's 4 MB _per process_. There is no way to store 4 MB in the MMU. The page table lives in DRAM. A special register — often called **CR3** on x86, the **page table base register (PTBR)** — holds the _physical address_ of the page table in memory. Translation follows that pointer into DRAM to find the PTE for the current access.

This means every memory access the program makes is preceded by a DRAM access to read the PTE. Without further tricks, paging would double memory traffic. Chapter 12 (the TLB) eliminates almost all of that overhead.

### 11.4 How Translation Works

Step-by-step, on every memory reference:

  1. **Extract VPN and offset.** Split the virtual address using the page size.
  2. **Find the PTE.** PTE address = PTBR + (VPN × PTE size).
  3. **Read the PTE from memory.** Check flags. If V = 0 or permissions fail, trap.
  4. **Extract PFN.** Concatenate PFN with offset to get physical address.
  5. **Issue the memory access** to physical address.

Steps 2-3 are where paging differs from base-and-bounds: the translation requires a memory access of its own. Every program access becomes _two_ memory accesses in the underlying hardware.

#### Example: Trace a virtual read

Machine: 16-bit virtual addresses, 16-bit physical addresses, 1 KB pages (10-bit offset, 6-bit VPN → 64 virtual pages). Program issues `load r1, [0x2048]`.

* VPN = `0x2048 >> 10` = `0x08`.
* Offset = `0x2048 & 0x3FF` = `0x048`.
* PTE for VPN `0x08` lives at PTBR + 8 × (PTE size). Say PTBR = `0x4000` and PTE size = 4 bytes. PTE address = `0x4020`.
* Hardware reads 4 bytes from `0x4020`. PTE says V = 1, W = 0, PFN = `0x2A`.
* The instruction is a load (read), not a write. W = 0 is fine. Permission check passes.
* Physical address = (`0x2A` << 10) | `0x048` = `0xA848`.
* Hardware fetches from physical `0xA848` and places the result in `r1`.

This took two DRAM trips: one for the PTE, one for the data.

### 11.5 Page Size Matters

Why pick 4 KB? Why not 64 bytes? Why not 4 MB?

#### Smaller pages

* _Pros._ Less internal fragmentation. A process that uses 5 KB of memory has at most 4 KB of waste with 4 KB pages, but would have almost zero waste with 64-byte pages.
* _Cons._ More entries in the page table. 4 GB of virtual memory at 64-byte pages = 64 million entries, versus 1 million at 4 KB. The table itself starts to eat memory.

#### Larger pages

* _Pros._ Fewer entries in the page table. 4 GB at 4 MB pages = 1024 entries. The table is tiny.
* _Cons._ More internal fragmentation. A process using 5 KB of memory wastes the rest of a 4 MB page.

4 KB emerged as a reasonable compromise in the 1970s and stuck. Modern systems additionally support _huge pages_ (2 MB, 1 GB on x86-64) for workloads that benefit: databases, virtual machines, scientific computing. The same hardware can use both sizes simultaneously, selecting size per mapping.

#### Common Misconception

_"A smaller page size is always more efficient because it wastes less memory."_

Only if you look at internal fragmentation alone. Smaller pages also mean more PTEs, more TLB entries (Chapter 12), more table walks on TLB misses, and more bookkeeping when swapping. For typical workloads, 4 KB comes out ahead of both extremes. The efficiency of a memory system depends on the balance of many factors, not any single one.

### 11.6 What Paging Buys Us

With paging we get all three goals of Chapter 9:

* **Transparency.** The process sees a contiguous address space. The hardware hides the scatter.
* **Efficiency.** (With TLBs — Chapter 12 — which are essential. Without them, paging would double memory traffic.)
* **Protection.** Per-page permission bits. Each page can be read-only or read-write, kernel-only or user-accessible, executable or not. The process cannot forge a PTE, because PTEs live in the kernel's memory and are referenced through the PTBR, which only the kernel can set.

We also get capabilities that base-and-bounds and segmentation could not offer cleanly:

* **Fine-grained sharing.** Map two processes' VPNs to the same PFN, and they share that page. Shared libraries, shared memory regions, copy-on-write forks — all built from this primitive.
* **Sparse address spaces.** A 64-bit process uses a few gigabytes of actual memory scattered across 256 TB of virtual address space. Without pages, the gaps would be impossible to represent efficiently. With pages, unused regions simply have no PTE (or a PTE with V = 0), and cost nothing.
* **Swapping.** Set V = 0 on a page, evict its frame to disk, and reclaim the frame. Later, when the process accesses the page, a fault tells the OS to read it back. The process is unaware. (Chapter 14.)
* **Lazy allocation.** `malloc` returns a virtual address. The OS allocates no physical frame until the first write to that page faults. (Chapter 14.)

#### Pattern

The crucial abstraction step is that paging _decouples the size of the virtual address space from the amount of physical memory_. A process can have a 64 TB virtual address space on a machine with 16 GB of RAM, because the mapping is partial — most pages have no mapping, and physical memory is consumed only by pages that are actually used.

Base-and-bounds and segmentation coupled these sizes. A process could not have more virtual memory than physical memory assigned to it, because the two were the same thing.

### 11.7 The Costs

Paging's costs are mostly absorbed by future chapters, but worth naming now:

  1. **Table space.** A single-level linear page table for a 32-bit address space with 4 KB pages is 4 MB per process. On a machine with 100 processes, that's 400 MB just for page tables. Chapter 13 introduces multi-level tables, which cut this to kilobytes per process.
  2. **Translation latency.** Every access is a PTE lookup plus the data access. Without caching, this doubles memory traffic. Chapter 12 (TLBs) caches PTEs and brings the common case down to near zero overhead.
  3. **OS bookkeeping.** The OS maintains per-process page tables, a free list of physical frames, a data structure mapping physical frames back to owners (for eviction), and code to handle page faults. Chapter 14 digs into this.

All three costs are manageable. None is zero. Every choice in the page-table design trades some of these off against the others.

### 11.8 Chapter Summary

* _Paging_ divides the address space into fixed-size pages and physical memory into same-size frames. A per-process _page table_ maps VPN → PFN.
* A _page table entry (PTE)_ holds the PFN along with protection and status bits: valid, writable, user, accessed, dirty, present.
* Translation splits a virtual address into VPN and offset, looks up the PTE (in DRAM, via the page table base register), checks permissions, replaces VPN with PFN, and passes offset through unchanged.
* Paging solves external fragmentation (all allocations are the same size) and enables fine-grained sharing, sparse address spaces, and lazy allocation.
* Naive paging costs table space and doubles memory traffic. Chapters 12 and 13 reduce both costs to near-zero in the common case.

Chapter 12 introduces the **translation lookaside buffer (TLB)** : a small, fast, fully-associative cache of recent PTEs inside the MMU. The TLB is not an optimization — without it, paging is too slow to be usable. The page fault story continues with a focus on _how translation gets fast._

### 11.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Define virtual page number (VPN), physical frame number (PFN), and page offset. How are they related during translation?

**Answer:** A _virtual page number_ is the upper bits of a virtual address that name which page the access falls into. A _physical frame number_ is the upper bits of a physical address naming which frame in physical memory. The _page offset_ is the lower bits, identifying a byte within a page. Translation replaces the VPN with the PFN (found in the page table) and keeps the offset unchanged. Physical address = PFN × page_size + offset.

**Q2.** What is a page table? Where is it stored, and how does the hardware find it?

**Answer:** A page table is a per-process array that maps VPN → (PFN + flags). It is stored in physical memory. The hardware finds it through the _page table base register_ (PTBR, called CR3 on x86), which holds the physical address of the currently running process's page table. On a context switch, the OS changes the PTBR to point to the new process's page table.

**Q3.** List three flags commonly found in a PTE and what each one means.

**Answer:**

* _Valid (V):_ whether this mapping is usable. If V = 0, any access to this VPN causes a trap.
* _Writable (W):_ whether writes are permitted. A W = 0 page is read-only; writes trap.
* _User (U):_ whether user-mode code can access this page. Kernel pages have U = 0; attempted user access traps. Other common flags include dirty, accessed, and present.

**Q4.** Why does paging eliminate the external fragmentation problem of segmentation?

**Answer:** Every allocation is the same size — one page frame. The free list of physical frames is homogeneous: a bitmap or simple stack suffices, and any free frame fits any request. There are no holes of the wrong size, because the allocator does not deal in variable sizes at all.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** A system uses 16-bit virtual addresses and 256-byte pages. How many virtual pages are there? How many offset bits does a virtual address contain?

**Answer:** Offset bits = log₂(256) = 8. VPN bits = 16 − 8 = 8. Number of virtual pages = 2⁸ = 256.

**Q6.** On the same system, physical memory is 32 KB. How many physical frames are there? How many bits in a PFN?

**Answer:** Frames = 32 KB / 256 B = 128. PFN bits = log₂(128) = 7. A physical address is PFN (7 bits) + offset (8 bits) = 15 bits, matching 32 KB.

**Q7.** Given the following tiny page table (VPN → PFN, with all valid):
    
    
    VPN 0 → PFN 4
    VPN 1 → PFN 2
    VPN 2 → PFN 7
    VPN 3 → PFN 1
    

and a 16-byte page size (4-bit offset, 2-bit VPN, 6-bit virtual addresses), translate the virtual addresses `0x00`, `0x1A`, `0x23`, `0x3F`.

**Answer:**

* `0x00` = `00 0000`: VPN 0, offset 0. PFN 4, physical = 4 × 16 + 0 = `64` (`0x40`).
* `0x1A` = `01 1010`: VPN 1, offset 10. PFN 2, physical = 2 × 16 + 10 = `42` (`0x2A`).
* `0x23` = `10 0011`: VPN 2, offset 3. PFN 7, physical = 7 × 16 + 3 = `115` (`0x73`).
* `0x3F` = `11 1111`: VPN 3, offset 15. PFN 1, physical = 1 × 16 + 15 = `31` (`0x1F`).

**Q8.** In Chapter 3, `fork` created a child process. Under segmentation (Chapter 10), forks required copying segments. Under paging, the OS can implement `fork` with _copy-on-write_ : share all pages read-only, and copy a page only when one of the two processes writes it. Describe how the W and V flags, plus a fault handler, implement this.

**Answer:** On `fork`, the OS creates a new page table for the child and copies all PTEs from the parent — but marks every PTE in _both_ tables with W = 0 (even though the underlying pages were originally writable). Physical frames are now shared between parent and child. Reads work normally in both processes.

When either process writes, the hardware checks W = 0 and traps to the OS. The OS recognizes this as a copy-on-write trap (not a permission error), allocates a new physical frame, copies the source frame's contents into it, updates the faulting process's PTE to point at the new frame with W = 1, and resumes the process. The other process still points at the original frame, also now with W = 1 (since it is no longer shared). Subsequent writes by either process proceed at full speed. Unmodified pages remain shared forever — which is the common case for program code and most read-only data. This makes `fork` cheap even for large processes.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** Paging doubles memory traffic by inserting a PTE read before each data access. Why is this not catastrophic in practice, given that it would be in theory? (Anticipate the TLB of Chapter 12 without requiring its details.)

**Answer:** Because programs exhibit **locality** : they access a small number of pages repeatedly in a short time window. If a small hardware cache holds the most recently used PTEs — the TLB — then the _second_ access to a given page finds the translation already cached and incurs no DRAM overhead. Measured hit rates on real workloads are typically 99%+ for a TLB of a few hundred entries. The amortized cost of translation is therefore a small fraction of 1% of a memory access, not 100%.

The deeper point: paging works because translation is highly repetitive on the timescale of execution. The _same_ VPN is translated billions of times per second. Caching the translation is not a minor optimization — it is what makes the abstraction viable. Without the TLB, paging would indeed double memory traffic and halve program speed; with the TLB, the overhead vanishes into noise.

**Q10.** Chapter 10 argued that internal fragmentation under fixed-size allocation is _bounded_. Give a precise bound for paging with page size `P` bytes and `R` distinct memory regions per process. Then argue why this bound is acceptable on modern machines with multi-gigabyte RAM, even though it grows with region count.

**Answer:** _The bound._ Each region wastes at most the unused tail of its last page. That is at most `P − 1` bytes. With `R` regions per process, total waste per process is at most `R × (P − 1)`. With `N` processes, system-wide waste is at most `N × R × (P − 1)` bytes.

_Why it is acceptable._ Take realistic numbers: `P = 4096`, `R ≈ 10` (code, data, bss, heap, stack, a few mmapped regions), `N = 1000` processes (a busy server). Upper bound on waste = 1000 × 10 × 4095 ≈ 40 MB. On a machine with 32 GB of RAM, that's 0.1%.

The bound grows with region count, but region count is itself bounded by program complexity, not by how much memory the program uses. A 10 GB process has the same `R` as a 10 MB process. Fragmentation is therefore _independent of working set size_ , which is the essential property — unlike external fragmentation, which scales with the number and variability of live allocations.

This is the central reason modern systems adopt fixed-size pages despite the internal waste. Predictable, small, constant-factor overhead is compatible with every scale of memory; unpredictable, workload-dependent external fragmentation is not.

---

## Chapter 12. Faster Paging with TLBs

### 12.1 Paging Is Too Slow

Chapter 11 left us with a working address translation scheme. Every virtual access becomes a PTE lookup in DRAM followed by the actual data access. Two memory trips per reference. In the absence of any optimization, paging _doubles_ memory traffic.

Modern CPUs issue roughly one memory reference per nanosecond on average. A DRAM access costs 50-100 nanoseconds. A program doing 10⁹ memory references per second would stall an extra 50-100 seconds for translation alone — in each second of wall time. The arithmetic is impossible; paging, as described, cannot coexist with fast execution.

The solution has been sitting in hardware since the late 1960s: cache the results of translation. A small hardware cache — the **translation lookaside buffer (TLB)** — holds recently used VPN → PFN mappings inside the MMU, so the common case avoids the PTE lookup entirely.

Plan B continues. Our memory reference `*x = 42` issues a virtual address — and from here on, the reference can follow one of two paths.

* **Fast path (TLB hit):** translation completes in one cycle, the data access proceeds, the whole reference costs ~1 cycle plus the DRAM access. This is the common case on workloads with locality.
* **Slow path (TLB miss):** the MMU walks the page table in memory, installs the translation, and retries. The walk cost dominates; on x86-64 it is several hundred cycles.

This chapter builds the fast path and characterizes when it applies. Chapter 13 refines the page-table layout that the miss path must walk; Chapter 14 handles the sub-case where the target page is not even present in memory (page fault). Read Ch.12-14 together and the full story of `*x = 42` — hit, miss with walk, fault with disk read — becomes one tree, not three disconnected scenarios.

### 12.2 What a TLB Is

#### Example: A simple TLB

Think of a TLB as a tiny, fast, fully-associative table inside the CPU's MMU. A typical configuration:

* 64 to 1024 entries
* Fully associative (any entry can hold any VPN → PFN mapping)
* Access time: 1 CPU cycle (compared to ~100 cycles for a DRAM PTE read)

Each TLB entry holds:
    
    
    ┌──────┬──────┬───────────────────────┐
    │ VPN  │ PFN  │ flags (V, W, U, ...)  │
    └──────┴──────┴───────────────────────┘
    

#### Pattern

On a memory access, the MMU first looks up the VPN in the TLB:

* **Hit:** the TLB returns the PFN and flags in 1 cycle. Hardware checks permissions, forms the physical address, and issues the data access. No DRAM PTE read.
* **Miss:** the MMU (or, on some architectures, the OS) walks the page table in DRAM, finds the PTE, installs it in the TLB (evicting some older entry), and retries the access. Extra cost: one DRAM read.

#### Definition: Translation lookaside buffer (TLB)

**Definition:** A _translation lookaside buffer_ is a small, fast, fully-associative hardware cache inside the MMU that stores recently used virtual-to-physical address translations. On a memory access, the hardware looks up the VPN in the TLB; a hit yields the translation immediately, and a miss requires consulting the page table in DRAM to populate the TLB.

#### Why fully associative

If the TLB were direct-mapped (each VPN indexes a fixed slot), two hot VPNs that mapped to the same slot would keep evicting each other — a conflict miss storm. Full associativity means any VPN can live in any slot, so the replacement policy decides eviction based on recency or frequency rather than address arithmetic. TLBs stay small (hundreds of entries) partly because fully associative hardware is expensive to build larger.

### 12.3 Why the TLB Works: Locality

The TLB is not magic. It works because programs exhibit **locality** : they reuse the same addresses, and nearby addresses, in short time windows.

#### Example: A loop over an array

Consider:
    
    
    int sum = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < 1000; i++) {
        sum += a[i];
    }
    

Suppose `a` is an array of 1000 integers (4000 bytes), stored contiguously starting at virtual address `0x4000`. Page size: 4096 bytes. The array occupies pages at VPN = `0x4` and possibly VPN = `0x5`.

First iteration, `a[0]` at VPN `0x4`: TLB miss, page table walk, install VPN `0x4`. Second iteration, `a[1]` at VPN `0x4`: TLB hit. ... 1021 more iterations, all TLB hits on VPN `0x4` (1024 integers fit in a 4 KB page). If `a` crosses into VPN `0x5` at some point, one more miss, then hits all the way.

Out of 1000 memory references, perhaps 2 were TLB misses. Hit rate: 99.8%. This is **spatial locality** : consecutive array accesses use the same page.

#### Example: A repeated function call

A program calls `strcmp()` a million times. The code of `strcmp` is on, say, one page. The stack frame where `strcmp`'s locals live is on another. That's two PTEs consulted for the function's duration. If the TLB holds both across calls (it usually does — they are hot entries, unlikely to be evicted), every call after the first is pure TLB hits. This is **temporal locality** : the same pages are accessed again and again.

#### Pattern

Workloads differ, but the vast majority of programs are dominated by a small number of hot pages. A TLB with a few hundred entries captures essentially all of them. Hit rates of 99%+ are routine in production workloads; even 99.9% is common for tight loops.

#### Common Misconception

_"A bigger TLB always gives better performance."_

TLB size has diminishing returns. Going from 64 to 256 entries often cuts miss rate by 90%. Going from 256 to 1024 might cut it another 50%. Going from 1024 to 4096 might be barely measurable. Meanwhile, the TLB is on the critical path of every memory access — making it bigger slows the hit path. Real designs aim for the knee of the curve, not the asymptote.

### 12.4 The Cost of a Miss

When the TLB misses, the MMU must find the PTE. Architectures differ in who does this:

**Hardware-managed TLB (x86, ARM):** a dedicated hardware unit called the **page table walker** reads the PTE from DRAM, installs it in the TLB, and retries the access. The OS never sees a miss in the common case. The walker knows how to traverse the architecture's specific page table format.

**Software-managed TLB (MIPS, SPARC):** on a miss, the hardware traps to the OS. The OS walks the page table (any format it chooses) and issues a special TLB-fill instruction. Slower per miss, but the OS has full flexibility over page table design.

Hardware-managed TLBs dominate modern general-purpose CPUs because the walker is faster than a trap handler and the architectural page table format is rarely the bottleneck.

#### Example: Walk cost

On x86-64 with 4-level page tables, a TLB miss requires _four_ DRAM reads: one per page table level. That's roughly 400 cycles. The actual data access adds another ~100. A miss costs about 500 cycles.

A hit costs 1 cycle. A miss costs 500. So a 1% miss rate makes the average translation cost `0.99 × 1 + 0.01 × 500 = 5.99 cycles`. The TLB shifts the amortized overhead from "doubles memory traffic" to "6× an uncached access, 1× with TLB hit."

At 99% hit rate, amortized overhead is about 5 cycles per access — noticeable but bearable. At 99.9% hit rate, it is about 1.5 cycles. At 99.99% hit rate (tight loop), under 1 cycle.

#### Pattern

TLB misses are rare but expensive. A workload with poor locality — random-access hash tables over large datasets, traversing a big graph — can see hit rates drop to 80%, at which point amortized overhead balloons. Database and VM engineers spend real effort keeping TLB hit rates high: huge pages (2 MB instead of 4 KB, so each TLB entry covers 512× more memory), careful data layout, prefetching.

### 12.5 Context Switches and the TLB

A TLB entry says "for the current process, VPN 0x4 maps to PFN 0x7C." When the OS context-switches to a different process, the entries are _wrong_ for the new process — its VPN 0x4 maps to a different frame.

The classic solution: **flush the TLB on every context switch.** Invalidate every entry. The new process starts with a cold TLB. Simple, correct, slow.

#### Example: The flush cost

Each flush discards hundreds of entries. The new process will TLB-miss on its first few hundred memory references, costing potentially tens of thousands of cycles. If context switches happen every 10 ms and each flush costs 50 µs of amortized miss recovery, that's 0.5% overhead from flushes alone — tolerable, but not free.

#### The fix: Address space identifiers

Modern CPUs tag each TLB entry with an **address space identifier (ASID)**. The TLB stores (ASID, VPN) → PFN. Lookups match both the ASID (set in a per-process register by the OS) and the VPN. On context switch, the OS simply changes the current ASID; the TLB need not be flushed. Entries for the old process remain, ignored by lookups for the new process, and available for reuse the next time the old process runs.

#### Pattern

ASIDs convert TLB management from "flush on switch" to "let cold entries age out naturally." Processes that context-switch frequently share the TLB capacity but do not force each other into cold-start misses. This is essential on machines with many active processes or frequent kernel/user boundary crossings.

#### Common Misconception

_"With ASIDs, the TLB never needs flushing."_

The TLB still needs flushing when:

* a page table entry is changed (the OS must invalidate that VPN in the TLB, on all cores that might have cached it)
* the whole page table is replaced (the OS may invalidate all entries for that ASID)
* the hardware changes modes in a way that invalidates translations

Most of these invalidations are _selective_ : invalidate a single VPN, not the whole TLB. Instructions like x86's `invlpg` do this.

### 12.6 Shootdowns

On a multicore CPU, every core has its own TLB. If the OS changes a PTE (say, invalidates a mapping because a page was swapped out), every core that has a cached copy of that translation must be told to drop it.

This is called a **TLB shootdown**. The procedure:

  1. The OS running on core A modifies the PTE.
  2. The OS sends an inter-processor interrupt (IPI) to every other core running processes that share this page table.
  3. Each receiving core invalidates the affected VPN in its TLB.
  4. Each receiving core acknowledges.
  5. Core A waits for all acknowledgments, then proceeds.

#### Example: A busy server

On a 64-core machine, a single TLB shootdown may need to interrupt and wait for up to 63 other cores. On microsecond-scale IPIs, this can cost tens of microseconds — an eternity compared to a 1-nanosecond TLB hit. High-frequency shootdowns are a documented performance pathology in heavily parallel workloads.

#### Pattern

Shootdowns are the cost of cache coherence extended to TLBs. CPUs maintain cache coherence for data in hardware; TLB coherence is mostly managed by software (the OS), because the OS understands process and page-table semantics the hardware does not. This is one of the places where _OS-level coordination leaks out of the abstraction and matters for performance._

### 12.7 Programming for the TLB

Most programmers never think about the TLB. Occasionally, one must:

* **Huge pages.** On Linux, `mmap` with `MAP_HUGETLB` or `madvise(MADV_HUGEPAGE)` uses 2 MB pages. One TLB entry now covers 2 MB instead of 4 KB — a 512× reach increase. Useful for databases, VMs, HPC.
* **Data layout.** Keep hot working set inside a small number of pages. Structure-of-arrays versus array-of-structures decisions affect TLB reach as well as cache behavior.
* **Batching syscalls.** Every kernel entry can disturb the TLB (or force flushes of partial entries). Programs that call syscalls millions of times per second can see measurable overhead.

#### Example: The `perf stat -e dTLB-load-misses` number

Run `perf stat ./myprogram` on Linux and look at `dTLB-load-misses`. If it is a small fraction of `instructions`, the TLB is fine. If it is 1% or more, the TLB is hurting you. The fix may be a data-layout change (pack hot data together) or a huge-page allocation.

#### Pattern

The TLB is a hardware component with visible performance consequences. It is not part of an OS course in the sense of "you, the OS author, will build this" — you will not. But the OS's page-table design, fork semantics, and huge-page policies affect TLB effectiveness profoundly.

### 12.8 Chapter Summary

* Paging without TLBs doubles memory traffic — unusable.
* A **TLB** is a small, fast, fully-associative hardware cache of recent translations. Hits cost ~1 cycle; misses cost a page-table walk — on x86-64 with a 4-level table, typically ~400 cycles for the walk plus ~100 cycles for the data access that triggered the miss, for a total of roughly 500 cycles.
* Programs hit the TLB at >99% rates because of **locality** — the same pages are accessed repeatedly over short time windows.
* On a context switch, the classical approach is to flush the TLB. Modern hardware tags entries with **ASIDs** so entries survive context switches.
* On multicore machines, PTE changes require **TLB shootdowns** : IPIs to every other core that may have cached the entry.
* Workload choices (huge pages, data layout, syscall frequency) can improve TLB hit rate measurably for TLB-intensive programs.

In Chapter 13, we return to the page table itself. A flat linear page table is 4 MB per process on a 32-bit machine and 512 GB on a 64-bit machine — absurd. Multi-level page tables make the table itself sparse, so unused virtual address space costs nothing. The page fault story continues.

### 12.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What does a TLB cache, and why does caching it help?

**Answer:** The TLB caches recent VPN → PFN mappings (with permission flags). Caching helps because without it, every memory access would require reading a PTE from DRAM before issuing the data access, roughly doubling memory traffic. With the TLB, repeated accesses to the same pages — the common case, due to locality — skip the PTE read and translate in 1 cycle.

**Q2.** Explain spatial and temporal locality, and give an example of each that benefits the TLB.

**Answer:**

* _Spatial locality:_ addresses near a recently accessed address are likely to be accessed soon. Example: iterating a packed array — consecutive elements share a page, so only the first access of each page misses the TLB.
* _Temporal locality:_ a recently accessed address is likely to be accessed again soon. Example: a loop calling the same function in a tight inner loop — the code and stack pages are accessed every iteration, so after the first iteration those pages' translations stay hot in the TLB.

**Q3.** What is an ASID, and what problem does it solve?

**Answer:** An _address space identifier_ is a tag attached to each TLB entry that names which process's translation the entry belongs to. Without ASIDs, the OS must flush the TLB on every context switch, because entries belonging to the old process are incorrect for the new process. With ASIDs, entries remain in the TLB across context switches — lookups match only the current process's ASID — so a process that context-switches out and back in may find its entries still cached.

**Q4.** What is a TLB shootdown, and when is it needed?

**Answer:** A _shootdown_ is a coordinated invalidation of a TLB entry (or entries) across multiple cores. It is needed when the OS modifies a PTE — for example, when it unmaps a page or changes permissions — because other cores may have cached the old translation and would otherwise continue to use the stale value. The OS issues inter-processor interrupts (IPIs) to the relevant cores, each core invalidates the affected VPN, and the originating core waits for acknowledgment before proceeding.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** A workload has a TLB hit rate of 99%. A hit costs 1 cycle and a miss costs 500 cycles (the 4-level page-table walk is ~400 cycles and the retried data access adds ~100, matching x86-64 in Section 12.3). What is the average translation cost per access?

**Answer:** 0.99 × 1 + 0.01 × 500 = 0.99 + 5.00 = **5.99 cycles** per memory access, on average. Compared to 1 cycle on a hit, the TLB adds about 5 cycles of amortized overhead — small but not trivial. At a 99.9% hit rate the overhead shrinks to about 1.5 cycles; at 99% it is 5.99; at 90% it would be 50.9 — a 50× tax on translation, which is why real systems work hard to keep hit rates above 99% (huge pages, careful layout, locality-friendly data structures).

**Q6.** A program has a TLB of 256 entries, 4 KB pages, and iterates through a 10 MB array in sequence. Estimate the miss rate and explain.

**Answer:** The array spans 10 MB / 4 KB = 2560 pages. The TLB holds 256 entries, so only the most recently used 256 pages fit. As the scan progresses from page 0 to 2559, old entries are evicted; by the time the scan wraps or the program iterates the array again, none of the needed entries remain.

In a _single sequential pass_ : the TLB misses on the first access of each page and hits on the next 1023 accesses within the same page. Miss rate ≈ 1 in 1024 ≈ 0.1% — excellent. But if the program iterates the array many times in a loop, each outer iteration replays the same pattern: the TLB is cold at the start of each pass because the earlier pages were evicted. Still ≈ 0.1% miss rate, because misses come only from crossing page boundaries regardless of pass count.

If the program instead accessed a _random_ 10 MB array — say, traversing a linked list scattered through it — miss rate could reach 100% (every access a different page, each one a miss). Huge pages would change this dramatically: with 2 MB pages, the same 10 MB fits in 5 pages, all TLB-resident.

**Q7.** A system running on a 16-core CPU. A process is running on all 16 cores (16 threads in one address space). The OS needs to unmap one page. Describe the sequence of events, and estimate the cost if each IPI takes 1 µs.

**Answer:**

  1. The OS (on some core A) modifies the PTE to V = 0.
  2. The OS sends an IPI to each of the other 15 cores.
  3. Each receiving core interrupts its current instruction, enters the handler, executes an `invlpg`-style instruction for the affected VPN, and sends an acknowledgment back.
  4. Core A waits for all 15 acknowledgments.
  5. Core A's own TLB is also invalidated (locally).
  6. Core A completes the unmap operation.

Cost: roughly 15 × 1 µs for the IPI round trips, plus the cost of 15 handlers running briefly. Total: 15-30 µs of wall time, during which 15 cores are performing shootdown work instead of running the program. In a workload that unmaps pages frequently (e.g., a memory-intensive application with lots of `munmap`), this can add up to measurable overhead.

**Q8.** Consider a `fork` implementation using copy-on-write (see Chapter 11 exercise). After `fork`, the OS marks all pages read-only in _both_ parent and child page tables. Does this require a TLB shootdown on multicore? Why or why not?

**Answer:** Yes — for every core currently running threads of the parent process (if the parent is multithreaded), the OS must shoot down the old (writable) TLB entries. If a parent thread on another core has cached a writable translation and issues a write before the shootdown completes, it will bypass the newly-installed read-only PTE and write directly to the page — violating the copy-on-write semantics.

For a single-threaded parent that only runs on the core doing the `fork`, a local TLB flush suffices; no IPIs are needed. For heavily multithreaded programs, `fork` becomes an expensive operation on multicore precisely because it forces a shootdown across every core running the program. This is one of the reasons high-performance servers prefer `posix_spawn`, `vfork`, or explicit thread pools to `fork`.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** The TLB is a cache, and like all caches it depends on locality for performance. Chapter 5 (scheduling) discussed cache affinity for CPU caches — the idea that moving a process to a different core loses the benefit of the old core's warm cache. Does TLB affinity work the same way? Describe how OS scheduling decisions can affect TLB hit rate in both directions.

**Answer:** Yes, with nuances. Each core has its own TLB. When a process migrates to a different core, its TLB on the original core is effectively lost to the process (though still present physically — it may even be valid if ASIDs are used, but the process is no longer on that core). The new core starts with a cold TLB for the migrating process, and must fault its hot pages back in.

So _moving_ a process loses TLB warmth — same as CPU caches. _Keeping_ a process on one core (affinity) preserves TLB state across context switches (via ASIDs), even when other processes share the core. _Many-to-many scheduling_ (any process can run on any core) tends to cause cold-TLB startup costs for every scheduling decision.

The OS balances this against load imbalance: a process pinned to a busy core may wait for CPU time while idle cores sit unused. CFS and most modern schedulers prefer to migrate only when load imbalance is substantial, and prefer to migrate to a _nearby_ core (same socket, same NUMA node) where physical memory is closer. The TLB is one of several reasons to prefer affinity; cache state and NUMA locality amplify it.

**Q10.** TLB hit rate is not a property of the CPU alone — it depends on workload and OS choices. Name three distinct levers by which the OS can influence a workload's TLB hit rate, and describe the tradeoff of each.

**Answer:**

_Lever 1: Huge pages._ The OS can back a mapping with 2 MB or 1 GB pages instead of 4 KB. A single TLB entry then covers much more memory, dramatically extending _TLB reach_ (the working-set size that fits in the TLB). _Tradeoff:_ huge pages cause more internal fragmentation (a 2 MB mapping uses 2 MB even if only 5 KB is needed), reduce sharing granularity, and complicate eviction/swapping. Some workloads (databases, VMs) win hugely; others waste memory.

_Lever 2: Process/thread placement._ The OS can place threads of one process on the same core (maximizing TLB hits across context switches of those threads) or on cores near their memory's NUMA node (reducing TLB miss _cost_ , because page-table walks hit local DRAM instead of remote). _Tradeoff:_ this reduces load-balancing flexibility. A process pinned to one socket may underuse the machine.

_Lever 3: Copy-on-write and demand paging._ The OS can lazily allocate pages on first access (demand paging) or share pages across processes until written (copy-on-write). Both reduce working set size for the common case — fewer live pages means fewer translations that must fit in the TLB. _Tradeoff:_ the first access to each page suffers a page fault (an expensive trap to the OS, plus a TLB miss when the newly-installed PTE is finally used). For short-lived processes, the faults may outweigh the savings.

The deeper point: TLB performance is a _systems_ concern, not merely a hardware one. The OS makes dozens of decisions every second — page placement, thread placement, fault handling, allocation granularity — and each one affects TLB effectiveness. A course on operating systems that did not connect hardware caches to OS-level decisions would miss the essential nature of the discipline: _the OS is the software half of the hardware-software contract that makes computers fast._

---

## Chapter 13. Multi-Level Page Tables

### 13.1 The Page Table Itself Is Too Big

Chapter 12 made translation fast. But the problem we skipped past at the end of Chapter 11 remains: **the page table itself is huge.**

On a 32-bit machine with 4 KB pages, a linear page table has 2²⁰ entries. At 4 bytes per entry, that is 4 MB per process. On a 64-bit machine, things get absurd. With 4 KB pages, a linear table would have 2⁵² entries; at 8 bytes each, the table is **32 petabytes per process.** The universe does not contain enough DRAM.

So we cannot use a linear table on 64-bit systems. We cannot afford one even on 32-bit systems with hundreds of processes (hundreds of processes × 4 MB = gigabytes of pure bookkeeping). Something has to give.

The insight: **most of the address space is unused, and the table's entries for unused regions waste space.** A typical 32-bit process might use 100 MB of virtual memory scattered across code, data, heap, stack, and some mmap'd regions — maybe 25,000 pages. That's 25,000 entries in use, out of 1,048,576 possible. The table is 2.5% dense.

**Multi-level page tables** exploit this sparsity. Instead of one flat table, use a _tree_ of tables, in which unused regions have no tables at all. The table grows with how much memory a process actually uses, not with how much it could theoretically address.

Plan B continues. Our `*x = 42` has translated through the TLB on the fast path. On a TLB miss, the hardware walks a page table. This chapter describes what that walk looks like when the table is multi-level.

### 13.2 A Two-Level Page Table

#### Example: Splitting the VPN

Take the 32-bit machine, 4 KB pages, 20-bit VPN. Split the VPN into two pieces:
    
    
    Virtual address (32 bits):
    ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────────┐
    │  PD idx  │  PT idx  │    offset    │
    │  10 bits │  10 bits │    12 bits   │
    └──────────┴──────────┴──────────────┘
    

* Top 10 bits: index into a **page directory** (PD), a top-level table of 1024 entries. Each entry points at a **page table** (PT), or is marked invalid.
* Middle 10 bits: index into the page table found through the page directory. Each page table holds 1024 PTEs.
* Bottom 12 bits: offset within the page, as before.

The page directory alone is 1024 entries × 4 bytes = **4 KB** — one page. If a process uses only a small fraction of its address space, most page-directory entries point to nothing, and most page tables don't exist.

#### Pattern

A page table now covers 1024 pages × 4 KB = **4 MB of virtual address space.** A process with 100 MB of used memory needs roughly 100 / 4 = **25 page tables** (plus the page directory). Total: 26 × 4 KB = **104 KB per process.** That's versus the 4 MB linear table — a 40× reduction.

#### Example: A walk

Process issues a 32-bit virtual address `0x12345678` (full 8 hex digits = 32 bits).

* Split into `(PD idx | PT idx | offset)`: shift and mask the full 32-bit address. 
    * **PD idx** = top 10 bits = `(0x12345678 >> 22) & 0x3FF` = `0x048` (binary `0001001000`).
    * **PT idx** = middle 10 bits = `(0x12345678 >> 12) & 0x3FF` = `0x345`.
    * **Offset** = bottom 12 bits = `0x12345678 & 0xFFF` = `0x678`.
    * (Equivalently: the 20-bit VPN is `0x12345678 >> 12 = 0x12345`; its top 10 bits give PD idx and its bottom 10 bits give PT idx. Both derivations yield the same numbers.)
* Look up PD entry at PD[0x048]. Say it contains PFN `0x200` (the page holding this page table). If PD[0x048] has V = 0, trap (no mapping).
* Read the PT at physical (`0x200` × 4096). Look up PT[0x345]. Say it contains PFN `0x3AF` with V = 1, W = 1.
* Physical address = (`0x3AF` << 12) | `0x678` = `0x3AF678`.

Two memory reads during translation: one for the PD entry, one for the PT entry. One more read gets the data. Three DRAM trips on TLB miss, compared to two for a linear table. The overhead of the extra level is small and almost always hidden by the TLB.

#### Definition: Multi-level page table

**Definition:** A _multi-level page table_ is a hierarchical translation structure in which the VPN is split into several indices, each selecting an entry in a table at its level. Each entry either points to the next level's table or indicates that the corresponding region of the address space is unmapped. Unmapped regions require no storage at the lower levels, so the total table size scales with used address space rather than maximum address space.

### 13.3 Deeper Hierarchies for 64-bit

On 64-bit systems with 4 KB pages, the address space is 2⁶⁴ bytes. Most implementations (x86-64, ARMv8) use only 48 bits of it (a 256 TB address space per process); the top 16 bits are required to match the sign of bit 47 (canonical form) and are not available for mapping.

To walk 48 bits with 12-bit offset, we need 36 bits of VPN. Split into 4 levels of 9 bits each, each level indexing 512 entries (9 bits) that fit in a 4 KB page (512 × 8 bytes per entry).
    
    
     9 bits │ 9 bits │ 9 bits │ 9 bits │ 12 bits
      L4    │  L3    │  L2    │  L1    │ offset
     (PML4) │(PDPT)  │ (PD)   │ (PT)   │
    

Translation walks 4 tables: PML4 → PDPT → PD → PT → page. Each table is one 4 KB page. A fully-populated process with all 256 TB mapped would require a _lot_ of tables, but real processes use tiny fractions, and 4-level tables remain light — typically tens of kilobytes per process.

#### Pattern

More levels mean smaller tables but more walk steps on a TLB miss. x86-64 uses 4 levels (soon 5 levels with PML5 for 57-bit virtual addresses). ARM64 uses 4 levels. RISC-V supports 3, 4, or 5 levels depending on the Sv mode. The sweet spot is the shallowest tree that keeps tables page-sized and address-space reach enough for the architecture.

A 4-level walk on a TLB miss costs 4 DRAM reads just for translation. At ~100 cycles per DRAM read, that's 400 cycles of raw translation. This is why the TLB is essential — and why huge pages (which skip a level of the walk) matter for workloads with poor TLB locality.

### 13.4 The Page Directory and the CR3 Register

Now the PTBR (recall from Chapter 11: x86 calls it CR3) holds the physical address of the top-level table (PML4 on x86-64). On a context switch, the OS changes CR3 to the incoming process's PML4 physical address. Every subsequent translation walks a different tree.

Changing CR3 also (absent ASIDs) invalidates the TLB, because the new process's VPN → PFN mappings are different. With ASIDs, each TLB entry is tagged and old entries remain valid for the old process's ASID.

#### Example: Memory to hold a process's tables

A process with 10 MB of actually-used virtual memory, scattered across code (a few MB), heap (a few MB), stack (a few hundred KB), and mmap (a few MB):

* **PML4:** 1 page (4 KB). Only 2-3 of its 512 entries are used (one for user code/data in low memory, one for user stack in high memory, possibly one for mapped files).
* **PDPT:** 2-3 pages.
* **PD:** maybe 5 pages.
* **PT:** covering 10 MB at 2 MB per table = 5 pages.

Total: roughly 13-15 pages = 50-60 KB per process. Even with 1000 processes, that's 50-60 MB of page tables — trivial on any modern machine.

A linear table for the same 48-bit address space would require 512 GB per process.

#### Pattern

The storage cost of page tables _scales with what the process uses_ , not with the total address space. This is the property that makes 64-bit virtual memory practical. You can have a 256 TB virtual address space without needing 32 PB of table.

### 13.5 Filling the Tables

Who creates these tables? The OS does, lazily.

#### Example: A process starts

When the OS creates a new process (via `fork` or `exec`), it:

  1. Allocates one physical frame for the PML4.
  2. Zeros it (all entries V = 0).
  3. Sets CR3 to the PML4's physical address.
  4. For each region the process needs — code, data, stack — the OS walks the tree and creates intermediate tables as needed. For each 4 KB virtual page, the OS allocates a physical frame and sets up the PT entry.

Actually, in modern OSes, even this is lazy: the initial setup creates PTEs only for regions that are _about to be used_. When the process first accesses an uninitialized virtual page, the hardware faults, the OS walks down to the correct PT (creating intermediate tables if needed), allocates a physical frame, fills in the PTE, and restarts the instruction.

#### Pattern: Demand paging to the tree

Not only are _physical pages_ allocated on demand — the _tables themselves_ are allocated on demand. A fresh process with nothing touched may have only a PML4 and nothing else. The first access populates a chain down to a leaf PT and one backing frame. The table grows with the process.

#### Common Misconception

_"Multi-level page tables are slower because they require more memory accesses."_

The statement is true per-walk but misleading overall. A linear page table with 4 MB per process pressures the cache and DRAM; multi-level tables with 60 KB per process leave cache and DRAM largely free. The walk cost is paid only on TLB misses, which are rare. Amortized over real workloads, multi-level tables are _faster_ , not slower, because the dramatic memory savings more than compensate for the occasional extra table-walk access.

Further, multi-level tables enable **huge pages** : if a PD entry's PS (page size) bit is set, the hardware treats the PD entry itself as a leaf mapping for a 2 MB page, skipping the PT level entirely. The walk is one level shorter, and one TLB entry covers 512× more memory. High-performance workloads routinely configure huge pages for this reason.

### 13.6 Sharing Subtrees

Two processes running the same program share their code pages. Multi-level tables naturally support sharing at any level:

* Share individual pages by having both PTs point to the same physical frame.
* Share a whole 2 MB region by having both PDs point to the same PT.
* Share a whole 1 GB region by having both PDPTs point to the same PD.

This is how shared libraries, shared memory, and the kernel's own mappings work. Every process has kernel pages mapped in the top of its address space, all pointing to the same physical pages. On a context switch that does not cross a privilege boundary, those mappings persist.

#### Example: Shared libraries

`libc` is loaded once into physical memory. Every process that uses it has a PTE (or a chain down to PTEs) pointing at those same physical frames, marked read-only. No matter how many processes share `libc`, only one copy exists in DRAM.

This saves enormous amounts of memory: a typical Linux system with 200 processes running might have several dozen shared libraries, each used by most processes. Sharing them as read-only mappings saves hundreds of megabytes over per-process copies.

#### Pattern

Fine-grained sharing is a consequence of page-level mapping and pointer-to-pointer table structure. Both base-and-bounds and segmentation struggled to share small amounts of memory without sharing whole large regions. Pages fix that, and multi-level tables keep the costs low.

### 13.7 Alternative Table Structures

Multi-level tables are the dominant approach today, but two alternatives occasionally appear:

#### Hashed page tables

Instead of a tree, a hash table indexed by VPN. Each slot (possibly a chain for collisions) holds PTEs. Memory usage scales with the number of mapped pages (like multi-level tables), but lookup doesn't require a tree walk — it's a hash and a collision-chain traversal. PowerPC and some early SPARC systems used this.

Downside: unpredictable walk cost (collision chains vary), and harder interaction with the TLB and huge pages.

#### Inverted page tables

One table system-wide, indexed by PFN rather than VPN. Each entry says "this frame holds VPN X of process Y." Memory scales with physical RAM, not with processes × address space — very compact on machines with many sparse-user processes.

Downside: translating a VPN requires searching the inverted table (or a hash structure built over it). Slower per walk. Used on some IBM systems (POWER, early Itanium).

#### Pattern

In practice, multi-level page tables plus a large TLB plus huge pages won. The structure is simple enough to walk quickly, compact enough to fit in modern memory, and supports sharing and huge pages naturally. Chapter 13 is about why this design prevailed, not why alternatives exist.

### 13.8 Chapter Summary

* A linear page table scales with _maximum_ address space — 4 MB per process on 32-bit, 32 PB on 64-bit. Both are impractical for production use.
* **Multi-level page tables** split the VPN into several indices, forming a tree. Each level's table is one page. Unmapped regions require no table allocation.
* Storage scales with _actual use_ , not maximum address space. A process using 10 MB of memory uses about 60 KB of table, not 32 PB.
* 64-bit systems typically use 4 levels (PML4 → PDPT → PD → PT on x86-64). Walk cost: 4 DRAM reads on TLB miss.
* Huge pages skip a level of the walk and make one TLB entry cover much more memory.
* Sharing is supported at every level: share a page, a 2 MB region, or a 1 GB region by having table pointers coincide.

Chapter 14 picks up the Plan B thread and asks what happens when the page has _no mapping_ or an _invalid_ mapping — the page fault. The OS response is the subject of the final Plan B chapter, where the promise from Chapter 9 (lazy allocation) is finally redeemed, and where swapping to disk enters.

### 13.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What is the main reason to use a multi-level page table instead of a linear one?

**Answer:** Storage. A linear page table has one entry per possible virtual page — 4 MB per process on 32-bit systems, 32 PB on 64-bit. A multi-level table stores entries only for regions the process actually uses. A typical process uses a small fraction of its address space, so multi-level tables consume kilobytes instead of megabytes (or petabytes).

**Q2.** In a two-level page table, what does the page directory hold? What does a page table hold?

**Answer:** The _page directory_ holds pointers (with flags) to page tables, one pointer per large chunk of virtual address space (e.g., 4 MB per entry on a 32-bit system with 4 KB pages and 10/10 split). Invalid entries indicate that the corresponding region has no mapping and no backing page table. A _page table_ holds PTEs, each mapping one virtual page to a physical frame. A typical page table covers 1024 pages × 4 KB = 4 MB of virtual address space.

**Q3.** On a TLB miss with a 4-level page table, how many DRAM accesses does the hardware make to satisfy one memory reference?

**Answer:** Five. Four reads to traverse the page table tree (L4 → L3 → L2 → L1) and one read for the actual data. (Some of these may be served from the CPU's data cache rather than DRAM, which typically makes the walk much faster than the raw 5 × DRAM estimate.)

**Q4.** What is a huge page, and how does it interact with a multi-level page table?

**Answer:** A _huge page_ is a mapping larger than the default page size, typically 2 MB or 1 GB on x86-64. The multi-level page table supports huge pages by allowing a higher-level entry to be a leaf mapping directly, skipping the lower levels. On x86-64, a PD entry with the PS bit set maps a 2 MB region — the PT level is bypassed. A PDPT entry with PS = 1 maps a 1 GB region. Huge pages shorten the walk by one (or two) levels and make one TLB entry cover 512× (or 512² ×) more memory.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** A 32-bit virtual address with a two-level page table, 4 KB pages, 10-bit PD index, 10-bit PT index. The page directory is at physical address `0x1000`. PD entry for index 5 is `0x3004 | V=1, U=1, W=1` (base of some PT). PT at `0x3004 << 12`? Actually PD entries hold PFN — the PT lives at PFN 3 (physical `0x3000`). PT entry for index 2 has PFN `0x5A` with V=1, W=1. Translate virtual address `0x01402ABC`.

**Answer:** Break `0x01402ABC` into (PD idx, PT idx, offset):

* PD idx = top 10 bits: `0x01402ABC >> 22` = `0x05` (decimal 5).
* PT idx = next 10 bits: `(0x01402ABC >> 12) & 0x3FF` = `0x002` (decimal 2).
* Offset = bottom 12 bits: `0x0ABC`.

Walk: PD[5] has PFN 3 → PT at physical `0x3000`. PT[2] has PFN `0x5A`, V = 1 → leaf mapping found. Physical address = (`0x5A` << 12) | `0x0ABC` = `0x5AABC`.

**Q6.** A 64-bit process uses only a 1 MB code region, a 4 MB heap, and a 256 KB stack. With 4 KB pages and a 4-level page table (PML4, PDPT, PD, PT, each indexing 512 entries), estimate the total size of the page tables.

**Answer:** Mapped virtual memory ≈ 1 MB + 4 MB + 256 KB ≈ 5.25 MB, which is about 1350 pages — but they are split across three disjoint regions (code low, heap mid, stack high), so we cannot collapse them.

* **PML4:** 1 page always. (4 KB)
* **PDPT entries:** One per used 512 GB region. All three regions might share one PDPT (user region) if they are close enough in virtual space. Assume 1-2 PDPTs: 1-2 pages.
* **PD entries:** One per used 1 GB region. Code, heap, stack likely live in distinct 1 GB chunks, so 3 PDs: 3 pages.
* **PT entries:** One per used 2 MB region. Code (1 MB → 1 PT), heap (4 MB → 2 PTs), stack (256 KB → 1 PT): 4 pages.

Total: roughly 9-10 pages = 36-40 KB. Versus a linear table for 2⁴⁸ bytes of address space (which would be ~32 PB), this is negligible.

**Q7.** A 32-bit process with 4 KB pages and a two-level page table. The process mmaps a single 4 KB file at virtual address `0xC0000000` (the highest page). What is the minimum amount of page-table memory required to make this mapping exist, assuming all other virtual address space is unused?

**Answer:**

* 1 page directory (4 KB).
* 1 page table for the PD entry corresponding to VPN `0xC0000`'s PD slot (4 KB).
* Within that page table, one PTE is filled in for the actual mapped page; the rest are zero.

Total: 8 KB of page-table memory. Plus the 4 KB for the mapped page itself. Even to map one page at the top of a 4 GB address space, you pay an 8 KB table overhead — a 300% multiplier. For a process that uses only a few pages, the page table itself can dominate. For realistic processes using hundreds of pages, the multiplier shrinks toward 1%.

**Q8.** Recall from Chapter 11 that copy-on-write `fork` shares pages between parent and child. On a multi-level page table, what exactly does `fork` copy?

**Answer:** `fork` copies the _page table structure_ , not the data pages. Specifically, it allocates a new PML4 for the child, then walks the parent's tree and allocates new intermediate tables as it goes. The leaf PTs, however, can be _shared_ — except that the writable bit must be cleared, so future writes trap and trigger copy-on-write.

More aggressive implementations go further: share even leaf PTs between parent and child (with all PTEs marked read-only), copying a PT only when the first divergence occurs. This is faster for `fork`-heavy workloads, at the cost of more complexity in the VM subsystem.

The _data pages_ themselves are never copied at `fork` time — that is the whole point of copy-on-write. They are copied only on the first write, one page at a time.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** Multi-level page tables make the page table's storage proportional to the process's used address space. TLBs make translation fast on the common path. Huge pages make TLB reach larger. Each of these three is an independent technique. Argue that together they form a coherent system whose whole is larger than the sum of its parts.

**Answer:** Each technique addresses a different bottleneck:

* _Multi-level tables_ solve the **storage** problem: the table no longer scales with maximum address space.
* _TLBs_ solve the **speed** problem: the table walk happens rarely, not on every access.
* _Huge pages_ solve the **reach** problem: a fixed-size TLB covers exponentially more memory when each entry maps 512× more.

But they also reinforce each other. Multi-level tables make huge pages cheap to support (just allow higher-level leaves — no structural change needed). Huge pages reduce table walk cost by shortening the tree traversal. The TLB caches huge-page entries just as cheaply as 4 KB entries, so the reach improvement is free on the fast path. Multi-level tables make demand-paging the tables themselves practical, because incrementally growing the tree is natural.

Without multi-level tables, huge pages would still help (fewer TLB entries needed) but the table itself would be unmanageable. Without TLBs, multi-level tables would be 4-5× slower per access, crippling the system. Without huge pages, some workloads (databases, VMs) would be TLB-bottlenecked no matter how good the table and the cache. Together, the three make 64-bit virtual memory practical at the scale of a modern data center; any two without the third leave a serious performance or practicality gap.

**Q10.** The chapter claims multi-level page tables won over hashed and inverted alternatives. But hardware design is rarely about a clean technical victory — it reflects ecosystems, compatibility, and history. Speculate on what would have to change for an inverted page table to make a comeback in a future architecture, and what applications might motivate it.

**Answer:** Inverted page tables consume memory proportional to _physical_ RAM, not to virtual-address-space-per-process. On a machine with 64 GB of RAM and 10,000 active processes (each sparsely populated), a multi-level table might use 10,000 × 60 KB ≈ 600 MB of table space. An inverted table would use ~64 MB — one entry per physical frame — regardless of process count.

Conditions under which this might become attractive:

* _Extreme process density._ Serverless/FaaS environments with thousands of cold processes per machine. If process count × per-process table size becomes comparable to physical RAM, inverted tables win.
* _Unified address space._ Systems where many processes share one logical address space (e.g., capability machines, hardware that unifies processes and threads) have less per-process diversity and may benefit from a single system-wide translation structure.
* _Hardware-assisted hash lookups._ If the CPU offers fast content-addressable TLB-adjacent structures or GPU-style parallel probes, the traditionally-slow hash lookup for inverted tables could become fast enough to be competitive.

Motivating applications: cloud-native microservices at extreme density, capability-based security models (e.g., CHERI-derived systems), and exokernels where the per-process state is minimized. None of these is mainstream today, which is why multi-level tables remain dominant. But architecture is not static — if any of these use cases grows to matter economically, table design will be revisited. The lesson: every "winning" system-level choice is contingent on workload and economics, not on pure technical merit.

---

## Chapter 14. Swapping Mechanisms and Page Faults

### 14.1 The Missing Piece

Five chapters ago, a program called `malloc(sizeof(int))`, received a virtual address, and assigned `42` to it. We watched that address navigate page tables, TLBs, multi-level trees, and MMU flag checks. One question remained: what happens when the hardware looks up a PTE and finds V = 0?

That question is the **page fault**. It is where the OS re-enters the story. For fifty nanoseconds at a time, translation runs entirely in hardware, the OS uninvolved. Then occasionally — the first access to a new page, a read of a swapped-out page, a write to a copy-on-write page — V = 0 appears, the hardware traps, and the OS takes control.

This chapter closes the Plan B thread. The page fault handler is the mechanism that redeems every promise the OS made in Chapters 9-13: lazy allocation, demand paging, copy-on-write, shared libraries, swapping. All of them are implemented the same way — set V = 0, let the hardware trap, handle the fault appropriately, return.

By the end of this chapter, `*x = 42` will have reached DRAM.

### 14.2 What Happens on a Page Fault

#### Example: The trap sequence

The CPU fetches `mov [x], 42`, where `x = 0x55f3a1b4a2a0`. The MMU walks the page table (miss the TLB), reaches the leaf PTE, finds V = 0. The CPU:

  1. Saves the faulting address in a special register (CR2 on x86).
  2. Saves the faulting program counter (so the instruction can be re-executed).
  3. Raises a **page fault exception** — a type of trap — and transfers to the kernel's page-fault handler.

The handler runs in kernel mode. It reads CR2 to learn which address faulted, reads the fault type flags (was it a read or a write? was the page not present or was a permission violation?), looks up the process's VMA (virtual memory area) metadata to decide what to do.

#### Pattern

The OS uses V = 0 as a signal, not just as an error. Every page fault is a question from the hardware to the OS: "this virtual page has no backing frame — what now?" The OS answers by consulting its _own_ records (the VMA list, the swap map) and performing whatever action is required.

#### Definition: Page fault

**Definition:** A _page fault_ is a CPU exception raised when a memory access refers to a virtual page whose PTE has V = 0 (or whose access mode violates the PTE's protection flags). The exception traps to the kernel's page-fault handler, which examines the faulting address and the process's memory metadata to decide whether to: (a) map a physical frame and resume, (b) load the page from disk and resume, (c) copy the page and resume (copy-on-write), or (d) kill the process with a segmentation violation.

### 14.3 The OS's View of Memory Regions

For the OS to handle a fault, it must know _what the faulting address is supposed to be_. The PTE says "nothing mapped." The OS's records say "that range is heap, 2 MB reserved, lazy."

#### Example: Virtual memory areas (VMAs)

On Linux, each process has a list of VMAs describing regions of its virtual address space:
    
    
    VMA list for process 4231:
    [0x0040_0000, 0x0045_0000)  r-x  /usr/bin/myprog       (text segment)
    [0x0045_0000, 0x0046_0000)  rw-  /usr/bin/myprog       (data segment)
    [0x0046_0000, 0x0066_0000)  rw-  [heap]
    [0x7f00_0000_0000, ...)     r-x  /usr/lib/libc.so.6    (shared library)
    [0x7fff_fe00_0000, ...)     rw-  [stack]
    

When a fault occurs, the handler walks this list. If the faulting address falls inside a VMA, the fault is legitimate — the region exists, but the page has not yet been backed. If the faulting address is outside any VMA, the fault is a bug — the process touched an unmapped address, and the OS delivers SIGSEGV.

#### Pattern

There are two layers of metadata:

* **Page tables (hardware-visible).** Used by the MMU for translation. May have V = 0 for pages that _should_ be mapped but aren't yet.
* **VMA list (OS-private).** Used by the OS on a fault to decide what to do. Describes which regions are legitimate even if their PTEs say V = 0.

Hardware only sees page tables. Software only makes decisions with the VMA list. The page fault is the bridge between them.

### 14.4 Kinds of Page Faults

Not all faults mean the same thing. The handler distinguishes:

#### Minor fault: first-touch allocation

The process accesses a page in its allocated heap. The VMA says "this range is valid heap." The PTE says V = 0. This is a _first-touch fault_ — the OS allocates a fresh physical frame, zeros it (for security: must not leak another process's old data), sets the PTE to V = 1 | PFN, returns. The process's instruction re-executes and now succeeds.

Cost: a few microseconds (zeroing a page is the slowest step).

#### Major fault: swapped-out page

The VMA says "this range is valid heap." The PTE says V = 0, and a special flag (or a record in the swap map) says "this page is on disk at swap slot S." The OS:

  1. Allocates a physical frame.
  2. Issues a disk read for slot S into that frame.
  3. Blocks the process (context-switches to another) until the read completes.
  4. On I/O completion, updates the PTE to V = 1 | PFN.
  5. Wakes the process. It re-executes the instruction.

Cost: a few milliseconds (the disk read dominates). Three orders of magnitude slower than a minor fault.

#### Copy-on-write fault

The PTE says V = 1, W = 0. The instruction is a write. The hardware raises a _permission fault_ (not a not-present fault, technically, but handled by the same machinery). The OS inspects: is this page supposed to be writable but marked read-only because of copy-on-write? If so:

  1. Allocate a new physical frame.
  2. Copy the existing frame's contents into the new frame.
  3. Update the faulting process's PTE: PFN = new frame, W = 1.
  4. Return. The instruction re-executes and succeeds (writes to the new frame).

The other process that shares the old frame still points at it. If it also writes later, _it_ will take a copy-on-write fault and get its own copy. If it doesn't, the page was copied only once — not 2, not 4, not however-many processes shared it.

#### Invalid fault: segmentation violation

The faulting address is not in any VMA. The process dereferenced a wild pointer. The OS sends SIGSEGV, which by default terminates the process and produces a core dump.

#### Stack growth fault

Variant of minor fault. The stack VMA may have a "may grow" flag; a fault just below the current stack bottom extends the VMA down by one page and then treats the fault as a minor allocation fault. This is how a growing recursion depth elastically expands the stack up to some maximum (often 8 MB by default on Linux).

#### Pattern

The page fault handler is a _dispatcher_. It reads the fault metadata and picks one of several procedures based on what the fault means. This dispatch is the interesting part of the handler — the per-case procedures are relatively simple.

### 14.5 Swapping to Disk

The biggest idea attached to page faults is **swap** : when physical memory is full, evict a page to disk. Reclaim the frame for another use. When the evicted page is later touched, fault it back in.

#### Example: The evict sequence

Physical memory is 8 GB. The working set of running processes totals 10 GB of live virtual pages. Some pages must leave DRAM.

The OS's page-replacement policy (Chapter 15) selects a victim frame holding VPN V of process P. The OS:

  1. Writes the frame's contents to a pre-reserved swap area on disk (if dirty — see below).
  2. Updates P's PTE for VPN V: V = 0, set a bit indicating "swapped," record the swap slot.
  3. Shoots down any cached TLB entry for V across cores.
  4. Reuses the freed frame for some other process's page or for a disk-cached file.

Later, P accesses VPN V. Page fault. Major fault this time — the OS reads the swap slot back into a freshly allocated frame, updates the PTE, and returns. P continues execution unaware that anything unusual happened (except for the 5 ms pause).

#### Dirty vs. clean pages

If the evicted page is _clean_ — it matches a copy already on disk (e.g., code loaded from an executable file, or a file mmap'd read-only) — the OS can drop it with no write. Bringing it back is a read from the original file, not from swap.

If the page is _dirty_ — it has been written since being loaded or allocated — the OS must preserve its contents by writing to swap before evicting.

The dirty bit (D) in the PTE distinguishes these cases. The hardware sets D = 1 on writes; the OS clears it when it flushes the page.

#### Pattern

Swap is optional but enormously useful. A machine without swap runs only as many programs as fit in physical RAM; a machine with swap can run programs whose combined size exceeds RAM, at the cost of slowdown when swapping is actively happening. Modern laptops typically have swap sized equal to or twice RAM. Modern servers often disable swap entirely (they prefer a killed process to a slow one) or use compressed swap in RAM (zswap, zram) rather than disk.

#### Common Misconception

_"Swap makes my computer slow."_

Swap makes _active swapping_ slow — the thrashing case where the working set exceeds RAM and pages are constantly moving in and out. Swap that is _configured but not actively used_ costs nothing. A machine with 32 GB of RAM and 8 GB of swap, using 20 GB of live memory, is no slower than a machine with no swap at all: the swap area sits idle.

Linux's `swappiness` tunable controls how eager the OS is to preemptively swap anonymous pages to make room for the page cache. A high value favors a larger cache at the cost of occasional major faults on anonymous pages. A low value keeps anonymous pages in memory at the cost of a smaller cache.

### 14.6 The Journey of `*x = 42`

We can now trace the complete journey of our running example from the opening of Chapter 9.

  1. `malloc(sizeof(int))` runs in user space. The C library's allocator looks at the heap's free list. If the free list has a suitable chunk, it returns a virtual address `0x55f3a1b4a2a0` into that chunk. The heap's VMA is already set up; PTEs for this range may or may not yet exist. Allocator bookkeeping lives in the heap itself, so the allocator may have touched some heap pages while working.
