  2. The program stores the address in `x`. No memory system activity — just a register write.

  3. The program executes `*x = 42`. The CPU issues a store to virtual address `0x55f3a1b4a2a0`.

  4. The MMU looks up the VPN in the TLB. If cached (unlikely on first access), it proceeds to step 9. If not:

  5. The MMU (or page-table walker) walks the multi-level page table. On x86-64, four memory reads: PML4, PDPT, PD, PT. On a fresh access to heap that hasn't been touched yet, the leaf PTE reads V = 0.

  6. The hardware raises a page fault. CR2 = `0x55f3a1b4a2a0`, fault type = write, page-not-present.

  7. The kernel's page fault handler runs. It walks the VMA list, finds the heap VMA, notes that this is a valid-but-unbacked page. Minor fault path: allocate a physical frame (say PFN `0x3AF`), zero it, update the PTE with V = 1, W = 1, U = 1, PFN `0x3AF`.

  8. The handler returns to user mode. The CPU re-executes `mov [x], 42`.

  9. The MMU looks up the VPN in the TLB. Still cold; re-walk the page table. This time the PTE says V = 1, W = 1, PFN `0x3AF`. Translate. Install in the TLB.

  10. The CPU issues the store to physical address `0x3AF000 | 0x2A0 = 0x3AF2A0`. The cache takes the write. Eventually, the value `42` reaches DRAM.

  11. Done. Time elapsed: a few microseconds for the fault, then a few nanoseconds for the subsequent accesses that reuse the now-warm TLB entry and data cache line.

#### Pattern

Every mechanism of Chapters 9-14 participated:

* **Address space (Ch. 9):** the virtual address was meaningful within the process's private view.
* **Segmentation/base-and-bounds (Ch. 10):** rejected in favor of paging.
* **Paging (Ch. 11):** split into VPN and offset, mapped via the PTE.
* **TLB (Ch. 12):** bypassed on first access; caches subsequent accesses.
* **Multi-level tables (Ch. 13):** the walk traversed four levels.
* **Page fault (Ch. 14):** materialized the promise from step 1 into a real physical frame.

Plan B concludes. `42` sits in DRAM. The six-chapter arc is complete.

### 14.7 Chapter Summary

* A **page fault** is a trap raised when the PTE says V = 0 or the access violates permissions. The kernel's handler decides what to do.
* The OS distinguishes **minor faults** (allocate a zero page), **major faults** (bring a page in from disk), **copy-on-write faults** (duplicate a shared page), **stack-growth faults** (expand the stack region), and **invalid faults** (kill the process with SIGSEGV).
* A **VMA list** tells the OS which virtual address ranges are legitimate; page tables tell the hardware how to translate them. The fault handler bridges the two.
* **Swapping** uses page faults to support workloads larger than physical RAM. Clean pages can be dropped and recovered from their original file; dirty pages must be saved to swap space.
* The full journey of a memory reference — from `malloc` to DRAM — uses every mechanism built in Chapters 9-14.

Chapter 15 revisits one piece left unspecified: which page should the OS evict when physical memory is full? That is the domain of **page replacement policies** — FIFO, LRU, clock, approximations, and the practical policies used by real kernels.

### 14.8 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What is a page fault, and who generates it?

**Answer:** A page fault is a CPU exception raised when a memory access's PTE has V = 0 (page not present) or when the access violates the PTE's protection flags. The hardware generates it during translation, and traps to the OS's page fault handler. The handler decides whether the fault represents a legitimate situation (allocate a page, bring it in from swap, do copy-on-write) or a bug (deliver SIGSEGV).

**Q2.** Describe the difference between a minor fault and a major fault.

**Answer:** A _minor fault_ is handled entirely in memory: the OS allocates (or repurposes) a physical frame, possibly copies a page from another process's frame or zeros it, and updates the PTE. Cost: microseconds. A _major fault_ requires disk I/O: the OS reads the page's contents from swap space or from a backing file into a physical frame. The process is blocked during the read, which takes milliseconds. Major faults are three orders of magnitude slower than minor faults.

**Q3.** What is a VMA, and why does the OS need it in addition to page tables?

**Answer:** A _virtual memory area_ is an OS-private record describing a range of virtual addresses that is legitimately mapped (or should be). Page tables, by contrast, only describe which virtual pages currently have physical backing. The VMA list is how the OS decides, on a fault, whether the fault is a legitimate demand (a region exists but the page hasn't been backed yet) or a bug (the process touched unmapped memory). Hardware only consults page tables; the OS consults VMAs to interpret faults.

**Q4.** What is the dirty bit, and why does the OS care about it during eviction?

**Answer:** The _dirty bit_ (D) in a PTE is set by the hardware whenever the page is written. The OS uses it during eviction: if the page is clean (D = 0 since last load), its contents match whatever is on disk (for file-backed pages) or can simply be discarded (for freshly-zeroed pages), so the OS can evict without writing. If the page is dirty (D = 1), the OS must write the page to swap or to its backing file before evicting, to preserve the process's modifications.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** A process calls `mmap(NULL, 1 GB, PROT_READ | PROT_WRITE, MAP_PRIVATE | MAP_ANONYMOUS, -1, 0)` and then touches only the first 10 MB of the mapping (one byte per page, 2560 pages). How many page faults does the program generate, and how much physical memory is consumed?

**Answer:** 2560 page faults, each a minor fault (first-touch, no disk I/O). Physical memory consumed: 2560 × 4 KB = 10 MB of backing pages, plus a small overhead for page tables (the tree needed to cover 1 GB of virtual address space is a few tens of KB). The other 1 GB - 10 MB = 1014 MB of the mapping is never touched and consumes no physical memory — the VMA exists, but the PTEs are still V = 0 and the physical frames have never been allocated. This is lazy allocation from Chapter 9 made concrete.

**Q6.** A system has 8 GB of RAM and 16 GB of swap. Three processes collectively want 20 GB of live memory. Describe the likely behavior and the resulting performance.

**Answer:** The system must keep roughly 8 GB resident at any time and shuttle the remaining 12 GB between RAM and swap. As long as each process's _current_ working set fits in RAM (with room for the other two), processes run at near-full speed with minor page faults when they touch new regions — major faults only when they revisit pages that were swapped out.

If the combined _active_ working set exceeds 8 GB, the system begins **thrashing** : every major fault evicts a page that a process will need soon, causing another major fault, and so on. CPU utilization may drop below 10% as every thread is blocked on disk I/O most of the time. Total throughput can collapse to a small fraction of normal, with each 5 ms major fault representing huge CPU idle time.

The cure is to reduce active working sets (kill a process, tune memory-heavy applications) or add more RAM. Swap is not a magic RAM multiplier; it is a pressure release for transient excess.

**Q7.** After `fork`, a parent process has a large heap, most of which is untouched by the child. Use copy-on-write to describe how many page faults occur in the child versus without COW, and how many physical pages are allocated.

**Answer:**

* _Without COW:_ `fork` copies the entire heap at fork time — one page per heap page, all at once, no faults needed during copy (kernel does it directly). If the heap is 1 GB (256 K pages), that's 256 K pages allocated and copied synchronously. Fork is slow.
* _With COW:_ `fork` marks both the parent's and child's heap PTEs read-only, sharing physical frames. No heap pages are copied. `fork` returns quickly. Subsequently, whenever the child writes to a heap page, a _COW fault_ occurs; the OS copies that one page and resumes. If the child only writes to 1% of the heap before exiting, only 1% of pages are copied — a 100× reduction in both time and memory.

The number of faults equals the number of written pages — from zero (if the child only reads) to the full heap size (if it writes everywhere). The physical page allocations are proportional: one new page per COW fault.

**Q8.** A server running out of memory under load sometimes gets killed by the Linux OOM (out-of-memory) killer. Relate this to swapping: why does the OS sometimes kill rather than swap, and how does the choice affect system behavior?

**Answer:** When memory demand exceeds RAM + swap capacity, no amount of swapping helps — some pages simply cannot be kept anywhere. The OS must kill a process to free memory. Before that final extreme, there are intermediate states:

* _Low memory, adequate swap:_ the OS swaps aggressively. Processes run but may pause for major faults. System remains responsive unless thrashing begins.
* _Low memory, exhausted swap:_ the OS has nowhere to put new evictions; it must either kill a process or deny new allocations. Linux fires the OOM killer, heuristically targeting the process with the worst "score" (usually the biggest, youngest, least-critical).
* _Thrashing:_ the OS may swap aggressively but make no real progress; every swap-in causes another swap-out. System becomes unusable. OOM killer may be invoked even with swap available, because the real problem is thrashing rather than capacity.

The choice between swap and kill is a tradeoff between _latency_ and _reliability_. Swap preserves liveness but may cause tail latencies to explode. Killing preserves responsiveness but terminates work. Modern production systems often disable swap precisely because a killed process is easier to deal with (restart automatically) than a slow process (whose SLA is silently violated). Many managed services in Kubernetes clusters set `swappiness=0` and size pods' memory limits so the OOM killer — not swap — is the relief valve.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** The chapter claims the OS uses V = 0 as a _signal_ , not just as an error. Enumerate as many OS uses of V = 0 (or other trap-inducing PTE states) as you can, and argue that this overloading is a strength rather than a confusion.

**Answer:** Uses of V = 0 (or fault-inducing PTE states):

  1. **First-touch allocation.** The page is valid per the VMA but has never been accessed.
  2. **Swapped-out page.** The page was in memory but was evicted; the swap map remembers where.
  3. **File-backed, not yet loaded.** The page is part of an mmap'd file; reading it triggers a read from the file.
  4. **Copy-on-write (W = 0 + legitimate write).** Share a page across processes until divergence.
  5. **Demand loading of executable pages.** Running programs don't load the whole binary at start; pages fault in as executed.
  6. **Guard pages.** A stack's red zone has V = 0 so an overrun traps immediately.
  7. **Tracking writes for incremental GC or checkpointing.** Mark pages W = 0 to detect writes between checkpoints.
  8. **Live migration of VMs.** Mark pages W = 0 to track which have changed since the last page copy.
  9. **Debugger breakpoints.** A debugger can mark an instruction's page unexecutable, so execution traps on reach.
  10. **Sanitizers.** ASAN etc. can mark freed memory V = 0 to catch use-after-free.

The overloading is a strength because the mechanism — "set a bit, handle the trap" — is cheap, fast, and versatile. Every new feature can exploit the same hardware machinery without adding instructions or changing the CPU. The OS is effectively a _programmable fault interpreter_ : the hardware says "this access is unusual," and the OS decides what that means in context. Rather than adding a distinct mechanism for each feature (a first-touch bit, a cow bit, a migrate bit), the OS collapses them all onto V/W/D and uses software metadata (VMAs, process records) to interpret. This is the same design wisdom seen in Chapter 4's trap mechanism and Chapter 12's TLB: a small, uniform hardware primitive becomes the substrate for rich software policies.

**Q10.** Plan B — the page fault story — traced a single memory reference from `malloc` (Chapter 9) to DRAM (Chapter 14). Step back and identify three general lessons about OS design that this six-chapter arc illustrates, each more abstract than the last.

**Answer:**

_Lesson 1: The OS delivers abstraction by enlisting hardware._ A process's view of memory is a private, flat address space. That abstraction is not implemented in OS code alone — it is a joint product of hardware (MMU, TLB, page walker) and OS (VMAs, page tables, fault handlers). Pages, TLBs, and faults are all hardware mechanisms; it is the OS that gives them _semantics_. Neither side could produce the abstraction independently; the abstraction lives in their collaboration.

_Lesson 2: Abstractions are preserved by trapping at the edge of the fast path._ On the common path, the hardware translates addresses in 1-2 cycles, the OS nowhere to be seen. On the exceptional path — a TLB miss that uncovers a fault — the hardware traps, and the OS handles the case in software. The OS is not a middleman on every operation; it is the _exception handler_ for the hardware's fast path. This pattern — fast default, slow custom — appears again and again: traps (Ch. 4), scheduling (Ch. 5-8), and, we will see, synchronization (Ch. 17), I/O (Ch. 22), file systems (Ch. 23-25). The OS manages what the hardware cannot or will not.

_Lesson 3: The right abstraction decouples what programs see from what the machine contains._ A process thinks it has 256 TB of memory. The machine has 16 GB. A process thinks memory is continuous. The machine has RAM scattered across NUMA nodes. A process thinks its memory is private. The machine shares pages across processes via identical mappings. These lies are the whole point. They let programs be written as if they ran alone on a boundless machine, while the OS and hardware work furiously behind the scenes to multiplex a real machine among many demanding, uncooperative, unaware users.

This three-lesson arc — _enlist hardware, mediate at exceptions, decouple view from reality_ — is the core design pattern of operating systems. Memory is the canonical example. The rest of the book applies the same pattern to CPUs (already done, Chapters 2-8), to synchronization (next, Chapters 16-21), to persistence (Chapters 22-25), and to isolation at scale (Chapter 26). Plan B was one instance of a master narrative.

---

## Chapter 15. Page Replacement Policies and Thrashing

### 15.1 Which Page Should Leave?

Chapter 14 closed the page-fault story. When physical memory is full and the OS must bring in a new page, it must first evict one. Which one?

This is the **page replacement problem**. It sounds arithmetic — just pick a page — but the choice has enormous consequences. A smart policy keeps the pages a process is about to use resident, so subsequent accesses hit memory. A naive policy evicts useful pages and causes cascades of major faults. In the worst case, the OS evicts a page just before the process accesses it, brings it back, evicts it again for the next new page, and so on — the system does no useful work, only shuttling pages in and out. This is **thrashing**.

The goal of a replacement policy: minimize the miss rate — the fraction of memory accesses that cause major faults. Equivalently, maximize hit rate. Every miss costs milliseconds (a disk read); every hit is nanoseconds. A small improvement in hit rate has an enormous effect on performance.

This chapter studies replacement policies in order of increasing sophistication: the theoretical optimum, simple heuristics, the LRU family, and the approximations that real operating systems use. It closes with thrashing — the pathology that all of these policies exist to prevent.

### 15.2 The Theoretical Optimum: Belady's MIN

#### Example: Looking into the future

Suppose the OS could see the future — it knew every memory access the running processes would make. Then the optimal policy is simple: **evict the page that will be used furthest in the future** (or never again). Call this policy **MIN** (or **OPT**), after Bélády's 1966 analysis.

Consider a reference stream of virtual pages: `0 1 2 3 0 1 4 0 1 2 3 4`. Physical memory holds 3 pages. When page 4 is accessed (the 7th reference), memory holds `{0, 1, 2}` or similar; one page must be evicted to bring in 4.

Future references: `0 1 2 3 4`. Evict 2, because it is used furthest in the future (distance 3). Or, if 2 will not be used at all after this, evict it.

#### Pattern

MIN minimizes misses by definition — no policy can do better, because MIN makes perfectly informed decisions. It serves as a _theoretical lower bound_ against which real policies are measured.

#### Common Misconception

_"MIN is what we should implement."_

MIN requires knowledge of the future. The OS has no such knowledge. Real workloads do not announce their access patterns in advance. MIN is a yardstick, not an algorithm. The question is: _how close can we get without a crystal ball?_

#### Definition: Page replacement policy

**Definition:** A _page replacement policy_ is the algorithm the OS uses to select which resident page to evict when a fault occurs and physical memory is full. Policies differ in how they approximate the optimum MIN using information available at runtime.

### 15.3 FIFO: Evict the Oldest

#### Example: FIFO replacement

Maintain a queue of resident pages in the order they were loaded. Evict from the head.

Reference stream `0 1 2 3 0 1 4`, 3 physical frames:
    
    
    Access: 0 1 2 3 0 1 4
    Queue:  0 1 2 3 0 1 4      (evict front as queue fills)
                 ↑ evict 0  → miss
                    ↑ evict 1  → miss (0 re-faulted)
                               ↑ evict 2
                                  ↑ evict 3, which was just re-added
    

FIFO does not care how often a page has been used. A page might be very hot, but if it was loaded first, it goes first.

#### Pattern

FIFO is easy to implement (just a queue) and has nothing to do with usage. Its performance on real workloads is poor — it evicts hot pages that happen to be old, while retaining cold pages that happen to be recent.

#### Belady's anomaly

FIFO has a strange property: _adding more memory can increase the miss rate_. László Bélády discovered a reference stream where FIFO on 4 frames has more misses than on 3. This surprising violation of the expected "more memory = fewer misses" is a sign that FIFO is not a well-founded policy.

Algorithms that avoid Belady's anomaly are called _stack algorithms_ : their set of resident pages at any time with M frames is a superset of the set with M−1 frames. LRU is a stack algorithm. FIFO is not.

### 15.4 LRU: Evict the Least Recently Used

#### Example: LRU replacement

Track, for each resident page, the time of its last access. On eviction, choose the page with the oldest access time.

The intuition: **recent past is a good predictor of near future.** A page accessed one second ago is likely to be accessed again soon; a page last touched an hour ago is probably done being used.

Same reference stream, same 3 frames:
    
    
    Access: 0 1 2 3 0 1 4
    Time:   1 2 3 4 5 6 7
    
    After 0 (t=1): resident {0}                     — miss (cold).
    After 1 (t=2): resident {0,1}                   — miss (cold).
    After 2 (t=3): resident {0,1,2}                 — miss (cold).
    After 3 (t=4): evict 0 (oldest, t=1).           — miss. Resident {1,2,3}, 3 at t=4.
    After 0 (t=5): evict 1 (oldest, t=2).           — miss. Resident {0,2,3}, 0 at t=5.
    After 1 (t=6): evict 2 (oldest, t=3).           — miss. Resident {0,1,3}, 1 at t=6.
    After 4 (t=7): evict 3 (oldest, t=4).           — miss. Resident {0,1,4}, 4 at t=7.
    

This stream is adversarial: the 3-frame cache is smaller than the distinct working set, so LRU misses on all 7 accesses — the first three are cold-start misses and the next four are capacity misses. That is LRU's worst case, not its typical behavior. On workloads with temporal locality — the common case — LRU performs close to MIN, because a recently used page in a real program is very likely to be used again soon.

#### Pattern

LRU captures temporal locality: pages used recently are kept, pages not used recently are dropped. This matches how most programs actually behave. For loops, data structures accessed repeatedly, and stacks of function calls, LRU is a near-optimal approximation.

#### Common Misconception

_"LRU is used in real operating systems."_

Not in its pure form. True LRU requires updating a time-stamp on _every_ memory access — something neither hardware nor software can afford to do at memory speed. Real kernels use _approximations_ to LRU. The exact mechanisms are the subject of Sections 15.6 and 15.7.

#### Definition: LRU

**Definition:** _Least Recently Used (LRU)_ replacement evicts the resident page whose last access is furthest in the past. Exact LRU requires tracking every access to every resident page, which is infeasible at hardware speed. LRU is a target that real policies approximate.

### 15.5 LFU and Other Policies

#### LFU: Least Frequently Used

Maintain a counter per page. On access, increment. On eviction, choose the page with the smallest count.

Good for workloads where some pages are intrinsically hotter than others. Bad for workloads where hotness shifts over time — a formerly hot page can accumulate a high count and stay resident long after it has cooled off.

#### Random

Pick a random resident page to evict. Surprisingly competitive: without a clear pattern, random selection avoids the pathological cases of FIFO and does not require timestamps or counters. But it leaves performance on the table for workloads with genuine locality.

#### Segregated: separate working-set tracking

Give each process a share of memory and let each run its own replacement. Or: distinguish _file cache_ pages from _anonymous_ pages and apply different policies to each. Modern kernels do this to some extent.

#### Pattern

No single policy wins on every workload. The OS must be general-purpose, so it typically implements an LRU-like policy with carefully tuned heuristics and workload-specific escape hatches.

### 15.6 Approximating LRU: Clock

#### Example: The Clock algorithm

Exact LRU is infeasible; random is suboptimal. Clock (a.k.a. Second Chance) is a practical approximation that uses one bit per page — the _reference bit_ (R or A), set by the hardware on every access.

Imagine all resident pages arranged in a circle, with a clock hand pointing at the "next candidate" for eviction.

On a fault, the OS advances the hand. For each page the hand passes:

* If R = 1, clear R to 0 and advance the hand. (Give this page a second chance.)
* If R = 0, evict this page.

Repeat until an eviction happens.
    
    
    Circle: page A (R=1), page B (R=0), page C (R=1), page D (R=1)
    Hand starts at A. A has R=1 → clear to 0, advance.
      B has R=0 → evict B. Done.
    

Pages that are accessed frequently have R=1 when the hand arrives; they survive (one access later, their R is cleared, so unless they're touched again, they'll be evicted on the next round). Pages that have been idle have R=0 and get evicted promptly.

#### Pattern

Clock approximates LRU with constant time per eviction (one hand advance per evicted page in the common case) and one bit per page (the R bit is set by hardware on every access — free). It is the basic mechanism behind most real-world page replacement implementations.

#### Enhancements

Many kernels use _two-handed clock_ : one hand clears R bits (advancing quickly), another hand evicts pages with R=0 (advancing more slowly). The gap between the hands is tuned to how aggressive eviction should be. This is essentially what Linux's _LRU lists_ implement.

Dirty pages are often handled separately: an evicted-but-dirty page must be written to swap, which is expensive; the OS prefers to evict clean pages first, or to write dirty pages in the background so they are clean when the hand arrives.

#### Common Misconception

_"Clock is a hack — we should use real LRU."_

Clock often _beats_ exact LRU in practice. Exact LRU has one pathology: a sequential scan of a large dataset that touches every page once, evicting all the actually-hot pages in the process. Clock's reference-bit-based aging is less aggressive and often preserves hot pages better on scans. Real systems use tuned clock variants because they work, not because they're forced to.

### 15.7 Page Replacement in Practice

#### Linux: LRU lists + active/inactive

Linux maintains two LRU-ordered lists per memory zone: the _active_ list and the _inactive_ list. New pages enter the inactive list. Pages on the inactive list that are accessed get promoted to the active list. Pages on the active list that haven't been accessed recently demote to the inactive list. Eviction always picks from the inactive list.

This is a two-level Clock: recently-accessed pages are protected on the active list; scan-like accesses that touch a page only once keep it on the inactive list, where it's cheap to evict.

#### Pattern

Real policies account for:

* **Page type:** anonymous (swap-backed) vs. file-backed, executable vs. data.
* **Dirty vs. clean:** writing dirty pages is expensive; the OS may evict clean pages preferentially and batch dirty writes.
* **Recency tiers:** hot pages on fast tiers, cold pages on slower tiers or swap.
* **Cgroup / container accounting:** on multi-tenant systems, ensuring no single tenant can blow the cache for everyone.

Linux's page allocator uses dozens of tunables and heuristics built on top of Clock/LRU to balance these factors. The resulting system is complex, but each piece is an engineering answer to a workload observation.

### 15.8 Thrashing

#### Example: When replacement fails

A workload needs a 10 GB working set. Physical memory is 8 GB. After the cache fills, every new access evicts a page that the process will need almost immediately — so the evicted page faults back in, evicting another imminently-needed page, and so on.

Over a short window, the CPU may be blocked waiting for disk 95% of the time. Throughput collapses. The system _is_ doing I/O, but the I/O is not making progress — it's just shuffling pages.

This is **thrashing**.

#### Definition: Thrashing

**Definition:** _Thrashing_ is a system state in which page replacement activity consumes most of the machine's CPU and I/O bandwidth, while making little forward progress because evicted pages are needed again almost immediately. Thrashing occurs when the combined working set of runnable processes substantially exceeds physical memory.

#### Pattern

The OS cannot cure thrashing by picking a better policy — no policy succeeds when the working set simply does not fit. The only real solutions:

* **Reduce the working set.** Swap out (or kill) an entire process to free enough memory that the remaining processes fit. This is called _admission control_. Linux's OOM killer is one implementation.
* **Add more physical memory.** The only permanent solution.
* **Page ahead intelligently.** Predict which pages will be needed and fetch them before they're faulted; combine multiple misses into one disk operation. Helps at the margins.

Paging-out an entire process is sometimes called **swapping** in the classical sense — as distinct from demand paging, which swaps individual pages. Classical swapping was common in early systems; modern systems use it as a last resort.

#### Common Misconception

_"A faster disk fixes thrashing."_

Faster disk makes thrashing less catastrophic — each major fault costs less. But if the workload cannot fit and the access pattern has no locality, no disk speed saves you. A solid-state drive at 10 µs per read is better than a spinning disk at 5 ms, but still 10,000× slower than RAM at 1 ns. The fix is to fit the working set in memory.

### 15.9 Chapter Summary

* Page replacement policies decide which resident page to evict when a new page must be brought in.
* **MIN** (Bélády's optimum) is the theoretical lower bound but requires future knowledge.
* **FIFO** evicts the oldest-loaded page; simple but poor, and suffers Belady's anomaly (more memory, more misses).
* **LRU** evicts the least recently used; approximates the optimum on temporally-local workloads but is too expensive to implement exactly.
* **Clock** approximates LRU using a per-page reference bit that the hardware sets on access. It is the basis of real kernel implementations.
* **Thrashing** occurs when the working set exceeds physical memory; no policy fixes it. Solutions involve reducing active memory or adding RAM.
* Real policies (e.g., Linux's active/inactive LRU lists) blend Clock-like aging with workload-specific heuristics.

This closes the memory arc. Chapter 16 opens Threading Plan C — **The Mutex.** We will watch a simple shared counter go from correct-in-sequential-code to broken-under-concurrency to fixed-with-a-lock. The concurrency arc runs six chapters (Ch. 16-21); it is one of the most intellectually dense parts of the book, and one of the most important.

### 15.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What is Belady's MIN, and why is it unimplementable?

**Answer:** MIN (also called OPT) is the replacement policy that evicts the page whose next use is furthest in the future. It minimizes miss rate over any given reference stream — no other policy can do better. It is unimplementable because it requires knowledge of future memory accesses, which the OS does not have. MIN serves as a theoretical lower bound for comparing real policies.

**Q2.** Describe Belady's anomaly. Why is it a concern?

**Answer:** Belady's anomaly is the counterintuitive phenomenon that, under FIFO, adding more physical frames can _increase_ miss rate on certain reference streams. It is a concern because it shows that FIFO is not a well-founded policy: "more memory" should always help, and an algorithm that can regress when given more resources has structural flaws. Stack algorithms (including LRU) do not suffer Belady's anomaly.

**Q3.** In the Clock algorithm, what does the reference bit tell us, and how does the hand use it?

**Answer:** The _reference bit_ (R) is set to 1 by hardware on every access to the page. The clock hand advances through resident pages; when it encounters a page with R = 1, it clears R to 0 and advances (giving the page a "second chance"). When it encounters a page with R = 0, it evicts that page. The effect: recently-accessed pages are preserved (their R is 1 when the hand arrives), while idle pages are evicted promptly.

**Q4.** Define thrashing. Why can't a better replacement policy fix it?

**Answer:** Thrashing is a state where the combined working set of running processes exceeds physical memory by enough that every evicted page is needed almost immediately, causing continuous major faults that dominate the machine's time. No replacement policy can fix it because the problem is _capacity_ , not _choice_ : whatever pages are resident, the missing ones are needed soon. The only real remedies are reducing the active working set (swap out or kill a process) or adding RAM.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Reference stream `A B C A B D A B C D`, 3 frames, using FIFO. Count misses.

**Answer:**

* A: miss, load. Mem={A}.
* B: miss, load. Mem={A,B}.
* C: miss, load. Mem={A,B,C}.
* A: hit. Mem={A,B,C}.
* B: hit. Mem={A,B,C}.
* D: miss, evict A (oldest). Mem={B,C,D}.
* A: miss, evict B. Mem={C,D,A}.
* B: miss, evict C. Mem={D,A,B}.
* C: miss, evict D. Mem={A,B,C}.
* D: miss, evict A. Mem={B,C,D}.

Total misses: 7 out of 10 references.

**Q6.** Same reference stream, 3 frames, using LRU. Count misses.

**Answer:**

* A: miss. Mem={A}.
* B: miss. Mem={A,B}.
* C: miss. Mem={A,B,C}.
* A: hit. (LRU order: B, C, A → A most recent.)
* B: hit. (Order: C, A, B.)
* D: miss. Evict C (least recent). Mem={A,B,D}. Order: A, B, D.
* A: hit. Order: B, D, A.
* B: hit. Order: D, A, B.
* C: miss. Evict D (least recent). Mem={A,B,C}. Order: A, B, C.
* D: miss. Evict A. Mem={B,C,D}.

Total misses: 6 out of 10. LRU beats FIFO by 1 miss on this stream.

**Q7.** A workload's working set is 6 GB. Physical memory is 4 GB. The disk delivers 500 MB/s (4 KB page = 8 µs per page). Estimate the maximum sustainable throughput of "useful" work if every access is a miss.

**Answer:** With every access a major fault, the CPU is blocked for disk I/O on every reference. If each page takes 8 µs to fetch, the system can service at most 1 / (8 µs) = 125 K page faults per second. Even if each page served, say, 1000 memory references before the next miss (locality at page granularity), that's 125 million memory references per second — two to three orders of magnitude slower than the CPU's actual memory bandwidth.

In practice, thrashing workloads see CPU utilization of under 10% and throughput collapses to a small fraction of the machine's non-thrashing rate. The only fix is to reduce the working set or add RAM.

**Q8.** A system implements Clock with a single hand. On average, how many pages does the hand visit per eviction? What determines the answer?

**Answer:** The hand visits, on average, `1 / (1 - R̄)` pages per eviction, where `R̄` is the fraction of pages with R = 1 when the hand arrives. If 50% of pages have R = 1 on arrival (moderately active), the hand visits 2 pages per eviction on average. If 90% have R = 1 (very active workload), it visits 10 pages — which amounts to clearing most R bits before finding a victim.

What determines R̄: the rate of page accesses relative to the rate of evictions. If evictions are rare (memory pressure is low), most pages have been touched since the last hand sweep, so R̄ is high. If evictions are frequent (memory pressure is high), the hand sweeps quickly, R bits get cleared between accesses, and R̄ is lower.

Two-handed Clock exists to decouple these rates: the leading hand clears R bits at a tunable pace, keeping the lagging hand's R̄ low and predictable regardless of eviction rate.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** LRU is an approximation of Belady's MIN based on a statistical claim: "recent past predicts near future." This claim is true for most programs. But some workloads violate it. Name one such workload and explain what replacement policy might serve it better.

**Answer:** _Sequential scans of large data_ violate LRU's assumption. Consider a program that reads a 100 GB file sequentially once. Each page is touched once, then never again. Under LRU, every page is "most recently used" at the moment it's read, so LRU treats the page as fresh and worth keeping. The result: the scan evicts the actually-hot pages (code, metadata, hash tables) in favor of stream-read pages that will never be touched again.

A better policy for this workload: **MRU** (Most Recently Used) or **scan-resistant** policies that recognize sequential access patterns and drop the read-once page immediately. Linux's `fadvise(FADV_DONTNEED)` and `MADV_SEQUENTIAL` hints allow programs to tell the kernel "I'm doing a one-time scan, don't pollute the cache." The kernel then uses a policy closer to MRU for that file.

More generally, the lesson: a single policy cannot serve all workloads. Real kernels combine a base policy (LRU-like) with workload-specific hints and separate-tier tracking. Linux's active/inactive list, ZFS's ARC (adaptive replacement cache), and databases' buffer-pool managers all encode some form of this lesson.

**Q10.** Thrashing is a system-level failure whose cure is not a better policy but a better resource allocation. Compare this to other OS-level resource problems (CPU starvation, disk queue overload, network congestion) and extract a general principle about when mechanism is the answer and when policy is.

**Answer:** Mechanism defines _what is possible_ ; policy defines _what to do given choices_. When demand fits capacity, policy matters — a good scheduler or replacement algorithm can deliver near-optimal throughput and latency. When demand exceeds capacity, policy merely reshuffles who suffers. No scheduling algorithm can give CPU time to every process when the sum of demands exceeds 100% CPU; no replacement algorithm can keep the working set resident when the working set exceeds RAM; no queueing algorithm can serve more requests than the disk can drain.

The general principle: **policy is a second-order lever that matters only in the over-provisioned regime.** In the under-provisioned regime, the right response is to change the mechanism, the scale, or the offered load — add CPUs, add RAM, add disks, or push load back to clients. Operations engineers recognize this pattern: when a system is slow but resources are busy but not saturated, tune policy. When a system is slow because a resource is saturated, the only real fix is capacity.

This principle applies at every layer:

* **CPU starvation:** a low-priority process never runs. Cure: fairness policy (CFS). Works if CPU has slack.
* **Scheduler thrashing:** ten processes all need CPU, scheduler overhead dominates. Cure: reduce runnable count. No scheduler can make 10 processes run on 1 CPU _fast_.
* **Disk queue overload:** requests accumulate faster than the disk drains. Cure: admission control, not a better I/O scheduler.
* **Network congestion:** packets queue faster than the link drains. Cure: backpressure and AIMD (additive increase, multiplicative decrease), not clever queueing.

The OS's replacement policy is one manifestation of a more general design wisdom: _at saturation, the only decision is who to reject._ Modern OS designers know this, which is why Linux's OOM killer exists and why high-performance servers often reject requests under load rather than queueing them. Thrashing was the original example; the lesson applies everywhere.

---

## Chapter 16. Concurrency — Threads and Race Conditions

### 16.1 A Counter That Cannot Count

Consider the following program:
    
    
    int counter = 0;
    
    void *increment(void *arg) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 1000000; i++) {
            counter = counter + 1;
        }
        return NULL;
    }
    
    int main(void) {
        pthread_t t1, t2;
        pthread_create(&t1, NULL, increment, NULL);
        pthread_create(&t2, NULL, increment, NULL);
        pthread_join(t1, NULL);
        pthread_join(t2, NULL);
        printf("counter = %d\n", counter);
        return 0;
    }
    

Two threads. Each increments `counter` one million times. The expected answer: `2000000`.

Run it on any multicore machine and you get something like:
    
    
    $ ./counter
    counter = 1289415
    $ ./counter
    counter = 1548203
    $ ./counter
    counter = 2000000
    $ ./counter
    counter = 1087411
    

The output is _different every time_. Sometimes it is right. Most times it is wrong. Worse, there is no pattern — the same program run twice produces different results.

This is the canonical **race condition**. The counter cannot count, because two threads are racing to update it and the hardware does not guarantee they take turns. Updates get lost.

This chapter opens the concurrency arc. Chapters 2-8 solved CPU virtualization: one CPU, many processes. Chapters 9-15 solved memory virtualization: one RAM, many address spaces. Now the problem is different: within a single process, several _threads_ share one address space and need to coordinate their updates to shared memory. The OS helps by providing threads, but the coordination is ultimately a joint effort between the OS, the hardware, and the programmer.

This chapter introduces **Threading Plan C: The Mutex.** We start with the broken counter above and spend six chapters turning it into a correct concurrent program, along the way learning locks, condition variables, semaphores, and the deadlock and race bugs that make concurrency the hardest topic in this book.

### 16.2 Threads

#### Example: Threads within a process

A **thread** is an independent stream of execution that shares an address space with other threads in the same process. Every thread has:

* Its own program counter (so different threads can be at different points in the code)
* Its own stack (so local variables and function calls don't interfere)
* Its own set of CPU registers

What threads _share_ :

* The code (text) segment
* The data and heap segments
* Open file descriptors, signal handlers, etc.

Two threads in the same process see the same `counter` variable. They see the same `malloc`'d objects. They see the same open files. They just have independent control flow.

#### Pattern

A process is fundamentally "an instance of a program." A thread is fundamentally "an instance of execution within a program." The OS schedules both; context-switching between threads of the same process is cheaper than between different processes (no page table change, no TLB flush if ASIDs are not used), which is why multithreaded programs are preferred over multiprocess programs when the workload needs shared memory.

#### Definition: Thread

**Definition:** A _thread_ is an execution context — program counter, stack, and register state — that shares an address space with other threads in the same process. Multiple threads in one process can execute different code paths simultaneously on multiple CPU cores, while accessing the same global and heap data.

#### How threads are created

POSIX threads (`pthread`) are the standard C interface on Unix. The basic call:
    
    
    pthread_create(&thread_id, attrs, function, arg);
    

Creates a new thread that starts running `function(arg)` concurrently with the caller. The caller continues its own execution. Eventually, both threads may be running on different cores.

`pthread_join(thread_id, NULL)` blocks until the named thread finishes. Analogous to `wait` for processes.

### 16.3 Why the Counter Breaks

#### Example: The three machine instructions

The C statement `counter = counter + 1` compiles to roughly three machine instructions:
    
    
    mov eax, [counter]    ; load the current value into register EAX
    add eax, 1            ; increment the register
    mov [counter], eax    ; store the register back into memory
    

Three steps: **load, modify, store.**

Now imagine two threads each want to do `counter = counter + 1`, starting when `counter = 5`.

**Possible execution order A (correct):**
    
    
    Thread 1:  load eax, counter     (eax=5)
    Thread 1:  add eax, 1             (eax=6)
    Thread 1:  store counter, eax     (counter=6)
    Thread 2:  load eax, counter     (eax=6)
    Thread 2:  add eax, 1             (eax=7)
    Thread 2:  store counter, eax     (counter=7)
    

Final `counter = 7`. Each increment is seen.

**Possible execution order B (broken):**
    
    
    Thread 1:  load eax, counter     (eax=5)
    Thread 2:  load eax, counter     (eax=5)
    Thread 1:  add eax, 1             (eax=6)
    Thread 2:  add eax, 1             (eax=6)
    Thread 1:  store counter, eax     (counter=6)
    Thread 2:  store counter, eax     (counter=6)
    

Final `counter = 6`. One increment is _lost_. Both threads read the same value, both added one, both wrote 6. The second store didn't overwrite a _newer_ value; it overwrote the old value and erased thread 1's update.

#### Pattern

The bug is not about which thread is "fast" or "slow." Both possibilities can happen on the same hardware depending on how the OS scheduler interleaves the threads, whether a timer interrupt falls between the load and store, whether one core is cached and another is not — a thousand little things. The order _is not deterministic_. The hardware is free to pick any interleaving, and every execution can pick a different one.

Even for this toy example the combinatorics explode. With two threads each executing 3 instructions, the number of distinct interleavings is (63)=20\binom{6}{3} = 20(36​)=20. For 2 threads of NNN instructions each it is (2NN)\binom{2N}{N}(N2N​), which grows exponentially: 252 for N=5N=5N=5, 184,756 for N=10N=10N=10. And of those 20 interleavings in our 3-instruction case, only 2 yield the correct answer — the ones where one thread's full load-modify-store completes before the other's begins. The other 18 lose at least one update. The bug's rate in practice is not quite 18/20 = 90% (some interleavings are far more likely than others on real hardware), but it is high enough that the broken counter example almost always produces a wrong answer in a tight loop. Hand-checking every interleaving is hopeless; correctness has to come from argument about invariants (the job of the next five chapters) rather than enumeration of traces.

#### Definition: Race condition

**Definition:** A _race condition_ is a situation in which the outcome of a computation depends on the timing or interleaving of concurrent operations. Race conditions are bugs: programs with race conditions may work most of the time, then fail under different scheduling or load, producing incorrect or inconsistent results.

#### Common Misconception

_"Adding`volatile` fixes the bug."_

`volatile` tells the compiler not to cache `counter` in a register across statements. It does not prevent two threads from interleaving their load-modify-store sequences. The bug is not in the compiler; it is in the interleaving. `volatile` is a tool for memory-mapped I/O and signal handlers, not for thread synchronization.

### 16.4 Critical Sections

#### Example: Identifying the problem region

The broken region is the three-instruction sequence load-modify-store. During those three instructions, if another thread interrupts, the update can be lost.

We want this property: when a thread executes load-modify-store on `counter`, _no other thread is allowed to touch`counter` until the current one finishes._

#### Definition: Critical section

**Definition:** A _critical section_ is a sequence of instructions that accesses shared data and must execute without interference from other threads. If multiple threads access the same shared data concurrently, and at least one access is a write, those accesses must be inside a critical section to avoid race conditions.

#### Pattern

The concurrent program's correctness reduces to identifying critical sections and ensuring **mutual exclusion** — that is, at most one thread executes a given critical section at a time.

#### What we need from the system

A mechanism that we can wrap around any critical section:
    
    
    enter_critical_section();
    counter = counter + 1;
    leave_critical_section();
    

Between `enter` and `leave`, we want a guarantee: no other thread is in a critical section for `counter` right now. That mechanism is the **lock** or **mutex**. Chapter 17 describes how it works. This chapter establishes _why it is needed._

### 16.5 The OS's Role and the Programmer's Role

Concurrency is hard because the responsibility is _divided_ :

* **Hardware** provides atomic instructions and memory-ordering guarantees (or doesn't — which matters in surprising ways).
* **OS** provides thread scheduling and primitives (locks, condition variables, semaphores).
* **Programmer** must use those primitives correctly to make programs race-free.

No single layer can solve the problem alone.

#### Example: What the OS cannot do

Could the OS simply make every assignment to a shared variable atomic? No — for three reasons:

  1. **The hardware doesn't support it at scale.** Making every memory access atomic would serialize all memory traffic. Performance would collapse.
  2. **The OS doesn't know what is shared.** A variable is just a memory location. Whether it is accessed by multiple threads is a property of the program, not of the memory. The OS has no way to infer this.
  3. **Even if it did, single instructions aren't the right granularity.** A critical section often spans multiple operations (read two fields and write back a derived value). Atomicity at the _instruction_ level doesn't give atomicity at the _operation_ level.

So the OS provides tools and the programmer uses them. The programmer must annotate their own critical sections.

#### Pattern

Concurrency cannot be transparent. Unlike address spaces (transparently private per process) or scheduling (transparently multiplexed CPU), concurrency requires _programmer awareness_. Getting concurrency right demands understanding the machine's concurrency model and applying primitives like locks. This is why concurrency is hard: it breaks the abstraction shield and exposes the programmer directly to the machine's nature.

### 16.6 Atomicity, Visibility, Ordering

The concurrency problem has three distinct aspects, each of which can break independently:

#### Atomicity

An operation is **atomic** if it appears to execute as one indivisible step — either it hasn't happened yet or it has completed, never in the middle. `counter = counter + 1` is _not_ atomic, as we saw.

Hardware provides atomic primitives: a single-word read is atomic (you never see half-updated bytes on a properly-aligned access). A single-word write is atomic. But a read-modify-write is _not_ atomic unless you use a special instruction (e.g., x86's `lock add` or the `cmpxchg` instruction — Chapter 17).

#### Visibility

Even if two threads run in some order, a write by thread 1 must eventually _become visible_ to thread 2. On a single-core CPU, visibility is immediate. On multicore with per-core caches, a write may sit in core 1's cache for some time before propagating. Until then, core 2 reads a stale value.

Hardware cache coherence protocols (MESI and cousins) ensure writes eventually propagate. But the _timing_ of propagation — how soon a read sees a recent write — is not instantaneous, and relying on it is fragile.

#### Ordering

Compilers and CPUs reorder instructions for performance. A write followed by an unrelated read may be reordered to read-then-write if the compiler determines the outcome is the same. From the writing thread's perspective, the program behavior is unchanged. But from another thread's perspective, the write happened _after_ the read, not before. This is called **memory reordering** and can produce surprising behavior in concurrent code.

#### Pattern

Synchronization primitives (locks, condition variables, atomics) address all three aspects: they make the locked region appear atomic, ensure writes in the region are visible to subsequent readers, and establish ordering between locked regions across threads. When we introduce locks in Chapter 17, keep these three aspects in mind — the lock does all of them at once.

#### Common Misconception

_"Multicore machines are just faster single-core machines."_

No. Multicore machines have fundamentally different concurrency behavior than single-core. On a single core, a sequential program can only be interrupted between instructions, not during them; atomicity at the instruction level is free. On multicore, two threads can be truly simultaneous; any sequence the programmer treats as a single logical step must be explicitly made atomic. Visibility and ordering introduce effects that simply do not exist on single-core.

Much legacy code was written for single-core machines and survives by accident. Moving such code to multicore exposes latent race conditions. Linus Torvalds called this "the hardest thing about the transition to multicore."

### 16.7 Thread Safety

A function is **thread-safe** if it can be called simultaneously from multiple threads without causing incorrect behavior. Some functions in the C standard library are thread-safe; some are not. The `strtok` function, for example, is not thread-safe (it uses a static internal state). The replacement `strtok_r` takes a caller-provided state and is thread-safe.

A shared data structure (list, hash table, tree) is thread-safe if its operations can be invoked simultaneously from multiple threads and maintain the data structure's invariants. Not all libraries provide thread-safe data structures; some provide thread-safe variants explicitly.

#### Pattern

Thread-safety is a property of code, not of data. The same piece of data can be accessed safely by one thread or safely by many depending on what code does the access. Documenting thread-safety is a library's responsibility; respecting it is the caller's.

### 16.8 Chapter Summary

* A **thread** is an execution context (PC, stack, registers) that shares an address space with other threads in the same process.
* Multiple threads executing on shared data can produce a **race condition** : the program's result depends on the nondeterministic interleaving of instructions.
* `counter = counter + 1` is three instructions (load, modify, store). Two threads' sequences can interleave such that one update is lost.
* A **critical section** is a code region that accesses shared data and must not be interrupted by other threads' critical sections on the same data.
* Correct concurrent programs require the programmer to identify critical sections and use synchronization primitives (locks, condition variables, semaphores) to enforce **mutual exclusion**.
* Concurrency has three aspects: atomicity, visibility, and ordering. Synchronization addresses all three.

Chapter 17 introduces the **lock** — the primary primitive for mutual exclusion — and shows how hardware atomic instructions (test-and-set, compare-and-swap) make correct lock implementations possible. We will fix the broken counter. The Plan C thread continues.

### 16.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What is the difference between a process and a thread?

**Answer:** A _process_ is an isolated execution environment with its own address space, file descriptors, and so on. A _thread_ is an execution context within a process — its own program counter, stack, and registers — that shares the process's address space with other threads. Two threads in one process see the same global variables and heap; two threads in different processes see only what they explicitly share (IPC, shared memory, files). Context-switching between threads is cheaper than between processes because the page table and many kernel structures are preserved.

**Q2.** The statement `counter = counter + 1` produces a race condition with two threads. Why would `counter++` have the same problem?

**Answer:** Both compile to the same three-instruction sequence: load the current value, increment, store. `++` is not a guarantee of atomicity — it is a notational convenience for the same load-modify-store pattern. Any language-level expression that implements "read, transform, write" is vulnerable to race conditions between two threads, regardless of the syntactic sugar used.

**Q3.** Define critical section and mutual exclusion.

**Answer:** A _critical section_ is a region of code that accesses shared data and must not execute concurrently with other critical sections accessing the same data. _Mutual exclusion_ is the property that at most one thread executes a given critical section at any time. Synchronization primitives (locks) enforce mutual exclusion around user-identified critical sections.

**Q4.** Concurrency correctness has three aspects — atomicity, visibility, ordering. Briefly explain each.

**Answer:**

* _Atomicity:_ an operation appears to execute all-at-once from other threads' perspective; no partially-completed state is observable.
* _Visibility:_ a write by one thread eventually becomes observable by other threads. On multicore with caches, this is not instantaneous without synchronization.
* _Ordering:_ the apparent order of operations is consistent across threads. Hardware and compilers may reorder instructions; synchronization prevents reorderings that would violate the program's intent.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Two threads each run `counter += 1` once, starting from `counter = 0`. List every possible final value of `counter`.

**Answer:** `1` or `2`. The canonical interleavings produce these:

* If thread 1 completes its load-modify-store before thread 2 begins, final value is 2.
* If both threads load 0 before either stores, both add 1 to 0, both store 1. Final value is 1.

On a multicore with store buffers, you might naïvely think a thread could read a very stale value from cache and produce 0, but hardware cache coherence ensures each thread's final read/write is consistent with the sequence _it observed_. Some reordering effects become visible on weaker memory models, but for a simple counter the outcomes are 1 and 2. The key point: _no sequential single-thread execution_ could produce 1 starting from 0 and incrementing twice. 1 is a concurrency artifact.

**Q6.** Suppose you run the broken counter program 100 times. Sometimes the result is exactly `2000000`. How is it possible for a race-prone program to occasionally produce the correct answer?

**Answer:** Race conditions don't _force_ incorrect results — they _permit_ them. If, during a particular execution, the scheduler happens to run each thread to completion without interleaving their load-modify-store sequences, or if each interleaving happens to pick an ordering where no updates are lost, the final answer is correct. This is precisely what makes race conditions insidious: _they may not manifest during testing_ , but they can manifest in production when the timing is different. A race-free program is correct by construction; a race-prone program that happens to produce correct results is correct by luck.

**Q7.** Write a short C program using two threads in which, without synchronization, thread 1 might read from a variable that thread 2 has not yet written — producing uninitialized-memory behavior. Describe the bug and how it relates to visibility.

**Answer:**
    
    
    int ready = 0;
    int data = 0;
    
    void *producer(void *arg) {
        data = 42;       // (A)
        ready = 1;       // (B)
        return NULL;
    }
    
    void *consumer(void *arg) {
        while (!ready) { }  // (C) spin until ready
        printf("%d\n", data); // (D)
        return NULL;
    }
    

The bug: the consumer observes `ready = 1` and reads `data`, expecting `42`. But:

* _Without atomicity:_ trivial in this example, since single-word reads/writes are atomic on aligned data. Not the issue here.
* _Without visibility:_ the producer's write to `data` may sit in a store buffer on its core; the consumer on another core may see `ready = 1` before `data = 42` has propagated. The consumer reads `data = 0`.
* _Without ordering:_ the compiler or CPU may reorder (A) and (B), completing the store to `ready` before the store to `data`. Consumer again reads `data = 0`.

Visibility is the most subtle: everything happens in the intended program order, but caches haven't synchronized yet. Adding a _memory barrier_ or using `atomic_store_explicit` with release/acquire semantics ensures both the order and the visibility. A lock or a condition variable gives both as a consequence of how they're implemented.

**Q8.** The `strtok` function in C's standard library is not thread-safe. Explain what kind of internal state it maintains and how two threads calling `strtok` concurrently can produce incorrect results.

**Answer:** `strtok(str, delim)` saves a pointer to its position in the string in a _static_ variable. On the next call with `NULL`, it picks up where it left off. If two threads call `strtok` concurrently, they stomp on each other's static state: thread 1's saved pointer is overwritten by thread 2's call, so when thread 1 calls `strtok(NULL, ...)` again, it gets a pointer into thread 2's string, not its own.

The result can be anything: reading past the end of an unrelated string, crashing, returning a nonsense token. `strtok_r` — the reentrant variant — takes a caller-provided pointer instead of using internal state, making each thread's state independent and the function thread-safe.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** The broken counter appears in virtually every concurrency course as the introductory example. Why is such a tiny, artificial example effective pedagogy, and what real-world systems suffer from essentially the same bug?

**Answer:** The tiny example works because it isolates the _core_ concurrency phenomenon — a shared mutable variable with nondeterministic interleaving — without distracting mechanics. The reader can hold the entire program in their head and still be surprised by the outcome. Surprising students is the pedagogical goal: "I thought 2,000,000 was deterministic; the machine says no."

The same bug appears at scale in:

* **Database transactions without locking.** Two transactions read a row, compute a new value, and write it back. Lost update, losing a deposit or double-spending an item.
* **Reference counting.** Two threads decrement a refcount, both reach zero at the same time, both free the object. Use-after-free.
* **Increment counters in metrics / telemetry.** A heavily-contended counter loses a nontrivial fraction of increments. Observability breaks: dashboards show lower rates than reality.
* **Bank account updates.** The classic textbook example — two simultaneous deposits, both read balance 100, both add their amount, both write back. One deposit vanishes.
* **Inventory systems.** Two requests check stock, both see 1, both decrement to 0, both ship. You oversell.

Each is the same bug the counter example demonstrates, dressed in application-specific clothing. The counter is not artificial; it is the essential form of a class of bugs that shows up constantly in real systems. Teaching it first is efficient.

**Q10.** A colleague proposes: "Just run all concurrent programs on a single core. Then no race conditions can occur." Evaluate this proposal seriously. What does it miss?

**Answer:** The proposal is superficially attractive and wrong in multiple ways:

  1. **It does not eliminate races, only mutates them.** On a single core, two threads do not run simultaneously, but they can still interleave at the instruction level (or between instructions, at preemption points). `counter = counter + 1` takes three instructions; a timer interrupt between the load and store still allows the other thread to run, read the old value, and cause a lost update. Single-core reduces the probability of some race timings but does not eliminate them.

  2. **It abandons multicore performance.** Modern machines have 8-128 cores. Restricting to one core leaves most of the hardware idle. Performance for anything CPU-bound collapses by a factor of N.

  3. **It does not address I/O-driven concurrency.** Even on one CPU, an I/O completion interrupt can preempt execution at an arbitrary instruction. The same race between two "non-simultaneous" execution contexts happens.

  4. **It forbids legitimate concurrent workloads.** Servers, databases, and browsers rely on true parallelism. The premise that you can avoid concurrency by running on one core assumes your workload doesn't need parallelism — which most do.

  5. **It does not avoid visibility or ordering hazards in distributed systems.** If the program interacts with remote services, the remote side is its own concurrency domain; running locally on one core doesn't help.

The deeper lesson: concurrency is not an artifact of multicore hardware. It arises whenever two execution contexts share mutable state, whether they run simultaneously (multicore) or interleaved (single-core with preemption). The right response is not to avoid concurrency but to reason about it with the right tools — locks, immutability, transactional state, message passing. The next five chapters build those tools.

---

## Chapter 17. Locks and Hardware Atomic Instructions

### 17.1 What We Need

Chapter 16 left us with a counter that loses updates because two threads execute `counter = counter + 1` concurrently. The fix is mutual exclusion: only one thread at a time may be in the critical section that reads, modifies, and writes the shared variable.

We want a primitive:
    
    
    lock(&m);
    counter = counter + 1;
    unlock(&m);
    

The promise: between `lock` and `unlock`, no other thread holds `&m`. Any other thread that calls `lock(&m)` waits until the current holder calls `unlock`.

Call this primitive a **lock** or a **mutex** (short for _mutual exclusion_). This chapter explains how a lock is _built_. The construction is surprisingly deep — naive implementations are either wrong or unacceptably slow, and correct implementations require cooperation between hardware (atomic instructions) and the OS (blocking and waking).

Plan C continues: the broken counter from Chapter 16 becomes correct by the end of this chapter.

### 17.2 What a Lock Must Guarantee

Three properties:

  1. **Mutual exclusion.** At most one thread holds the lock at a time. Every thread in its critical section is alone.
  2. **Progress (no deadlock).** If no thread holds the lock and some thread wants to, some thread must eventually acquire it. The lock doesn't freeze when it's available.
  3. **Bounded waiting (fairness).** A thread trying to acquire the lock doesn't wait forever while other threads keep acquiring and releasing it. (Some locks relax this.)

These three come from Dijkstra's 1965 analysis of the mutual exclusion problem. They are the minimum; real locks also want low overhead in the uncontended case and efficient blocking in the contended case.

### 17.3 First Attempt: Just a Flag

#### Example: The naive attempt
    
    
    int locked = 0;
    
    void lock() {
        while (locked == 1) { /* wait */ }
        locked = 1;
    }
    
    void unlock() {
        locked = 0;
    }
    

Does this work? No. It suffers the same bug as the counter.

Thread 1 sees `locked == 0`, is about to set `locked = 1`. Thread 2, on another core, sees `locked == 0` at the same time. Both set `locked = 1`, both enter the critical section. Mutual exclusion fails.

The sequence of "read `locked`, check, write `locked`" is itself not atomic. We built a lock using ordinary memory operations — the same operations that needed protection in the first place. Circular.

#### Pattern

To build mutual exclusion, we need a _primitive_ that is already atomic at the hardware level — something that reads a memory location and conditionally writes it in a single indivisible step. No sequence of ordinary loads and stores can achieve this on multicore hardware without help. Hardware support is required.

### 17.4 Hardware Atomic Instructions

Modern CPUs provide special instructions that read-modify-write a memory location atomically. Three common ones:

#### Test-and-Set (TAS)

Atomically: read the value at an address, write a new value, return the old value.
    
    
    // Pseudocode for hardware test-and-set.
    // The hardware guarantees that the read and write inside the
    // atomic block below happen as a single, indivisible memory
    // transaction — no other core's load or store to *addr can
    // be interleaved between them. "return old" is just the
    // function returning a register value; it sits outside the
    // atomic transaction.
    int test_and_set(int *addr, int new_val) {
        int old;
        atomic {
            old = *addr;       // read  ┐ one
            *addr = new_val;   // write ┘ indivisible transaction
        }
        return old;
    }
    

On x86, the equivalent is `xchg` (which is implicitly `lock`-prefixed for memory operands) or `lock bts`. ARM uses load-linked/store-conditional pairs (`ldrex/strex` on ARMv7, `ldxr/stxr` on ARMv8) that retry until a single uncontested read-modify-write window is observed — a slightly weaker form that builds TAS semantics in software.

#### Compare-and-Swap (CAS)

Atomically: read the value at an address, compare it to an expected value, and _only if they match_ , write a new value. Return whether the write happened.
    
    
    bool compare_and_swap(int *addr, int expected, int new_val) {
        if (*addr == expected) {
            *addr = new_val;
            return true;
        }
        return false;
    }
    

x86: `cmpxchg` (with `lock` prefix for atomicity). ARM: `ldrex/strex`. This is a more general primitive than TAS: it lets you conditionally update based on whether the value is what you last saw.

#### Fetch-and-Add (FAA)

Atomically: read a value, add a constant, write it back, return the old value.
    
    
    int fetch_and_add(int *addr, int amount) {
        int old = *addr;
        *addr = old + amount;
        return old;
    }
    

x86: `lock xadd`. Useful for counters that need to be incremented atomically without a lock at all.

#### Pattern

Each of these instructions does in one atomic hardware step what a sequence of ordinary loads and stores cannot. The hardware ensures — via cache coherence protocols with special buses or messages — that during the atomic instruction, no other core can access the memory line being updated. All other cores see the change as complete, or not yet begun, but never mid-flight.

#### Definition: Atomic instruction

**Definition:** An _atomic instruction_ is a CPU instruction that performs a read-modify-write on a memory location as a single indivisible step. During its execution, no other CPU core can observe the location in an intermediate state. Atomic instructions are the foundation on which all mutual-exclusion primitives are built.

### 17.5 Building a Spinlock

#### Example: Test-and-set spinlock

With TAS in hand, a correct spinlock:
    
    
    int locked = 0;
    
    void lock(int *l) {
        while (test_and_set(l, 1) == 1) {
            /* spin */
        }
        // now *l == 1 and we own it
    }
    
    void unlock(int *l) {
        *l = 0;
    }
    

Trace: both threads call `test_and_set(&locked, 1)` simultaneously. The hardware serializes the two instructions. One of them — say thread 1 — executes first. It reads 0, writes 1, returns 0. Loop exits; thread 1 holds the lock.

Thread 2's TAS executes next. It reads 1 (thread 1's write), writes 1 (no change), returns 1. Loop continues; thread 2 spins. When thread 1 calls `unlock`, `*l = 0`. On thread 2's next iteration, TAS reads 0, writes 1, returns 0. Thread 2 enters.

Mutual exclusion holds. Progress holds (whoever TAS's first gets the lock). Fairness is weak — thread 2 could lose every race to an unfortunately-timed thread 3 — but for most workloads, this is acceptable.

#### Pattern: Busy waiting

The spinlock _busy-waits_ : the waiting thread consumes CPU doing nothing but testing the lock. If the critical section is short (a few microseconds or less), the waste is minimal and often better than the alternative (blocking). If the critical section is long or heavily contested, spinning wastes CPU that other threads could use.

#### Definition: Spinlock

**Definition:** A _spinlock_ is a lock implemented by busy-waiting. A thread that cannot acquire the lock repeatedly attempts to acquire it in a tight loop, consuming CPU until the lock becomes available. Spinlocks are efficient when critical sections are short and contention is low.

#### Common Misconception

_"Spinlocks are always bad because they waste CPU."_

Spinlocks are _optimal_ when the lock is held briefly. Blocking a thread (context-switch out, wait on a queue, context-switch back in) costs thousands of cycles. If the lock will be released in a few hundred cycles, spinning is cheaper. Linux's kernel spinlocks are used precisely because most critical sections in the kernel are very short. The problem with spinlocks is contention over long holds, not the mechanism itself.

### 17.6 The Counter, Fixed
    
    
    int counter = 0;
    int counter_lock = 0;
    
    void *increment(void *arg) {
        for (int i = 0; i < 1000000; i++) {
            lock(&counter_lock);
            counter = counter + 1;
            unlock(&counter_lock);
        }
        return NULL;
    }
    

Now the counter cannot lose updates. At any moment, at most one thread is between `lock` and `unlock`, and therefore at most one thread is executing `counter = counter + 1`. The three-instruction sequence is protected. Final value: always `2000000`.

The fix is small (one lock, two lines) and complete. The cost:

* Every increment now takes an atomic instruction plus the lock protocol overhead.
* Contention: when two threads both want the lock, one waits. On four million combined increments across two threads, the total number of lock acquisitions is two million per thread — four million atomic operations total. Much of that time, one thread is spinning.

If we want higher performance, we can use `fetch_and_add(&counter, 1)` directly — no lock at all, just an atomic instruction — but only because increment-by-1 happens to have a matching atomic primitive. For more complex critical sections (multiple fields, conditional updates), the lock is the right tool.

#### Pattern

The lock is a _general-purpose_ primitive. It protects arbitrary critical sections, not just single-word updates. It adds overhead in exchange for generality. Atomic instructions alone cover specific patterns — counters, flag sets, linked-list head swaps — but cannot protect multi-step critical sections without building a lock.

### 17.7 Blocking Locks (Mutexes)

For long critical sections, spinning is wasteful. Replace spinning with blocking: the waiting thread asks the OS to put it to sleep, and the OS wakes it when the lock is released.

#### Example: Mutex using a queue
    
    
    typedef struct {
        int locked;
        queue_t waiters;
    } mutex_t;
    
    void lock(mutex_t *m) {
        while (test_and_set(&m->locked, 1) == 1) {
            // could not acquire; sleep
            enqueue(&m->waiters, current_thread);
            park();  // ask the OS to put this thread to sleep
        }
    }
    
    void unlock(mutex_t *m) {
        m->locked = 0;
        thread_t *w = dequeue(&m->waiters);
        if (w) unpark(w);  // wake one waiter
    }
    

Cost: a lock acquisition under contention now costs a context switch (thousands of cycles). An uncontended acquisition is still one atomic instruction — very fast.

#### Pattern

Modern mutexes blend spinlock and blocking: spin briefly (hoping the current holder releases quickly), then fall back to blocking if still not acquired. Linux's futexes (fast user-space mutexes) implement this pattern: the fast path is one atomic instruction and never enters the kernel; the slow path makes a syscall to block.

#### Common Misconception

_"A mutex is more expensive than a spinlock."_

In the uncontended case, both are essentially equivalent — one atomic instruction. Under contention, the mutex can be _cheaper_ because waiting threads don't consume CPU while blocked; the machine can schedule other productive work. For critical sections longer than a few microseconds, mutexes almost always beat spinlocks on multithreaded systems.

### 17.8 Memory Barriers

Locks must also handle the visibility and ordering aspects of concurrency, not just atomicity.

#### Example: The invisible write

Suppose:
    
    
    int data = 0;
    int ready = 0;
    
    // Thread 1:
    lock(&m);
    data = 42;
    unlock(&m);
    
    // Thread 2:
    lock(&m);
    int d = data;
    unlock(&m);
    

Does thread 2 see `data = 42`? Yes — if the lock implementation includes a **memory barrier** that ensures:

* All writes _before_ `unlock` are visible to other cores (release semantics).
* All reads _after_ `lock` see the latest committed values (acquire semantics).

Without barriers, thread 1's write to `data` might sit in a store buffer when thread 2 reads. Thread 2's read of `data` might be prefetched from an older cache line. Either way, thread 2 could observe `data = 0` despite the locks.

#### Pattern

Locks are bundled with appropriate memory barriers. You don't need to think about barriers when using a lock — the lock guarantees visibility and ordering as part of its contract. You only need to think about barriers when writing lock-free code with raw atomics.

#### Definition: Memory barrier

**Definition:** A _memory barrier_ (or _fence_) is a CPU instruction that prevents the CPU and compiler from reordering memory operations across the barrier. _Acquire_ barriers prevent later reads from moving before earlier ones. _Release_ barriers prevent earlier writes from moving after later ones. _Full_ barriers prevent both. Synchronization primitives use barriers to ensure visibility and ordering alongside atomicity.

### 17.9 Lock-Free Alternatives

Atomic instructions allow some operations to be made correct _without_ locks. Called **lock-free**.

#### Example: Lock-free counter using FAA
    
    
    int counter = 0;
    
    void increment() {
        fetch_and_add(&counter, 1);
    }
    

No lock. No waiter queue. The hardware atomically increments. Concurrent incrementers cannot lose updates, because each FAA is indivisible.

Lock-free data structures (queues, stacks, hash tables) can provide correctness with much higher throughput than lock-based equivalents under high contention. They are famously hard to implement correctly: the ABA problem, reader/writer memory ordering, and epoch-based reclamation all come up.

#### Pattern

Lock-free programming trades "correctness is relatively easy to reason about" (locks) for "scalability under heavy contention" (lock-free). Most systems use locks except in specific hot paths where lock-free is worth the complexity.

### 17.10 Chapter Summary

* A **lock** provides mutual exclusion around a critical section. Properties: mutual exclusion, progress, (usually) fairness.
* Locks cannot be built from ordinary reads and writes — a lock implemented that way suffers the same race conditions it tries to prevent.
* **Atomic instructions** (test-and-set, compare-and-swap, fetch-and-add) are the hardware primitive. They perform read-modify-write atomically at the cache-line level.
* A **spinlock** uses an atomic instruction in a loop. Efficient for short critical sections; wasteful for long ones.
* A **mutex** or **blocking lock** has the spinlock's fast path but blocks (via the OS scheduler) under contention. Efficient for long critical sections.
* Locks also handle **visibility** and **ordering** via memory barriers, not just atomicity.
* **Lock-free** programming uses atomic instructions directly, without a lock, for specific operations where the atomic is sufficient.

In Chapter 18 we apply locks to concurrent data structures: linked lists, hash tables, queues. We will see that "add a lock" is not always the right design, and that scalability often requires finer-grained locking or lock-free approaches. The Plan C thread continues.

### 17.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Why can't a lock be implemented using only ordinary loads and stores on a multicore machine?

**Answer:** Any sequence of ordinary loads and stores is itself subject to the race conditions it is trying to prevent. Two threads can simultaneously execute "read the flag; if zero, set to one" and both succeed in setting the flag, because the sequence is not atomic. Building mutual exclusion from non-atomic primitives is circular. At least one hardware-supported atomic operation is required to break the cycle.

**Q2.** Define test-and-set, compare-and-swap, and fetch-and-add.

**Answer:**

* _Test-and-set:_ atomically read a value at an address, write a new value, return the old value. Used to attempt lock acquisition.
* _Compare-and-swap:_ atomically compare the value at an address to an expected value; if they match, write a new value; return whether the write happened. Used for optimistic updates.
