* _Fetch-and-add:_ atomically add a constant to the value at an address, return the old value. Used for concurrent counters.

All three are indivisible at the hardware level; intermediate states are never observed by other cores.

**Q3.** What is the difference between a spinlock and a blocking mutex, in terms of what a waiting thread does?

**Answer:** A _spinlock_ waiter loops, executing atomic instructions to test the lock. The thread stays runnable and consumes CPU. A _blocking mutex_ waiter, after a brief spin, asks the OS to block the thread (context-switch to another) and to wake it when the lock is released. A spinlock waiter uses CPU while waiting; a mutex waiter does not.

**Q4.** Why is a memory barrier necessary inside a lock implementation?

**Answer:** Without barriers, writes inside the critical section may be reordered or remain cached on one core, so another core acquiring the lock next may not see them. Locks provide _release_ barriers on unlock (publishing prior writes) and _acquire_ barriers on lock (subscribing to the latest published writes), ensuring the critical section's updates are visible to the next locker.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Write a correct spinlock using compare-and-swap instead of test-and-set. Show the `lock` and `unlock` functions.

**Answer:**
    
    
    void lock(int *l) {
        while (!compare_and_swap(l, 0, 1)) {
            /* spin */
        }
    }
    
    void unlock(int *l) {
        *l = 0;  // or: compare_and_swap(l, 1, 0)
    }
    

`compare_and_swap(l, 0, 1)` attempts to transition from 0 to 1 atomically; it succeeds only if the lock is currently free. The loop retries until the transition succeeds.

**Q6.** A programmer increments a shared counter 10 million times from one thread and 10 million times from another. Compare three implementations: (a) `counter++` without synchronization, (b) `counter++` inside a spinlock, (c) `fetch_and_add(&counter, 1)`. Which gives the correct answer? Which is fastest?

**Answer:**

* _(a) Unsynchronized:_ final value is incorrect (much less than 20 million). Fastest per operation but returns a wrong answer.
* _(b) Spinlock:_ final value is correct (20 million). Each increment requires a lock acquisition (atomic instruction) plus a release (one write). Under contention, some increments spin briefly waiting for the other thread. Per-op cost: roughly 100-500 cycles.
* _(c) Fetch-and-add:_ final value is correct (20 million). One atomic instruction per increment. No spinning, no lock. Per-op cost: roughly 20-50 cycles.

The `fetch_and_add` version is both correct and fastest because the operation "atomically increment" matches a hardware primitive exactly. The spinlock version is correct but pays for generality we don't need for this simple update. The lesson: if your critical section is a single atomic-primitive-sized operation, use the atomic directly.

**Q7.** A server process has a critical section that updates a linked list. Average critical-section duration is 10 microseconds. Critical sections are attempted at 100,000 times per second across all threads. Would you use a spinlock or a blocking mutex? Why?

**Answer:** _Blocking mutex._ Reasoning:

* 100,000 acquisitions/sec × 10 µs each = 1 second of cumulative critical-section work per second of wall time. That means under high concurrency, the lock is saturated; waiters accumulate.
* With a spinlock, waiting threads consume CPU. On a 4-core machine, if 4 threads all want the lock, 3 are spinning for ~10 µs each — 30 µs of wasted CPU per contention event, times 100,000 events/sec = 3 seconds of wasted CPU per second. The machine is overloaded just on spinning.
* With a mutex, waiters block. The OS schedules other productive work during their wait. The machine is not saturated by spinning.

For critical sections shorter than a microsecond or two, or for extremely low contention, a spinlock might beat a mutex (no context-switch cost). But 10 µs critical sections at 100 K/sec is squarely in "use a blocking mutex" territory.

**Q8.** A bank-transfer function needs to lock two accounts (the source and the destination) before transferring money. How might this cause problems when two transfers happen at once? (Anticipate deadlock without full detail.)

**Answer:** Consider two simultaneous transfers: one from account A to account B, another from account B to account A. If each transfer acquires the source lock first, then the destination lock:

* Transfer 1: acquires lock on A. Blocks waiting for lock on B.
* Transfer 2: acquires lock on B. Blocks waiting for lock on A.

Both transfers are blocked forever — each holding a lock the other needs, each waiting for a lock the other holds. This is **deadlock.** It breaks the "progress" guarantee: no thread can make progress even though locks are available in some sense (they're each held by the blocked thread, which can never release).

The fix: impose a _lock order_. Always lock accounts in a deterministic order (e.g., lower account number first). Transfer 1 locks A then B; Transfer 2, seeing B < A if that's the order, locks B then A first — but also in the same deterministic order. Wait, concretely: if the rule is "lock lower account number first," Transfer 2 (B-to-A) locks min(A,B) first, which is the same as Transfer 1 locks, so one waits for the other rather than deadlocking.

Deadlock and its prevention are Chapter 21's topic in depth.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** Chapter 17 emphasizes that locks require hardware support (atomic instructions). Meanwhile, Chapter 4 showed that limited direct execution (LDE) requires hardware support (trap instructions). Compare the two: in what sense is the OS "helpless without hardware" for both concurrency and CPU virtualization, and why is this appropriate rather than embarrassing?

**Answer:** In both cases, the OS cannot achieve the required property purely in software — the hardware must provide a primitive that the software alone could not construct. For LDE, the primitive is the trap (user-kernel transition). Without a hardware trap, user code and kernel code would have no way to interact safely. For concurrency, the primitive is the atomic instruction. Without atomics, no software construction can provide mutual exclusion on multicore.

Far from being embarrassing, this reflects a deep and correct design principle: _build abstractions out of primitives that fit the nature of the resource being abstracted._ CPU virtualization requires crossing protection boundaries, which fundamentally requires hardware enforcement of those boundaries. Concurrency control requires serialization of simultaneous operations, which fundamentally requires hardware serialization (via cache-coherence protocols or memory-bus arbitration).

Software builds _policies_ — when to schedule, what to lock around, how to sequence operations. Hardware provides _mechanisms_ — the raw ability to trap, the raw ability to atomically update. OS designers who forget this lesson end up trying to implement concurrency in pure software and finding it works on their testing machine and fails randomly in production. The right separation is: hardware gives one small, correct primitive; software composes that primitive into richer abstractions.

**Q10.** Lock-free programming is faster under high contention but much harder to get right. Speculate on where this tradeoff belongs: when should a system designer reach for locks, and when should they reach for lock-free?

**Answer:** Locks are the default for nearly all concurrent code. They are easy to reason about, they correctly handle the common cases (short sections, moderate contention), and they compose — you can wrap a lock around an arbitrary critical section without understanding the internal algorithm.

Lock-free pays off when:

  1. **Contention is extreme.** Hundreds of threads pounding on a single counter or queue. Lock contention becomes the bottleneck; lock-free structures scale linearly.
  2. **The critical section is tiny.** Single-word updates (refcount, flag) that have an exact atomic primitive — no need for a lock at all.
  3. **Latency matters and blocking is unacceptable.** Real-time systems, kernel IRQ handlers, audio/video path. A blocked thread cannot miss its deadline.
  4. **The operation has a clean lock-free pattern.** Enqueues on single-producer queues, epoch-based memory reclamation, double-buffered state transfers. Not every algorithm has such a pattern.

When NOT to use lock-free:

  1. **Ordinary application code.** The complexity cost almost always exceeds the performance benefit.
  2. **Critical sections that span multiple state changes.** Locks handle this naturally; lock-free requires transactional memory or elaborate protocols.
  3. **Code that must be maintained by many programmers.** Lock-free is notoriously subtle; one wrong memory barrier and correctness is gone. Locks are legible; lock-free is alchemy.

The general principle: **correctness first, performance second, complexity a distant third.** Start with locks. If profiling reveals lock contention is the bottleneck, consider finer-grained locks. Only if fine-grained locks still don't scale, reach for lock-free — and only for the specific hot path that demands it. This is the conventional wisdom of operating-system and database designers, forged from decades of getting it wrong before getting it right.

---

## Chapter 18. Lock-Based Concurrent Data Structures

### 18.1 Beyond the Counter

Chapter 17 fixed the broken counter. In real programs, shared state is rarely a single integer. It is linked lists that queue work, hash tables that map identifiers to objects, trees that index sorted data. Each needs thread-safe access. The question this chapter answers: _how do we lock a data structure?_

The naive answer — "wrap every method in one global lock" — works but often scales terribly. Many real data structures support _more concurrency_ than a single lock allows, and extracting that concurrency is an art. This chapter progresses from the simplest locking (one big lock) through finer-grained locking (one lock per element) to hand-over-hand traversal and reader-writer locks. The Plan C thread continues: we extend locks from protecting one variable to protecting whole collaborative data structures.

### 18.2 Concurrent Counter, Revisited

#### Example: The concurrent counter

Wrap the counter in a struct with its own lock:
    
    
    typedef struct {
        int value;
        pthread_mutex_t lock;
    } counter_t;
    
    void counter_init(counter_t *c) {
        c->value = 0;
        pthread_mutex_init(&c->lock, NULL);
    }
    
    void counter_increment(counter_t *c) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&c->lock);
        c->value++;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&c->lock);
    }
    
    int counter_get(counter_t *c) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&c->lock);
        int v = c->value;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&c->lock);
        return v;
    }
    

Every method locks, reads or modifies, unlocks. Correctness is easy to see: no two method calls overlap, so updates don't interleave.

#### Pattern

This is called **coarse-grained locking** or the _big lock_ approach. One lock guards the entire data structure. Simple to implement, simple to reason about, and scales to a few threads. Under heavier contention (dozens of threads) it becomes the bottleneck: threads serialize even when they're doing non-conflicting things.

#### Scaling problem

Suppose 16 threads each call `counter_increment` in a tight loop — so every operation is _inside_ the critical section and there is essentially no non-critical work to overlap. On a 16-core machine, we'd hope for 16× the throughput of a single thread. Measure it: you get something in the 1×–2× range (typical in-loop contention micro-benchmarks on x86-64 report 1.2×–1.8× for a single coarse lock, dominated by cache-line bouncing and lock-acquire overhead), because only one thread holds the lock at a time. The other 15 are in line. We _bought a multicore machine_ and _got single-core performance._

The pessimistic "1.5×" figure assumes a workload where the critical section dominates — increment a counter, do nothing else. If threads do substantial work _outside_ the critical section, Amdahl-style reasoning applies: throughput improves roughly as 1/(s+(1−s)/p)1/(s + (1-s)/p)1/(s+(1−s)/p) where sss is the serial fraction and ppp is the core count. With a 1% serial fraction, 16 cores deliver about 14× speedup; with a 50% serial fraction, just under 2×. Always measure before assuming a given lock is the bottleneck.

#### Sloppy counter: improve scalability

The classic fix: each thread has a _local_ counter. Periodically, each thread adds its local delta to a global counter under the lock. Reads of the global value are slightly "sloppy" — may be behind by up to `threshold × num_threads`.
    
    
    typedef struct {
        int global;
        int local[MAX_THREADS];
        pthread_mutex_t lock;
        int threshold;
    } sloppy_counter_t;
    
    void sloppy_increment(sloppy_counter_t *c, int tid) {
        c->local[tid]++;
        if (c->local[tid] >= c->threshold) {
            pthread_mutex_lock(&c->lock);
            c->global += c->local[tid];
            c->local[tid] = 0;
            pthread_mutex_unlock(&c->lock);
        }
    }
    

Now most increments are lock-free (just a local array bump). The lock is taken only every `threshold` increments, so contention is divided by `threshold`. At threshold = 1024, contention on the global lock is negligible even with 16 threads.

#### Pattern

**Per-thread state + occasional synchronization** is a general scalability pattern. If you can defer and batch synchronization, you can make concurrent access cheap. The sloppy counter is one instance; Linux's per-CPU counters and Java's `LongAdder` are others.

#### Common Misconception

_"Sloppy counters are wrong because reads lag."_

For many uses, reads don't need to be exact. Metrics, debug counters, approximate progress indicators — all tolerate a small lag. When exact reads are required (e.g., bank balances), sloppy counting is inappropriate. The design tradeoff — give up read freshness for write throughput — must be chosen consciously.

### 18.3 Concurrent Linked List

#### Example: The big-lock linked list
    
    
    typedef struct node {
        int value;
        struct node *next;
    } node_t;
    
    typedef struct {
        node_t *head;
        pthread_mutex_t lock;
    } list_t;
    
    void list_insert(list_t *L, int value) {
        node_t *n = malloc(sizeof(node_t));
        n->value = value;
        pthread_mutex_lock(&L->lock);
        n->next = L->head;
        L->head = n;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&L->lock);
    }
    
    bool list_lookup(list_t *L, int value) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&L->lock);
        node_t *cur = L->head;
        while (cur) {
            if (cur->value == value) {
                pthread_mutex_unlock(&L->lock);
                return true;
            }
            cur = cur->next;
        }
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&L->lock);
        return false;
    }
    

Works. But under heavy lookup workloads, a long list is traversed entirely under the lock, blocking all other operations — even readers that are only examining different parts of the list.

#### Pattern: Hand-over-hand locking

One lock per node. A traversal holds a lock on the _current_ node while acquiring the lock on the _next_ , then releases the previous. At any time, the traversing thread holds at most two locks. Other threads can traverse the same list concurrently, as long as they don't meet at the same node.
    
    
    typedef struct node {
        int value;
        struct node *next;
        pthread_mutex_t lock;
    } node_t;
    
    bool list_lookup(list_t *L, int value) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&L->head->lock);
        node_t *cur = L->head;
        while (cur) {
            if (cur->value == value) {
                pthread_mutex_unlock(&cur->lock);
                return true;
            }
            if (cur->next) pthread_mutex_lock(&cur->next->lock);
            pthread_mutex_unlock(&cur->lock);
            cur = cur->next;
        }
        return false;
    }
    

In theory, this permits many threads to traverse different segments of the list in parallel. In practice, the per-node lock overhead — every node visit is a lock-unlock pair — often makes hand-over-hand _slower_ than the big-lock version under moderate contention. The benefit appears only on very long lists with very high concurrency.

#### Pattern

**Fine-grained locking** is appealing but has high constant-factor overhead. Use it only when you've measured that coarse-grained locking is actually the bottleneck.

#### Common Misconception

_"More locks = more concurrency = better performance."_

Often false. Every additional lock adds overhead to operations that touch it. Many real systems use coarse-grained locks for small structures and fine-grained locks only for very large ones. Java's `ConcurrentHashMap` historically used 16-way striped locking; modern versions use a hybrid of optimistic reads and locking, because pure fine-grained locking's overhead was not worth the concurrency gain on typical workloads.

### 18.4 Concurrent Queue

A queue with a single lock serializes all operations. But in a queue, producers and consumers touch _different ends_. Michael and Scott's two-lock queue exploits this:
    
    
    typedef struct {
        node_t *head;
        node_t *tail;
        pthread_mutex_t head_lock;
        pthread_mutex_t tail_lock;
    } queue_t;
    
    void queue_enqueue(queue_t *q, int value) {
        node_t *n = malloc(sizeof(node_t));
        n->value = value;
        n->next = NULL;
        pthread_mutex_lock(&q->tail_lock);
        q->tail->next = n;
        q->tail = n;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&q->tail_lock);
    }
    
    bool queue_dequeue(queue_t *q, int *value) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&q->head_lock);
        node_t *old = q->head;
        node_t *new_head = old->next;
        if (new_head == NULL) {
            pthread_mutex_unlock(&q->head_lock);
            return false;
        }
        *value = new_head->value;
        q->head = new_head;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&q->head_lock);
        free(old);
        return true;
    }
    

A producer and a consumer can operate simultaneously without blocking each other. Two producers still serialize (they share `tail_lock`); two consumers still serialize (they share `head_lock`). The design uses a **dummy head** (a sentinel node) to decouple the two ends even when the queue is empty.

#### Pattern

**Decompose a data structure into independent regions, and lock each separately.** A queue has two independent ends. A hash table has independent buckets. A tree has independent subtrees. Wherever operations touch disjoint regions, you can scale throughput linearly in the number of concurrent threads.

### 18.5 Concurrent Hash Table

A hash table with one lock serializes all access. A hash table with one lock _per bucket_ permits as many simultaneous operations as there are buckets.
    
    
    #define BUCKETS 1024
    
    typedef struct {
        list_t lists[BUCKETS];  // each list has its own lock
    } hash_t;
    
    void hash_insert(hash_t *H, int key) {
        int b = key % BUCKETS;
        list_insert(&H->lists[b], key);
    }
    
    bool hash_lookup(hash_t *H, int key) {
        int b = key % BUCKETS;
        return list_lookup(&H->lists[b], key);
    }
    

If two operations hash to different buckets, they proceed in parallel. If they hash to the same bucket, they serialize — which, with a well-distributed hash function and 1024 buckets, is rare.

#### Pattern

**Striped locking:** divide the keyspace into N stripes (buckets), use one lock per stripe. Contention is divided by N. The hash function's distribution determines how close to N× the actual speedup is.

This is one of the most common patterns in real concurrent data structures. Java's old `ConcurrentHashMap`, Go's `sync.Map`, C++'s `concurrent_unordered_map`, and countless others use striped locking as the foundation.

#### Common Misconception

_"One lock per key would give perfect concurrency."_

One lock per key means, for a hash table with millions of keys, millions of lock objects. The memory overhead swamps the concurrency benefit. Striped locking with a modest number of stripes (16, 64, 1024) is almost always the right balance: enough stripes to distribute contention, few enough that lock overhead is negligible.

### 18.6 Reader-Writer Locks

Some data structures have mostly reads with rare writes: a configuration structure, a DNS cache, a routing table. Ordinary locks serialize readers needlessly — two readers could safely execute simultaneously.

A **reader-writer lock** permits many simultaneous readers _or_ one exclusive writer, but not both.

#### Example: Using rwlock
    
    
    pthread_rwlock_t rw;
    
    void read_op() {
        pthread_rwlock_rdlock(&rw);
        // multiple readers can be here simultaneously
        ...
        pthread_rwlock_unlock(&rw);
    }
    
    void write_op() {
        pthread_rwlock_wrlock(&rw);
        // writer is alone
        ...
        pthread_rwlock_unlock(&rw);
    }
    

#### Pattern

RW locks sound strictly better than plain locks, but have hidden costs:

* Tracking reader count requires an atomic increment-decrement, which is itself contended.
* A persistent trickle of readers can _starve_ writers (no point in time is reader-free).
* RW lock implementations are complex; a poorly-tuned implementation can be slower than a plain mutex even for read-heavy workloads.

Use RW locks only when reads dominate writes by a factor of 10 or more, and measure to confirm the benefit.

#### Common Misconception

_"Reader-writer locks are a strict improvement over mutexes."_

For moderate contention or low read:write ratios, the overhead of RW locks typically exceeds the concurrency benefit. Many programs that use RW locks would be faster with plain mutexes. Modern alternatives — read-copy-update (RCU) in the Linux kernel, seqlocks — are often better for extreme read-heavy workloads.

### 18.7 Lock Granularity: The Tradeoffs

Every concurrent data structure involves choosing how much state each lock guards. The spectrum:

* **Coarse (one lock for everything):** simple, correct, often fast under low contention, slow under high contention.
* **Medium (lock per logical unit, e.g., bucket):** balances concurrency with overhead. The common choice.
* **Fine (lock per element):** maximum theoretical concurrency but high overhead and complexity. Suitable only for very large, heavily-contended structures.
* **Lock-free:** no locks, only atomic instructions. Maximum scalability, maximum implementation difficulty.

#### Pattern

The right granularity depends on:

* **Read/write ratio.** Read-heavy workloads benefit from RW locks or RCU.
* **Contention pattern.** Uniform random access favors striping; hot-spot access favors per-element locks or lock-free.
* **Data structure size.** Small structures favor coarse locking (overhead of per-element locks is higher than lock contention).
* **Programming effort.** Coarse locks ship tomorrow; fine-grained locks ship next quarter; lock-free ships... someday.

The honest advice: start with the coarsest locking that correctly solves the problem. Profile. If lock contention is measurably hurting performance, move to the next level of granularity. _Never_ design a fine-grained or lock-free data structure from scratch without first establishing that coarser designs are insufficient.

### 18.8 Chapter Summary

* A **concurrent data structure** is one that maintains correctness under multi-threaded access.
* **Coarse-grained locking** (one lock for the whole structure) is simple, correct, and often fast enough.
* **Sloppy counters** use per-thread local state with occasional batched updates to the global state. Reduces contention dramatically at the cost of read freshness.
* **Hand-over-hand locking** permits concurrent linked-list traversal but has high constant-factor overhead.
* **Two-lock queues** separate head and tail locks, allowing producers and consumers to operate in parallel.
* **Striped hash tables** use one lock per bucket. The common case for concurrent maps.
* **Reader-writer locks** permit many simultaneous readers or one exclusive writer. Useful for read-heavy workloads, but have hidden overhead.
* **The right granularity** depends on workload, size, and contention. Start coarse; refine if necessary.

Chapter 19 introduces **condition variables** , the primitive for threads to _wait for events_ rather than _compete for locks_. Producer-consumer, bounded buffers, and blocking queues all need this. The Plan C thread continues.

### 18.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What is coarse-grained locking, and why is it often the right first choice?

**Answer:** _Coarse-grained locking_ protects an entire data structure with a single lock; every operation acquires that lock before accessing any part of the structure. It is simple to implement, easy to reason about, and has low per-operation overhead. When contention is low or critical sections are short, it performs as well as any more complex approach. It is the right first choice because more granular designs should be adopted only when measurement shows the single lock is the bottleneck.

**Q2.** Explain the idea behind a sloppy counter. Why is it faster, and what is the tradeoff?

**Answer:** A sloppy counter gives each thread a private local counter. Increments happen locally without synchronization. Periodically (when the local count exceeds a threshold), a thread atomically adds its local value to the global counter under a lock. This is faster because most increments touch only local memory and cause no contention. The tradeoff: reads of the global counter can be stale by up to `threshold × num_threads` — the value doesn't reflect the pending local increments. Acceptable for metrics; unacceptable for values that must be precise.

**Q3.** In a two-lock queue (Michael-Scott), why is a dummy head node needed?

**Answer:** The dummy node ensures that `head` and `tail` are always distinct pointers, even on an empty queue. Without it, an empty queue would have `head == tail`, so an enqueue (modifying `tail`) and a dequeue (modifying `head`) would both need to synchronize — defeating the purpose of separate locks. With a dummy node, the head and tail point at different nodes always (the first real element is always `head->next`), so enqueues and dequeues operate on distinct memory and can run concurrently.

**Q4.** Why do reader-writer locks help only when reads greatly outnumber writes?

**Answer:** RW locks have overhead that a plain mutex does not: atomic reader-count tracking, separate read/write queues, and sometimes more complex memory barriers. In workloads with balanced reads and writes, this overhead exceeds the concurrency benefit — you pay for machinery to permit parallel reads, but the rare writes still exclude readers, canceling much of the gain. When reads dominate (say, 99% reads), parallel read paths deliver large speedups that more than pay for the overhead.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Rewrite the counter example as a sloppy counter using a threshold of 100. Show the code and estimate throughput improvement over a single-lock counter for 8 threads incrementing 10 million times each.

**Answer:**
    
    
    typedef struct {
        int global;
        int local[MAX_THREADS];
        pthread_mutex_t lock;
    } sloppy_t;
    
    void sloppy_inc(sloppy_t *c, int tid) {
        c->local[tid]++;
        if (c->local[tid] >= 100) {
            pthread_mutex_lock(&c->lock);
            c->global += c->local[tid];
            c->local[tid] = 0;
            pthread_mutex_unlock(&c->lock);
        }
    }
    

Single-lock counter at 8 threads: all lock acquisitions serialize. If each inc takes ~200 cycles under contention, total time ≈ 80 M ops × 200 cycles ≈ 16 B cycles, which is ~5 seconds on a 3 GHz CPU. Effective throughput: ~16 M inc/s on an 8-core machine — roughly the same as one thread.

Sloppy counter, threshold 100: each thread does 99 fast local increments and 1 lock-acquiring batch update per 100 ops. Contention on the global lock is divided by 100. Local ops take ~5 cycles each. Total ≈ 80 M × 0.99 × 5 + 0.8 M × 200 ≈ 400 M + 160 M ≈ 560 M cycles ≈ 0.2 seconds. Throughput ≈ 400 M inc/s — 25× faster than single-lock on 8 cores.

**Q6.** A hash table has 1024 buckets and uses striped locking. A workload has 10,000 threads each performing 1,000 lookups. If the hash distribution is uniform, what fraction of lookup attempts will encounter an already-locked bucket?

**Answer:** For any single lookup, the probability that a specific bucket is currently held by another thread depends on how many other threads are simultaneously operating on that bucket. With uniform distribution, at any moment each thread is (roughly) in one bucket; 10,000 threads distributed across 1,024 buckets means ~10 threads per bucket on average.

At any instant when a thread wants to lock bucket B, the probability some other of the ~9 other threads in bucket B holds the lock depends on what fraction of each thread's time is spent in the critical section. If a lookup takes 1 µs and threads loop every 1 µs, each thread is locking 50% of the time, so the probability some other thread of the 9 in the same bucket is locked is roughly `1 - 0.5^9 ≈ 99.8%`.

Reality: with uniform hashing and 1024 buckets, contention is per-bucket and is itself 10-way serialized, not 10,000-way. The effective throughput is still high — ~1024 × serialized-single-bucket — which is much better than a single-lock table's ~1× throughput. Striping moved the bottleneck from system-wide to per-bucket; with 1024 stripes, the bottleneck is usually no longer locking but hashing or cache traffic.

**Q7.** Suppose the Linux kernel maintains a global list of processes. Would you use a big lock, hand-over-hand locking, or RCU (read-copy-update)? Justify.

**Answer:** The global process list is read extremely frequently (every `ps`, every `/proc` traversal, every accounting update) and modified rarely (process creation/exit). Writes are short; reads can be very long (walking the whole list).

* _Big lock:_ every `ps` blocks process creation for the duration of the walk. Unacceptable — a slow `ps` could cause user processes to block.
* _Hand-over-hand:_ permits parallel readers but the per-node lock overhead is high, and writers still interact with the traversal in complex ways.
* _RCU (Read-Copy-Update):_ readers walk the list with _no locks at all_ ; writers build a new version of the list and atomically swap in the new head. The old version is freed when all readers that might be using it finish. Read cost: zero synchronization. Write cost: higher than normal but rare.

The Linux kernel uses RCU for the process list (and for routing tables, networking data structures, file-system dentries — anywhere reads dominate). It is the right answer for this workload. (RCU is an advanced topic beyond this chapter; the exercise asks you to _recognize_ where it belongs, not to implement it.)

**Q8.** Suppose you are designing a hash table with 128 buckets. A workload has high read-to-write ratio (99:1). Would you use plain mutexes per bucket, reader-writer locks per bucket, or both? Explain.

**Answer:** RW locks per bucket. Reasoning:

* Reads dominate (99%); they are rarely blocked by writes. Most of the time, multiple readers in the same bucket should run in parallel.
* Writes are rare (1%); they do block readers, but briefly.
* Per-bucket RW locks give the best of both worlds: striping across buckets for inter-bucket parallelism, RW within-bucket for intra-bucket read parallelism.

An alternative is RCU (as above), but RCU has complex memory reclamation semantics. For a regular application with a hash table, per-bucket RW locks are simpler and nearly as fast.

Plain mutexes per bucket would work but would serialize readers within a bucket unnecessarily. Given the extreme read skew, RW locks recover most of that lost parallelism.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** The chapter advocates starting with coarse locks and refining only when necessary. Anti-pattern: a novice designer reads this chapter and immediately builds a hand-over-hand linked list and a striped hash table. What goes wrong?

**Answer:** Several things likely go wrong:

  1. **Time-to-delivery explodes.** Fine-grained locking takes 10× longer to get right than a big lock. The program ships late.
  2. **Correctness bugs proliferate.** Each fine-grained structure has its own subtle invariants (hand-over-hand's ordering, RW locks' memory ordering). Novices get these wrong, often in ways that manifest only under rare timing. Bugs escape to production.
  3. **Performance regresses.** Hand-over-hand linked lists are often _slower_ than big-lock lists under moderate contention (because each node lock is ~50 cycles of overhead). The novice measures and sees the "clever" design is worse than the simple one.
  4. **Maintenance burden explodes.** Other engineers reading the code cannot verify correctness by inspection. Bugs compound over time.

The correct workflow: build the simplest correct thing. Measure. If a data structure is a bottleneck, _then_ refine. Most applications never need more than big-lock data structures, because lock contention is rarely the bottleneck — it's usually network, disk, or algorithm complexity.

The deeper lesson: **optimization is a tax on simplicity, and should only be paid when the measurement shows the tax is less than the benefit.** This is one of the most consistently-validated lessons in systems engineering, and one of the hardest for talented-but-inexperienced programmers to internalize.

**Q10.** Concurrent data structures are often designed in pairs: a "simple version" (e.g., `sync.Map` in Go) and a "fast version" (e.g., `map` with `sync.RWMutex`). Actually, Go's `sync.Map` was introduced specifically for read-heavy, contention-heavy workloads where the RWMutex version was too slow. Why might a language standard library offer both rather than replace the simple one?

**Answer:** Different workloads have different optimal designs. Offering both serves the range:

* _Low contention / balanced access:_ plain map + `sync.RWMutex` is fastest and most memory-efficient. Its operations are direct. Its memory layout is cache-friendly. For everyday hashtables, it is the right choice.
* _High contention / read-heavy / key space rarely changes:_ `sync.Map` uses a dual-structure (read-only and dirty maps) so reads are nearly lock-free. For hot caches with stable keys, it is dramatically faster. But for workloads with many writes or frequently-changing keys, `sync.Map` is _slower_ than the plain map — the dual-structure overhead exceeds the benefit.

If the language replaced the simple version with the fast version, users with simple workloads would pay for machinery they don't need. By offering both, the language:

  1. Keeps the simple case simple and fast.
  2. Provides an escape hatch for the specialized case.
  3. Forces the user to consciously opt into the specialized trade-offs.

This is the generally correct pattern: **default should be simple; complexity should be opt-in**. It appears throughout systems software: filesystems (ext4 vs. XFS vs. ZFS), schedulers (CFS vs. real-time), allocators (default vs. jemalloc vs. tcmalloc), network stacks (BSD vs. DPDK). Every such pair embodies the same compromise: _give users the easy answer by default; give them the expert answer on demand_.

---

## Chapter 19. Condition Variables

### 19.1 Waiting for Something to Happen

Chapter 17 and 18 taught threads to _compete_ for shared resources via locks. Sometimes a thread doesn't need to compete — it needs to _wait_. For example:

* A consumer thread has no work to do until a producer thread puts work on a queue.
* A worker thread should block until a signal tells it to exit.
* A reader should wait until a file's data has been loaded into a buffer.

None of these needs are satisfied by spinning on a lock. The thread isn't contending for the lock; it's waiting for a _condition_ to become true. Spinning would waste CPU while nothing happens.

This chapter introduces the **condition variable** — the primitive for threads to wait for conditions and be signaled when those conditions become true. Condition variables work in partnership with locks, not as replacements. Together, they solve the classic **producer-consumer** problem (Chapter 19's running example) and more generally every problem involving "wait for something."

Plan C continues: locks protect data; condition variables let threads coordinate on _state_.

### 19.2 A Concrete Problem: Joining a Thread

#### Example: Naive join

A parent thread launches a child, then wants to wait for the child to finish:
    
    
    volatile int done = 0;
    
    void *child(void *arg) {
        // do work
        done = 1;
        return NULL;
    }
    
    void thread_join_naive() {
        pthread_t t;
        pthread_create(&t, NULL, child, NULL);
        while (done == 0) { /* spin */ }
    }
    

Correct under some conditions, broken in others: spinning consumes CPU (perhaps starving the child), and on weakly-ordered hardware the write to `done` may be reordered or invisible without barriers.

A better approach: the child signals _done_ ; the parent blocks until signaled.

#### What we need

Two operations:

* `wait(cond, mutex)` — atomically release `mutex` and block until signaled; on wake, re-acquire `mutex`.
* `signal(cond)` — wake up one thread waiting on `cond`.

Combined with a lock and a state variable (like `done`), these give us the basic pattern:
    
    
    int done = 0;
    pthread_mutex_t m;
    pthread_cond_t c;
    
    void *child(void *arg) {
        // do work
        pthread_mutex_lock(&m);
        done = 1;
        pthread_cond_signal(&c);
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&m);
        return NULL;
    }
    
    void thread_join() {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&m);
        while (done == 0) {
            pthread_cond_wait(&c, &m);
        }
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&m);
    }
    

The parent blocks in `pthread_cond_wait`, consuming no CPU. The child, when done, signals. The parent wakes and sees `done == 1`.

#### Pattern: The Mesa semantics

Notice the `while (done == 0)` loop, not `if`. On **Mesa semantics** (used by pthreads), a thread that is signaled _might wake up even when the condition isn't true_ — because signaling is a hint, not a guarantee. The safe pattern is:
    
    
    while (condition_not_yet_true) {
        cond_wait(&c, &m);
    }
    

Always re-check the condition after waking. Never use `if` where the concurrency protocol calls for `while`.

Two distinct situations force this re-check; it helps to separate them:

  1. **Stolen wake-up (racing waiter).** `signal` releases one waiter. By the time that waiter reacquires the mutex, another waiter (or a fresh arrival) may have raced in, taken the mutex first, consumed the condition (e.g., dequeued the only item), and released the mutex. Our signaled waiter now sees the condition false. The signal was not lost — it _did_ wake a thread — but the value it was meant to deliver was claimed by someone else. `while` handles this by re-sleeping.

  2. **Spurious wake-up (implementation artifact).** POSIX explicitly allows `pthread_cond_wait` to return even when no thread called `signal` or `broadcast`. Some kernels wake waiters to re-evaluate after signal delivery, process migration, or timed-wait expiration bookkeeping; portable code must assume this can happen at any time. The condition is false and no signal was emitted. `while` handles this too.

The invariant Mesa guarantees is weaker than "every signal reaches the thread you expected": it guarantees only that _a`signal` call, issued while at least one thread is parked in `cond_wait`, will wake at least one such thread_. If no thread is currently waiting, the signal is a no-op and vanishes — this is why the mutex + check-condition-before-wait pattern exists (Section 19.3): it ensures a waiter is committed to the queue before releasing the mutex, so a signaler holding the mutex cannot "miss" it. Given that discipline, no signal is silently dropped, but the woken thread is not guaranteed to be the one that will ultimately do the work. This is why the condition must be re-checked, not merely re-entered, on every wake-up.

Hoare semantics (the original design by C.A.R. Hoare) would have transferred the lock and the guarantee of the condition directly from signaler to signaled thread, making `if` sufficient — but at the cost of requiring immediate context switches and strict waiter-queue ordering. Every major modern system (pthreads, Java, C++ `std::condition_variable`, Go's `sync.Cond`) uses Mesa semantics because they are simpler for the OS to implement and scale better; the programmer pays for that with the `while` loop.

#### Definition: Condition variable

**Definition:** A _condition variable_ is a synchronization primitive that lets threads wait for a condition to become true and be notified when it changes. It is always used with a lock: the waiter holds the lock while checking the condition, releases the lock atomically while sleeping, and reacquires the lock before returning from `wait`. The signaler changes the condition under the lock and calls `signal` or `broadcast`.

### 19.3 Why the Lock Matters

The lock in `wait(cond, mutex)` is not optional decoration. It protects against a classic race:

#### Example: The missed signal

Without atomicity between "check condition" and "wait":
    
    
    // Waiter:
    if (done == 0) {
        // <--- signaler sets done=1 and signals here
        cond_wait(&c);   // blocks forever; the signal has already happened
    }
    

The waiter checks `done`, sees it's 0, decides to wait. In the gap between the check and the call to `wait`, the signaler sets `done = 1` and signals. The signal arrives before the waiter is waiting, so it is _lost_. The waiter then calls `wait` and blocks forever.

The fix: hold the lock across the check _and_ the wait. `cond_wait` releases the lock atomically as it begins to sleep, so the signaler cannot change `done` and signal between the check and the sleep. The signaler, waiting for the lock, runs only after the waiter is safely asleep.

#### Pattern

This is a classic "test and go" race — the canonical bug in homegrown synchronization. Condition variables are carefully designed to eliminate it. The atomic release-and-sleep in `wait` is the crucial primitive.

#### Common Misconception

_"I don't need a lock; I can just use the condition variable."_

No — you need both. The lock protects the state (`done`) and prevents the missed-signal race. The condition variable provides the waiting/signaling mechanism. They are complementary; every wait must be wrapped by a held lock.

### 19.4 Producer-Consumer: The Canonical Example

#### Example: Bounded buffer

Producers put items into a fixed-size buffer. Consumers take items out. If the buffer is full, producers wait. If the buffer is empty, consumers wait.
    
    
    #define BUFFER_SIZE 10
    int buffer[BUFFER_SIZE];
    int fill = 0, use = 0;
    int count = 0;
    
    pthread_mutex_t m;
    pthread_cond_t not_empty, not_full;
    
    void put(int value) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&m);
        while (count == BUFFER_SIZE) {
            pthread_cond_wait(&not_full, &m);
        }
        buffer[fill] = value;
        fill = (fill + 1) % BUFFER_SIZE;
        count++;
        pthread_cond_signal(&not_empty);
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&m);
    }
    
    int get() {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&m);
        while (count == 0) {
            pthread_cond_wait(&not_empty, &m);
        }
        int value = buffer[use];
        use = (use + 1) % BUFFER_SIZE;
        count--;
        pthread_cond_signal(&not_full);
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&m);
        return value;
    }
    

Two condition variables: `not_empty` (waited on by consumers, signaled by producers) and `not_full` (waited on by producers, signaled by consumers). The `while` loops retry the condition after each wake-up, handling Mesa-style spurious wake-ups and the possibility that another thread raced ahead.

#### Pattern

Producer-consumer is the universal template for concurrent pipelines. Worker pools, message queues, I/O buffers, channel-based concurrency, actor systems — all are variations on this pattern. Master it, and you have the core mechanics for most real concurrent programs.

#### Why two condition variables

If producers and consumers share one condition variable (`data_changed`), a signal from a producer (buffer no longer empty) might wake up _another producer_ rather than the waiting consumer. The sleeping producer sees the buffer still full, re-waits, and we make no progress. Separate condition variables direct signals to the right kind of waiter.

#### Common Misconception

_"`signal` is always enough; `broadcast` is overkill."_

Usually `signal` is right — wake one waiter. Sometimes `broadcast` is required — wake all waiters — when the change affects multiple waiters or when the signaler doesn't know which waiter is relevant. A common case: when a writer lock is released and multiple readers are waiting, `broadcast` lets them all proceed. `signal` would leave the remaining readers unnecessarily blocked until the next writer finishes.

Use `signal` by default, `broadcast` when you have a specific reason (multi-waiter unblock, or waiter criteria you can't distinguish).

### 19.5 Signaling Protocols

#### Signal inside the lock vs. outside
    
    
    // Inside-lock signal:
    pthread_mutex_lock(&m);
    // modify state
    pthread_cond_signal(&c);
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&m);
    
    // Outside-lock signal:
    pthread_mutex_lock(&m);
    // modify state
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&m);
    pthread_cond_signal(&c);
    

Inside-lock signaling is safer: the waiter's `wait` is guaranteed to have released the lock before any signal reaches it. Outside-lock signaling is slightly faster (waker doesn't immediately re-enter a contended lock, so waker avoids a context switch), but requires careful reasoning about races. Default to inside-lock signaling unless profiling shows the cost matters.

#### Pattern

Many POSIX implementations optimize both paths. The performance difference is small; correctness and clarity win. Until you are writing a kernel or a database, inside-lock signaling is the right default.

### 19.6 The Thundering Herd

#### Example: Broadcast misuse
    
    
    void release_all_waiters() {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&m);
        pthread_cond_broadcast(&c);
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&m);
    }
    

All waiters wake up simultaneously. Each tries to re-acquire the lock. One succeeds; the rest fail and go back to sleep (in `wait`'s lock reacquisition). A stampede. On N waiters, N−1 wake-ups were wasted.

This is the **thundering herd** : many threads woken by one signal, almost all of whom find nothing to do and sleep again. Common in server applications, kernel wait queues, web servers handling arrivals.

#### Fix: Directed signals

Use `signal` (not `broadcast`) when only one waiter can proceed. Use distinct condition variables for distinct waiting conditions — separate consumer waits (`not_empty`) from producer waits (`not_full`) as above.

When broadcast is genuinely needed (e.g., "all readers can proceed"), design the wait so that each waiter can check whether the condition applies to it specifically before doing significant work.

#### Pattern

The thundering herd appears in many guises: network connect() on a listening socket with multiple threads waiting, Linux's old accept() thundering herd, NFS client revalidation, kernel wait queues. Fixing it usually requires making the wake-up more selective.

#### Common Misconception

_"Thundering herds are rare."_

They are endemic. Anywhere many threads wait for "something that might be ready," a broadcast creates one. Modern Linux has a dedicated fix — `EPOLLEXCLUSIVE` — for the specific case of accept(). Database buffer pool managers carefully manage wait lists. The presence of broadcast in your code is a signal to examine: is every waiter going to find work, or are most going to re-sleep?

### 19.7 A Complete Bounded-Buffer Server

#### Example: Multi-threaded server

Combine producers, consumers, and a bounded buffer to build a simple worker-pool server:
    
    
    void *worker(void *arg) {
        while (1) {
            int task = get();       // blocks if queue empty
            process(task);
        }
        return NULL;
    }
    
    int main(void) {
        pthread_mutex_init(&m, NULL);
        pthread_cond_init(&not_empty, NULL);
        pthread_cond_init(&not_full, NULL);
    
        for (int i = 0; i < NUM_WORKERS; i++) {
            pthread_t t;
            pthread_create(&t, NULL, worker, NULL);
        }
    
        while (true) {
            int task = accept_connection();  // receives incoming work
            put(task);                         // blocks if queue full
        }
    }
    

This is the skeleton of every worker-pool server in the world. Nginx, Apache's prefork mode, most thread-pool frameworks — they differ in features and polish, but the core is producer-consumer with condition variables.

#### Pattern

Separating _accept_ from _process_ via a bounded queue gives:

* Back-pressure: if workers fall behind, the queue fills, and `accept` blocks. The client waits; the server doesn't spiral out of control.
* Flexibility: add more workers to scale; tune queue size to trade memory for smoothness.
* Isolation: slow workers don't break fast ones; a poisoned task affects only its worker.

This pattern — bounded queue between stages — is one of the most important design idioms in concurrent systems. Called **staged event-driven architecture (SEDA)** when elaborated into a full framework.

### 19.8 Chapter Summary

* A **condition variable** lets a thread wait for a condition to become true and be signaled when it does.
* Condition variables always work with a **lock** : the waiter holds the lock, the wait releases it atomically, and the wake-up reacquires it.
* **Mesa semantics** (POSIX) require a `while` loop around `wait`, not `if` — wakes can be spurious, and another thread may race ahead.
* The **bounded buffer** / producer-consumer problem uses two condition variables, one per waiting condition, to avoid signaling the wrong kind of thread.
* **Signal** wakes one; **broadcast** wakes all. Prefer `signal` to avoid thundering herds.
* Signal **inside the lock** unless you have a specific reason not to.

Chapter 20 introduces **semaphores** — a single primitive that combines counting and synchronization, which can often replace both locks and condition variables in simpler problems. The Plan C thread continues.

### 19.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What is a condition variable, and why is it always used with a lock?

**Answer:** A _condition variable_ is a synchronization primitive that lets a thread block until a condition becomes true. It is used with a lock to avoid the missed-signal race: the waiter must check the condition atomically with beginning to wait. The lock serializes the check and the atomic "release lock, sleep, reacquire on wake" sequence inside `wait`. Without the lock, a signaler could change the condition and signal in the gap between the check and the sleep, and the signal would be lost.

**Q2.** Why must waiters use `while (cond_not_yet_true) wait(...)` instead of `if`?

**Answer:** Under Mesa semantics (used by POSIX), a signaled thread may wake and find the condition no longer true — perhaps because another waiter has already consumed the signal, or because the OS allowed a spurious wake-up. With `while`, the thread re-checks and re-sleeps if necessary. With `if`, the thread proceeds into the critical section under a false assumption and produces incorrect behavior.

**Q3.** In the bounded-buffer example, why are two condition variables needed?

**Answer:** Producers wait when the buffer is full; consumers wait when it is empty. If a single condition variable signaled any waiter, a consumer's signal (buffer no longer empty) might wake another consumer (which still sees nothing to consume) or a producer (which doesn't need to be woken). Two condition variables — one for each class of waiter — let signals reach only the relevant waiter.

**Q4.** What is the thundering herd, and why is it a concern?

**Answer:** A _thundering herd_ is a situation where many threads are simultaneously woken (typically by `broadcast` or by a resource becoming available) and all attempt to proceed, but only one or a few can succeed — the rest waste CPU and immediately go back to sleep. Concerning because it can cause scheduling storms, wasted context switches, and scalability bottlenecks even on lightly-loaded systems. The fix is usually directed signaling (`signal` vs `broadcast`) or hardware/OS features like `EPOLLEXCLUSIVE`.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Write pseudo-code for a barrier using a mutex and one condition variable. A barrier blocks N threads until all N have arrived; then releases them all.

**Answer:**
    
    
    typedef struct {
        int N;
        int arrived;
        pthread_mutex_t m;
        pthread_cond_t all_arrived;
    } barrier_t;
    
    void barrier_wait(barrier_t *b) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&b->m);
        b->arrived++;
        if (b->arrived == b->N) {
            b->arrived = 0;  // reset for next use
            pthread_cond_broadcast(&b->all_arrived);
        } else {
            int generation = ...;  // see below
            while (b->arrived != 0) {  // careful: needs generation tracking
                pthread_cond_wait(&b->all_arrived, &b->m);
            }
        }
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&b->m);
    }
    

Subtle correctness issue: after broadcasting, the barrier is reset. If a fast thread re-enters `barrier_wait` before a slow one wakes up, the slow thread's loop predicate may be re-satisfied prematurely. Real barrier implementations use a _generation counter_ : each wait records the current generation, and the loop continues until the generation has advanced. This is why barriers, despite their simplicity, are often implemented as library primitives rather than roll-your-own.

**Q6.** In the bounded buffer, why does `put` signal `not_empty` (not `not_full`)? And why inside the lock, not outside?

**Answer:** `put` adds an item to the buffer. This potentially unblocks a consumer waiting on "not empty" — because the buffer now _has_ an item. It does not benefit any producer waiting on "not full" (nothing has been consumed, so the buffer is no less full).

Signaling inside the lock ensures that the state change (the new item plus the incremented count) and the signal are visible to the waiter atomically. Outside-lock signaling would be correct here too (and slightly more efficient — the waker doesn't immediately contend for the lock it still holds), but inside-lock is the safer and more common pattern.

**Q7.** A producer pushes 1 million items into a bounded buffer of size 10; a consumer drains them. Estimate the number of context switches between producer and consumer. Justify.

**Answer:** The consumer runs out of items every ~10 items; the producer fills up every ~10 items. A rough estimate:

* The consumer drains the 10 items. The buffer becomes empty; the consumer blocks on `not_empty`.
* The producer fills the buffer up to 10. On each signal, nothing changes until the producer has filled enough for the consumer to run. In practice, as soon as the producer puts the first item after the buffer was empty, it signals and the consumer may resume (though may not until the producer has proceeded further).
* Once the consumer starts again, the producer might run to fill a few more slots, then block on `not_full` when the buffer is full.

Ballpark: the producer and consumer alternate roles every ~5-10 items, so ~100,000 to 200,000 context switches over 1 million items. Each switch costs ~1-10 µs; total context-switch overhead might be 0.1-2 seconds. A real implementation might buffer batches to amortize this (10-100 items per "round"), dropping switch count by 10-100×. This is why production message queues often batch and use larger buffers.

**Q8.** Suppose in the bounded buffer, `put` uses `signal` but the buffer goes from 0 to 1. Three consumers are waiting. Which one wakes up? What if we use `broadcast` instead?

**Answer:** With `signal`, one consumer wakes (the OS picks which one; often FIFO, but not guaranteed). The other two remain asleep. This is correct — there is only one new item, and only one consumer can have it.

With `broadcast`, all three consumers wake. One gets the item. The other two find the buffer empty (another thread got there first), re-loop in their `while`, and go back to sleep. This is a **thundering herd** : two of the three wake-ups were wasted, costing three context switches for work that required one.

The lesson: use `signal` when only one waiter can proceed; use `broadcast` only when all waiters can (e.g., condition transitions from "can't proceed" to "all can proceed" simultaneously, such as releasing a write lock for multiple readers).

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** The pattern `while (cond_not_true) cond_wait(...)` appears almost everywhere condition variables are used. But a naive programmer might write `if (cond_not_true) cond_wait(...)`. Explain, with a specific scenario, how the `if` version can produce incorrect behavior — and argue that this reveals a deep design principle about Mesa semantics.

**Answer:** _Concrete scenario:_ a bounded buffer with 3 consumer threads and 1 producer thread.

* Consumers A, B, C each call `get()`. Buffer is empty, so all three block on `not_empty`.
* Producer calls `put(x)`, signaling `not_empty`.
* Consumer A wakes, re-acquires the lock, finds `count == 1`, consumes item, decrements count, signals `not_full`, releases lock, returns.
* But the OS also scheduled Consumer B as a spurious wake (POSIX permits this). B reacquires the lock. If B's code used `if`, it proceeds _as if the condition were still true_ , tries to consume item from count=0, reads stale buffer memory or underflows count.

Even without spurious wakes, the scenario is: A gets the signal and runs first, consuming the item. B and C wake next; if their code used `if`, they'd proceed with count=0. With `while`, they re-check, see count=0, and re-sleep.

_The design principle:_ Mesa semantics prioritize implementation flexibility over programmer convenience. The OS is allowed to wake "extra" threads, and it is allowed to schedule wake-ups in any order, because these allowances give the OS maximum scheduling freedom. The cost is that every waiter's code must be robust against spurious wakes — which is enforced by the `while` loop pattern.

The alternative, **Hoare semantics** , guarantees that when a signaler signals, the signaled thread runs _immediately and exclusively_ before any other thread gets the lock. This guarantees the condition holds on wake, but restricts the OS's scheduling freedom severely. Hoare semantics is easier to program but harder to implement; Mesa is harder to program but gives the OS freedom to schedule efficiently. The worldwide consensus (POSIX, Java, C#, Go) is Mesa — the programming cost of `while` loops is small, and the implementation freedom is worth it.

**Q10.** Condition variables and the bounded buffer form one of the most reused concurrency patterns. Look around for analogous "bounded queue between producers and consumers" in your own experience — in operating systems, networks, web servers, or elsewhere — and enumerate what role the bounded queue plays in each.

**Answer:** The pattern is ubiquitous. Some instances:

* **Network socket buffers.** TCP's send and receive buffers are bounded queues between application and network. Back-pressure: when the receive buffer is full, the kernel sends a zero-window ACK and the sender stops. The consumer (the app) reads; the producer (the network) pushes; the buffer smooths speed mismatches.

* **Disk write caches.** The OS's page cache is a bounded buffer between the application's writes and the disk. Writes return quickly (into RAM); the background flush eventually writes to disk. Back-pressure: when dirty pages exceed a threshold, writes are throttled.

* **Web server request queue.** Accept()'d connections wait in a queue for worker threads. Producers: the accept loop. Consumers: worker threads. Back-pressure: when the queue fills, `accept` returns EAGAIN or blocks.

* **Audio/video pipelines.** Each stage (decoder, filter, renderer) has a small buffer feeding the next. Back-pressure: if the renderer is slow, the filter's output queue fills, the filter blocks, the decoder's queue fills, the decoder pauses. The whole pipeline is paced by the slowest stage.

* **Worker pools in any language's concurrency library.** Go's channels, Java's `BlockingQueue`, Python's `asyncio.Queue`, Rust's `mpsc::channel` — all are bounded (or unbounded with caveats) producer-consumer queues.

* **Microservice messaging systems.** Kafka, RabbitMQ, SQS — at the service level, producer microservices push messages; consumer microservices pull. Back-pressure: when consumers fall behind, messages accumulate up to retention limits, then are dropped or producers blocked.

The common role: **the bounded queue is the universal mechanism for decoupling producers and consumers with back-pressure.** It smooths out speed mismatches, isolates failures, enables scaling consumers independently of producers, and prevents runaway buffering that would lead to memory exhaustion. The humble producer-consumer pattern — which we will now see as one of the most load-bearing ideas in systems design — is everywhere because the problem it solves is everywhere.

---

## Chapter 20. Semaphores

### 20.1 One Primitive to Rule Them All?

Locks and condition variables are two distinct primitives. Locks enforce mutual exclusion; condition variables let threads wait for arbitrary predicates. In 1965, Edsger Dijkstra proposed a single primitive that can play the role of both: the **semaphore**.

A semaphore is, at heart, a counter with two atomic operations:

* `wait(s)` (or `P(s)`, from the Dutch _passeren_ — to pass): decrement the counter; if it was zero, block until it becomes positive.
* `post(s)` (or `V(s)`, from _vrijgeven_ — to release): increment the counter; if any threads are blocked, wake one.

That's the whole primitive. Two operations, one integer. Yet with it, you can implement locks, condition-variable-style waiting, producer-consumer queues, reader-writer locks, barriers, and more — each with a couple of lines of code.

Semaphores are intellectually elegant. In practice, locks + condition variables are more expressive for complex coordination, which is why POSIX exposes both. But semaphores are still widely used — for signaling, counting available resources, and as the building block of many library primitives. This chapter introduces them, shows how they unify locking and waiting, and illustrates their power with canonical examples.

Plan C continues: semaphores complete the concurrency toolkit.

### 20.2 The Semaphore Interface

#### Definition: Semaphore

**Definition:** A _semaphore_ is an integer counter with two atomic operations. `wait` (P) atomically decrements the counter; if the counter is or becomes negative, the calling thread blocks until the counter becomes non-negative. `post` (V) atomically increments the counter; if the counter had been negative (meaning threads were blocked), one waiter is unblocked.

Initial value matters: a semaphore initialized to `k` permits `k` concurrent passes through `wait` before any thread blocks. This is the key flexibility.

#### POSIX interface
    
    
    #include <semaphore.h>
    
    sem_t s;
    sem_init(&s, 0, 3);    // unnamed semaphore, shared within process, initial value 3
    sem_wait(&s);          // P
    sem_post(&s);          // V
    sem_destroy(&s);
    

#### Example: A semaphore as a lock (binary semaphore)

Initialize with 1. Then `wait` is "acquire" and `post` is "release":
    
    
    sem_t m;
    sem_init(&m, 0, 1);
    
    void critical_section() {
        sem_wait(&m);
        // at most one thread at a time
        sem_post(&m);
    }
    

This works because the counter starts at 1. The first thread calls `wait`, decrements to 0, proceeds. The second thread calls `wait`, tries to decrement to -1, blocks. When the first calls `post`, the counter returns to 0 (the waiting thread would have made it 0 if unblocked), the waiter wakes and enters.

A semaphore initialized to 1 is called a **binary semaphore** and behaves like a mutex.

#### Pattern

A semaphore with initial value `k` allows up to `k` threads into the protected region concurrently. `k = 1` gives mutual exclusion; `k > 1` gives bounded concurrency — useful when a resource has a fixed number of slots (database connections, parking spots, license seats).

### 20.3 Semaphore as a Counting Resource

#### Example: Connection pool

A database exposes up to 10 simultaneous connections. A server has 100 threads; each occasionally needs a connection. Using a semaphore initialized to 10:
    
    
    sem_t connections;
    sem_init(&connections, 0, 10);
    
    void handle_request() {
        sem_wait(&connections);    // wait for a connection slot
        db_connection_t *conn = acquire_connection();
        // ... use conn ...
        release_connection(conn);
        sem_post(&connections);    // release the slot
    }
    

At most 10 threads can hold a connection at once. Requests 11, 12, ... block until one of the first 10 releases its connection.

This is a common pattern: semaphores gate access to resources of limited count. No locks, no condition variables, no state to manage — just a semaphore.

#### Pattern

When the thing you are synchronizing around is "N indistinguishable slots," a semaphore is the cleanest tool. When slots have identity (specific keys, specific connections), you need more structure, usually a queue of available objects plus a semaphore for count.

### 20.4 Producer-Consumer with Semaphores

#### Example: Bounded buffer with three semaphores
    
    
    #define BUFFER_SIZE 10
    int buffer[BUFFER_SIZE];
    int fill = 0, use = 0;
    
    sem_t empty;      // count of empty slots
    sem_t full;       // count of filled slots
    sem_t mutex;      // mutual exclusion for buffer access
    
    void init() {
        sem_init(&empty, 0, BUFFER_SIZE);  // all slots empty initially
        sem_init(&full, 0, 0);              // no full slots initially
        sem_init(&mutex, 0, 1);
    }
    
    void put(int value) {
        sem_wait(&empty);          // wait for an empty slot
        sem_wait(&mutex);
        buffer[fill] = value;
        fill = (fill + 1) % BUFFER_SIZE;
        sem_post(&mutex);
        sem_post(&full);           // signal that a slot is now full
    }
    
    int get() {
        sem_wait(&full);           // wait for a full slot
        sem_wait(&mutex);
        int value = buffer[use];
        use = (use + 1) % BUFFER_SIZE;
        sem_post(&mutex);
        sem_post(&empty);          // signal that a slot is now empty
        return value;
    }
    

The three semaphores capture three distinct concerns:

* `empty`: how many slots are free (producers decrement; consumers increment).
* `full`: how many slots have data (producers increment; consumers decrement).
* `mutex`: ensures only one thread at a time accesses `buffer`, `fill`, `use`.

No explicit `while` loops, no condition-variable protocol. The semantics come from `empty` and `full` starting at the right values and being updated symmetrically.

#### Pattern

Semaphores are _self-documenting_ for counting problems. The code for producer-consumer with semaphores is often shorter and clearer than the condition-variable version — once you get used to the idiom.

#### Common Misconception

_"Semaphores are always simpler than condition variables."_

Semaphores shine for counting. For complex predicates (`count > 0 && shutdown_requested == false`), condition variables are more flexible because the predicate is evaluated in user code. You can always emulate one with the other, but the natural fit matters for clarity.

### 20.5 Order Matters: `sem_wait` Order Can Deadlock

#### Example: The wrong order

In the bounded buffer above, notice the order: `sem_wait(&empty)` then `sem_wait(&mutex)`. What if we swap them?
    
    
    void put_broken(int value) {
        sem_wait(&mutex);       // acquire lock first
        sem_wait(&empty);       // then wait for empty slot
        // ...
    }
    

Deadlock is possible. Producer acquires `mutex`. Buffer is full; producer blocks on `empty`. Consumer tries to acquire `mutex` — blocked. `empty` cannot be incremented. Deadlock.

The rule: wait on the _counting_ semaphore _before_ acquiring the _mutex_. The mutex is the last thing you grab and the first thing you release. This minimizes the duration for which the mutex is held and avoids the nested-wait trap.

**Why "count-first, mutex-last" is deadlock-free.** A rigorous argument goes through the waits-for graph. Deadlock requires a cycle in which every blocked thread is holding a resource that another blocked thread needs. Under the correct order — (i) wait on `empty`/`full`, (ii) acquire `mutex`, (iii) work, (iv) release `mutex`, (v) post `full`/`empty` — a thread blocked on a counting semaphore holds _no_ resources (it has not yet acquired the mutex), so no other thread is waiting on something it holds. The waits-for graph edge from "blocked on empty/full" goes only to "someone must post the count" — and that someone (a consumer draining the buffer, or a producer filling it) is not blocked on anything the count-waiter holds, because it can freely acquire the mutex, do its work, release the mutex, and post. Formally: the graph of "can block B from making progress" has no cycles, because counting-semaphore waiters hold no other resource and mutex-waiters block only on the unique mutex that is guaranteed to be released by whoever holds it (whose critical section is finite and terminates with a post). The wrong order breaks this: a mutex-first waiter holds the mutex while blocked on the count, creating the cycle.

#### Pattern

Semaphore-based code has subtle ordering constraints. The right order is:

  1. Wait on counts (empty, full).
  2. Acquire mutex.
  3. Perform operation.
  4. Release mutex.
  5. Post counts.

Violating the order invites deadlock. This is one of the ways semaphores can be _harder_ to get right than lock + condvar, despite their individual simplicity.

### 20.6 Reader-Writer Lock from Semaphores

#### Example: A simple RW lock
    
    
    typedef struct {
        sem_t lock;     // mutex for reader counter
        sem_t writelock;// grabbed by writers; held by first reader
        int readers;
    } rwlock_t;
    
    void rw_init(rwlock_t *rw) {
        sem_init(&rw->lock, 0, 1);
        sem_init(&rw->writelock, 0, 1);
        rw->readers = 0;
    }
    
    void rw_rdlock(rwlock_t *rw) {
        sem_wait(&rw->lock);
        rw->readers++;
        if (rw->readers == 1) sem_wait(&rw->writelock);   // first reader blocks writers
        sem_post(&rw->lock);
    }
    
    void rw_rdunlock(rwlock_t *rw) {
        sem_wait(&rw->lock);
        rw->readers--;
        if (rw->readers == 0) sem_post(&rw->writelock);   // last reader releases
        sem_post(&rw->lock);
    }
    
    void rw_wrlock(rwlock_t *rw) {
        sem_wait(&rw->writelock);
    }
    
    void rw_wrunlock(rwlock_t *rw) {
        sem_post(&rw->writelock);
    }
    

The first reader "claims" the write-lock on behalf of all readers. The last reader "releases" it. Writers grab the write-lock directly. This implements RW semantics using only semaphores and a counter.

#### Pattern

The construction is elegant. It also illustrates semaphores' expressive power — two semaphores and one integer encode a non-trivial synchronization pattern. In practice, real RW locks use more sophisticated implementations to prevent writer starvation and to handle priority inheritance, but the core idea is visible here.

#### Common Misconception

_"This RW lock starves writers."_

Yes — _under sustained reader load_. The precise condition is: writers are starved when the arrival rate of new readers is high enough that the reader count `rw->readers` never decrements to 0. In that regime, the first reader grabbed `writelock`, and because new readers keep arriving before the last reader leaves, `readers` stays ≥ 1 and `writelock` is never released — so a waiting writer's `sem_wait(&writelock)` never returns. If the reader stream pauses (arrivals stop long enough for existing readers to all `rw_rdunlock`), the last reader posts `writelock`, and the waiting writer proceeds. In other words: starvation is a property of workload, not of the algorithm alone — a bursty reader workload with quiet gaps is fine; a continuous high-rate reader workload is not.

Fair RW implementations add a "writer waiting" flag that blocks new readers when a writer is pending, converting the unbounded wait into a bounded one regardless of arrival rate. The simple semaphore version exposes the starvation risk concretely and motivates the fairer designs used in production (Linux's `rwsem`, Java's `ReentrantReadWriteLock` with fairness option, etc.).

### 20.7 How Are Semaphores Implemented?

Under the hood, a semaphore is a counter plus a wait queue plus a mutex:
    
    
    typedef struct {
        int value;
        pthread_mutex_t m;
        thread_queue_t waiters;
    } sem_t;
    
    void sem_wait(sem_t *s) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&s->m);
        s->value--;
        if (s->value < 0) {
            // must wait
            enqueue(&s->waiters, current_thread);
            pthread_mutex_unlock(&s->m);
            park();   // block until woken
        } else {
            pthread_mutex_unlock(&s->m);
        }
    }
    
    void sem_post(sem_t *s) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&s->m);
        s->value++;
        if (s->value <= 0) {
            // someone is waiting; wake them
            thread_t *t = dequeue(&s->waiters);
            unpark(t);
        }
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&s->m);
    }
    

**Reading negative values.** In this implementation `s->value` can go below zero, and the negative magnitude has a concrete meaning: it is the **number of threads currently parked on the waiter queue**. If `value = -3`, three threads called `sem_wait` after the semaphore was already exhausted and are now blocked. Each `sem_post` increments `value` by 1, and the `value <= 0` test after the increment is equivalent to "there is still at least one parked waiter, so wake one." Some textbooks and real implementations define `sem_wait` to block _before_ decrementing when `value == 0`, so that `value` is always clamped at ≥ 0; the two definitions are observationally equivalent (same blocking/waking behavior) but differ in whether the integer itself or a separate counter holds the queue length. POSIX's `sem_getvalue` explicitly leaves this a quality-of-implementation choice.

Semaphores are implemented _using_ locks and condition variables (or low-level equivalents). They are not magic — they are a specific, useful packaging of the same primitives. In the kernel, they are typically built on futexes (fast user-space mutexes) or the kernel's own wait-queue machinery.

#### Pattern

The composition — build high-level primitives (semaphores, RW locks, barriers) on top of low-level primitives (atomic instructions, basic locks, condition variables, futexes) — is pervasive in concurrent systems. Each level exposes a cleaner API; users at higher levels rarely need to reach down. This is a disciplined ecosystem, and one of the reasons modern threading libraries are far more usable than the hand-rolled versions of the 1970s.

### 20.8 When to Use What

Three primitives now available:

* **Lock (mutex):** mutual exclusion. Use for critical sections. Pair with condition variables for waiting.
* **Condition variable:** wait for a predicate to become true. Always used with a lock.
* **Semaphore:** counting resource or signaling event. Self-contained.

Guidelines:

* If you need to protect a critical section, use a mutex.
* If you need to wait for some state to change, use a condition variable (with a mutex).
* If you need to gate access to a fixed number of slots, use a semaphore.
* If you need a simple signal between threads ("I'm done, you may proceed"), a binary semaphore works. Condition variables work too.

Don't mix primitives arbitrarily. A consistent style — either "mutexes and condition variables everywhere" or "semaphores everywhere" — is usually more readable than a grab-bag.

#### Pattern

Every general-purpose concurrency library offers all three. POSIX, Windows, Java, C#, C++, Rust, Go — each has its own flavor, but the concepts are universal. Learn the patterns once; apply them with local dialect variations.

### 20.9 Chapter Summary

* A **semaphore** is an atomic counter with `wait` (decrement, block if negative) and `post` (increment, wake one waiter).
* A **binary semaphore** (initial 1) acts like a mutex.
* A **counting semaphore** (initial k) gates access to k slots of some resource.
* **Producer-consumer** with semaphores uses three semaphores: `empty` (initially N), `full` (initially 0), `mutex` (initially 1).
* **Order matters:** wait on counting semaphores _before_ acquiring the mutex, release in reverse order.
* Semaphores can implement **reader-writer locks** and other higher-level primitives.
* Semaphores are themselves built on locks, condition variables, or kernel wait queues. They are a packaging convention, not a fundamentally new primitive.

Chapter 21 concludes the concurrency arc with **deadlock** and other concurrency bugs. The same Plan C counter that went from broken to fixed over the last five chapters will meet its final enemy: subtle correctness bugs that survive code review and fail only in production. The Plan C thread concludes.

### 20.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Define `wait` and `post` on a semaphore.

**Answer:** `wait(s)` atomically decrements the counter `s`; if the decrement makes the value negative, the calling thread blocks until the counter becomes non-negative (due to a `post` from another thread). `post(s)` atomically increments the counter; if the value was negative before the increment, it wakes one blocked thread.

**Q2.** What is a binary semaphore, and why does it behave like a mutex?

**Answer:** A binary semaphore is initialized with value 1. Calling `wait` once decrements to 0 (the thread proceeds); a second concurrent `wait` would decrement to -1 and block. Only when the first thread calls `post` does the counter return to 0 and the blocked thread wake up. At most one thread is past `wait` at a time — exactly the mutual-exclusion behavior of a mutex.

**Q3.** Why does the bounded-buffer code wait on `empty` _before_ acquiring `mutex`?

**Answer:** If a producer acquired `mutex` first and then waited on `empty`, and the buffer was full, the producer would block holding the mutex. Any consumer trying to drain the buffer would first need to acquire the mutex, which is held by the blocked producer. Deadlock.
