Waiting on `empty` first means the producer blocks without holding the mutex, so consumers can run, drain the buffer, and post `empty`, waking the producer.

**Q4.** When would you choose semaphores over mutex + condition variable?

**Answer:** When the problem is fundamentally about counting (resource slots, buffer counts, connection pools), semaphores are cleaner: the count is directly in the primitive, and the code is shorter. For arbitrary predicates, condition variables are more flexible because the predicate is plain code. Many programs use both: mutexes with condition variables for most synchronization, plus the occasional semaphore for counting. The choice is usually stylistic, but for pure counting problems, semaphores win.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Implement a thread-safe `join`-like function using a semaphore: the parent thread calls `my_join(&t)` and blocks until child thread `t` calls `my_done()`.

**Answer:**
    
    
    sem_t done_sem;
    sem_init(&done_sem, 0, 0);  // initial 0 — wait will block
    
    void *child(void *arg) {
        // work
        sem_post(&done_sem);
        return NULL;
    }
    
    void my_join() {
        sem_wait(&done_sem);
    }
    

The semaphore starts at 0. The parent's `sem_wait` decrements to -1 and blocks. The child's `sem_post` increments back to 0, waking the parent. Clean, one-line join.

For multiple children, initialize the semaphore to -(N-1) conceptually (or equivalently, have the parent call `sem_wait` N times). Or, more commonly: use a counter and condition variable. The semaphore approach scales with effort; mutex+condvar is more flexible.

**Q6.** A "turnstile" is a synchronization point where N threads arrive, wait for all N to arrive, then release all. Implement a turnstile using two semaphores.

**Answer:**
    
    
    sem_t mutex;
    sem_t turnstile;
    int count = 0;
    int N;
    
    void turnstile_arrive() {
        sem_wait(&mutex);
        count++;
        if (count == N) {
            // I'm the Nth; release everyone
            for (int i = 0; i < N; i++) sem_post(&turnstile);
        }
        sem_post(&mutex);
        sem_wait(&turnstile);  // wait to be released
    }
    
    // Init: sem_init(&mutex, 0, 1); sem_init(&turnstile, 0, 0);
    

The Nth arrival posts to the turnstile N times (once per waiter), each waiter's `sem_wait` decrements and proceeds. This is a barrier.

**Q7.** Is the following usage correct? If not, explain.
    
    
    sem_wait(&s);
    // ... do work that may throw exceptions or return early ...
    sem_post(&s);
    

**Answer:** Correct only if the code between `wait` and `post` always reaches the `post`. If the code can throw, return early, or have a branch that skips the post, the semaphore is "leaked" — its count is permanently off by one, so one fewer thread can enter in the future.

The fix: use a scope-guard (C++ RAII, Go `defer`, Rust `Drop`, or manually structure the code with a single post at the end). Semaphores (and locks) leaked this way are an endemic bug in codebases that have error paths but inconsistent cleanup discipline.

**Q8.** A web server wants to limit requests to 100 concurrent handlers. How would you use a semaphore to enforce this?

**Answer:**
    
    
    sem_t request_limit;
    sem_init(&request_limit, 0, 100);
    
    void handle_request(request_t *req) {
        sem_wait(&request_limit);       // wait for slot
        process(req);
        sem_post(&request_limit);        // release slot
    }
    

At most 100 threads are past `sem_wait` at a time. Request 101 blocks until one of the first 100 completes. Trivial to implement, no lock needed, no condition variable.

To reject (return 503) instead of block on saturation, use `sem_trywait` (non-blocking attempt) and respond with "too busy" if it fails. Many production servers do this to apply back-pressure to clients rather than queuing requests.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** Semaphores were Dijkstra's 1965 proposal for a universal synchronization primitive. Decades later, POSIX provides semaphores, mutexes, and condition variables as separate primitives, and many modern systems (Go, Rust, Java) prefer channels or futures over raw semaphores. What does this evolution tell us about the search for "the right" synchronization primitive?

**Answer:** The evolution shows that **no single primitive is optimal for all coordination problems**. Semaphores are elegant for counting but awkward for rich predicates. Mutexes + condition variables are flexible but require careful protocol discipline. Channels are natural for pipelines but clumsy for shared-mutable-state problems. Futures are great for one-shot results but overkill for repeated coordination.

The search for "the right" primitive is a misguided frame. The right toolkit is multiple primitives, each with strengths, and enough common vocabulary that programmers can pick the best fit. Modern languages reflect this: Go has channels _and_ mutexes _and_ waitgroups; Rust has Mutex _and_ RwLock _and_ channel-like crates _and_ async/await. The expert programmer knows when to reach for which.

The deeper lesson: **concurrency is fundamentally diverse.** Some problems are about mutual exclusion; some about waiting; some about communication; some about counting; some about pipelining. Each has a well-matched primitive. A single primitive that solves all of them exists (locks + condvars, or raw atomics) but at a cost of clarity. The evolution toward multiple primitives is a recognition that clarity matters more than uniformity.

This is a general principle beyond concurrency. Programming languages offer multiple loop constructs (for, while, do-while), multiple collections (array, list, map, set), multiple visibility modifiers (public, private, protected). The uniform answer is always possible; the diverse answer is almost always better.

**Q10.** Semaphores are 60 years old. They are still in wide use. What does this longevity teach us about how concurrency primitives mature — and what predictions might we make about the primitives in wide use 60 years from now?

**Answer:** _On longevity._ Semaphores are simple, correct, and expressive for a well-defined class of problems. They had a long period of being the primary primitive (1960s-1980s), were supplemented by mutexes + condvars (1980s-1990s), and are now part of a mature ecosystem of primitives. Their longevity comes from being _sufficient_ for their niche and _small enough_ to implement and teach easily. Primitives that survive are primitives whose specification is stable, whose implementation is simple, and whose use cases recur.

_On the next 60 years._ Several likely trends:

  1. **Message-passing primitives will grow.** Channels, actors, and similar abstractions scale better across distributed systems than shared-memory synchronization. As systems become more distributed (cloud, edge, microservices), shared-memory concurrency becomes a smaller fraction of the total.

  2. **Transactional primitives will grow.** Software transactional memory (STM) and hardware transactional memory (HTM) remove the need for programmers to reason about locks at all. They are still niche — ~20 years of research haven't produced a universal winner — but the trend of "programmer describes what, not how" suggests this will continue.

  3. **Async/await or similar primitives will become universal.** Most new languages (Rust, Swift, Python, C#) have adopted some form. They're not replacements for locks — you still need locks inside async code — but they are becoming the default for I/O-heavy coordination.

  4. **Semaphores, mutexes, and condition variables will still exist.** They have proven themselves. At the lowest levels (kernel, hot paths in databases, embedded systems), they are likely to remain. Above them, higher-level abstractions will dominate.

  5. **New primitives will emerge from hardware.** Persistent memory, hardware lock elision, and architectures that blur CPU and memory boundaries will create synchronization needs we don't yet have primitives for.

The common thread: **primitives survive by being simple and sufficient for their purpose.** New primitives arise to serve new classes of problems, but they rarely displace old ones in domains where the old ones work. Expect more primitives over time, not fewer.

---

## Chapter 21. Deadlock and Concurrency Bugs

### 21.1 The Last Enemy

Plan C began with a broken counter. Over five chapters we built up locks, condition variables, and semaphores, each fixing one kind of failure. The counter is now correct. But concurrent programs fail in ways that even perfect use of these primitives cannot prevent: the program can still freeze, or produce subtly wrong behavior that survives every unit test and manifests only on a production Tuesday.

This chapter catalogs the residual dangers. The biggest is **deadlock** : a set of threads each holding a resource the others need, none able to proceed. Others are subtler: ordering violations that cause garbage outputs, atomicity violations that flash incorrect intermediate states, data races that appear-then-disappear. Each has recognition patterns, prevention techniques, and detection tooling.

Plan C concludes. We've taken the simple `counter++` from broken to correct to robust-against-the-final-enemies. The lessons here are the senior engineer's, not the undergraduate's — most of them cannot be absorbed in one reading but accumulate with experience.

### 21.2 Deadlock

#### Example: The bank transfer

Two threads, two accounts, each transfer acquires both account locks:
    
    
    void transfer(account_t *from, account_t *to, int amount) {
        pthread_mutex_lock(&from->lock);
        pthread_mutex_lock(&to->lock);
        from->balance -= amount;
        to->balance += amount;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&to->lock);
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&from->lock);
    }
    

* Thread 1: `transfer(A, B, 100)`. Acquires lock on A. Blocks waiting for B.
* Thread 2: `transfer(B, A, 50)`. Acquires lock on B. Blocks waiting for A.

Both threads are forever blocked. Neither will release the lock it holds; neither can get the lock it needs.

#### Definition: Deadlock

**Definition:** A _deadlock_ is a state in which a set of threads are each blocked waiting for a resource held by another thread in the set, such that no thread can proceed. Once entered, deadlock is permanent — without outside intervention, the threads remain blocked forever.

#### Coffman's conditions

A deadlock occurs if and only if all four of these hold simultaneously:

  1. **Mutual exclusion.** At least one resource can be held in an exclusive mode (i.e., only one thread at a time).
  2. **Hold-and-wait.** A thread holds at least one resource while waiting for another.
  3. **No preemption.** Resources cannot be forcibly taken from the thread holding them; only the holder releases them.
  4. **Circular wait.** A cycle exists in the "thread waits for resource held by" graph.

Break any one condition, and deadlock cannot occur.

**A note on self-deadlock and re-entrance.** A thread that already holds a non-reentrant lock and then tries to acquire the same lock again will deadlock against itself — it waits forever for a lock whose only holder is itself. This is the degenerate case where all four Coffman conditions hold _simultaneously within one thread_ : the lock is mutually exclusive (condition 1), the thread holds it and is now waiting for it (condition 2), it cannot be forcibly revoked (condition 3), and the waits-for relation collapses into a length-1 "cycle" (condition 4, in its degenerate form — strict statements of Coffman define the cycle over _distinct_ threads, which is why this case is sometimes called out separately). Reentrant locks (e.g., `pthread_mutex_t` initialized with `PTHREAD_MUTEX_RECURSIVE`, or Java's `ReentrantLock`) break condition 1 for the original holder: a thread that already owns the lock is granted it again immediately, and the implementation tracks a recursion count so the lock is fully released only when the count returns to zero. Many codebases avoid recursive locks on style grounds — preferring to refactor so no code path re-enters the same lock — because the re-acquire pattern can mask genuine lock-ordering bugs in nested call chains.

### 21.3 Preventing Deadlock

#### Strategy 1: Lock ordering

Impose a global order on locks. Every thread acquires locks in the same order (e.g., lower address first, lower ID first). Circular wait is broken — if Thread 1 holds A (lower), it will never wait for a thread holding a higher lock; the thread holding the higher lock will acquire A _after_ the higher lock, not before.
    
    
    void transfer(account_t *from, account_t *to, int amount) {
        account_t *first = (from->id < to->id) ? from : to;
        account_t *second = (from->id < to->id) ? to : from;
        pthread_mutex_lock(&first->lock);
        pthread_mutex_lock(&second->lock);
        // ... do transfer ...
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&second->lock);
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&first->lock);
    }
    

Now Thread 1 and Thread 2 both lock lower-ID first. One succeeds; the other waits without holding the inverse. No circular wait. No deadlock.

#### Pattern

Lock ordering is the **default and strongest defense** against deadlock. Every codebase with concurrent locking should document its lock order, and code reviews should check adherence. Violations are the single most common source of deadlock in practice.

#### Strategy 2: Lock timeouts

If `pthread_mutex_timedlock(&lock, 100ms)` returns timeout, release all currently-held locks, back off, retry. This breaks hold-and-wait: a waiting thread doesn't wait forever.
    
    
    if (pthread_mutex_timedlock(&to->lock, deadline) != 0) {
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&from->lock);
        return RETRY;
    }
    

Downside: correctness requires the caller to handle retries robustly. If retries themselves race, progress can stall. Livelock (threads busily undoing each other's work) is a risk.

#### Strategy 3: Try-lock everywhere

Replace blocking `lock` with non-blocking `trylock`. If the trylock fails, release all held locks and retry. Same progress hazards as timeouts, but avoids the timeout value tuning.

#### Strategy 4: Make locks preemptable

The OS or runtime system can detect a deadlock and forcibly terminate or roll back a thread. Databases do this constantly (transaction abort and retry). At the OS level, this is rare — once a thread has modified user-visible state, preempting it is tricky.

#### Pattern

Prevention (lock ordering) is better than detection and recovery. The complexity of handling deadlock recovery robustly is very high; the discipline to order locks consistently is modest. Almost all real systems choose prevention.

#### Common Misconception

_"Deadlock is rare; we can handle it when it happens."_

Deadlocks have a nasty property: they tend to happen under load, when you can least afford downtime. A system that deadlocks once a year in production was probably deadlocking once a day in development, suppressed only by lighter loads. The difference between 365× less likely and _literally never_ is the difference between a timid lock-ordering discipline and a strict one.

### 21.4 Atomicity Violations

#### Example: Firefox bug

A famous bug in early Firefox:
    
    
    // Thread 1:
    if (thd->proc_info) {
        fputs(thd->proc_info, stderr);
    }
    
    // Thread 2:
    thd->proc_info = NULL;
    

Thread 1 checks that `proc_info` is non-NULL, then reads it. Between the check and the read, Thread 2 can null it. Thread 1 then calls `fputs(NULL, stderr)` — crash.

The fix: combine the check and the read into a single critical section with a lock.
    
    
    pthread_mutex_lock(&thd->lock);
    if (thd->proc_info) {
        fputs(thd->proc_info, stderr);
    }
    pthread_mutex_unlock(&thd->lock);
    

#### Definition: Atomicity violation

**Definition:** An _atomicity violation_ is a bug where a sequence of operations that was intended to execute as an indivisible unit is interrupted by another thread, causing incorrect behavior. The programmer reasoned "I check then act," but between the check and the act, another thread modified the state.

#### Pattern

Atomicity violations are the most common class of concurrency bug in real code. They're often hidden inside seemingly-innocent statements: "check-then-act" (as above), "read-modify-write" (the counter example), "test-then-commit" (database writes), "check-then-load" (cache checks).

The fix is always: identify the invariant the programmer assumed (the value doesn't change between check and act), and hold a lock that enforces it.

### 21.5 Order Violations

#### Example: The initialization race
    
    
    Thread *t;
    
    void *worker(void *arg) {
        // assumes t is initialized
        work_on(t);
    }
    
    void main() {
        pthread_t p;
        pthread_create(&p, NULL, worker, NULL);
        // later...
        t = allocate_thread_state();
    }
    

The worker starts running before `t` is initialized. It reads uninitialized memory. Crash.

#### Definition: Order violation

**Definition:** An _order violation_ is a bug where two operations in different threads were expected to execute in a specific order, but the synchronization doesn't enforce the order. The program works when operations happen to run in the intended order and fails when they don't.

#### Pattern

Order violations are prevented by explicit synchronization. Possibilities:

* Use a semaphore or condition variable: the second operation waits until the first has completed.
* Use a barrier: all initialization completes before work begins.
* Use sequential code: don't spawn the worker until initialization is done.

In the example above, initializing `t` _before_ `pthread_create` would fix the race.

### 21.6 Data Races

#### Definition: Data race

**Definition:** A _data race_ occurs when two threads access the same memory location without synchronization, and at least one of the accesses is a write. Data races produce undefined behavior at the language level — the program may appear to work, but compilers are permitted to emit code that does arbitrary things in the presence of a race.

#### Example: A subtle race
    
    
    bool stop = false;
    
    void *worker(void *arg) {
        while (!stop) {
            do_work();
        }
        return NULL;
    }
    
    void stop_worker() {
        stop = true;
    }
    

Seemingly benign: one thread sets `stop = true`, the other eventually notices. In practice:

* The compiler may hoist `stop` out of the loop, reading it once and caching the result. The worker never sees the update.
* On multicore, the write from `stop_worker` may take milliseconds to propagate. The worker reads its stale cached copy.

The fix is to mark `stop` as atomic:
    
    
    atomic_bool stop = false;
    
    while (!atomic_load(&stop)) {
        do_work();
    }
    
    // and
    atomic_store(&stop, true);
    

Now the compiler knows `stop` is concurrently accessed and emits appropriate loads/stores with the necessary memory barriers.

#### Pattern

Data races are _undefined behavior_ in C, C++, Java (on non-volatile/atomic fields), and many other languages. The compiler is allowed to assume no data races and optimize accordingly. Your code may work today and break after a compiler upgrade. Always use atomic types or locks for shared mutable state.

#### Common Misconception

_"If the loads and stores are single-word, there's no race."_

The hardware will not tear a single aligned read/write, but the compiler is allowed to optimize around accesses it doesn't know are concurrent. A plain `bool stop` in a loop may be cached in a register and never re-read. The language specification — not the hardware — is what forces you to use atomics.

### 21.7 Deadlock Detection at Runtime

Tooling exists to find concurrency bugs in practice:

#### ThreadSanitizer (TSan)

A compile-time instrumentation (in GCC/Clang) that tracks every memory access and every synchronization event. When two unsynchronized accesses to the same location occur (at least one a write), TSan reports the race. Catches data races and some atomicity violations.

Runtime overhead: 5-15× slowdown. Used during testing, not production.

#### Helgrind / DRD (Valgrind)

Similar to TSan: runtime data-race detection via Valgrind. Works without recompiling, slower than TSan, still useful.

#### Lockdep (Linux kernel)

Tracks lock-acquisition orders. If any two threads acquire the same two locks in opposite orders (a potential deadlock), Lockdep warns. Used during kernel development, not in production builds.

#### Runtime deadlock detectors

Java's `jstack`, Go's race detector, runtime thread-dump analyzers can detect deadlock at the moment it happens: if all threads are blocked and no progress is being made, print the call stacks and the locks each thread holds.

#### Pattern

Testing concurrent code requires tooling beyond unit tests. The bugs are too sparse, too timing-dependent. Every production codebase using concurrency should have TSan or equivalent running in CI, and should have dashboard alerts for thread deadlock indicators in production.

### 21.8 Lessons from a Real Study

Researchers (Lu et al., 2008) analyzed 105 concurrency bugs in widely-used open-source programs (MySQL, Apache, Mozilla, OpenOffice). Findings:

* **Atomicity violations: 51%.** The most common bug class.
* **Order violations: 24%.** Second most common.
* **Deadlocks: 30% of non-data-race bugs.** Roughly one in three concurrency bugs is a deadlock.
* **Bug duration in source:** many bugs lived in the code for months or years before triggering.
* **Triggering conditions:** most bugs triggered only under specific load patterns; some required specific CPU timings (2+ threads racing on a short window).

#### Pattern

The lesson: concurrency bugs are **common, long-lived, and hard to reproduce.** The best defenses:

  1. **Minimize shared mutable state.** If a variable isn't shared, it can't be raced on.
  2. **Use higher-level abstractions.** Channels, futures, message passing reduce the surface area for races.
  3. **Use tooling.** TSan, static analyzers, model checkers catch bugs that testing misses.
  4. **Code review specifically for concurrency.** Standard code review doesn't catch these; a reviewer who specifically looks at locks, order, and shared state does.
  5. **Default to simpler patterns.** Single producer, single consumer, immutable data after initialization, per-thread state — these are robust against most concurrency bugs by construction.

### 21.9 Chapter Summary

* **Deadlock** requires four conditions (mutex, hold-and-wait, no preemption, circular wait). Break any one to prevent it.
* **Lock ordering** is the standard deadlock prevention: always acquire multiple locks in a consistent order.
* **Atomicity violations** (check-then-act under a race) are the most common concurrency bug. Fix by expanding the critical section.
* **Order violations** occur when one thread assumes another has completed; fix with explicit synchronization.
* **Data races** are undefined behavior; use atomic types or locks for shared mutable state.
* **Tooling** (ThreadSanitizer, lockdep, etc.) is essential for finding concurrency bugs that unit tests miss.

This concludes the concurrency arc and Plan C. The broken counter from Chapter 16 has traveled through locks, condition variables, semaphores, and finally through the minefield of deadlock and atomicity bugs. Real concurrent programs navigate all of this; concurrency is the hardest topic in this book, and also the one where small details matter most.

Chapter 22 begins the persistence arc — the final major topic. We leave concurrency and enter the world of disks, file systems, and crash consistency. Plan A's thread will make one more appearance: Chapter 23 revisits the shared file opens that a multi-process OS must coordinate.

### 21.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** List Coffman's four conditions for deadlock.

**Answer:** (1) Mutual exclusion: at least one resource is held exclusively. (2) Hold-and-wait: a thread holds one resource while requesting another. (3) No preemption: resources cannot be forcibly taken. (4) Circular wait: a cycle exists in the "waits-for" graph. All four must hold simultaneously for deadlock. Breaking any one prevents it.

**Q2.** What is lock ordering, and why does it prevent deadlock?

**Answer:** _Lock ordering_ is the convention that every thread acquires multiple locks in the same globally-consistent order (e.g., always lock the lower-numbered mutex first). It prevents deadlock by breaking the circular-wait condition: if all threads approach locks in the same order, no thread ever holds a "high" lock while waiting for a "low" one, so no cycle can form.

**Q3.** Define atomicity violation and give one example.

**Answer:** An _atomicity violation_ is a bug where a sequence of operations meant to be indivisible is interrupted by another thread, producing incorrect behavior. Example: `if (p != NULL) use(p)` where another thread sets `p = NULL` between the check and the use. The programmer assumed the check's result remained valid during the use; the interruption invalidates it. Fix: hold a lock across both the check and the use.

**Q4.** What does it mean for a data race to be "undefined behavior"?

**Answer:** At the language level (C, C++), "undefined behavior" means the compiler and runtime make no promises about what happens. The program may produce correct output, incorrect output, crash, corrupt memory, or behave arbitrarily. The compiler is _allowed_ to assume the race does not occur and to optimize accordingly. This means a program with a data race may work today and break tomorrow after a compiler upgrade or with different optimization levels. The fix is to eliminate the race using atomic operations or locks.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Apply lock ordering to fix the bank-transfer deadlock. Show the modified code.

**Answer:**
    
    
    void transfer(account_t *from, account_t *to, int amount) {
        account_t *first = (from->id < to->id) ? from : to;
        account_t *second = (from->id < to->id) ? to : from;
        pthread_mutex_lock(&first->lock);
        pthread_mutex_lock(&second->lock);
        from->balance -= amount;
        to->balance += amount;
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&second->lock);
        pthread_mutex_unlock(&first->lock);
    }
    

Both `transfer(A, B)` and `transfer(B, A)` now lock `min(A, B)` first. No circular wait.

**Q6.** The dining philosophers problem: five philosophers sit at a round table with five forks between them. Each philosopher alternates between thinking and eating; to eat, they need both the fork on their left and the fork on their right. If each picks up the left fork first, then tries the right, what goes wrong?

**Answer:** Deadlock. All five philosophers pick up their left fork simultaneously. Now each holds one fork and waits for the next (the neighbor's right fork, which is some other philosopher's left). Circular wait: philosopher 0 waits on 1, 1 on 2, 2 on 3, 3 on 4, 4 on 0. No one can eat.

Fixes:

* _Lock ordering:_ one philosopher picks up right fork first. This philosopher either succeeds (no deadlock) or becomes part of a non-cyclic wait chain.
* _Timeout + retry:_ if the right fork isn't available in T seconds, drop the left and try again later.
* _Global semaphore limiting concurrency:_ only 4 philosophers can "try to eat" at a time; the 5th must wait. Breaks hold-and-wait at scale.

**Q7.** Examine the following code. What kind of bug does it contain?
    
    
    void parent() {
        pthread_t t;
        pthread_create(&t, NULL, child, NULL);
        shared_buffer = malloc(1024);
        init_data(shared_buffer);
        pthread_join(t, NULL);
    }
    
    void *child(void *arg) {
        use(shared_buffer);
        return NULL;
    }
    

**Answer:** Order violation. The child may run immediately after `pthread_create` and read `shared_buffer` before the parent has set it. The child will read `NULL` (or uninitialized memory) and crash or corrupt data.

Fixes:

* Initialize `shared_buffer` _before_ calling `pthread_create`.
* Use a condition variable: the child waits until a "buffer ready" flag is set.
* Use a barrier: the child waits at the barrier; the parent arrives after initializing.

**Q8.** Is the following code race-free? Why or why not?
    
    
    bool flag = false;
    
    // Thread 1:
    // ... does work ...
    flag = true;
    
    // Thread 2:
    while (!flag) { /* spin */ }
    printf("go!\n");
    

**Answer:** No, this contains a data race. `flag` is accessed without synchronization. The compiler may cache `flag` in a register inside Thread 2's loop and never re-read memory, causing Thread 2 to spin forever. Even if it does re-read, visibility on multicore may be delayed, and memory reordering may cause Thread 2 to see `flag = true` before the prior work (which Thread 2 might also access) is visible.

Fix: declare `flag` as `atomic_bool` (or `_Atomic bool` in C11) and use `atomic_load` / `atomic_store` (or simply assign to an atomic variable, which uses sequential consistency by default). This inserts the necessary memory barriers and tells the compiler not to cache the variable.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** The Lu et al. 2008 study found that atomicity violations are the most common concurrency bug. Speculate on why, given the primitives we've studied (locks, condvars, semaphores) should make atomicity easy to enforce.

**Answer:** Several reasons:

  1. **Atomicity boundaries are implicit.** Locks protect explicit critical sections; the programmer must _recognize_ that a sequence needs to be atomic. Often, the programmer doesn't realize an innocent-looking `if (x != NULL) use(x)` is a two-step operation vulnerable to interruption.

  2. **Locks scope differently than programmer intent.** A programmer writes `lock; if (x != NULL) use(x); unlock` and then later adds an early return inside the `if`. Now some code paths exit without unlocking; or the programmer splits the function and the atomicity crosses function boundaries.

  3. **Correctness depends on invariants that aren't stated.** "Between these two operations, x does not become NULL" is an invariant the programmer has in their head. If another part of the code doesn't know this and modifies x, the invariant breaks silently.

  4. **Time-of-check to time-of-use (TOCTOU) bugs are everywhere.** Even outside concurrency — file system checks, permission verifications — the same pattern recurs. The concurrency-adjacent form is ubiquitous because the underlying reasoning error is natural.

  5. **Testing rarely catches them.** Unit tests run without concurrency. Integration tests with concurrency rarely have the specific timing to trigger the race. The bug sits dormant.

The fix is _habits_ , not _primitives_ : learn to recognize check-then-act patterns and reflexively protect the whole sequence.

**Q10.** Concurrency is often called "the hardest topic in systems programming." Reflecting on the six chapters of the concurrency arc, what do you think makes concurrency _specifically_ hard — that is, harder than, say, memory virtualization or file systems?

**Answer:** Several factors combine:

  1. **Non-determinism.** Sequential code runs the same way every time. Concurrent code can produce different results on different runs, even without input changes. You cannot reliably reproduce a bug to debug it. This breaks the standard debugging workflow.

  2. **Hidden state.** Memory caches, store buffers, pipeline effects, speculative execution — all are invisible to the programmer and can cause surprising behavior. The programming model ("simple sequential memory") diverges from the machine's reality ("complex distributed memory").

  3. **Cross-cutting correctness.** A function's correctness doesn't depend only on its own logic, but on what _any other function anywhere_ does concurrently. You cannot verify a function in isolation; you must consider all concurrent contexts.

  4. **Imperfect abstractions.** Locks promise mutual exclusion but can deadlock. Atomic operations promise atomicity but have surprising memory-ordering semantics. Every abstraction has edge cases, and the edges are exactly where bugs live.

  5. **Testing is weak.** Traditional testing rarely exercises the specific timing that triggers bugs. You need specialized tools (stress tests, fuzz tests, model checkers, sanitizers) and even then, some classes of bugs escape.

  6. **Reasoning transfers poorly.** What you know about sequential code — "if I set x = 5, then x = 5 afterward" — does not hold. Reasoning about concurrency requires re-learning fundamentals.

Compared to memory virtualization or file systems: memory is a _local_ problem (one access at a time, well-defined behavior) and file systems are a _batch_ problem (operations complete, and their completion is visible). Concurrency is a _global, instantaneous, non-reproducible_ problem. This is why it is harder, and why it rewards disciplined conservative design: lock-ordering conventions, minimization of shared state, preference for message passing, use of tools. The concurrency programmer's wisdom is "simpler is safer" — not because complex designs can't be correct, but because verifying complex concurrent designs is so expensive that simplicity is almost always the better engineering choice.

---

## Chapter 22. I/O Devices and Disks

### 22.1 Why Persistence Matters

The memory chapters left us with a volatile machine: DRAM loses its contents on power-off, and virtual memory is entirely gone when the process exits. A spreadsheet you typed into memory disappears when the laptop is closed. Real programs need **persistence** — data that survives power loss, process exit, and reboots.

The OS provides persistence via two abstractions:

* **Files** and **directories** — a tree-structured namespace with arbitrary-length byte arrays.
* **The block device** — raw access to a disk as a sequence of fixed-size blocks.

This chapter covers the bottom half: how disks, SSDs, and other storage devices actually work, and how the OS drives them. Chapters 23-25 build the file system on top.

The gap between memory and storage is enormous. DRAM reads take ~100 ns; disk reads take ~10 ms. That's 100,000× slower. SSD reads are ~100 µs — 1000× slower than DRAM. No amount of cleverness closes that gap entirely. The OS's job is to mask the latency wherever it can (caching, prefetching, asynchrony) and to expose the remaining cost as predictably as possible.

### 22.2 The I/O Subsystem

#### Example: A canonical system

A modern PC's I/O architecture:
    
    
    CPU  ─┬── memory bus ── DRAM
          │
         chipset/IOMMU
          │
         PCIe ─── NVMe SSD
          │
         PCIe ─── Network card
          │
         USB   ─── keyboard, mouse, storage
    

I/O devices hang off one or more buses. The CPU communicates with devices through:

  1. **Memory-mapped I/O (MMIO).** The device's registers appear at specific physical addresses. The CPU reads/writes them like memory, but the accesses go to the device (not DRAM).
  2. **Direct Memory Access (DMA).** The device, instructed by the CPU, moves data between its own buffers and host DRAM _without_ the CPU copying byte-by-byte. Frees the CPU to do other work during transfers.
  3. **Interrupts.** When a device finishes an operation, it asserts an interrupt line, which triggers a handler in the OS. This is asynchronous from the CPU's perspective.

#### Pattern: The device driver

A **driver** is the OS code that mediates between user software and the device. It knows the device's MMIO register layout, command set, and interrupt conventions. It exposes a uniform interface (read block, write block, get status) so the rest of the OS doesn't care about manufacturer specifics.

Drivers are the most numerous kind of OS code. A running Linux kernel typically has thousands of drivers linked in (or loaded as modules). Each is a bridge between a specific device and a specific OS interface.

#### Definition: Device driver

**Definition:** A _device driver_ is a module of OS code that implements the generic OS interface (e.g., "read a block") for a specific class of devices (e.g., "NVMe SSD controller"). The driver speaks the device's specific command/response protocol via MMIO, DMA, and interrupts, while exposing a uniform abstraction upward. Drivers are what let the OS support new hardware without rewriting the rest of the system.

### 22.3 Performing I/O: Polling, Interrupts, DMA

#### Example: Reading one disk block

Naïve polling:
    
    
    1. OS writes "read block N" to the device's command register.
    2. OS loops: read device's status register; if "busy," loop.
    3. When device reports "done," OS reads data bytes one at a time.
    

This works but wastes CPU. During the ~10 ms wait, the CPU does nothing but spin. On a 3 GHz CPU, that's 30 million wasted cycles.

#### Interrupts

Rather than polling:
    
    
    1. OS writes "read block N" command.
    2. OS context-switches to another runnable process. Does useful work.
    3. Device finishes, raises interrupt.
    4. CPU traps into interrupt handler.
    5. Handler wakes the process that was waiting for the data.
    

The CPU is busy with other work during the 10 ms; the cost of the interrupt itself is ~1 µs. The CPU's effective utilization improves dramatically.

#### DMA

For large transfers, even byte-by-byte CPU copies are wasteful:
    
    
    1. OS sets up DMA: "transfer block N into this buffer in memory."
    2. Device reads from disk, writes directly into the specified memory range.
    3. Device raises interrupt when done.
    4. OS wakes the waiting process; data is already in memory.
    

DMA removes the CPU from the data path entirely. The CPU pays only the setup cost and the interrupt cost, regardless of transfer size.

#### Pattern: The I/O performance ladder

* **Polling only:** terrible CPU efficiency; simple.
* **Polling + small data:** OK if I/O is rare.
* **Interrupts:** CPU efficient, good for most devices.
* **Interrupts + DMA:** efficient for large transfers.
* **Polling for high-throughput devices:** sometimes polling beats interrupts again (NVMe SSDs in high-performance scenarios) because interrupt overhead exceeds poll cost when devices are very fast.

The right choice depends on device speed and I/O frequency. Modern NVMe drivers often use a hybrid: poll briefly (hoping the I/O completes quickly), fall back to interrupts for longer waits.

### 22.4 The Spinning Disk

For decades, the dominant storage was the **hard disk drive (HDD)** — spinning platters with a magnetic read/write head.

#### Example: Anatomy of an HDD

* **Platters.** One or more magnetic disks, typically 3.5" or 2.5" diameter.
* **Tracks.** Concentric circles on each platter, holding data.
* **Sectors.** Fixed-size units (512 B or 4 KB) along each track.
* **Heads.** Read/write magnetic heads, one per platter surface.
* **Arm.** Moves heads radially across the platter to reach different tracks.
* **Spindle.** Rotates platters, typically at 7200 or 10,000 rpm.

To read sector S on track T:

  1. **Seek:** move the arm to track T. Short seeks (adjacent tracks) complete in under 1 ms; long seeks (edge to edge) take 10–15 ms; the _average_ random seek on modern 7200-rpm HDDs is 4–8 ms, with older or slower drives sitting closer to 10 ms. We use 10 ms as a worst-case "average random" baseline in later exercises unless stated otherwise.
  2. **Rotational latency:** wait for sector S to pass under the head. Half a rotation on average; at 7200 rpm, ~4 ms.
  3. **Transfer:** read the sector. ~0.01 ms for a single sector.

Total: ~5–15 ms per random read, typical (older or laptop 2.5″ drives reach 20 ms). The _seek_ and _rotational_ costs dominate; the actual transfer is negligible.

#### Pattern: Sequential vs. random

A single random read costs ~10 ms. But reading the _next_ sector on the same track costs only the transfer time (~0.01 ms) — no seek, no rotational delay.

* **Sequential reads:** ~100 MB/s throughput.
* **Random reads:** ~100 IOPS (each a new seek + rotational delay).

That is a **1000× difference**. HDD-aware file systems (FFS, ext2, NTFS) were explicitly designed to lay out data sequentially, minimizing seeks. When the workload is inherently random (databases, web servers with large working sets), HDDs impose a hard ceiling on throughput.

#### Common Misconception

_"Disks are just slow memory."_

Disks are slow _and_ nonuniform. A sequential pattern on disk can outperform a random pattern on DRAM on bandwidth. Ignoring access-pattern matters is the single biggest mistake in designing storage systems. File systems, databases, and log-structured architectures all exist to exploit this asymmetry.

#### Definition: HDD performance characteristics

**Definition:** An _HDD_ (hard disk drive) is a storage device with a rotating magnetic platter and a moving read/write head. Its performance is characterized by _seek time_ (head repositioning), _rotational latency_ (waiting for the target sector), and _transfer rate_ (data read/written while the sector is under the head). Sequential access is dramatically faster than random access.

### 22.5 The Solid-State Drive

HDDs are mechanical. SSDs use non-volatile flash memory — no moving parts, vastly lower latency.

#### Example: Anatomy of an SSD

* **Flash cells.** Transistor-based storage cells holding bits via trapped charge.
* **Pages.** Read/write granularity, typically 4-16 KB.
* **Blocks.** Erase granularity, typically 128-512 pages (512 KB - 8 MB).
* **Controller.** Flash translation layer (FTL) maps logical addresses to physical flash locations; does wear leveling, garbage collection, error correction.

Performance (typical 2024-era consumer drives; datacenter NVMe is faster still):

* **Random read:** ~100 µs on SATA SSDs; ~25–50 µs on consumer NVMe; under 10 µs on high-end datacenter NVMe. Hundreds to thousands of times faster than HDD.
* **Random write:** similar latency to random read in the common case, but see _write amplification_ below.
* **Sequential throughput:** 500 MB/s for SATA SSDs; 3–7 GB/s for PCIe Gen4 NVMe; 12+ GB/s for Gen5 NVMe.
* **Tail latency.** Under heavy write load and background garbage collection, SSDs can exhibit tail latencies of 500 µs to several ms — dramatically higher than the "typical" figures. Systems that care about p99 latency (databases, real-time serving) budget for this, not the median.

SSDs are "flat" in a way HDDs aren't: random and sequential access have similar per-operation cost, so workload access patterns matter less. But they have unique quirks:

#### Write amplification

A flash _block_ can only be erased as a whole. To modify a single page:

  1. Read the entire block into memory.
  2. Erase the block.
  3. Modify the target page in memory.
  4. Write the whole block back.

In practice the FTL avoids this by writing modified pages to a _new_ block and garbage-collecting later, but the effective write cost is still higher than the logical write size. Write amplification is not a single mechanism but the **composition** of several:

* **Garbage collection (GC) overhead** — when the FTL reclaims a partially-used erase block, it copies the still-valid pages to a new block before erasing. The factor depends on how full the drive is: a drive at 50% utilization yields ~2× GC amplification, 80% utilization ~5×, 95% utilization closer to 20× (because each erasure recovers fewer free pages).
* **Wear-leveling copies** — periodically the FTL relocates cold, long-lived data onto high-wear cells so writes spread evenly; typically a small additional factor of ~1.1–1.5×.
* **Log-structured bookkeeping** — the FTL maintains metadata (logical→physical maps, erase counts) that must itself be written; typically ~1.05–1.2×.

These factors compose multiplicatively. On a drive at 50% utilization with mixed workloads you see total amplification around 2–3×; at 80% utilization, 5–8×; on nearly-full drives under heavy small-random-write load, 10–20× is reported in the field. A 4 KB user write typically produces 8–24 KB of actual flash writes, with "10×" widely cited as a worst-case planning figure. Sequential writes, or writes aligned to erase-block boundaries, incur far less amplification because GC has few still-valid pages to copy — which is why log-structured and copy-on-write file systems play well with SSDs (Chapter 25).

#### Wear leveling

Each flash cell can be written a limited number of times (thousands to tens of thousands for consumer SSDs). If the FTL wrote the same physical cell repeatedly, that cell would fail quickly. Wear leveling spreads writes across the whole flash, so all cells age uniformly. A well-designed SSD can survive many years of typical use.

#### Pattern

SSDs have opened the door to workloads impossible on HDDs (random-access databases, frequent small writes, fast startup). But the OS still benefits from treating storage as slow relative to DRAM, and from minimizing write volume. The old wisdom "lay out data sequentially" is less critical but still helps; the new wisdom "amortize writes" is more critical because of amplification and wear.

#### Common Misconception

_"SSDs are just faster HDDs."_

SSDs are faster and have fundamentally different failure modes. HDDs fail mechanically; SSDs fail electrically (cells wear out, controllers have firmware bugs that can corrupt data). Backup discipline matters either way, but the specifics differ. Software tuned for HDDs (e.g., ext2 with heavy journaling) may not be optimal for SSDs (which prefer fewer writes, larger transfers).

### 22.6 Common I/O Abstractions in the OS

#### Block devices

A **block device** is a storage device exposed to the OS as an array of fixed-size blocks. The OS reads and writes via LBA (logical block address). File systems are built on top of block devices.

#### Character devices

A **character device** handles streams of bytes rather than fixed blocks. Terminals, serial ports, keyboards, mice are character devices. Access is usually byte-at-a-time, with no seeking.

#### Network devices

Network cards expose their own interfaces — packet send/receive, not block I/O. The OS exposes them through higher-level abstractions (sockets, Chapter 26).

#### Block cache

The OS maintains a **block cache** (also called the _page cache_ on Linux) that holds recently-accessed disk blocks in DRAM. Subsequent reads of the same block hit the cache instead of the disk. Writes may be deferred and batched to the disk asynchronously.

The block cache is transparent: programs that call `read()` on a file see fast or slow reads depending on whether the block is cached, but the interface doesn't change. Linux's page cache unifies this with the filesystem's directory/file view, so mmap-ed files and regular read/write share the same underlying buffers.

#### Pattern

The OS builds a hierarchy of caches: CPU L1/L2/L3, DRAM, SSD (when used as cache for slower tier), HDD, tape. Each layer amortizes the next layer's latency. Every modern system is some combination of these caches, and much of the OS's performance work is about keeping the working set in the fastest tier.

### 22.7 Scheduling I/O

When many I/O requests are pending, the OS must decide which to issue first.

#### HDD-specific scheduling

For HDDs, rearranging the order of pending requests can minimize seek time. The **elevator algorithm (SCAN)** sweeps the arm from one edge of the disk to the other, serving all requests in that direction, then reverses. **SSTF (shortest seek time first)** picks the closest request. Linux historically had several I/O schedulers (CFQ, Deadline, Anticipatory) tuned for HDDs.

#### SSD-specific scheduling

SSDs don't benefit from seek-minimizing scheduling (seeks are free). They benefit from batching writes (reduce write amplification) and from not blocking reads behind writes (reads are much more latency-sensitive). Linux's `mq-deadline` and `kyber` schedulers are SSD-aware.

#### Pattern

I/O scheduling is device-specific. The right algorithm depends on the physics. Modern kernels expose a framework (blk-mq) that lets each driver pick its own scheduler.

### 22.8 Chapter Summary

* Persistent storage is hierarchically slower than DRAM. HDDs are ~10 ms per random read; SSDs ~100 µs; DRAM ~100 ns.
* The OS communicates with devices via **MMIO** , **DMA** , and **interrupts**.
* A **driver** bridges a specific device to the generic OS interface.
* **HDDs** have seek and rotational costs; sequential access is ~1000× faster than random.
* **SSDs** have uniform access cost but suffer write amplification and wear; controllers manage these via flash translation layers.
* The OS maintains a **block cache / page cache** that caches disk blocks in DRAM.
* **I/O scheduling** is device-specific: HDDs benefit from seek minimization; SSDs from write batching.

Chapter 23 introduces the file system — the OS's abstraction for persistent storage. Files and directories give programmers a clean way to think about persistence. Threading Plan A returns briefly when we discuss shared file opens and how file descriptor tables interact with processes.

### 22.9 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** What are the three main mechanisms the CPU uses to communicate with I/O devices?

**Answer:** (1) _Memory-mapped I/O (MMIO):_ the device's control registers appear at specific memory addresses; CPU reads/writes them like memory but accesses go to the device. (2) _Direct memory access (DMA):_ the device transfers data directly between its own buffers and host DRAM without CPU involvement, after the CPU sets up the transfer. (3) _Interrupts:_ the device signals the CPU when an operation completes, causing the CPU to trap into a handler.

**Q2.** Why is sequential access on an HDD so much faster than random access?

**Answer:** A random access on an HDD requires a _seek_ (moving the arm, up to 15 ms) and a _rotational wait_ (up to one revolution, 8 ms at 7200 rpm). A sequential access to the next sector incurs neither — the arm is already on the right track, and the next sector is about to pass under the head. The actual data-transfer time is microseconds. So random I/O is dominated by mechanical delays (~10 ms), while sequential I/O is limited only by transfer speed (~0.01 ms per sector). The ratio is ~1000×.

**Q3.** What is write amplification, and why is it a concern for SSDs?

**Answer:** _Write amplification_ is the phenomenon where writing L bytes of user data causes more than L bytes of physical flash writes — because SSDs can erase only whole blocks (hundreds of pages), modifying one page typically requires copying a whole block's worth of data. The FTL's garbage collection also causes additional internal writes. Write amplification is a concern because each flash cell can endure only a limited number of writes before failing; higher amplification shortens the drive's life and also reduces throughput (more physical work per logical write).

**Q4.** What does a device driver do?

**Answer:** A driver is OS code that implements a generic OS interface (read block, write block, poll status) for a specific class of devices. It knows the device's command protocol, register layout, and interrupt conventions. It exposes a uniform abstraction upward so the rest of the OS doesn't depend on the specific device manufacturer. Drivers are how the OS supports thousands of different devices without rewriting the kernel for each.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** An HDD has an average seek time of 10 ms, rotates at 7200 rpm, and transfers at 100 MB/s. Estimate the time to read 1000 random 4 KB blocks.

**Answer:**

* Seek per access: 10 ms.
* Rotational latency: half a revolution = 0.5 × (60 / 7200) = 4.17 ms.
* Transfer time per block: 4 KB / 100 MB/s = 0.04 ms.

Per access: ~14.2 ms. For 1000 accesses: 14.2 seconds.

Interestingly, the same 4 MB (1000 × 4 KB) read _sequentially_ would take 4 MB / 100 MB/s = 40 ms — 350× faster.

**Q6.** An SSD has 4 KB page size, 512 KB erase block size, and 1000-write endurance per cell. An application writes 4 KB of data to a fresh page, then modifies that 4 KB 999 times in place (assume no wear leveling, which is unrealistic but illustrative). How many actual flash writes occur, and why?

**Answer:** Without wear leveling or copy-on-write optimization: each modification requires (1) reading the 512 KB block, (2) erasing the block (counting as one write cycle for every cell in the block), (3) writing 512 KB back. That's 128 pages × 999 modifications = 128,000 page writes to represent 999 × 4 KB = 4 MB of logical writes. Write amplification: 128×. Without any wear leveling, all 128 pages of the block hit their endurance after only 1 modification each; the rest of the writes land on worn-out cells and cause errors.

With a proper FTL + wear leveling, each modification is redirected to a _different_ fresh page; the original is marked invalid and eventually garbage-collected. Writes are amplified (due to GC) but spread over all the drive's cells. A drive with 1 TB of flash cells at 1000 endurance each can absorb ~1 PB of physical writes before wearing out — enough for many years of typical use.

**Q7.** An application reads 1 GB of data from an SSD (NVMe, 3 GB/s sustained read). How long does the read take at the raw device level? Why might the user observe a significantly longer time?

**Answer:** Raw device time: 1 GB / 3 GB/s = 333 ms. User-observed time may be longer because:

* _Context switches and OS overhead:_ each read syscall has overhead; small reads incur more overhead than large ones.
* _Buffer copying:_ the OS copies data from the disk into a page cache, then into the user buffer. Two memory copies add DRAM bandwidth time.
* _Interrupt overhead:_ each I/O completion raises an interrupt, costing ~1 µs. If reads are split into many small transfers, thousands of interrupts add latency.
* _Block cache misses:_ first-time reads populate the cache; subsequent reads of the same data are much faster. The first pass pays full I/O cost.

Typical user-observed throughput for 1 GB sequential reads: 1-2 GB/s, depending on read size. Using `O_DIRECT` (bypass cache) and large transfers (1 MB) approaches the raw device limit.

**Q8.** The Linux kernel has many I/O schedulers (none, mq-deadline, kyber, bfq). Why provide multiple? How would you choose for (a) a desktop, (b) a database server, (c) a cold-storage archive?

**Answer:** Different workloads have different priorities:

* _(a) Desktop:_ bfq (budget fair queueing) prioritizes interactive apps over background work, keeps UI responsive. Fine-grained fairness matters; throughput not critical.
* _(b) Database server:_ mq-deadline or kyber. Databases issue many concurrent I/Os; low tail latency for reads matters. Deadlines ensure no request starves. Heavy writes get batched.
* _(c) Cold-storage archive (HDDs):_ mq-deadline or simple none. Sequential throughput matters more than latency. A dumb scheduler often suffices because the workload is already large sequential operations.

The existence of multiple schedulers is a recognition that no single policy is optimal for all workloads — the same lesson we saw for page replacement and synchronization primitives.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** Storage costs have dropped exponentially for decades: 1 GB of HDD storage cost 1000 USD in 1990, 0.01 USD in 2025. Speed has _not_ dropped nearly as fast. What are the architectural consequences of this asymmetry?

**Answer:** Storage capacity grows much faster than storage bandwidth. The ratio of disk size to disk throughput — the _full-scan time_ — has grown from minutes to hours. A 1 TB HDD in 2025 takes ~3 hours to read sequentially; a 20 TB HDD takes 2 days. This has multiple consequences:

* **Full-index or full-table scans become unviable.** Databases depend on indexes, not scans, and on caching working sets in RAM or SSD.
* **Backup strategies change.** Full backups become impractical at large scales; incremental and differential backups dominate.
* **Storage hierarchies deepen.** Cold data lives on slow/cheap tiers; hot data lives on fast/expensive tiers. Policies for moving data between tiers become a central concern.
* **Checksums and error correction matter more.** More bytes = more opportunities for errors. ZFS, Btrfs, and modern databases add strong integrity checks that older systems lacked.
* **Data locality trumps compute scaling.** Moving a byte off-storage is now more expensive than the computation done on it. "Send computation to data" architectures (Hadoop, Spark) arose from this asymmetry.

The deeper lesson: _capacity growth without bandwidth growth inverts assumptions about system design._ What was cheap (full scans) is now expensive; what was expensive (random access with SSDs) is now cheap. OS and application design must adapt to the storage reality of the decade.

**Q10.** SSDs made random I/O 100× faster than HDDs. What might a hypothetical new storage technology that made random I/O 100× faster than _DRAM_ change about OS design?

**Answer:** If persistent storage were faster than DRAM, several assumptions would invert:

  1. **The distinction between memory and storage blurs.** The OS would no longer need a separate paging system and file system. A single namespace could contain all persistent state. Persistent memory products (Intel Optane, though discontinued) were a step in this direction.

  2. **Process state could be inherently durable.** Today, a process's memory is lost on crash. With durable fast memory, memory _is_ storage; a crash would leave work intact (modulo consistency concerns, which become the dominant issue).

  3. **Transactional semantics become central.** If memory is durable, every write must be considered from a consistency standpoint. Database-style transactions and logs become the fundamental primitive for all software, not just databases.

  4. **Garbage collection changes.** Long-lived objects in durable memory can outlive processes; how do you reclaim them? Reference counting with durable increment/decrement? Epoch-based GC at the OS level?

  5. **Security changes.** Data that previously "went away" when a process exited now persists. Privacy and forensic-recovery concerns intensify. Secure erasure becomes a real challenge.

  6. **Applications simplify.** Today, apps carefully marshal state between in-memory and on-disk representations. If they're unified, the marshalling goes away — but new coordination problems (which process "owns" which region? who's responsible for recovery?) replace it.

Storage Class Memory (persistent memory) research from 2010-2023 explored many of these. The market hasn't validated it yet, but the design conversation is live. If a successful technology emerges, the next decade of OS design will look very different — just as virtual memory and page tables remade the design conversation of the 1970s. Technology shifts underneath the OS; the OS adapts or is replaced by one that does.

---

## Chapter 23. The File System Interface

When the hardware-level story of Chapter 22 ended, we had raw blocks on a disk. A block is a 4 KB region of persistent bytes, indexed by an integer address. That is a cold abstraction. No programmer wants to think in block numbers. No programmer wants to remember that their essay lives at blocks 18,294 through 18,301 and the images are in blocks 94,112 through 94,288. What programmers want is a _name_ for the essay and a _name_ for the images, and they want the operating system to keep track of which blocks back each name.

That is what a file system provides. A file system is the abstraction that turns a flat array of blocks into a tree of named files that programs can open, read, write, and share.

This chapter introduces the interface that file systems expose. We defer the question of _how_ the bytes are actually laid out on disk to Chapter 24. Here we are interested only in the contract: what does a file _look like_ to a program, what operations are available, and what happens when two processes open the same file at once?

That last question is why this chapter is a deliberate callback to the threading story of Chapters 2 through 8. Processes are isolated in memory — we built page tables to guarantee that. But processes are _not_ isolated in the file system. A parent and a child who share a file descriptor share more than a number; they share a seek pointer, a set of flags, and a live reference to an open file. The file system is where independent processes coordinate on shared persistent state, and the mechanism for that coordination is built on top of fork and exec, the very primitives that introduced process isolation in the first place.

* * *

### 23.1 What Breaks Without a File System?

Before we introduce the abstraction, let's look at the world without it. Suppose your disk driver exposes only two calls: `read_block(n, buffer)` and `write_block(n, buffer)`. You want to write a text editor.

The first problem is _naming_. Where does the user's document live? You could ask the user: "please remember that your novel is at blocks 40,000 through 40,250." Nobody will tolerate that. You could maintain a mapping yourself, writing it to block 0: "block 0 contains a table that says document X is at block 40,000." But now every application that wants to access files must agree on the format of block 0. If two applications disagree, one will overwrite the other's table and destroy everyone's data.

The second problem is _sharing_. Suppose the editor is running and you want to print the same document. Both programs need to read the blocks. If the editor is in the middle of rewriting blocks 40,000–40,250 and the printer reads them concurrently, the printer sees garbage — half old document, half new. There is no protocol for "I am currently writing this file, do not read it."

The third problem is _growth_. Documents grow. If the novel was 250 blocks and you add a chapter, it becomes 260 blocks. The blocks 40,250 through 40,260 might already belong to your spreadsheet. Now you need a free-block allocator, a policy for where to put the new chapter, and a way to update your mapping. Every application needs to reimplement this, and they will disagree.

The fourth problem is _lifetime_. When the user deletes a document, the blocks should be returned to the free pool. When a process crashes mid-write, the half-written blocks should not become zombies. There is no coordinator for this.

A file system solves all four problems by being the single coordinator that the kernel interposes between programs and the raw disk.

* * *

**Example: opening a file.**

Here is what a C program does to read the first 100 bytes of a file:
    
    
    int fd = open("/home/alice/novel.txt", O_RDONLY);
    char buf[100];
    read(fd, buf, 100);
    close(fd);
    

Four calls. No block numbers. No free-block bookkeeping. No conflict with other readers. The file system handles all of it. The programmer names the file by a _path_ — a human-readable string — and the OS returns a small integer, the _file descriptor_ , which stands in for the open file throughout the program's lifetime.

* * *

**Pattern.** The file system turns three primitive questions — _where is this data?_ , _who else is using it?_ , and _how does it grow?_ — into a single abstract object called a file, and hides the block-level answers behind a narrow interface.

**Definition:** A **file** is a named, variable-length sequence of bytes that persists independently of any process. The file system is the kernel subsystem that maps human-readable names to files and coordinates access to them.

* * *

### 23.2 Files Are Identified by Inodes, Not Names

The first surprise when you look at how Unix file systems work is that names and files are _not_ the same thing.

Consider:
    
    
    link("/home/alice/novel.txt", "/home/alice/backup.txt");
    

This call does not copy the file. It creates a second name that points to the same underlying file. If Alice edits `novel.txt`, the edit is visible through `backup.txt` too. They are the same file with two names.

How is this possible? Because internally, the file system identifies files by a number called the **inode number** , not by name. A directory is simply a table that maps names to inode numbers. `novel.txt` and `backup.txt` both map to, say, inode 1,294,771. The inode is the object that owns the data blocks. Names are just pointers to inodes.

This separation matters for three reasons.

_Reason 1: renaming is cheap._ Renaming a 1 GB file from `draft1.pdf` to `final.pdf` does not touch the file's data. It only updates one entry in one directory.

_Reason 2: a file can live in multiple places at once._ A program in `/usr/bin/python` and a symlink at `/usr/local/bin/python` can both refer to the same executable. They are the same file, not two copies.

_Reason 3: deleting a file is really "unlinking a name."_ When you run `rm novel.txt`, you are not destroying the file; you are removing one name that pointed to an inode. If no names remain _and_ no processes have the file open, then the file system reclaims the data blocks. This is why, on Unix, you can delete a file that a running program still holds open — the name disappears but the data survives until the last descriptor is closed.

**Definition:** An **inode** is a fixed-size record, identified by a unique number, that stores a file's metadata (size, owner, permissions, timestamps) and the locations of its data blocks. Names in directories refer to inode numbers; a file with no remaining names _and_ no open descriptors is deleted.

* * *

#### Common Misconception

_"`rm` deletes the file."_ Not quite. `rm` calls `unlink()`, which removes one name. If other names point to the same inode (via hard links), the file remains. And even if the last name is gone, if a running process still has the file open, the kernel keeps the data alive until that process closes its descriptor. This is why, on Linux, you can update a running binary without crashing the running copy: the old inode lingers, nameless but alive, until the process exits.

* * *

### 23.3 The File API: Open, Read, Write, Close, Seek

Unix gives programs five core operations on files. We will look at each in turn.

**`open(path, flags)`** takes a path (a string like `/home/alice/novel.txt`) and a set of flags (read-only, read-write, create, truncate, append) and returns a small integer called a **file descriptor**. The descriptor is what the program uses to refer to this open file from now on. Paths are resolved once, at open time; after that, the descriptor points at the inode, and renaming the file on disk does not affect the open descriptor.

**`read(fd, buf, n)`** reads up to `n` bytes from the current position of the open file into the caller's buffer. The call returns the actual number of bytes read. Reads past end-of-file return 0.

**`write(fd, buf, n)`** writes `n` bytes from the caller's buffer into the file at the current position. If the position is past the end, the file grows. If the file doesn't have enough allocated blocks, the file system allocates more.

**`lseek(fd, offset, whence)`** moves the current position. `whence` can be `SEEK_SET` (absolute), `SEEK_CUR` (relative to current), or `SEEK_END` (relative to end). Seeking past the end is legal and creates a _sparse file_ — a hole that reads as zero but occupies no blocks.

**`close(fd)`** releases the descriptor. The OS flushes any pending writes (for buffered I/O) and decrements the reference count on the open file description. If this was the last reference, the kernel releases its in-memory state.

* * *

**Example: copying a file with the five-call API.**
    
    
    int in  = open("input.txt",  O_RDONLY);
    int out = open("output.txt", O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_TRUNC, 0644);
    char buf[4096];
    ssize_t n;
    while ((n = read(in, buf, sizeof buf)) > 0) {
        write(out, buf, n);
    }
    close(in);
    close(out);
    

Five distinct calls do everything. No block numbers, no allocator, no naming conflict. The programmer thinks only about bytes and files.

* * *

#### Why the descriptor is a small integer

You may wonder: why is `fd` a small integer and not, say, a pointer to some kernel structure? The answer is isolation. If `fd` were a pointer, a buggy program could write to it and dereference anything in the kernel. By making it an opaque integer that indexes into a per-process table, the kernel controls what it resolves to, and the user program can never construct a valid descriptor out of thin air. This is the same pattern as process IDs and the handles we saw in Chapter 3.

* * *

### 23.4 Directories and the Namespace

A directory is a file whose contents are a list of `(name, inode number)` pairs. You cannot read a directory with `read()` — the kernel forbids it, because a program that reads the raw bytes would bind itself to the on-disk format. Instead, you use `opendir()` and `readdir()`, which return the entries one at a time in a format-independent way.

Directories can contain other directories. This gives rise to the **directory tree** , rooted at `/`. A **path** like `/home/alice/novel.txt` is a recipe for walking the tree: start at root, look up `home`, look up `alice` in the resulting directory, look up `novel.txt` in that one. Each lookup is an inode fetch plus a scan of that inode's directory contents.

Two special entries exist in every directory: `.` refers to the directory itself, and `..` refers to its parent. This means `/home/alice/..` is equivalent to `/home`. The root directory's `..` refers to itself, a subtle but important base case.

* * *

#### Hard links vs soft links

We already met hard links: multiple names pointing to the same inode. Hard links have two restrictions: they must live on the same file system (inode numbers are only unique within one file system), and they cannot point to directories (allowing that would let you create cycles that break tree-walking tools).

A **symbolic link** (or **soft link**) removes both restrictions by being a different kind of object entirely. A symlink is a tiny file whose contents are a path. When the OS resolves a path and hits a symlink, it substitutes the symlink's contents and keeps walking. If the target moves or disappears, the symlink becomes **dangling** — it still exists, but resolving through it fails.

* * *

**Pattern.** A directory is a file, and a file system is a tree of files named through directories. This recursive structure lets the OS manage an arbitrarily deep namespace with the same code that manages individual files.

**Definition:** A **hard link** is a directory entry that maps a name to an existing inode; removing a hard link decrements the inode's link count but does not delete the file unless the count reaches zero. A **symbolic link** is a file whose contents are a path string; resolving a path through a symlink substitutes that string and continues.

* * *

### 23.5 File Descriptors, the Open File Table, and the Inode

Now we arrive at the subtle part, the part that connects back to Chapter 2.

When a program calls `open()`, _three_ layers of state are created, not one. This is where many students form mental models that are close, but not quite right, and where bugs involving shared files originate.

**Layer 1: the per-process file descriptor table.** Each process has its own small table, indexed by the integer descriptor. Entry `i` of process P's table holds a pointer (really, a reference) into the next layer. By convention, entries 0, 1, and 2 are standard input, standard output, and standard error. The table is _not_ shared across processes by default.

**Layer 2: the system-wide open file table.** Each entry here represents an _open file instance_ — a specific `open()` call's state. It contains the current seek position, the open flags (read-only? append? non-blocking?), and a reference to the next layer. Two different processes that each `open()` the same file independently get _two_ entries in this table: their seek positions are independent.

**Layer 3: the in-core inode (the v-node).** This is the in-memory image of the on-disk inode. It is shared by all open file table entries that point to the same file.

The three layers look like this:
    
    
    Process A                   System                 Kernel
    fd table                    open file table        inode cache
    [0] ---> stdin
    [1] ---> stdout
    [2] ---> stderr
    [3] ---> Open(novel, pos=42) --+
                                    +----> inode 1294771 (novel.txt)
    [4] ---> Open(novel, pos=0)  --+
    

Process A has opened novel.txt _twice_. The two descriptors, 3 and 4, point to different open file table entries, which have different seek positions. Both entries point to the same inode. A read through fd 3 advances position 42; a read through fd 4 advances position 0. The file's bytes are shared, but each open has its own cursor.

Now contrast that with:
    
    
    Process A (parent)          System                 Kernel
    fd table                    open file table        inode cache
    [3] ---> Open(novel, pos=42) --+
                                    +----> inode 1294771
                                    ^
    Process B (child after fork)   |
    fd table                        |
    [3] --------------------------- +
    

Process B was forked from A, and fork copies the fd table but does _not_ duplicate the open file entries. Both processes' fd 3 points to the _same_ open file entry. They share a seek position. If A reads 100 bytes, the position in the shared open-file entry advances by 100, and B's next read picks up where A left off.

This is not a bug. It is the mechanism that makes shell pipelines work. When you type `ls | grep txt`, the shell forks a child for `ls` and a child for `grep`, arranges that their standard descriptors point at the two ends of a pipe, and both sides happily read and write through the same open file entries, advancing a shared position in lockstep.

* * *

#### Common Misconception

_"Two processes opening the same file will interfere with each other's cursor."_ No — independent `open()` calls produce independent open file entries, each with its own seek position. They interfere only at the inode level (both see the same bytes). Shared seek positions arise only from _inheriting_ a descriptor through `fork()`, or from explicit `dup()` on a descriptor within a single process.

* * *

### 23.6 Dup, Fork, Exec: How Descriptors Flow Between Programs

We said in Chapter 2 that `fork()` creates a child that is nearly an exact copy of the parent, and `exec()` replaces the child's program with a new one. The file system interface depends on specific behavior of these calls.

**`fork()`** copies the parent's fd table into the child. Entries point at the same system-wide open file entries, not duplicates. This means parent and child share seek positions for inherited descriptors. The reference count on each shared open file entry is incremented.

**`exec()`** by default _preserves_ the fd table. The new program starts running with whatever descriptors the old program had open. This is how `ls` ends up writing to a pipe: the shell opened the pipe, arranged fd 1 to point at the pipe's write end, then exec'd `ls`. The new program doesn't know about the pipe, but fd 1 is already wired up, and when `ls` calls `write(1, ...)`, the bytes flow into the pipe.

The exception is descriptors marked with the **close-on-exec** flag (set via `fcntl`). Those are automatically closed by the kernel during `exec`. A file descriptor that a program opened for its own bookkeeping, but that should not leak into a program it launches, should be marked close-on-exec. Forgetting this is a classic security bug.

**`dup(fd)`** and **`dup2(fd, newfd)`** add another entry in the fd table that points at the same open file entry. Both descriptors share a seek position. This is how you redirect standard output to a file:
    
    
    int f = open("output.txt", O_WRONLY | O_CREAT | O_TRUNC, 0644);
    dup2(f, 1);   // fd 1 now points at the same open file entry as f
    close(f);     // we don't need the original f anymore; fd 1 still works
    // ... exec the program; its writes to stdout land in output.txt
    

`dup2` is not creating a copy of the file. It is creating a new _name_ (the descriptor number) for an existing open. When the program later writes to fd 1, it advances the same position that `f` would have advanced.

* * *

**Pattern.** The three layers — per-process descriptor, shared open file entry, shared inode — support exactly three useful sharing modes: (1) two independent opens of the same file, which share only the inode; (2) `fork()`, which shares both the descriptor's open file entry and its seek pointer across processes; (3) `dup()`, which creates aliases within one process. Every Unix trick involving file descriptors is built from these.

* * *

### 23.7 Permissions and the Check at Open Time

Every file has an owner (a user ID), a group (a group ID), and a set of permission bits — read, write, execute — for three classes: owner, group, and everyone else. When a program calls `open(path, O_RDONLY)`, the kernel checks the caller's user ID and group ID against the file's permissions and refuses if they don't grant read access.

The check happens _at open time_ , not at each read. Once a descriptor is obtained, subsequent reads and writes do not re-check. This has two consequences.

First, if the file's permissions are changed _after_ a program has opened it, the program keeps its access. An `open()` that was legal at 9:00 continues to work at 10:00 even if someone revoked the permission at 9:30. The OS trusts the descriptor.

Second, and more importantly, this supports privilege-dropping patterns: a program that needs to read a privileged file can open it while running as root, drop its privileges (switch to a non-root user ID), and continue reading. The descriptor remains valid. This is how programs like `passwd` work.

* * *

#### Concept: Setuid, Setgid, and the Sticky Bit

Three special permission bits modify the default check. **Setuid** causes the program, when executed, to run as the file's owner, not the caller. **Setgid** does the same for groups. The **sticky bit** , when set on a directory, restricts deletion so that only a file's owner can unlink it — this is what keeps one user from clearing out `/tmp`.

These bits are powerful and dangerous. A setuid binary owned by root is effectively a kernel extension; a single bug in it is a local root exploit. Modern systems use capabilities to give programs fine-grained privileges rather than the all-or-nothing of setuid root, but setuid remains widespread.

* * *

### 23.8 Pipes, FIFOs, and Special Files

Not every file descriptor refers to a named file on disk. Unix has a tradition: "everything is a file." The file descriptor interface is reused for many things.

**Pipes** are unnamed, kernel-internal byte queues. `pipe()` returns two descriptors, a read end and a write end. Bytes written to one come out of the other, in order. Pipes are created by one process and passed to children via `fork()`; they die when all descriptors are closed.
