**FIFOs** , also called named pipes, are pipes that have a name in the file system. `mkfifo("/tmp/myfifo", 0644)` creates one. Any process can `open()` it for reading or writing, and bytes flow. A FIFO has no data blocks on disk; it is a rendezvous point.

**Device files** , typically under `/dev`, are not files at all — they are handles for the kernel's device drivers. `/dev/sda` refers to the first disk; `/dev/null` discards writes; `/dev/urandom` produces random bytes. The filesystem-like interface lets command-line tools manipulate devices without special APIs.

**Sockets** look like file descriptors too, though their full API (connect, bind, accept) is larger than the five-call interface.

The point: by giving all of these things file descriptors, the kernel lets programs be written once and work with many kinds of streams. A program that reads from fd 0 does not care whether fd 0 is a terminal, a file, a pipe, or a network socket.

* * *

**Pattern.** The file descriptor interface is a polymorphic handle. By making many different kernel objects presentable through the same five-call API, the kernel enables programs to compose — piping, redirecting, filtering — without knowing the type of what they are reading from or writing to.

* * *

### 23.9 Buffering and the Real Cost of write()

A call to `write(fd, buf, n)` does not always mean "the bytes are on disk when this call returns." In fact, it almost never means that.

The kernel maintains a **page cache** (sometimes called the buffer cache) — an in-memory copy of recently-used file blocks, living in RAM. When you write, the kernel copies your bytes into the cache and marks those pages dirty. The write returns immediately. The dirty pages are flushed to disk later, by a background daemon, according to a policy (typically 5–30 seconds, depending on the system).

This is a massive performance win. A typical program writes in small chunks (4 bytes, 80 bytes), and the disk can only efficiently accept full blocks (4 KB). The cache accumulates small writes into full blocks before they hit the disk. It also lets reads hit memory for recently-touched data instead of going to disk. In steady state, most reads and writes never touch the disk at all.

The cost is that **the bytes are not durable until the flush happens.** If the power fails between the write call and the flush, your bytes are gone — the program was told the write succeeded, but the disk never actually got the data. Programs that need durability must call `fsync(fd)`, which blocks until all dirty pages belonging to that file have been flushed to the physical disk (and the disk itself has acknowledged them, assuming write caches are honest).

`fsync` is expensive. A database that calls `fsync` on every transaction is limited by the disk's sync rate, which on a traditional HDD is about 100 operations per second. This is why databases batch: group many logical writes into one `fsync` call.

* * *

**Definition:** The **page cache** is an in-memory cache of file data that absorbs reads and writes without always touching the disk. **fsync** forces pending writes for a file through the cache and onto stable storage.

* * *

#### Common Misconception

_"A successful`write()` means the data is on disk."_ It does not. It means the data is in the kernel's cache and will be written to disk eventually. For durability you must call `fsync` (or `fdatasync`, or open with `O_SYNC`). Forgetting this is one of the most common bugs in programs that claim to be crash-safe.

* * *

### 23.10 Summary

A file system replaces raw blocks with named, variable-length byte sequences called files, organized in a directory tree. Files are identified internally by inode numbers; names are entries in directories that point to inodes. The interface is the five core calls — open, read, write, lseek, close — supplemented by directory and link operations.

The subtle connection to threading, deferred since Chapter 8, lives in the three-layer structure of file access: the per-process descriptor table, the system-wide open file table, and the shared inode. Descriptors flow between processes through `fork()` and `dup()`, and this is how pipelines, redirections, and every shell trick involving `|`, `<`, and `>` is implemented. Two processes that share a descriptor through fork share a seek position; two processes that each open the same file independently do not.

Finally, the file system's performance depends on the page cache: writes return long before bytes hit the disk. This is fast in the common case and dangerous when durability matters; programs that need survivability across crashes must call `fsync`.

Chapter 24 opens the black box. We will see how the file system actually represents files on disk: how an inode stores the locations of its data blocks, how the free space bitmap works, how directories are organized internally, and how a well-designed file system minimizes the expensive disk seeks that Chapter 22 warned us about.

* * *

### 23.11 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A process calls `open("A.txt", O_RDONLY)` and receives fd 3. It then calls `fork()`. The child calls `read(3, buf, 100)`. The parent then calls `read(3, buf, 100)`. How many bytes does the parent read, and starting from which position?

**Answer:** The parent reads 100 bytes starting at byte 100. After `fork()`, parent and child's fd 3 entries point at the _same_ open file entry, which has a shared seek position. The child's read advanced that shared position to 100. The parent's subsequent read starts where the child left off and advances the shared position to 200.

**Q2.** Explain why `rm` on a file that is currently open by a running program does not immediately free the disk blocks.

**Answer:** `rm` calls `unlink()`, which decrements the inode's link count (number of names pointing to it). If the count reaches zero _but_ there are still open descriptors pointing at the inode, the file system keeps the inode and data blocks alive. The blocks are freed only when the last name is gone _and_ the last descriptor is closed. This allows programs to use deleted files as private scratch space that cleans itself up on exit.

**Q3.** A program opens file F twice, getting fd 3 and fd 4. It reads 50 bytes through fd 3, then reads 50 bytes through fd 4. What bytes does the fd 4 read return?

**Answer:** Bytes 0–49. Two independent `open()` calls create two independent open file entries, each with its own seek position initialized to 0. The read through fd 3 advances fd 3's position to 50, but does not affect fd 4's position. The fd 4 read starts at 0.

**Q4.** Why is reading a directory with `read()` forbidden on modern systems, even though a directory is technically a file?

**Answer:** Directory layouts are file-system-specific. ext4 directories look different from XFS directories and different again from NTFS. If programs could `read()` directories and parse the bytes, they would bind themselves to one format, and changing the format would break every such program. The `opendir`/`readdir` interface returns entries in a format-independent structure, insulating programs from the on-disk representation and letting file systems evolve.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Write pseudocode for a shell that implements the pipeline `ls | wc -l` using `pipe()`, `fork()`, `dup2()`, and `exec()`. Explain what each file descriptor looks like in each process at each step.

**Answer:**
    
    
    int p[2];
    pipe(p);          // p[0] = read end, p[1] = write end
    if (fork() == 0) {       // child 1: ls
        dup2(p[1], 1);       // stdout now points to the write end
        close(p[0]); close(p[1]);   // close originals; fd 1 still works
        exec("ls");
    }
    if (fork() == 0) {       // child 2: wc
        dup2(p[0], 0);       // stdin now points to the read end
        close(p[0]); close(p[1]);
        exec("wc -l");
    }
    close(p[0]); close(p[1]); // parent closes both ends
    wait(); wait();
    

The parent's `close(p[1])` is essential: if the parent left the write end open, `wc` would hang waiting for more input because the pipe would not receive EOF until _all_ write ends are closed — including the unused one in the parent.

**Q6.** A program opens a file, then repeatedly calls `write(fd, buf, 100)` one million times. Halfway through, power fails. What might the file look like on disk when the machine reboots? What could the program have done to guarantee a different outcome?

**Answer:** The file might contain some prefix of the intended data — possibly nothing, possibly a few thousand bytes, possibly nearly everything. Each `write()` only updated the kernel's page cache; the page cache flushes asynchronously. The length of the flushed prefix depends on how often the flusher ran and how much was dirty. To guarantee specific bytes made it to disk, the program should call `fsync(fd)` after the writes it wants to be durable. `fsync` blocks until the OS confirms the data reached stable storage. An alternative is `open(..., O_SYNC)`, which makes every `write` block until durable — safer but much slower.

**Q7.** Explain the difference, at the level of the three-layer model, between the following two programs:
    
    
    // Program 1:
    int f = open("A", O_RDONLY);
    int g = open("A", O_RDONLY);
    
    
    
    // Program 2:
    int f = open("A", O_RDONLY);
    int g = dup(f);
    

**Answer:** In Program 1, two separate `open()` calls produce two entries in the per-process fd table (`f` and `g`) that point at _two different_ open file table entries, each with its own seek position. Reads through `f` do not affect `g`'s position.

In Program 2, `dup(f)` creates a new fd table entry `g` that points at the _same_ open file table entry as `f`. Both `f` and `g` share a seek position. Reads through `f` advance `g`'s position and vice versa. Both point to the same inode (obviously), but the crucial difference is at the middle layer: shared vs unshared open file entries.

**Q8.** A web server uses a sandboxed worker process to handle untrusted requests. The worker is forked from a parent that opens a log file and an audit database. Which of these descriptors should be marked close-on-exec, and why?

**Answer:** Both. If the worker is compromised, any descriptor it inherits is accessible to the attacker. The log file gives the attacker write access to log entries (they can hide their tracks); the audit database gives the attacker write access to audit records. Both should be marked close-on-exec so that when the worker calls `exec()` to load the handler binary, the descriptors are automatically closed. The parent can separately send a restricted log descriptor to the worker through a Unix socket after the exec, if the worker actually needs logging.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** In Chapter 2 we introduced `fork()` as the mechanism that creates isolated processes. In this chapter we saw that `fork()` also _shares_ state — specifically, seek positions inside the open file table. How is this consistent? Isn't it a hole in process isolation? Argue either (a) that it _is_ a hole and should be considered a design flaw, or (b) that it is a carefully-designed feature and is essential to Unix's power. Give concrete examples to support your position.

**Answer:** This question rewards either answer well-defended. A good (b) argument: Unix's power comes precisely from this sharing. Pipelines, redirections, and the entire shell vocabulary depend on the child inheriting descriptors and writing into them. If `fork()` duplicated the open file table entries, then `ls | grep` would not work — the child's write to the pipe would go to a different open file entry and the parent wouldn't see it. Isolation is about memory and address spaces, not about every kernel object. The OS deliberately separates the two layers of file state — the per-process table (isolated) and the system-wide open file table (shareable) — so that sharing is opt-in (through inheritance) rather than forced or forbidden.

A good (a) argument: The sharing is easy to forget. A programmer who mentally models processes as isolated will be surprised when a child that reads from an inherited descriptor also advances the parent's cursor. Many bugs in multi-process daemons come from exactly this surprise. A cleaner design would duplicate open file entries on fork (like memory is duplicated) and require an explicit `dup_shared()` for the pipeline case.

Both positions are defensible. The best answer engages with the tension rather than resolving it one way.

**Q10.** The chapter argues that `fsync` is necessary for durability because `write()` returns before bytes reach the disk. But suppose we designed an alternative OS where every `write()` went synchronously to disk (equivalent to always opening with `O_SYNC`). What would be faster in this hypothetical OS? What would be dramatically slower? Would you still need `fsync`? And what does your answer reveal about _why_ Unix's asynchronous-by-default design is the one that won?

**Answer:** Under synchronous-by-default, _small-write-heavy workloads_ (editors saving 80-byte updates, database logs) would be dramatically slower — each 80-byte write would cost ~10 ms on an HDD, limiting such workloads to ~100 writes/second. _Large sequential writes_ (copying a multi-GB file) would be similar in both designs, since the disk can accept big writes efficiently. _Read-heavy workloads_ would be unchanged, since reads already benefit from the page cache in both designs.

You would not need `fsync`, since every write would already be durable on return. But you would immediately _want_ something like the page cache back — programs would start buffering in user space (which they already do, in `fwrite` and friends). You'd end up reinventing the cache one level up, with less information and more complexity.

The insight: the OS's choice to buffer by default pushes performance up by orders of magnitude for the overwhelmingly common case, and exposes `fsync` as an escape hatch for the rare case where you need durability. The alternative forces everyone to pay for durability even when they don't need it, and ends up with worse results because user-space buffering is dumber than kernel-space buffering. Unix's design is a bet that durability is rarer than speed. For editors, compilers, and most applications, that bet is right. For databases, it's wrong, and `fsync` is how databases opt out.

---

## Chapter 24. File System Implementation

Chapter 23 introduced the file system interface — files, inodes, directories, descriptors — but left the implementation as a black box. We now open the box. How are files actually laid out on a disk made of 4 KB blocks? How does the OS find an inode by number? How does it decide where to put a new file's data? How does it keep the free list accurate without losing it on a crash?

We will build up the answer in three stages. First, a minimally functional file system — call it **vsfs** , the "very simple file system." It will be correct and simple, and it will be painfully slow on real disks. Then we will examine the two problems that make it slow and see the classic fix, the **Fast File System (FFS)** , introduced by McKusick et al. in 1984, whose ideas still shape Linux's ext2, ext3, and ext4. Finally, we will look at what modern file systems add on top: extent-based allocation, delayed allocation, and larger block groups.

The persistence story in this chapter is still about the _steady state_ — how a file system works when nothing crashes. Chapter 25 handles what happens when the power fails mid-operation, which is a qualitatively different problem that needs journaling or copy-on-write.

* * *

### 24.1 What Breaks Without a Layout?

Start with a blank 1 GB disk — 262,144 blocks of 4 KB each. You need to store files, directories, and some bookkeeping. Let's see what goes wrong with naive designs.

_Naive design 1: write files contiguously, starting from block 0._ The first file occupies blocks 0 through k-1. The second starts at block k. This works until the first file _grows_. If file 1 wants to add blocks, they must go after file 2's blocks, which means file 1 is no longer contiguous. Or file 2 must move, which means every reference to file 2 must be updated. Either the file system is contiguous and cannot grow files, or it is not contiguous and needs a different mechanism.

_Naive design 2: give each file a linked list of blocks._ Each block's last 4 bytes point to the next block. This avoids the growth problem. But reading a file requires _one disk seek per block_ , because you cannot know block N+1 until you have read block N. A 400 KB file is 100 blocks; reading it takes 100 disk seeks — a full second on an HDD. Intolerable.

_Naive design 3: keep all the block pointers in one central table (FAT style)._ A single table indexed by block number, where each entry gives the next block in that file's chain. This is how FAT file systems work. It is better than per-block pointers because we can walk the chain entirely in the table, but the table is huge and must be cached or traversed for every open, and fragmentation grows without bound.

The insight that underlies modern file systems is _per-file indexing_ : each file gets its own small index structure (an inode) containing pointers to its data blocks. The inode is small and fits in a single disk block. You find a file's inode once, and then you have direct pointers to all its data blocks — no chains to traverse.

**Pattern.** When a large structure must be variable-length, don't store chains or central tables — store _per-object indices_ that aggregate pointers compactly. File system inodes, memory page tables, and database record headers all use this pattern.

* * *

### 24.2 The On-Disk Layout of vsfs

Our very simple file system divides the disk into five regions:
    
    
    [ Superblock | Inode Bitmap | Data Bitmap | Inode Table | Data Blocks ]
    

**Superblock** (block 0). One block containing metadata about the file system: how many inodes exist, how many data blocks exist, where each region starts, the file system's magic number and version. Read once at mount time.

**Inode bitmap.** One bit per inode, indicating whether it is free or allocated. If the file system has 80,000 inodes, this is 10,000 bytes — less than 3 blocks.

**Data bitmap.** One bit per data block. For a 1 GB file system with 4 KB blocks, there are 262,144 data blocks, so the bitmap is 32 KB — 8 blocks.

**Inode table.** An array of inodes. Each inode is typically 256 bytes, so 16 inodes fit per 4 KB block. If the file system has 80,000 inodes, the inode table is 20 MB — 5,000 blocks.

**Data region.** Everything else. Files' actual bytes live here.

Given this layout, the inode for inode number _k_ lives at a fixed, computable offset: `inode_table_start + k × inode_size`. No search. The inode bitmap tells us which numbers are free. When we create a new file, we scan the bitmap for a 0 bit, flip it to 1, and we have an inode number.

* * *

### 24.3 Inside an Inode

An inode holds the metadata of a file and a set of pointers to its data blocks. Here is a representative layout (sizes vary by file system):
    
    
    size: 8 bytes        total length in bytes
    uid/gid: 8 bytes     owner and group
    mode: 4 bytes        type (file/dir/symlink) and permissions
    atime/mtime/ctime: 24 bytes  timestamps
    nlink: 4 bytes       number of hard links
    blocks: 8 bytes      number of allocated blocks
    direct[12]: 48 bytes 12 direct block pointers (4 bytes each)
    indirect: 4 bytes    pointer to indirect block
    double_indirect: 4   pointer to double-indirect block
    triple_indirect: 4   pointer to triple-indirect block
    

The interesting part is the block pointers. An inode has room for 12 **direct pointers** , each naming one 4 KB data block. That covers files up to 48 KB. For larger files we need more pointers than fit in the inode.

Enter the **indirect block**. The indirect pointer names a disk block that is _itself_ nothing but pointers — 4 KB / 4 bytes = 1,024 more pointers. Add 1,024 × 4 KB = 4 MB of addressable data. Now files up to 4.048 MB are supported.

For still-larger files, the **double indirect** pointer names a block of pointers, each of which names a block of pointers, each of which names a data block: 1,024 × 1,024 × 4 KB ≈ 4 GB.

The **triple indirect** gives 1,024³ × 4 KB ≈ 4 TB.

This "~4 TB" figure assumes the classic vsfs layout in which _every_ pointer slot — at every level of the indirect tree — addresses exactly one 4 KB block. Real production file systems don't do this any more: **ext4** (the dominant Linux FS since ~2008) uses **extents** in the inode itself — a small tree of `(start_block, length)` ranges — instead of a fixed pyramid of direct/indirect/double/triple pointers. With extents, a 4 TB file of mostly sequential data is represented by a handful of extent entries rather than a billion individual pointers. Consequences worth keeping in mind when you read a modern file-system manual:

* The vsfs "maximum file size = sum-of-pointer-levels" arithmetic does not apply to ext4/XFS/NTFS/btrfs/ZFS. Their size limits come from extent-tree geometry plus on-disk integer widths (ext4 tops out at 16 TiB per file with default 4 KB blocks, much more with larger blocks).
* Metadata overhead for large contiguous files is dramatically lower — the inode may be self-describing without any external index blocks at all.
* Small, fragmented files (many non-contiguous one-block runs) lose this advantage; the extent count grows, and extent-tree depth can eventually match or exceed the indirect-pointer tree.

vsfs's indirect-pointer design remains the right mental model for understanding how an inode turns a file offset into a disk-block address, and for the arithmetic of maximum file size as a function of pointer depth. When reading about a modern file system, translate "indirect pointers" to "extent-tree nodes" and expect the specific numbers to differ.

* * *

**Example: a 10 MB file.** The first 48 KB live in blocks pointed to by `direct[0..11]`. The next 4 MB live in blocks pointed to from the indirect block. The remaining ~6 MB live in blocks pointed to from the first ~1,500 entries of the double-indirect tree. Opening this file and reading the first 48 KB requires one inode read plus 12 data reads. Reading byte 1,000,000 requires one inode read, one indirect block read, and one data block read.

* * *

**Pattern.** The indirect-block scheme is a classic **variable-depth index** : small files pay nothing beyond the inode itself (common case fast), and large files pay one extra lookup per additional level of the tree (rare case correct). The cost is logarithmic in file size.

**Definition:** An **inode** contains a file's metadata plus up to 12 direct pointers to data blocks, one pointer to an **indirect block** (which holds up to 1,024 further pointers), one **double-indirect** pointer, and one **triple-indirect** pointer. This structure supports files from 0 bytes to several terabytes while keeping the inode itself fixed-size.

* * *

### 24.4 Directories Are Files of Entries

A directory is a file whose type bit in the mode field says "directory." Its data blocks contain a sequence of entries:
    
    
    [ inode# | entry_len | name_len | type | name ]
    

Each entry has a fixed header followed by a variable-length name. `entry_len` tells how far to skip to the next entry. When a file is deleted, the entry is not physically removed — instead, the previous entry's `entry_len` is increased to "swallow" the dead entry, marking it as free space inside the directory. This is a common pattern: tombstones in place of deletion to avoid shifting other entries.

Reading a directory (e.g., `ls`) means opening the directory file and walking through its entries. Looking up a specific name means walking through until we find a match. For small directories this is fast; for directories with 100,000 entries, linear scan is slow, and modern file systems use an indexed variant (ext4's `htree` uses a hash tree). vsfs uses linear scan and pays for it when directories grow large.

When you create a file in directory D, three things happen. The file system allocates a free inode (flip a bit in the inode bitmap). It allocates free data blocks for the new file if needed. And it adds an entry `(inode#, name)` to D's data, which may require D itself to allocate a new data block if its current ones are full.

* * *

### 24.5 Reading and Writing, Step by Step

**Reading`/foo/bar.txt`.** The kernel starts at the root inode (a well-known number, typically 2). It reads the root inode from the inode table. It then reads the root directory's data block(s), searching for the name `foo`. That gives an inode number; it reads that inode from the inode table. If `foo` is a directory, the kernel reads `foo`'s data blocks, searches for `bar.txt`, finds an inode number, reads that inode. Finally, to read the file's contents, the kernel reads the appropriate direct (or indirect) block pointers and fetches the data blocks.

Each step is a disk access. A path of depth 4 requires at least 8 disk reads before the first byte of data is returned. In practice, the inode cache and page cache absorb most of this cost after the first access.

**Writing to a new file.** You call `open("/foo/newfile", O_CREAT | O_WRONLY)`. The kernel walks the path to `/foo` (same as before), reads `/foo`'s inode, reads `/foo`'s data blocks to confirm `newfile` doesn't exist. Then it allocates a new inode (read inode bitmap, find free bit, flip it, write bitmap). It initializes the inode and writes it to disk. It adds an entry `(inode#, "newfile")` to `/foo`'s data (possibly writing that block). Finally it returns a file descriptor.

When you then call `write(fd, buf, 4096)`, the kernel allocates a free data block (read data bitmap, find free bit, flip it, write bitmap), writes the bytes to that block, and updates the inode's `direct[0]` pointer plus `size` field (writes the inode again). That is 2 reads and 4 writes for one write call. In practice the page cache batches updates and the actual flushes are deferred — but when they eventually happen, all four writes must occur, and the order matters (which is what Chapter 25 is about).

* * *

#### Common Misconception

_"Writing a byte to a file causes one disk write."_ No — it causes several: the data block, the inode (because `size` or `mtime` changes), possibly the indirect block, possibly the data bitmap (if a new block was allocated). The page cache hides this cost in the common case, but the cost is real and becomes visible when you call `fsync` or when the system is under memory pressure.

* * *

### 24.6 Why vsfs Is Slow on Real Disks

vsfs is correct. It is also painfully slow. Two specific problems.

**Problem 1: Inode / data block separation.** The inode table is one region of the disk; the data blocks are a different region. Reading a small file requires seeking from the inode table to the data region, then back for the next file. Ten files means twenty seeks across the surface of the disk. On a modern NVMe SSD this is merely slow; on a 2010-era HDD this is catastrophic.

**Problem 2: Random placement within the data region.** When the file system allocates a new data block by scanning the data bitmap for any free bit, it typically picks the first one — which might be 10 GB away from the file's previous blocks. Over time, a file's blocks are scattered across the disk, and reading the file sequentially requires many seeks. This is the **fragmentation** problem from a different angle than Chapter 10's memory fragmentation: here, fragmentation is about _locality_ rather than bookkeeping, and the cost is disk seek latency.

Both problems share a root cause: vsfs has no notion of locality. It treats the disk as a flat array and ignores the physics of seek time. A smarter file system exploits locality.

* * *

### 24.7 The Fast File System (FFS): Cylinder Groups

In 1984 McKusick, Joy, Leffler, and Fabry published "A Fast File System for UNIX" introducing what became known as FFS (or UFS). The central idea is to partition the disk into many small regions, each with its own superblock, inode table, bitmap, and data region. These regions were called **cylinder groups** (because they aligned with HDD cylinders) or, in modern systems, **block groups**.

The layout of each group:
    
    
    [ Super | Inode Bitmap | Data Bitmap | Inode Table | Data Blocks ]
    [ Super | Inode Bitmap | Data Bitmap | Inode Table | Data Blocks ]
    [ Super | Inode Bitmap | Data Bitmap | Inode Table | Data Blocks ]
    ...
    

The superblock is replicated across groups to survive single-block corruption. But the important change is that **inodes and their data blocks can now live in the same group**. Reading a small file in group 7 seeks to group 7's inode table, then to group 7's data region — a short seek, not a long one.

The allocation policy enforces this:

* When you create a new file in directory D, put the file's inode in the _same_ group as D's inode. Files in the same directory cluster in the same group.
* When you create a new directory, put it in a group that is currently underused. This balances groups and prevents one group from filling up while others remain empty.
* When you allocate data blocks for a file, prefer blocks in the same group as the file's inode.

The payoff is dramatic. For workloads like reading all files in a directory — `grep`, `ls -l`, compilation — FFS's grouping converts many long seeks into many short ones. Throughput improves by a factor of 5 to 10 on HDDs.

* * *

**Pattern.** The FFS design is a classic **data locality optimization**. When the cost of access depends on spatial proximity (as with mechanical disks), arrange logically-related data physically close. Exactly the same principle that motivates cache-line-aware data structures and NUMA-aware memory allocators.

**Definition:** A **block group** (or cylinder group) is a partition of the disk containing its own inode table, block bitmap, and data blocks. The file system's allocator places related files in the same group to reduce seek distance.

* * *

### 24.8 Two More FFS Ideas: Block Size and Fragments

**Bigger blocks.** Early Unix used 512-byte blocks. FFS noticed that most files were either very small or very large — the distribution is bimodal. Using 4 KB or 8 KB blocks dramatically reduced the bookkeeping for large files (fewer pointers per file, fewer indirect blocks) and increased throughput per I/O.

**But wait** — bigger blocks waste space on small files. A 200-byte file in an 8 KB block wastes 7,800 bytes. FFS introduced **fragments** : each block can be subdivided into fragments of, say, 1 KB. A small file uses fragments; when it grows past 8 KB, its fragments are promoted to a whole block. This balances throughput (big blocks for big files) with space efficiency (small files use fragments). The fragment idea survived in subsequent file systems like UFS2 and FreeBSD, though Linux's ext-family uses a different approach.

* * *

### 24.9 What Modern File Systems Add

**Extents.** ext4, XFS, and NTFS replace individual block pointers with **extents** — `(start_block, length)` pairs. A 10 MB file of contiguous blocks might be described by a single extent `(block=42000, length=2560)` rather than 2,560 individual pointers. This shrinks metadata and speeds up I/O. The inode holds a few extents inline, and a tree of extents handles huge files.

**Delayed allocation.** Instead of picking data block locations at write time, the file system deliberately waits. While the dirty pages sit in the page cache, the application might write more to the same file. When the flush finally happens, the file system knows the _total_ size and can allocate one large contiguous extent, rather than 100 fragmented small ones. ext4 and XFS both do this. It improves locality dramatically for "small writes that aggregate into a big file" patterns.

**Larger block groups.** Modern disks are hundreds of GB, so FFS's small groups no longer match the hardware. ext4 uses block groups of 128 MB and uses _flex_bg_ to combine multiple groups' bitmaps and inode tables into one contiguous region for faster scans.

**Inline small files.** Some file systems (XFS, btrfs) store very small files directly inside the inode, saving one block access for files under a few hundred bytes. A clever mixed-size optimization.

* * *

**Pattern.** The evolution from vsfs → FFS → ext4 follows a clear direction: each step exploits more information about access patterns and hardware. Keep metadata small (extents vs pointer arrays), delay decisions until more information arrives (delayed allocation), cluster related data (block groups), and collapse small data into existing structures (inline files). These are not file-system-specific tricks; they are general principles for any layered storage.

* * *

### 24.10 Caches Above, Caches Below

Two caches matter for file system performance, and they sit at different layers.

**Above the file system: the page cache.** Introduced in Chapter 23. It caches file contents — blocks of bytes indexed by `(inode, offset)`. Most reads hit here and never touch the file system code below. The page cache is unified with the virtual memory system (Chapter 14): a memory-mapped file and a read/written file share the same cache pages.

**Below the file system: the buffer cache.** Before the unified page cache (which Linux adopted in 1999), there was a separate cache that held disk blocks indexed by `(device, block#)` — the cache used by the block layer rather than the file system. This included metadata blocks: inodes, indirect blocks, bitmaps. Modern Linux still has block-cache-like behavior but integrated into the page cache.

The important point: when a program reads a file, the kernel _first_ checks the page cache by `(inode, offset)`. On hit, no file system code runs at all. On miss, the file system computes which disk block holds the needed bytes and issues a read. That read may itself be satisfied from the buffer cache if another process recently read the same block. Only on a miss at both layers does a physical disk access occur.

For compilation-like workloads, where the same header files are read thousands of times, the hit rate on the page cache approaches 100%, and the file system's actual disk operations become rare.

* * *

#### Common Misconception

_"A file system is the layer that talks to the disk."_ Mostly false. In steady state, the file system rarely touches the disk — the page cache and block cache absorb the vast majority of operations. The file system's disk-facing code runs on cache misses and on explicit flushes (`fsync`, periodic writeback). This is why a file system's design can be relatively slow on paper yet feel instantaneous in practice: the caches hide the cost.

* * *

### 24.11 Summary

A file system implementation turns the disk into a structure: superblock, inode table, block bitmaps, and data regions. Each file's metadata lives in a fixed-size inode whose pointers form a variable-depth index — 12 direct pointers for small files, indirect and double-indirect blocks for larger ones. Directories are files whose contents are variable-length entries mapping names to inode numbers.

The simple design (vsfs) is correct but slow on mechanical disks because it ignores locality. FFS's key contribution was partitioning the disk into block groups and clustering related files in the same group, cutting seek costs by 5–10× for real workloads. Modern file systems add extents (compact block pointers), delayed allocation (better placement), and inline small files (zero-block-access for tiny files).

Two caches — the page cache and the block/buffer cache — sit above and below the file system and absorb most I/O. A file system's disk access pattern matters mostly on cache misses and on the durable path out to stable storage.

Chapter 25 addresses the problem we have so far ignored: what happens when the power fails during one of these multi-write operations? An inode may have been updated but its data blocks not yet written, or the data bitmap may be out of sync with the inode's claims. The steady-state design of this chapter assumes clean sequences of writes; the crash-consistency story is what makes file systems actually reliable.

* * *

### 24.12 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** A vsfs file system has 4 KB blocks, 4-byte block pointers, 12 direct pointers in each inode, and one indirect, one double-indirect, and one triple-indirect pointer. What is the maximum file size?

**Answer:** Direct pointers cover 12 × 4 KB = 48 KB. Indirect adds 1,024 × 4 KB ≈ 4 MB. Double-indirect adds 1,024² × 4 KB ≈ 4 GB. Triple-indirect adds 1,024³ × 4 KB ≈ 4 TB. Total: roughly 4 TB plus some (the smaller terms are negligible). The exact figure: 48 KB + 4,194,304 bytes + 4,294,967,296 bytes + 4,398,046,511,104 bytes ≈ 4.0 TB.

**Q2.** Explain in one sentence why a directory is represented as a file in Unix-style file systems, rather than as a separate kind of object.

**Answer:** Representing directories as files lets the same data-block allocator, block pointer scheme, and inode infrastructure handle both — there's no duplicate logic for growing, shrinking, or indexing directory contents versus regular file contents.

**Q3.** An inode is 256 bytes. The inode table must hold 80,000 inodes. How many 4 KB blocks does the inode table occupy, and what is the maximum number of files this file system can hold?

**Answer:** 80,000 × 256 bytes = 20,480,000 bytes = 5,000 blocks of 4 KB. The maximum number of files is 80,000 minus any reserved inodes (typically 11, for root plus system inodes) — so ~79,990.

**Q4.** Why does vsfs's allocator, which picks any free block from the data bitmap, cause fragmentation, even if no files are ever deleted?

**Answer:** Files are created and grown in interleaved ways. If files A and B are both open and growing, and A writes one block, then B writes one block, then A writes again, A's blocks will be separated by B's. Even without deletion, the allocator's lack of locality awareness scatters each file's blocks across the disk. FFS's block groups mitigate this by keeping a file's blocks in the same small region.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** Compute the number of disk I/Os needed to read the first byte of `/a/b/c.txt` in vsfs, assuming all caches are cold and no inodes have been read before. Assume `/`, `/a`, and `/a/b` are directories that each fit in one data block.

**Answer:**

  1. Read root inode (1 I/O).
  2. Read `/`'s data block (1).
  3. Search it, find inode# for `a`; read that inode (1).
  4. Read `/a`'s data block (1).
  5. Find inode# for `b`; read that inode (1).
  6. Read `/a/b`'s data block (1).
  7. Find inode# for `c.txt`; read that inode (1).
  8. Read `c.txt`'s `direct[0]` data block (1).

Total: 8 I/Os. After the first access, most of these are cached and subsequent accesses cost far less. This is why file system performance is bad under cold-cache conditions and excellent once the working set is cached.

**Q6.** Design an allocation policy for a workload in which 90% of files are smaller than 16 KB and 10% are between 1 MB and 1 GB. What should the block size be, and should you use fragments, extents, or both? Justify your choices.

**Answer:** A good answer: use a medium block size (4 KB) as the default, and use extents rather than per-block pointers for the 10% of large files. Extents compress their metadata dramatically (1 MB file = 1 extent, not 256 pointers), and the 4 KB block size is a reasonable compromise for the small-file majority. Fragments are unnecessary at 4 KB — 16 KB files use 4 blocks, wasting at most 3 KB × half = ~1.5 KB on average, which is tolerable. If the file system must support 512-byte files efficiently, inline storage (store bytes directly in the inode) is better than fragments, because it eliminates the data-block access entirely.

**Q7.** A file system uses 4 KB blocks and has 10,000 block groups. You write 100 small files (each 4 KB) into directory D. With FFS's default policy, where do those files' inodes and data blocks live?

**Answer:** All 100 inodes and all 100 data blocks live in the same block group as D's inode (assuming that group has space). FFS's policy clusters files with their parent directory. If D's group runs out of inodes or data blocks, FFS spills to a neighboring group. The result is that reading all 100 files in sequence requires only short seeks within one block group, not seeks across the whole disk.

**Q8.** Explain why delayed allocation improves locality for this workload: a program calls `fopen("a.log", "a")` and then loops, calling `fwrite` 10,000 times with 100-byte payloads. Contrast with an immediate-allocation policy.

**Answer:** Each `fwrite` buffers in the `stdio` library, so only occasional syscalls reach the kernel. With immediate allocation, each syscall causes the file system to pick and record a data block location; over time these blocks scatter as other files intersperse. With delayed allocation, the file system does not commit to block locations until the flush. By the time the flush happens, the total appended data is (say) 1 MB; the file system allocates a single contiguous extent of 256 blocks, not 256 individual blocks chosen over time. The log file ends up contiguous on disk, and subsequent reads of it are fast. ext4 and XFS both implement this and both see dramatic improvements on append-heavy workloads.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** FFS's block group policy was motivated by HDDs, where seek time is enormous (~10 ms) compared to read time. SSDs have near-zero seek time. Does the FFS design still matter on SSD-only systems, or is it a legacy optimization? Argue both sides, and then decide what you would do if designing a new file system targeted exclusively at SSD.

**Answer:** A good argument for "legacy": on SSDs, random access is effectively free. There is no seek penalty. So clustering data in block groups yields no throughput win. A new file system could treat the device as flat and allocate freely.

A good argument for "still matters": (a) even on SSDs, large contiguous extents perform measurably better than scattered small I/Os because of the I/O submission overhead, page cache prefetching, and device-internal mapping tables. (b) Block groups still serve as a _locality hint_ to the device — grouping logically-related writes helps the SSD's garbage collector. (c) The block group structure is good for _failure isolation_ : a corrupted bitmap only costs you one group, not the whole file system. Finally (d), RAID and layering devices above SSDs (like LVM, dm-crypt, file-servers-over-network) reintroduce seek-like costs, so pure SSD assumptions may be brittle.

The best decision: retain block groups (for failure isolation and as a locality hint) but relax the clustering policy — allow any free extent in any group, since the seek cost is gone. This is roughly what XFS does, and it is a good middle ground.

**Q10.** Both memory management (Chapters 9–15) and file systems (this chapter) use the word "allocation" and both manage variable-length objects in a fixed-size-block medium. List three concrete parallels between the two problems. Then list one place where they diverge — a problem one faces that the other does not — and explain why the divergence exists.

**Answer:** Three parallels: (1) Both use indirect indexing: page tables map virtual pages to physical pages; inodes map logical file offsets to disk blocks. Both are trees of pointers. (2) Both suffer from fragmentation and use similar techniques: external fragmentation in memory ↔ disk fragmentation; memory's segmentation approach ↔ file systems' extent-based approach. (3) Both have caches that absorb access cost: TLBs for page tables ↔ page cache for file data.

One divergence: file systems must survive _crashes_. Memory is volatile; if the power fails, everyone's state is lost and no one complains. But file system state must be consistent across a crash that happens in the middle of an update. This forces file systems to care about _ordering_ of writes (which we will see in Chapter 25) in a way that memory management never does. The reason is the layer's purpose: file systems exist _because_ of persistence, while memory management exists _despite_ the absence of persistence. The same mechanical problem (allocate variable-length objects from fixed-size blocks) leads to different engineering concerns when durability is added to the requirements.

---

## Chapter 25. Crash Consistency — Journaling and Log-Structured File Systems

Every file system operation we have studied so far — creating a file, writing a block, extending a directory — actually involves several disk writes. Creating a file updates the inode bitmap, writes the new inode, and modifies the parent directory's data block. Appending to a file updates the data bitmap, writes a new data block, and updates the inode's size and pointers. These are multi-write operations.

Disks do not perform multi-write operations. A disk performs writes one at a time. Some of those writes complete; some are in flight. Then, inevitably, something goes wrong: the power fails, the kernel panics, a disk cable is yanked. The file system reboots with a partially-completed operation on its hands. Half an inode exists, pointing to data blocks that were never allocated. Or the data blocks were allocated but the inode doesn't point to them, and they are neither used nor free. Or the parent directory references an inode that was never created.

This chapter is about the problem of crash consistency: how does a file system ensure that, whatever state the disk is in when the power returns, the file system can recover to a coherent view? There are two main approaches, and each produced a family of real file systems. **Journaling** writes intentions ahead of actions; the recovery step replays the log. **Log-structured file systems (LFS)** never modify data in place at all — every update is an append to a log, and the file system is effectively a running log of operations.

We will see both approaches, and we will see why journaling won the engineering race in the 1990s–2000s (all mainstream Linux file systems are journaled) while LFS ideas won the conceptual race (they shape how flash-friendly file systems, databases, and version control systems are designed today).

* * *

### 25.1 What Breaks Without Crash Consistency?

Consider appending one block to file F in vsfs. The operation requires three disk writes:

  1. Write the new data block (call it D).
  2. Write the updated inode for F (I), whose pointer now includes D's block number and whose size has grown by 4 KB.
  3. Write the updated data bitmap (B), with D's bit flipped from free to allocated.

What can the crash interrupt? Any subset of these writes may have completed when the power cuts. Let's enumerate the eight subsets and, for each, state what the resulting on-disk state means and how a journaling file system (Section 25.3 onward) handles it.

_Crash 0 — nothing written._ The operation simply never happened. Consistent; no recovery needed under any scheme.

_Crash 1 — only D written._ The data block exists with the new bytes, but B still says that block is free (so the allocator might hand it to another file, corrupting F's content) and I does not point to it. Outcome: D is orphaned garbage until overwritten. No data loss of existing F content, but the new append is lost. _Under journaling:_ if the transaction's commit record is not on disk, recovery discards the log entry and the orphaned D is left as free bytes (later reclaimed harmlessly when its block is rewritten). If the commit _is_ on disk, replay re-writes I and B to match D.

_Crash 2 — only I written._ F's inode now claims a block that B says is free. When the allocator later hands that block to another file G, both F and G's inodes point to the same block. Writes to G corrupt F's data, or vice versa. This is the worst kind of corruption: silent, shared-block, damaging files unrelated to the crash. _Under journaling:_ this state cannot persist — journaling forbids writing I before the commit record, so after recovery either the commit is present and replay re-writes D and B, producing a consistent state, or the commit is absent and replay discards the speculative I update.

_Crash 3 — only B written._ B says the block is allocated but I doesn't point to it. The block is permanently leaked — never freed, never used. Slow space drain but no data corruption. _Under journaling:_ same logic as Crash 2; either replay completes the transaction or the isolated B update is undone.

_Crash 4 — D and I but not B._ F's inode correctly points to D, and D has the right bytes. But B says D is free, so the allocator will hand D to the next file that needs a block. Silent shared-block corruption again. _Under journaling:_ commit-record presence decides replay vs discard, both of which restore consistency.

_Crash 5 — D and B but not I._ Block is allocated and has valid bytes, but no inode claims it — permanent leak. _Under journaling:_ replay or discard.

_Crash 6 — I and B but not D._ F's inode points to D and B marks it allocated, but D was never written, so it contains stale bytes. F silently reads garbage from its own file. This is the scenario that **ordered-mode journaling** specifically defends against: it forces D to disk _before_ the metadata commit, so this crash state cannot produce stale-data reads. _Under full data journaling:_ the log contains D's intended bytes; replay writes D correctly.

_Crash 7 — all three complete._ Perfectly consistent. Great.

Seven of the eight possibilities leave the file system inconsistent in the non-journaled world. A naive file system, after a crash, cannot tell which possibility occurred — it has lost the _intent_. Journaling's contribution is precisely to store the intent ahead of the writes, so recovery can always decide "replay" or "discard" from on-disk evidence alone.

* * *

**Pattern.** The crash consistency problem is a distributed systems problem in miniature. Multiple state updates must all happen atomically with respect to external observation (reboot), but the medium (disk) offers only single-write atomicity. The trick, as with distributed commit protocols, is to arrange that after a crash you can either _complete_ the operation or _undo_ it, not be stuck halfway.

* * *

### 25.2 The Pre-Journaling World: fsck

Before journaling, Unix systems ran a program called **fsck** (file system check) after every unclean shutdown. fsck walked the entire file system: scanning the inode table, reading every inode, validating the block pointers against the data bitmap, checking that directory entries pointed to valid inodes, checking that link counts matched the number of directory references.

fsck could fix some inconsistencies. If a data block was claimed by an inode but marked free in the bitmap, fsck marked it allocated. If a block was marked allocated but no inode claimed it, fsck added it to the **lost+found** directory (well, if it contained recognizable data; otherwise it was silently reclaimed). If link counts were wrong, fsck fixed them.

Two things were terrible about fsck.

First, it took a long time. A full fsck on a 1 GB disk took 10–20 minutes in the 1990s. On a 1 TB modern disk, it would take hours. For a server that must reboot quickly, this was intolerable.

Second, fsck could not recover user data. If I was updated but D was not, fsck had no way to know what bytes _should_ have been in D. The inode claims a block; the block has stale content. fsck doesn't know that's wrong; the file system looks internally consistent.

Worse, fsck assumes most of the metadata is correct. A single-bit error in a crucial superblock field could confuse fsck into making the file system worse.

The industry needed a faster, more correct approach. That approach was journaling.

* * *

### 25.3 Journaling: Write the Intention First

The core idea: before modifying any on-disk structure, first write a **log record** (or journal entry) describing what you are about to do. The journal is a dedicated region of the disk, treated as a circular buffer. Only after the log record is safely on disk do you begin the actual modifications. If a crash happens during the modifications, recovery replays the log entries, completing any half-finished operations.

This is **write-ahead logging** (WAL), borrowed from databases.

For our append-one-block example, journaling turns three writes into six — but with a guarantee.

**Phase 1: Journal write.**

  1. Write a transaction-begin record: TxBegin.
  2. Write the planned new bytes of D (into the log).
  3. Write the planned new inode I (into the log).
  4. Write the planned new bitmap B (into the log).
  5. Write a transaction-end record: TxEnd.

**Phase 2: Checkpoint (apply to actual locations).** 6\. Write D to its real location. 7\. Write I to its real location. 8\. Write B to its real location.

**Phase 3: Free the log entry.** 9\. Mark the log region as free.

During recovery: scan the journal. For each transaction that has both TxBegin and TxEnd, replay its contents by applying the changes to the real locations. If TxEnd is missing, the transaction was not committed — discard it. The partial writes to real locations that may have happened mid-crash get re-applied from the log, ensuring completion.

This works because the log is _idempotent_ : replaying a complete transaction after a partial checkpoint is safe. Either the real locations already have the right bytes (and the replay writes the same bytes), or they don't (and the replay fixes them).

* * *

**Definition:** **Journaling** is the technique of recording a description of each planned file-system update to a sequential log before performing the update, so that after a crash, incomplete transactions can be either replayed to completion or discarded based on whether they have an end marker.

* * *

### 25.4 Why a Commit Record Is Essential

The TxEnd record is often written as a single **commit record** whose presence on disk is the atomic marker that says "this transaction is complete and can be replayed." Without it, recovery has no way to distinguish a fully-logged transaction from a half-logged one.

A subtlety: disks may reorder writes. If you submit writes in the order TxBegin, D, I, B, TxEnd, the disk may actually write them as I, B, TxBegin, TxEnd, D. If a crash occurs after TxEnd is on disk but before D, recovery will find a valid-looking transaction whose log content is corrupt. The replay writes the "old" D (whatever bytes are in the log entry for D).

File systems defend against this by issuing a **flush barrier** before the commit record. The barrier ensures all earlier log writes are durable before TxEnd is written. This is why `fsync` is expensive: it triggers flush barriers.

With the barrier, the guarantee is: if TxEnd is on disk, all of D, I, B (in the log) are on disk, and replay will correctly produce the final state.

* * *

#### Common Misconception

_"Journaling doubles the write cost."_ It does, in the worst case, but the actual cost is much less because: (a) log writes are sequential, which is fast on all devices; (b) multiple small transactions are batched into a single commit, amortizing the barrier cost; (c) the journal is typically small (a few hundred MB) and mostly sits in the page cache. In practice, journaling adds 10–30% write overhead, a price everyone accepts for the crash safety.

* * *

### 25.5 Data Journaling vs Metadata Journaling

A complete journal includes the data blocks (D above) as well as metadata (I and B). This is **data journaling**. It is maximally safe: after recovery, both file content and file system structure are consistent.

But logging every data block doubles disk writes: each write goes first to the log, then to its real location. For a file-copy workload, this is 2× the bandwidth. Too expensive for most systems.

Most Linux file systems default to **metadata journaling** : log only inode, bitmap, directory, and other metadata updates — not the file contents themselves. The log guarantees that the _file system structure_ is consistent after a crash, but individual file contents may be stale (the old bytes) or garbage (never written). The crash-safety claim is weaker: "after a crash, `fsck` is not needed and the file system mounts cleanly, but some files may have incomplete updates."

ext3 has three modes:

* **journal** (data journaling): log everything. Safest, slowest.
* **ordered** (metadata journaling with ordering): log metadata; write data blocks _before_ their metadata is committed. This prevents the "inode points to unallocated block with stale bytes" outcome (Crash 6 above). Balanced; Linux default.
* **writeback** (metadata journaling without ordering): log metadata; write data whenever, in any order. Fastest, least safe — you can get garbage in files after a crash.

The **ordered** mode is the sweet spot most systems use. It combines metadata safety with data ordering, at modest performance cost.

**How "ordering" is actually enforced.** The word "before" above is not an application-level convention — it is an I/O-layer guarantee implemented by a **write barrier** (a.k.a. cache flush / FUA — Force Unit Access). The sequence the kernel issues for one ordered-mode transaction is:

  1. Dispatch the data-block writes for D to the disk (they enter the disk's write cache, may be reordered internally).
  2. Issue a _barrier_ : the block layer sends a FLUSH (and/or sets FUA on subsequent writes), forcing the disk to commit everything currently in its write cache to stable media before acknowledging further writes.
  3. Only after the barrier acknowledgement, write the metadata-update entries and the commit record into the journal.
  4. Issue another barrier so the commit record itself is durable before checkpoint writes proceed.

Without step 2's barrier, "ordered" would be honored only in the kernel's submission order, not in the on-disk durability order — the disk is free to reorder writes in its volatile cache. Barriers are exactly what converts submission-order into durability-order. This is also why `fsync` is expensive (it triggers one of these barriers), and why disks with broken or lying FLUSH behavior have historically caused silent file-system corruption on real hardware.

* * *

**Pattern.** Whenever a consistency property is too expensive to enforce fully, find the _partial_ version that gives the most important guarantees. Metadata journaling with ordering gives file system integrity (no leaks, no corruption) without paying for data journaling's full cost. This trade-off appears elsewhere: eventual consistency in distributed systems, read-committed isolation in databases, weak memory models in CPUs.

* * *

### 25.6 Log-Structured File Systems: Treat the Disk as a Log

In 1992, Rosenblum and Ousterhout proposed a different approach: **log-structured file systems (LFS)**. Their argument: if journaling writes everything to a log and then also to real locations, why not skip the second step and just _always write to the log_?

In LFS, the entire disk is a log. Writes happen at a single location — the **tail** of the log. All writes are sequential. A write to file F is handled by appending the new data and the new inode _to the end of the log_. The inode's location in the log becomes F's current inode, replacing the old one.

This gives incredible write throughput: all writes are sequential, which is the disk's best case. There are no seeks for writes. Small random updates become large sequential ones.

The challenge: reads. To find file F's inode, we need to know where F's current inode lives in the log. Since the inode moves every time F is modified, its location is not fixed. LFS solves this with an **inode map** , a small table mapping inode numbers to their current log locations. The inode map is itself updated by appending a new version to the log; its location is recorded in a **checkpoint region** at a known position.

The real problem is **free space**. As writes append forever, the log fills the disk. Old versions of files are still in the log but no longer referenced. LFS needs a **cleaner** (like a garbage collector) that scans old log regions, copies still-live data to the current tail, and reclaims the old regions as free. The cleaner is complex and its cost can dominate.

* * *

**Definition:** A **log-structured file system** treats the entire disk as an append-only log, so every write is sequential. A garbage-collecting cleaner reclaims space occupied by old (superseded) data. A checkpoint region and an inode map allow reads to find the current version of each file.

* * *

#### Why LFS ideas endured even though LFS mostly lost

LFS was implemented in Sprite (a research OS), but never became a dominant production file system for general-purpose disks. Journaled file systems like ext3 and XFS offered 80% of the benefit with much less complexity, and the cleaner's cost proved unpleasant in real workloads.

But the idea — write sequentially, clean later — is now everywhere.

_Flash SSDs_ are internally log-structured. Flash can be written only to erased pages, and erasing happens in large blocks. SSD firmware implements a log-structured flash translation layer (FTL) that maps logical blocks to physical pages and runs an internal cleaner. Every SSD you have ever used is running LFS concepts inside.

_Modern file systems for flash_ like F2FS (used in Android) are directly log-structured. ZFS and btrfs use copy-on-write, which is a close cousin.

_Databases_ use log-structured merge-trees (LSM-trees) for write-heavy workloads. LevelDB, RocksDB, Cassandra, and HBase all use LSM-trees, and LSM is LFS for key-value stores.

_Distributed systems_ like Kafka, Kinesis, and write-ahead logs in general are append-only logs with retention policies and replication — LFS on a cluster.

Rosenblum and Ousterhout's insight — _prefer sequential writes at the cost of more complex reads_ — is one of the most consequential ideas in systems software, even if LFS itself is not the file system on your laptop.

* * *

### 25.7 Copy-on-Write: The Modern Peer of Journaling

Journaling and CoW are the two production-grade answers to the crash-consistency problem in use today. **Copy-on-write (CoW)** file systems — ZFS (Sun/Oracle, since 2005), btrfs (Linux, production since ~2014), APFS (Apple, shipped on every Mac and iPhone since 2017) — are not a niche third option. Between macOS, iOS, and large-scale storage servers, more end-user bytes live on CoW file systems today than on any single journaled file system; what keeps journaling dominant on Linux servers is ext4's maturity and predictability, not a clear technical advantage.

In a CoW file system, _no block is ever overwritten_. To modify a data block, the file system allocates a new block, writes the updated content, and then updates the inode to point at the new block. But updating the inode is itself a write; rather than overwrite the inode, CoW writes a new inode. That means the directory containing the inode must also be updated — again, without overwriting. The update propagates up to the root of the file system tree.

The file system's root pointer (the _über-block_ in ZFS) is stored in a single location. _That_ pointer is the only thing updated in place, and it is updated atomically (a single 8-byte write protected by checksums). Before the root swap, the new version is invisible; after the swap, it is fully visible. No intermediate inconsistent state can persist.

**Why CoW is often the superior design on modern hardware.** Several features fall out "for free" that journaled file systems have to add as separate machinery or cannot offer at all:

* **End-to-end data checksums.** Because every block is rewritten on modification, CoW file systems can checksum each block and propagate the checksums up the tree. The root's checksum thus validates the entire state. Silent bit-rot on the disk is detectable and, on mirrored storage, repairable.
* **Near-free snapshots.** A snapshot is just "keep a previous root pointer alive." No data has to be copied.
* **Atomic transactions beyond single writes.** The root-swap commits _all_ buffered modifications at once, which naturally extends to multi-file operations.
* **Self-healing on redundant storage.** ZFS and btrfs can silently correct a bad block from its mirror using the block's own checksum as the oracle.

The trade-off: every write propagates to the root of the tree, so small writes touch many blocks (write amplification). Reference counting (or a garbage collector) reclaims old versions once no one uses them. On spinning disks the write amplification hurts; on SSDs — where the hardware is already log-structured internally — CoW's write patterns align naturally with what the device wants.

* * *

**Pattern.** Three techniques for crash consistency, all solving the same problem with different trade-offs:

* **Journaling** : modify in place, but record intentions first. _Good for small updates, modest overhead._
* **Log-structured** : never modify in place; always append. _Good for write-heavy workloads, needs cleaning._
* **Copy-on-write** : never modify in place; update via tree copy with atomic root swap. _Good for snapshots and checksums, costs write amplification._

Different applications pick different answers.

* * *

### 25.8 The Ordering Problem and Why fsync Is Slow

Everything in this chapter has hinged on _ordering_ : writes must happen in a specific sequence relative to each other, and a commit record must reach disk _after_ the writes it commits. The OS controls the sequence via **flush barriers**. `fsync(fd)` triggers a flush barrier for fd's data plus whatever metadata is needed.

Modern disks, both HDD and SSD, have caches. When a program writes, the data goes into the disk's cache, and the disk reports success. But the bytes are not yet on stable storage. A power failure at this moment loses them. The flush barrier tells the disk "push your cache to the platters/flash and confirm." This takes milliseconds on HDD, hundreds of microseconds on SSD.

A database that calls `fsync` per transaction is bottlenecked by the flush rate. On an HDD (~10 ms per flush), you get ~100 transactions/second. On SSD (~0.1 ms), you get ~10,000. Faster storage (NVMe SSD, Optane, battery-backed RAM) is the modern solution; database-level techniques like group commit (batch many transactions into one flush) are the software solution.

Some disks lie about flushes for benchmarks. If you buy a consumer disk, confirm that it honors flushes correctly. Writing a database or file system on a lying disk gives excellent performance and random data loss.

* * *

#### Common Misconception

_"fsync is a software feature; it always works."_ No — fsync's guarantee depends on the disk honoring flush requests. Cheap disks with dishonest firmware will report "flushed" without actually flushing, in order to look faster. This is why enterprise disks have (often battery-backed) write caches: the write is "durable" the moment it reaches the battery-backed RAM, and the flush cost vanishes. Without a battery, the disk must do a physical flush, which is slow.

* * *

### 25.9 Summary

A crash in the middle of a multi-write file system update leaves the disk in a state that is not a valid file system: inodes may reference unallocated blocks, data may be orphaned, bitmaps may be out of sync. The pre-journaling fix was to run a long, imperfect repair program (fsck) at every boot.

Journaling reframes the problem: before modifying the real data structures, write the intended update to a sequential log. After a crash, replay the log — committed transactions get applied, incomplete ones get discarded. Data journaling logs everything; metadata journaling with ordering logs metadata while ensuring data writes happen first, giving most of the safety at a fraction of the cost.

Log-structured file systems go further: treat the entire disk as a log, write only sequentially, and run a cleaner to reclaim stale space. LFS itself is rare today, but the idea shapes flash firmware, write-optimized databases (LSM-trees), and distributed logs.

Copy-on-write offers a third approach: never overwrite, propagate updates to the root, swap roots atomically. Used by ZFS, btrfs, APFS.

All three strategies rely on _ordering guarantees_ from the storage hardware. Flush barriers are the mechanism that makes the ordering real; `fsync` is how applications request them; slow fsync is the cost of durability.

Chapter 26 closes the book with a view of where these ideas extend: virtualization (whole OSes running inside OSes), containers (lightweight isolation built from namespaces and cgroups), and distributed systems (the consequences when one machine's persistence guarantee is not enough).

* * *

### 25.10 Exercises

#### Concept Check

**Q1.** Describe in two sentences what would happen after a crash if a file system performed only metadata journaling without write ordering, and a user had been copying a 1 GB video into the file system.

**Answer:** The file system structure would be consistent — the file's inode, size, and block pointers would agree. But the data blocks pointed to by the inode might contain stale bytes from previously-deleted files (or whatever was there before), because the metadata journal committed before the actual data writes reached disk. The user would open the video file and see garbage or content from some unrelated, deleted file.

**Q2.** Why is the commit record the last thing written in a journal transaction, and why does writing it require a flush barrier first?

**Answer:** The commit record's presence is the atomic marker that says "all the other log records for this transaction are valid and can be replayed." Writing it first would risk a crash that leaves a committed-looking transaction with missing or partial log entries, which recovery would incorrectly replay. The flush barrier ensures that all earlier log records are durably on disk before the commit record exists; only with that ordering does the commit record's presence reliably imply the transaction's log is complete.

**Q3.** What does fsck do on a journaled file system, and why is it much faster than on an unjournaled one?

**Answer:** On a journaled file system, fsck mostly just replays the journal — a sequential scan of a small region (often under 100 MB). It replays committed transactions and discards incomplete ones. It does not walk the entire inode table or verify every block pointer. Unjournaled fsck must walk the whole file system, which scales with disk size. On a 1 TB disk, unjournaled fsck can take hours; journaled recovery takes seconds.

**Q4.** Explain how ext3's "ordered" mode protects against a specific failure scenario that "writeback" mode does not.

**Answer:** In writeback mode, metadata is journaled but data writes are unordered relative to metadata commits. A file's inode can be committed (pointing at a newly-allocated block) before the data for that block is written. After a crash, the file's inode points at a block whose content is stale or garbage. Ordered mode forces data writes to complete _before_ their metadata's journal commit, so after a crash, any metadata that claims data exists will actually have correct data backing it. The file might be missing an append (if the entire transaction didn't commit), but it will not contain silent garbage.

#### Application Problems

**Q5.** A file system uses data journaling. Computing the write bandwidth: a user writes 1 GB of data. How many bytes are actually written to the disk, including all journal and checkpoint activity? Assume metadata overhead is negligible.

**Answer:** 2 GB. The 1 GB is first written to the journal (1 GB), then later checkpointed to its real location (1 GB). This 2× overhead is the reason data journaling is rarely the default. (Metadata journaling writes the data once to its real location and logs only the small metadata updates, so the overhead is under 10% typically.)

**Q6.** Design a group-commit scheme for a database whose workload is 10,000 small transactions per second, each needing fsync. Describe the algorithm and the trade-off.

**Answer:** A good sketch:

* Each transaction, after writing its log record, does _not_ issue fsync immediately. It enqueues itself in a pending list.
* A single dedicated thread batches pending transactions: every T microseconds (e.g., 1 ms) or when N transactions have accumulated (e.g., 100), it issues one fsync covering all their log records.
* All batched transactions complete only after the fsync returns. Their return is delayed by up to T microseconds, but they share one fsync instead of 10,000 separate ones.

Trade-off: per-transaction latency rises by ~1 ms (waiting for the batch), but throughput can rise from ~100 txns/sec (on HDD) to several thousand because fsync calls are amortized. This is exactly what MySQL's `innodb_flush_log_at_trx_commit` controls and what every production database implements.

**Q7.** An LFS file system has run for six months on a 1 TB disk. The user has written a total of 2 TB over that time (most writes overwriting old versions). How much live data is on the disk, and what does the cleaner's workload look like?

**Answer:** Live data depends on how much was overwritten — but at most 1 TB (the disk size), and likely less (~500 GB if the working set is heavily rewritten). The log contains 1 TB of a mix of live data and stale versions. The cleaner must scan log segments, identify which blocks are still referenced by the current inode map, copy the live ones to the log's tail, and mark the old segment free. Its work is proportional to _stale_ bytes, which on a rewrite-heavy workload is large. The cleaner's CPU and I/O cost is LFS's main performance risk — if cleaning can't keep up with writes, the log fills and new writes block.

**Q8.** Compare the disk write amplification of three approaches for a workload that updates 4 KB of data: (a) in-place overwrite, (b) metadata journaling (ext3 ordered), (c) copy-on-write (ZFS-style).

**Answer:**

* **In-place overwrite** : writes the 4 KB data block. ~4 KB.
* **Metadata journaling (ordered)** : writes the 4 KB data block, then writes log metadata (~1 KB) then checkpoints metadata (~1 KB). Total ~6 KB.
* **Copy-on-write** : writes a new 4 KB data block, then a new inode (4 KB — inodes live in 4 KB blocks in COW systems), then new directory block (4 KB), ..., up to the root. Depth of tree typically 3–5. Total 16–20 KB.

COW's write amplification is the largest but buys snapshot support and strong integrity (every block is checksummed and the root swap is atomic). Journaling pays modest amplification for safety. In-place pays nothing but loses consistency on crash.

#### Think Deeper

**Q9.** Both write-ahead logging in databases and journaling in file systems solve "multi-step update that must survive a crash." In both cases the log is written _before_ the data. Yet databases and file systems evolved mostly independently — DB research from the 1970s onward, FS research with journaling appearing in the 1990s. Why did the file system community take so long to adopt WAL, given that database researchers had already solved the problem?

**Answer:** This is a speculative question with several reasonable answers.

* Hardware changed. In the 1980s, HDDs were small (10s of MB) and fsck's cost was tolerable. In the 1990s, disks grew to GBs, fsck became impractical, and the industry needed an alternative. Necessity drove adoption.
* File systems have harder failure patterns than databases. A database controls all access through one engine; a file system is accessed concurrently by every program. File systems had to invent their own version of WAL that worked with the existing kernel interfaces.
* Backward compatibility. New file systems had to mount old disks. It's easier to retrofit than to redesign.
* Research diffusion. The systems community and database community had limited overlap until the 1990s. ACM SIGMOD and USENIX rarely shared papers then.

The best answer engages with two or three of these plausibly.

**Q10.** Argue for or against the following claim: "In ten years, all file systems will be log-structured, because SSDs are log-structured internally and any layer above them that does in-place updates is redundant."

**Answer:** A good argument for: flash's internal FTL already implements LFS. A host file system that writes in-place is making the FTL do extra work — it maps the "in-place" update to a new flash page anyway. A natively log-structured host file system matches the hardware and can coordinate with the FTL (via TRIM, zoned namespaces, or ZNS SSDs) to reduce double-garbage-collection overhead.

A good argument against: LFS was invented once, mostly failed once, and has succeeded commercially only in specialized contexts (Android F2FS, some storage appliances). Journaled file systems are good enough, well-understood, and entrenched. The write amplification gap between journaling and LFS, on modern NVMe SSDs with parallelism, is small — a few percent. The complexity of LFS (cleaner, inode map, checkpoint) is not worth it for a small win. Meanwhile, ZNS and direct-flash interfaces are moving toward letting database engines bypass the file system entirely for performance-critical paths, not toward making the file system more LFS-like.

The best answer notices that "log-structured" and "copy-on-write" share many properties, and that btrfs/ZFS/APFS are already something like log-structured for their metadata, even if they don't advertise it. The trajectory is not "LFS wins" but "the distinction blurs."

---

## Chapter 26. Virtualization, Containers, and Distributed Systems

Twenty-five chapters ago, we began with a single promise: the operating system is the abstraction layer between bare metal and programs, and its job is to create illusions — of dedicated CPUs, of infinite memory, of reliable storage — on top of hardware that provides none of these things.

That was the OS of the 1990s and 2000s: one kernel running on one machine, multiplexing its resources among many user programs.

The OS of the 2020s looks different. Your laptop runs macOS, but the Python project you are developing also runs inside a Docker container on Linux, which itself may be running inside a Lima VM on macOS, which is hosted on an Apple M-series chip that virtualizes its own cores. Your work eventually deploys to AWS, where a Kubernetes cluster orchestrates containers across hundreds of machines, each of which is a VM on top of a hypervisor on top of physical hardware in a data center. Every layer uses OS concepts — isolation, scheduling, virtual memory, file systems, concurrency — and each layer adds new ones.

This closing chapter surveys three such layers built on top of the single-machine OS story we have told: **virtual machines** , which run a complete OS inside another OS; **containers** , which share one kernel among many isolated userlands; and **distributed systems** , where the abstraction is no longer "this machine's resources" but "the cluster's resources." All three are natural extensions of the concepts this book developed. The goal of the chapter is not to teach you Docker or Kubernetes — it is to show you that you already know most of what makes them work.

* * *

### 26.1 Why Virtualize Again?
